While you read
  1. Ch 16–19: the money turns up. She copies one figure three times before she can make herself get it right, then finds a class the parish stopped paying fourteen months before her mother died. Clear on the page, or does it read like an accountant's chapter?
  2. Ch 20–23: the ring, the word Idle, her father's answer, then the lie. Four chapters of rooms and drawers in a row — does the escalation carry them, or does the book sit down here?
  3. Ch 24–26: she crosses the bridge and does not knock. The failure is meant to be the not-knocking. Does it read that way, or does the crossing take the weight?
  4. Ch 27: the book in the mattress, seventy-six rows, and the discovery that her mother could write. Did you see it coming from ch 1, or does it feel planted late?
  5. Ch 28–29: she signs for herself, and the office shows her three pages, the third with a name scraped off it and two letters left. In 29 she works out that the scraped line four above her own entry in the parish book carries the same two letters, which means one hand had both books on the same table in 1806, which means the office she belongs to was taken out of the book on purpose. Is that a find or a theory?
  6. The promise on page one: she says she died and the whole of it was given back, and ch 31 finally says what that bought — one winter with the book, and not her mother's life. Enough for a four-book series, or does the return still feel like a frame rather than a plot?
  7. Ch 19: she goes to the vestry with her hands empty and asks Rooke a question about a dead person's quarter. He tells her to bring him a paper and she never does. Is that enough of a runway for what he does in 28?
  8. Ch 31: five days, four lines, and then the two things she has not let herself know — that the lock was never turned, so her mother needed no key, and that her father read that hand in 1828 and never said a word. Does the reveal land as a turn, or does it arrive too late to be believed?
  9. Ch 31, last page: she stops and decides to go and ask, and takes the basin off the shelf, and knocks twice. Too neat a closing gesture, or the right one?
  10. Length: about 71,800 words in thirty-one chapters. Ch 31 now runs long to carry the reveal and the decision. Is the speed at the end right, or is it thin?
A Literary Gothic Mystery  ·  The Register of Uses, Book One

What She Was Made For

Her mother walked twelve miles in the dark once a month for twenty years. Nobody in the house knew. The parish register says she never left it.
1

The Fifth of March

My mother died upstairs while I was downstairs keeping a clock.

I have been given that morning twice.

The first time I had the hood off the long-case in the passage and the weights in my lap, and I heard her go, and I sat still, and I counted to nine before I stood up, and by then there was nothing to be done that could not be done just as well in the morning.

The second time I put the weights down on the flag and went up.

I am not going to dress it up. I died too. It was not dramatic and I have nothing to add. There was a great deal of paper in my life and very little of it was about me, and when I was done with it I was put in the ground in a parish I had never chosen, and the man who read over me got my name right, which was more than most.

Then I was here, in my own bed, eighteen years old, with the light in the wrong place.

The room was mine at eighteen. Same jug, same chip in the rim, same square of brown paper pasted over the crack in the window. My hands were a girl’s hands. The knuckle of my right forefinger had no groove worn into it, and it was the absence of that groove I believed first, before the room, before the light, before I had thought of my mother at all.

It was the fifth of March. I was eighteen years and a day old.

My mother had eleven months.

I got as far as the third one on the quilt and stopped, because counting is what I do when I am frightened and I did not want to spend my morning at it.

Then I laughed. It came out of me there in the dark of my own room and I put my hand over my mouth, because it was six in the morning and my mother was asleep, and there was nothing funny in that house at all.

I have thought since that the laugh was the only sensible thing I did that day. A woman who is given a year back and spends it being careful has misunderstood the gift.

✦

The stair had a third step that turned under the ball of the foot. The landing window had the same crack, mended with paper and flour paste, gone brown at the corners. There was a dead moth on the sill that had been there since the autumn before. I had never swept it up in nineteen years. I did not sweep it up now.

My mother’s door stood two inches open, the way she left it when she slept.

I am good at doorways. I can stand in one for a quarter of an hour and look like a woman deciding whether to come in. What I am actually doing is counting, usually, or listening to a clock, or waiting for somebody else to speak first, which is the same thing with better manners.

She was on her side with one arm out of the covers. Her cap had come loose and there was a grey plait on the pillow, thin at the end, tied with a bootlace. A shirt hung over the chair with one sleeve turned and the needle still in it. My father’s shirt. She would go on mending it until the day she died, and she did, and I have the shirt.

My mother was forty-one and had the face of a woman who had been forty-one for years.

The thimble was on the sill where she left it when the light went. Brass, worn smooth on top, too big for her, which she said was a comfort because it meant she could get it off.

I picked it up.

It is not a vision. It is not a dream, and it is not a memory, and I have never described it to a clergyman without being offered tea and a woman to talk to. It is more like a room remembering its own use.

A hand came up out of the dark at the side of the bed. Her hand, but not as I was looking at it. Her hand doing a thing. Needle in, point through, the little bite of it, the thread drawn out with a snap you could hear. Poor light. Fast, and no misses.

Somewhere behind it a fire was being fed with something that had not quite dried. The smell came before the warmth.

Then a sleeve went along the sill, and the dust on the sill turned over in a line and lay in an arc, the way dust lies when an arm has been through it.

It was a thimble again. My mother was breathing, the room was cold, and the arc was still in the dust.

Three seconds. It is always three seconds. I have timed it with a watch I had just mended, which is either the most scientific thing I have ever done or the most stupid.

Here is the rule, and I will put it down as I learned it, which was not all at once.

A thing will tell you what it was made for. Give me a thimble and I will show you a woman sewing. Give me a key, and I will show you a door being locked, and the hand that locked it, and whether the hand hurried. Dead things are honest. They have no reason not to be.

Living things are not honest, and I cannot read them, and I have never once tried, and that is the one rule I have never broken.

Something comes away with me, too. Not the thing itself. A sort of owing. I put the thimble back where it had been and stood with my hand on the sill for no reason at all, waiting for something that had already finished.

The arc stays. That is the part I could never explain to a bishop’s man without sounding like a woman who has been at the gin.

✦

She woke at half past seven, the way she always did, and lay looking at the ceiling for a while before she remembered she had a body.

“You’re dressed,” she said.

“I’ve been up.”

“Since when?”

“Six.”

She turned her head on the pillow. Her eyes were the same as ever, pale and quick. People took the flatness of them for coldness. It was attention.

“Is the fire in?”

“It’s laid.”

“Then light it, girl, don’t stand there describing it.”

I lit the fire. She watched me do it, and said nothing about the kindling being too big, which was how I knew she was tired.

When it caught, she said, “There’s bread in the tin. Cut it thick and don’t give your father the end piece, he’ll say it’s the best and then he’ll want it every day.”

I cut the bread. She ate half of hers and put the rest on the plate and turned it so the bitten edge was facing the wall, which she did so that nobody would see how little she had eaten, and which everybody saw.

“The bishop’s man comes a week on Thursday,” I said.

“Mm.”

“What will he want?”

“The accounts. Your father’s throat. A look at the book.” She turned her cup round without drinking from it. “Well,” she said. “A week.”

“What will you say?”

I had a mouthful of bread and I used it. When I had swallowed I said, “What does anybody say.”

She looked at me. She had a way of looking at you that was not unkind and did not stop.

“They say what they were told to say,” she said.

She turned her face to the wall.

There was a space of time in that room. I have measured a great many silences in my life, since, because measuring is what I do with a thing I cannot mend, and I would put that one at eleven seconds. The fire took a stick. Somewhere down the road a cart went by with a loose iron on it.

I had four words ready. I had had them ready for eleven months, these eleven months, spent once already, and not one of them used.

I opened my mouth.

What I said was, “Do you want the window open?”

“No,” she said. “I want my shawl.”

“Where is it?”

“Behind you. The chest. Under the winter things.”

The chest was at the foot of her bed and the lid had been heavy since I was a child. Sheets, then the winter things, then a brown paper parcel of something that had been a dress. At the bottom, under all of it, a piece of blue cloth folded round something hard.

The cloth was the only thing in that house that smelled of the road. Outside, weather, dust off a cart, somebody else’s parish. I got it up in both hands. It was tied with string, and the knot was a proper one, made by somebody who expected to untie it again.

“Whose is this?”

She did not turn over. “Leave that.”

“It’s not—“

“Leave it, Mercy.”

She never told me not to do a thing. Not once, in eighteen years. She told me what to eat and to keep my hands out of the flour and to stand up straight when the bishop’s man came, and that was the whole of her authority over me, and all of it was on my side.

So I put it back under the winter things and let the lid down on it.

It went on the list. Third, I think. Fourth at the latest.

✦

The list is a thing I keep in my head because paper is not safe in a house with a parish in it. It has forty-one items on it now. That morning it had two: the draught in the chimney, and the blue cloth.

By nine there was a third, and she was standing at the door with her hands in her apron.

“Hettie Fenn,” she said, as if I might not know her, which I did, because I had copied her mother’s line into the book four times a year since I was fourteen.

Her mother had been nine days in the ground. The office was washing, and it was worth four pounds eleven a year and a coal allowance, and in the other life it had gone to a woman from the next parish who had a cousin on the board, and Hettie Fenn had gone into service at twelve miles and I had not thought about her again for nineteen years.

“I want it,” she said.

“The board gives it.”

“The board gives what’s written.” She had a way of standing with her weight on one hip, like a woman holding a child that was not there. “You write.”

“I copy.”

“You write fair. My mother said. She said the fair copy is what goes up.”

That was true, and it was the sort of true that costs a person something to say out loud in a doorway at nine in the morning. I stood in the door and looked at her and did the arithmetic I always do, which is not about money.

If I wrote her name in the column, the board would have to argue with a written thing. Boards do not argue with written things. They argue with people, and they win, and a written thing they can only strike out, and striking out takes a meeting and a reason and a man from Carrow, and men from Carrow do not come for a washerwoman.

If I did not write it, the cousin’s wife would have it by Lady Day and Hettie Fenn would be gone by Whitsun.

“What do you want for it?” I said.

She looked at me as though I had asked her to name a price for her own hand.

“Nothing.”

“Then I’ll want something,” I said. “Not money. A thing you know.”

She thought about it. The wind came round the corner of the house and took the corner of her apron and she held it down without looking at it.

“There’s a woman lives at the last house in the lane,” she said. “Down where it goes to the water. Your mother used to put bread on her step. Tuesdays, mostly. Sometimes a Friday.”

I did not move.

“Did she go inside?”

“Never. She’d put it down and come back up the lane and she never once looked behind her.” Hettie looked at her boots. “My mother said not to talk about it. My mother’s dead now, so I’m talking.”

“What does the woman look like?”

“Tall. Grey. She doesn’t look at you.” Hettie shifted her weight. “My mother said she was somebody’s mistake.”

I wrote Fenn, Hettie in the fair copy standing at the table with my coat still on, because a thing you have decided to do is a thing you should do before you think of a reason not to, and I did it in ink, and I did not blot it, and the ink is still there.

✦

My father came down at ten and stood in the doorway with his hand on the frame.

He did not come in. That was how he did things now. He stood where he could see and be seen, and let the room come to him.

“You’ve had the book out,” he said. His voice was a low thing with a tear in it, and it cost him to push it across a room.

“The quarter’s not due till the twenty-fifth.”

“I know when it’s due.”

He came in then, and sat, and looked at the fair copy without touching it, the way a man looks at a horse he is thinking of buying.

“Fenn,” he said.

“Her mother’s nine days in the ground.”

“The board gives the office.”

“The board gives what’s written.”

He was quiet for a while. Rain came off the roof and hit the flag by the door in threes.

“That’s my sentence,” he said. “You’ve taken it off me.”

“Then say the next one.”

“There isn’t a next one. That’s the whole trouble with a sentence like that.” He turned the leaf round with one finger so it faced him. “You’ve put her in the washing, and the washing is four pound eleven, and the board will meet on the nineteenth and the man from Carrow will say who put her there, and somebody will say the daughter, and the daughter will say the father signs, and the father—“

He stopped, because of his throat, and drank the tea I had put down beside him, and the tea was too hot, and he drank it anyway.

“And the father signs,” I said.

“And the father signs.”

He looked at the leaf for a long time. Then he shut it, and squared it, and pushed it back across the table to me with two fingers, which is how he had pushed papers across that table for twenty years, and which was the only thing about him that had not changed.

“Your mother’s line,” he said. “Read it.”

“I’ve read it.”

“Read it again. With the light over your shoulder. And when you’ve read it, come and tell me what it says, and I’ll tell you whether you’re to write it down.”

He got up. At the door he stopped.

“There’s a man coming from Carrow,” he said.

“The bishop’s man.”

“Not the bishop’s. Ours. Rooke.” He had his hand on the frame again. “He’s been sent back to audit the parish. Twenty years of books, in a fortnight.”

“Audit.”

“That’s the word he used.” My father looked at the window. “Don’t let him see a new hand in the book.”

✦

Rooke came at eleven with the commission in a leather case.

I had known him in the other life, in the way a child knows a man who comes to the house four times a year. He had been eleven years at that office and had signed four thousand papers. He had a coat that had been good in another decade and a way of coming into a house as though he had been asked to and had not decided yet.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Your father.”

“His throat is bad.”

“I heard it from the road.” He set the case on the table and did not open it. “I am not here about the quarter.”

“No.”

“The quarter is not due until the twenty-fifth and I can count to twenty-five.” He took his hat off and held it. “I am here about a page.”

The fire took a stick.

“Which page?”

“The one with your mother’s office on it.”

I have been asked since how I kept my face. The answer is that I have spent my life keeping my face while other people read out numbers, and it is not a skill, it is a habit, and habits are cheaper than courage.

“She’s in the eleventh class,” I said. “Warmth. It carries coal and tallow and eleven and fourpence.”

“I know what it carries. I wrote it.”

He said that without any weight on it at all, and then he put his hat down on the table and straightened it, and I understood that he had been carrying that sentence about for some time and had not found a place to put it.

“You wrote it,” I said.

“In ninety-eight. I had been here four years.” He looked at the window, at the rain, at anything that was not me. “It was a fair copy and your father signed it and I witnessed it, and I have thought about it perhaps four times a year since, which is not often enough to be a conscience and often enough to be a nuisance.”

“Then you know what’s four lines above her.”

He was quiet.

“I know there is a hole four lines above her,” he said. “Everybody at that office knows there is a hole in this book. It is one of the things they say to a new man, the way they tell you about the well that has no bottom.”

“And now Carrow wants the page.”

“Carrow wants the page.” He picked his hat up again. “I am to take it up on the twenty-fourth, with the returns, and I am to swear to it, and I am to say whether the book is fair.”

“Say it isn’t.”

“I cannot say it isn’t. I have not looked at it.” He said it evenly. “I am telling you before I look, Miss Ashby, because I have been eleven years in this parish and you are the only person in it who has ever asked me a second question.”

“You’re telling me so I’ll have it ready.”

“I am telling you,” he said, “so that when I open that book on Monday, whatever is in it will be there because you left it, and not because I surprised you.”

He put his hat on.

“You could refuse,” I said. “You could say the book is not fit to travel.”

“I could.” He was at the door. “And then Carrow would send a man who has never been in this parish, and he would take the page out with a knife, the way they did at Carrow in ninety-eight, and he would not tell you first.”

✦

I read it that evening, with the light over my shoulder, as my father had said.

My mother’s line was four leaves in. Ashby, Mary Ann. Eleventh class. Coal, tallow, linen, eleven shillings and fourpence.

Four lines above it, at the top of the leaf, there was a line I had copied forty times a year since I was fourteen and had never once read.

It had been scraped. Not crossed out, not struck through, not inked over. Scraped, with a knife, the way you take a mistake off parchment, and the parchment had gone thin there and furred at the edges, and the whole entry was gone except two letters at the end.

ce.

Eighteen hundred and six, and the rest of it taken off with a blade, so that the page said nothing for four lines and then said ce.

A parish book is not corrected with a knife. Ink is cheap. A knife is a confession.

I took it down to him.

✦

He was sitting by the fire with his hands empty. I had not seen him do that in my life.

“Read me the line,” he said.

“Ashby, Mary Ann. Eleventh class. Coal, tallow, linen, eleven shillings and fourpence.”

“That’s the line.”

“That’s not what I asked about.”

“I know.” He looked at the fire. “There’s a hole four lines above your mother. I have known that hole since I was a boy in this house. I have never put my finger in it.”

“Somebody put a knife in it.”

“Somebody did.”

“Do you know what was on it?”

“No.” He said it flatly, and then, because he was an honest man and honesty was the thing he could still afford, he said it again with the rest of it. “No. But I know the hand that scraped it, because I heard it done. I was nine years old, or ten, and I lay at the top of that stair and I heard a knife going over paper for the better part of a night, and in the morning there was a hole in the book and nobody in this house said a word about it, and nobody has said a word about it since.”

The fire took a stick.

“Who was in the house?”

“Everybody who is in it now,” he said, “except you.”

I stood there with the fair copy against my skirt and did the arithmetic, and the arithmetic did not come out to a number. It came out to a room, and a stair, and a nine-year-old boy listening.

“What do I write in the copy?”

He thought about it for long enough that the kettle went off and he did not get up to see to it.

“Write the line as it stands,” he said. “Write the hole as a hole. And when Rooke comes back on Monday, ask him what he signed the year he came here.”

“Why him?”

“Because he is the only man in this parish who signs his name at the bottom of other people’s work,” my father said, “and a man who signs is a man who has decided to be responsible for something. Ask him what he decided to be responsible for.”

I went up with the candle.

Downstairs the clock struck twelve and then struck it again four minutes later, because it gained four minutes a day and nobody had ever thought it worth stopping.

2

What Mr. Craddock Wanted

The little room is on the north side of the church, and it has one window with a diamond in it that somebody put in wrong in 1791, which is why the light comes through crooked and lands on the table in a shape like a broken kite.

I had been in it perhaps six times in my life. My father did not keep it locked. He kept it shut, which is a different thing, and there is no lock in this parish that a person who has been in the house twenty years cannot open.

On the sixth of March I opened it at seven in the morning with the fair copy under my coat, because the fair copy does not leave the house, and the reason the fair copy does not leave the house is that a page carried across a yard is a page that can be read by somebody else.

Rooke was already there.

The room smelled of cold lamp oil and old paper, and under that of wet wool, which was him. The flagstones had kept the night in them. I felt it come up through my boots and settle in my ankles, and I set the fair copy down and put my hands flat on either side of it, because a person who is going to be looked at should have something under her hands.

He had four volumes out on the table and a lamp turned down low, and he had his coat off, which I had never seen, and his sleeves were turned back twice, neatly, the way a man does it when he expects to be at it for a while. There was ink in the crease of his right thumb, old ink, the sort that goes in and stays a week. He had a pen in his hand and he was not writing with it. He was using it to keep his place.

There were two chairs. He had taken the one with its back to the wall and left me the one with its back to the door, and I have never decided whether that was courtesy or habit.

“You’re early,” I said.

“So are you.” He did not look up. “Shut the door. The draught moves the leaves and I have been sorting them by year.”

I shut the door.

The four volumes were the parish books. Not the current one. The old ones, the ones that go up to Carrow when the new series starts and come back when somebody at Carrow has time to be finished with them, which is generally never.

“Those aren’t supposed to be here,” I said.

“Nothing is supposed to be here. This room is supposed to be a place where things are kept.” He turned a leaf over with the back of one finger. “You are the fair copy.”

“I’m the daughter.”

“You are the fair copy and the daughter, and one of those is a job.” He looked up then. “Sit down. You are not going to stand there being a daughter at me all morning.”

He passed me the foul copy to work from, and for a moment we were both holding the same book. His thumb was on the cover a hand’s width from my fingers, and there is no way to take a book out of a man’s hand except to take it, so I took it, and the smell of wet wool came off him with it and stayed in my cuff past noon.

I put that down because a person who keeps accounts ought to keep all of them, and that is an account.

I sat down.

He worked. I worked. For perhaps a quarter of an hour there was nothing in that room but a pen, a fire that wanted coal, and a man turning pages at the rate of about one every four seconds, which is the rate of a man who is not reading but counting.

“Ninety-one,” he said.

“What?”

“The books go back to ninety-one. There is no ninety, and there is no eighty-nine, and the man at Carrow who sent me the list says there ought to be six more.”

“Perhaps they were lost.”

“Perhaps they were lost,” he agreed, in a voice that agreed with nothing.

I kept copying. The quarter is a long thing and my hand was out of practice in the way a hand is out of practice after nineteen years, which is to say not at all, because a hand does not forget, and that is the trouble with hands.

Copying is not writing. Writing is a thing you do to a page; copying is a thing the page does to you. You set the foul copy at your right hand and the fair at your left, and you go down the column a line at a time, and after a while the words stop being words and become shapes, and the shapes have a rhythm, and if you are any good at all the rhythm carries you and you can go an hour without once knowing what you have written.

I was good. That was the trouble.

The diamond in the window was wrong in a way that had a use. It threw the light down in a long bent shape that crawled across the table as the morning went on. At eight it lay across the floor. At half past nine it reached the corner of his sleeve, and he moved his arm without looking, the way a man moves away from a draught he has lived with for years.

His boots were under the table, and the toe of the left one was four inches from the toe of mine. Neither of us moved it. I noticed that at the time and I have never been able to make it into a fact.

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Mm.”

“What did you sign the year you came here?”

The pen stopped.

He did not look up for long enough that I heard the fire take a stick and let it go.

“I signed a great many things the year I came here,” he said. “It was eighteen hundred and four. There was an enclosure, and a valuation, and a return of the poor, and a list of the men who had gone for soldiers, and I signed all of them, and I could not tell you now what was in any one of them.”

“My father says you would remember.”

“Your father is a charitable man.” He turned a page. “Ask me something with a date in it and I will do better.”

“Ninety-eight.”

The pen went down on the table. Not thrown. Set down, the way a man sets down a pen when he has decided he will not need it for a moment.

“Ninety-eight,” he said, “is the year of the bad winter and the second valuation, and I signed the valuation, and I signed the schedule of offices, and I signed eleven death entries in six weeks, and one of them was a woman I had known since she was a girl.”

He looked at me.

“Ask me the question, Miss Ashby.”

“I don’t know the question.”

“Then you are not ready to hear the answer, and I am not going to hand it to you in a room with a draught in it.” He picked his pen up. “When you know the question, come and ask it, and I will tell you whether I am going to answer.”

✦

I worked until eleven, and I did not think about the book, because copying is a thing that does not let you think.

At eleven I reached for the ninety-one volume to check a figure, and my hand went flat on the cover, and the room went away.

A man was sitting where I was sitting, in a coat that was not this decade, with a candle and a pen and his sleeve on the table to keep the ink off it. He had a ring on his little finger. He wrote the way a man writes when he has been told to write a thing he does not agree with: fast, and once, and without going back.

He finished a line and stopped, and he did not look at what he had written. He looked at the wall.

Then the room came back, with Rooke in it, and the crooked light, and my hand on a cover that was warm where my hand had been.

Three seconds. It is always three seconds.

“You went somewhere,” Rooke said.

“I was thinking.”

“You were not thinking. You were not in the room.” He had put down his pen and he was looking at me the way he looked at a page. “Miss Ashby. I have been at this trade for twenty-seven years and I have seen two kinds of clerk. There is the kind who goes away when the work is dull, and there is the kind who goes away because something in the work has spoken to him. Which are you?”

“Ask me on Monday.”

“On Monday I will be asking you with a witness in the room.”

“Then ask me softly.”

He did not smile. But the corner of his mouth moved, one degree, and I have thought about that one degree more than I should have.

✦

At noon the bell went for the school, and the sound came through the wall flat, the way it does when you are inside the thing that is ringing. I went out to the yard to get the cold out of my eyes. The day had turned. There was a wind from the west that smelled of the reservoir and of somebody’s chimney, and the rooks were up over the churchyard making the noise rooks make, which is the sound of an argument nobody wins.

Craddock came round the corner of the porch while I was still in the yard.

He was the overseer of the poor, which in this parish meant he decided who was on the list and who was not, and he had held it for nine years, and he had a way of coming into a room sideways, as though the room might close on him.

“Miss Ashby.” He looked at the fair copy, which was open. “That is not your book.”

“It is my father’s book and I am his hand.”

“His hand.” Craddock put his own hand flat on the table, well away from the paper. “His hand was in the yard this morning. It did not look like a hand that has been writing.”

“His hand was in the yard because his throat is bad and a man with a bad throat can still lift a hurdle.”

“You are very quick.”

“I have been quick since I was nine. It never got me anything.”

Rooke had not moved. He sat with his four volumes and his pen, and he might have been a piece of furniture, except that furniture does not watch.

Craddock turned a leaf of the fair copy with two fingers, the way a man turns over a dead bird to see what killed it.

“Fenn,” he said.

“Her mother is nine days in the ground.”

“Her mother is nine days in the ground and the office is not vacant until Lady Day.” He said it mildly. “There is a procedure.”

“There is a column.”

“There is a procedure and then there is a column, and you have written the column before the procedure, and that is the wrong order.” He straightened up. “Who told you to do it?”

“Nobody told me.”

“Somebody told you. Somebody always tells a person who has never done a thing before.”

“I have been doing this since I was fourteen.”

“You have been copying since you were fourteen. Copying is not doing.” He put his finger on the name. “This is doing. This is the first thing you have ever done in this room, and you did it between nine and ten on a Thursday with the door open, and you did not blot it.”

I looked at the ink. It was dry and it was black and it was not blotted.

“I did not blot it,” I said. “I do not regret it.”

“That is a pity,” Craddock said, “because the board meets on the nineteenth and I will have to tell them you said so.”

“Tell them.”

“And I will have to tell them that the daughter of the parish clerk, whose throat is bad, has begun writing in the parish book in her own hand.” He turned for the door. “There is a word for that.”

“There are several.”

He stopped at the door and looked back at me, and there was nothing angry in his face at all, which was worse than anger.

“Your mother’s line,” he said. “Eleventh class.”

“Yes.”

“The eleventh class had thirteen names in ninety-eight. It had fourteen in ninety-nine.” He said it slowly, as though he were reading it out to a man who could not read. “Nobody at that office can tell me who added the fourteenth, or when, or out of what money. I have asked. I have asked for nine years, in writing, and every letter I have had back has been polite and has not contained an answer.”

“You think my father added it.”

“I think your father signed the page.” Craddock put his hat on. “I am not your enemy, Miss Ashby, whatever you have decided. I am a man with a book that does not add up, and I have found that the only person in this parish who can make it add up is the one who copies it. That is why I am telling you.”

He went out sideways.

The door shut and the room was smaller. That is a thing I have noticed about rooms: they are the size of the person who has just left them.

My hand had begun to shake. It does that after. Not during. During, there is nothing at all, and that is the dangerous part, because a thing that costs you nothing while you are doing it will send you the bill at four o’clock.

I put both hands flat on the table and pressed until it stopped.

✦

Rooke had not said a word through any of it. He said one now.

“Well?”

“Well what?”

“He is not wrong about the column and the procedure.” He squared his four volumes, one, two, three, four. “He is wrong about you.”

“How would you know?”

“Because you did not blot it.” He stood up and put his coat on and did the buttons from the bottom, which is the way a man does it who has dressed in the dark for years. “Blotting is what a person does when they are afraid of what they have written. You are not afraid of what you wrote. You are afraid of what it will cost, and those are two different fears, and only one of them makes a person careful.”

“And which one am I?”

“You are the second.” He picked up his hat. “It will cost you more.”

✦

I went home the long way, past the mill and down the lane, because the short way goes by the school and at one o’clock the school is all voice.

The mill was working. The wheel threw water up over the sluice in a sheet, and the light came through the sheet and made a rainbow in it that lasted about as long as a breath, and then the wheel took the water back and made another one. Mrs. Gow’s boy was on the wall with a stick, hitting the rainbow where it came out of the water, and he could not have told you why he was doing it.

Above the mill roof, in the north, there was a smudge that was not cloud. Carrow. Eleven miles of road, a registry with a dome, forty-one clerks, and a room full of ledgers, and from this gate it was a stain on the sky the colour of a bruised pear.

I stood at the gate and looked at it longer than a woman carrying a fair copy ought to stand at a gate.

Hettie Fenn was coming up the lane with a bucket, and she stopped when she saw me, and then she came on, because a person with a bucket has an excuse to be going anywhere.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Miss Fenn.”

She set the bucket down. It was half full and the water in it was doing the thing water does when it has been carried, which is to keep moving after the carrying has stopped.

“Mother Gow says you wrote me in.”

“Mother Gow says a great deal.”

“She says the board will strike me out at Lady Day and I’ll go to Carrow with the rest.” She looked at the bucket rather than at me. “Is that true?”

“It is not untrue.”

“Then say the true part.”

I have been asked a great many questions in my life by people who did not want the answer. That was the first one in a while that did.

“The true part is that the office is not vacant until Lady Day, and I wrote you in before it was vacant, and a man named Craddock has noticed, and he will tell the board on the nineteenth.” I shifted the fair copy under my arm. “The true part is also that I would do it again on Monday.”

She thought about that for as long as it takes to lift a bucket.

“Then I’ll tell Mother Gow you said the second part,” she said, “and not the first,” and went on up the lane with the water moving in the bucket, one long slop and then a short one, the way it does.

My mother was sitting up with the shirt in her lap, and she had done the sleeve and started the collar, which meant she had been at it all morning, which meant she had not slept after I left.

“You were at the church,” she said.

“At the little room.”

“With the books.”

“With the books.”

She sewed for a while. She was faster than she had any right to be, and the needle went in and out with a snap you could hear, the same snap, the same three seconds, and I stood in the door and did not go in.

Her hands were the one thing about her the illness had not got at. Everything else had gone thin: the wrists, the throat, the skin under the eyes, the voice. But she could still put a needle through four layers of linen and bring it out where she meant it, and she could do it in the dark, and she had been doing it in the dark for thirty years.

I watched her do six stitches. There is a way of watching a person sew that is really a way of asking them a question, and she knew it, and she let me get to six.

The room had one window and the window had one curtain and the curtain had been a dress. That is the kind of house it was. Everything in it had been something else first, and my mother was the only one of us who had never been anything else.

“Did you touch the ninety-one?” she said.

The room got very quiet.

“Why would I touch ninety-one?”

“Because it is the oldest one and it is the one with the ring in it.” She bit the thread off. “Mercy. Listen to me now, because I am going to say this once and then I am going to go back to this collar and I am not going to say it again.”

I came in and sat on the end of the bed.

“When you were a child,” she said, “I used to think, if she ever learns to read a thing the way she reads a clock, this parish will not be able to keep anything from her. And I prayed about it. I have not prayed about much.”

“What did you pray?”

“That you would be slow.” She looked at me. “You were not slow. You were patient, which is worse. Patience is the thing that waits for the right question, and I have watched you wait for eleven years, and I have been grateful for every year of it.”

“Grateful.”

“Because as long as you were waiting, you were safe.” She put the shirt down. “Now you have started. And the moment you start, they will know.”

“Who will know?”

She picked the shirt up again.

“You want a name,” she said. “You have always wanted a name for a thing before you would leave it alone. That is why you will be good at this and why it will hurt you.” She drew the needle through. “I am not going to give you a name. I am going to give you a piece of advice, and it is worth more than a name.”

“What is it?”

“Whatever you find in that book,” she said, “do not take it to the man from Carrow. Take it to the man from Carrow’s clerk.”

I did not say anything for a moment.

“Rooke.”

“I did not say a name.” She turned the collar. “I said a clerk. A clerk is a man who has spent his life being told what to write and has never once been asked what he thinks. If you want a thing to move in this parish, do not push it. Find the man who has been holding it still for thirty years, and ask him what he thinks.”

Downstairs the clock struck one.

3

Five Votes to Two

The vestry met on the seventh of March, which was a Saturday, and a Saturday is the worst day for it, because the men come in from the fields with their boots on and their minds on the market and they will vote for anything that lets them get back out again.

The room behind the church is the little room’s older sister. It has a table twelve feet long that was given by a man who made his money in malt and repented of it in stone, and it has a window that faces north so that nobody can be warmed by the proceedings.

I was summoned by a note delivered by the clerk’s boy at eight in the morning. The note said, The Vestry will examine the matter of the fair copy at eleven. Attend.

It did not say you may attend. It said attend, which is what you write to a person you intend to talk about rather than to.

My father was already in the yard when I came down. He had his coat on and his hat in his hand and he was standing at the gate looking at the road, and he did not turn round.

“You’ll come,” I said.

“I’ll come.”

“With your throat.”

“With my throat.” He put his hat on. “A man who cannot speak is a great convenience to a meeting. They will let me sit at the end of the table and they will be very kind to me, and they will decide everything while I am being kind back.”

“Then write.”

He looked at me.

“Write what?”

“Whatever you would say if you could.” I got my coat. “You have been writing other people’s words for twenty years. Write one of your own and I will read it out.”

He stood there a moment with his hand on the gate.

“Mercy,” he said. “If I write it, they will know it was me.”

“Yes.”

“And they will take it out of you.”

“Yes.”

He nodded slowly, the way a man nods when he has been shown a bill and has decided to pay it.

I had two hours. I spent one of them in my room doing a thing that had no dignity in it at all. I wrote the two page numbers on a slip of paper the width of my thumb and put it up my sleeve, where a woman can keep a thing and no man will ask her to turn out her pockets, because a woman’s sleeve is the one place in this parish that nobody has ever thought to search.

I put on the merino, which had been my mother’s and had been turned twice. There is a glass over the basin and it is old and it gives a face back half a second late, so that a person combing her hair is always combing the hair of a woman who has just stopped. That is the closest I have come to seeing myself the way other people see me, and I do not recommend it.

It is four hundred yards from our gate to the vestry door. I walked it in the cold with the slip in my sleeve and counted the doors, which is a habit, and there are nineteen, and three of them have no paint left on the bottom because of the water.

✦

There were nine of them at the table and one empty chair at the end, which was for my father, and one chair against the wall, which was for me.

The room smelled of wet boots and snuff and a fire that had been lit at nine and had not taken. There was a green cloth on the table and it had been darned in three places with black thread, and the darning was the worst I have ever seen, and it had been done by a man who did not want to pay a woman to do it. There were two candles, and it was full daylight, and they had lit them anyway, because a meeting with candles in it is a meeting.

I walked to the chair against the wall, which is eleven steps from the door, and I took them slowly. A woman who walks quickly into a room where she is to be examined has already apologized for something.

Nobody said good morning. That is a thing about the vestry. It does not say good morning to a person it intends to talk about.

Mr. Barrow sat at the head because he is the largest ratepayer in the parish and because the table had been given by his wife’s father, and he had a way of sitting with both hands flat on the wood as though he were keeping it from floating off.

Mr. Pike the rector sat at his right and did the praying, which took a minute and a half and mentioned the poor twice.

Craddock sat at the far end with a book of minutes open in front of him and a pen already uncapped, which is the most frightening thing a man can do at a meeting.

Mr. Rye sat next to him, who farms the low ground and has the cough that goes with it. Mr. Hobbs the smith sat with his arms folded, and he is the only man at that table who has ever made a thing with his hands, and it shows, because he does not talk about procedure as though it were weather. Mr. Sparrow kept the shop and the post office and knew every letter that came into this parish before the person it was addressed to did, and he had the look of a man who had come to hear something he already knew.

Rooke sat on the side, apart, with the Carrow commission in its leather case in front of him and his hands folded on top of it. He had not opened it.

“Miss Ashby,” Mr. Barrow said. “Sit down.”

I sat down against the wall.

“This is not a trial,” he said.

“No, sir.”

“It is an enquiry into a procedure.” He looked round the table for support and got it. “You are not accused of anything.”

“No, sir.”

“You are here to answer a question about a hand.”

“Yes, sir.”

“Good.” He settled back. “Mr. Craddock.”

Craddock stood up, which he did not need to do, and read out the minutes of the last meeting in a voice that made them sound like scripture, and then he read out the item.

“Item four. The fair copy of the quarter ending Lady Day, 1831, has been entered in a hand other than the clerk’s, namely the hand of his daughter, and an office has been assigned in that copy before the vacancy was declared. The overseer asks the vestry to determine whether the copy is fair.”

He sat down.

“It is fair,” I said.

Nine faces turned.

“You will be asked,” Mr. Barrow said.

“I am answering.”

“You will be asked,” he said again, and this time there was a little iron in it.

They asked me. It took an hour.

I am not going to put down the whole hour, because most of it was a large man asking me whether I had done a thing and me saying that I had, in eight different ways, and a rector saying that we must all remember that the poor are with us always, and a farmer at the far end of the table saying that in his experience the poor are with us more often than that.

I will put down the three minutes that mattered.

The three minutes were not consecutive. They were spread out over the hour the way currants are spread over a bun, and I have put them together here, because a person who tells a thing in the order it happened is a person who has not yet thought about it.

Craddock asked me whether I knew the rule about vacancies.

I said I knew it.

He asked me to state it.

I stated it: that an office does not die with the person, that it is held by the parish until the next quarter day, that no one may be put into it before the vacancy is declared, and that the copy is not fair if it anticipates the declaration.

“And yet you entered a name,” he said.

“I entered a name in the column marked to be filled.”

“There is no such column.”

“There is in the ninety-one book.” I said it to the table, not to him. “It was struck out of the form in ninety-four, and the reason it was struck out is that in ninety-three this vestry lost an office for eleven months because nobody wrote down who was to have it, and the money went back to Carrow, and this parish had to apply for it again, and the application took two years.”

The room was quiet.

Mr. Barrow turned his hands over on the table.

That is what he does when he is thinking. He turns them over and looks at his own palms as though the answer might be written there, and he has the softest hands of any man in this parish, and everybody at that table has noticed, and nobody has ever said it.

“Is that in the minutes?” he said.

“It is in the ninety-one book,” I said. “Page two hundred and eleven. I copied it last Tuesday.”

“You copied it last Tuesday,” Craddock said, “and yet you did not think to mention it when you wrote a name into a column that does not exist.”

“The column does exist. It exists in the book that this vestry’s own rule was written in.” I put my hands in my lap. “You have been asking for nine years where the money came from, Mr. Craddock. I am telling you where it went. It went back to Carrow in ninety-three and it came back in ninety-five, and somebody wrote a new rule in ninety-four so that it could never go back again, and the new rule is the rule you are using to say my copy is not fair.”

Craddock did not answer at once.

Mr. Pike said, “That is a very interesting piece of history,” in a voice that meant it was not.

“It is not history,” I said. “It is a column.”

✦

They made me wait in the church while they voted, which is the custom, and I sat in the second pew from the back with my hands in my lap and the cold coming up through the stone.

The church has a clock. It is a bad clock. It had been nine minutes out since Easter and nobody had thought it worth mentioning, because the church clock is not the sort of thing anyone is paid to be right about.

I sat and listened to it not being right.

There is a window over the south door with three saints in it, and the middle one has lost his head, and the glass where his head was is a plain pane of a green that nobody makes any more. In the afternoon, when the sun is on it, that green comes down onto the flagstones and lies there like something spilled.

It was not afternoon. It was eleven o’clock on a Saturday in March, and the green was not there, and I sat in the cold and looked at the place where it would be.

I counted the pews, which is thirty-one. I counted the bosses on the roof, which is not possible from the second pew from the back. I put my hand into my sleeve and touched the slip of paper, and then I took my hand out again, because a person who is seen touching a thing she has hidden has already told the room what it is.

My father came out first, and he did not come to me. He went down the aisle and out of the door into the yard, and I understood that he had been asked to go out, and that he had gone, and that this was the thing he had been afraid of since the gate.

Then Craddock came out and stood in front of me with his hat in his hand and read out the resolution, because he is a man who likes to do a thing properly even when the thing is an injustice.

“The vestry resolves, five to two, that the fair copy of the Lady Day quarter is not fair, and that it be remade by the clerk or by a hand approved by this vestry, and that the said hand be not the hand of his daughter until the vestry has been satisfied as to her competence and her authority.”

“Competence,” I said.

“And authority.”

“Which two voted against?”

He looked at me for a moment.

“Mr. Rooke,” he said, “and Mr. Nunn.”

“Nunn is a tenant.”

“Mr. Nunn holds four acres and a cow and has a vote in this vestry and has had it since ninety-six, and you will not make me say otherwise in a church.” He put his hat on. “Miss Ashby. I want you to understand that I did not do this because I dislike you.”

“I know why you did it.”

“Then say it, and we will see if you are right.”

“You did it because a copy that a woman can make is a copy that a woman can alter,” I said, “and a copy that a woman can alter is a book that nobody can swear to, and if nobody can swear to it the money stops, and if the money stops the parish goes to the board with its hat in its hand, and the board is where you have been trying to get for nine years, and now you will get there with a reason that nobody can argue with.”

Craddock stood very still.

“You are wasted in that room,” he said at last.

“I am wasted in this parish.”

He almost smiled. Then he went out into the yard, and I heard him say something kind to my father, and I hated him for it.

✦

Rooke came out last. He had his case under his arm and his hat not on his head.

“You voted against,” I said.

“I did.”

“And then you signed it.”

“I did.” He turned his hat over in his hands. “I am the auditor. It is my signature that makes a resolution lawful. If I had refused to sign, the resolution would have gone to Carrow unsigned, and Carrow would have sent it back with a note, and the note would have taken six weeks, and in six weeks your copy would have been remade by somebody’s nephew and the Fenn woman would be in service at twelve miles.”

“So you signed it to save her.”

“I signed it to save the six weeks.” He put his hat on. “Do not make me a better man than I am, Miss Ashby. I have spent thirty years signing things I did not agree with because the alternative was a delay that hurt somebody poorer than me. It is not a principle. It is a habit that has worn a groove, and I am too old to climb out of the groove.”

“That is a very fine speech.”

“It is a true one, which is rarer.” He turned to go and then stopped. “Thursday.”

“What of it?”

“Thursday the twelfth, at two, in that room, I begin the audit in earnest. Twenty years of books. I will need a hand to turn the pages and read out the figures, because my eyes are bad after four and my hands are worse.”

“The vestry has ruled on my hand.”

“The vestry has ruled on the fair copy. It has not ruled on the audit. The audit is Carrow’s, and I am Carrow’s, and I will have who I like.” He looked at me. “Be there at two. Bring a pencil, not a pen. A pencil is a thing you can rub out, and I would like you to have the option.”

He took a pencil out of his coat and held it out. It was not a new one. It had been sharpened with a knife and the wood was still pale at the point.

“Take this one. It is mine. It will give you a reason to come back.”

I took it. Our fingers did not touch, and that is the whole of what happened, and I have never once told it to anybody as if more had.

He was three feet away. I know because I had been counting the flags between us since he came out of the door, which is a habit, and not a useful one, and the only one I have never been able to break.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Your father is standing at the gate and he has not moved since the vote.”

“I know.”

“He will want to say something to you and he will not be able to, and then he will say something else, and it will be about the weather.” He put his hat on. “Let him.”

He went down the path.

I stood in the church door and watched him go, and behind me the clock struck eleven, and then struck it again four minutes later.

4

The Sack in the Market

Carrow market is on the tenth of every month and the tenth of March was a Tuesday, which meant the carriers went up in the dark and came back in the dark and everybody in the cart spent the day pretending they had not been up since four.

Mrs. Gow’s cart holds nine if nobody is carrying a pig. There were seven of us and a pig, so we were eight, and the pig was under the bench with a sack over its head because a pig that can see the road will scream at it.

I sat at the back with my knees against a barrel. In front of me sat Mrs. Gow, who is the miller’s wife and therefore the second most important woman in the parish, and beside her sat Mrs. Nunn, who is nobody’s wife in the eyes of the vestry but is Nunn’s wife in the eyes of God and the parish, and behind me sat a boy with a crate of hens who did not say one word in fourteen miles.

“Your mother’s not come,” Mrs. Gow said, without turning round. “That’s twice.”

“She’s not well.”

“She was not well in January and she came in January.”

“She was not well in January and she should not have come in January.”

Mrs. Gow turned round then. She has a face like a loaf: broad, warm, and with a hard crust if you press it.

“You’ve got a sharp way with you since your birthday,” she said. “I noticed it. Other people have noticed it.”

“People have been noticing things about me since I was born. It is the only inheritance I have.”

Mrs. Nunn laughed, and then put her hand over her mouth, and Mrs. Gow looked at her until she stopped.

✦

Carrow is not a city. This matters, because a person who has never been further than the village will hear the word and think of somewhere with a cathedral.

Carrow is a town of four streets and a market square, and it has a registry office, and a bank, and a clock on the assembly rooms that is right, and a road out of it that goes to somewhere else. That is the whole of it. But in the square on the tenth of the month there are more people than my parish has in a year, and every one of them is selling something, and half of them are selling it badly.

The square is not one market. It is four, laid on top of each other.

There is the food market, which is onions and pigs and men in aprons shouting prices at each other as though they were arguing. There is the hardware market, which is nails and hinges and a man who will mend a pot for a penny and does it badly. There is the cloth market, which is quiet, because cloth is the one thing in Carrow that is looked at before it is bought.

And then, along the north side under the assembly rooms, there is the other one.

The signs there move. Not the letters. The letters are painted and they hold still. It is the small painted things under the words that move: a boot that takes three steps and stops, a teapot that pours nothing into a cup that fills anyway, a gilt hand that opens and closes as though it were counting. The paint is old and the motion is not, and nobody in the square looks at it twice. It is the farmer’s wife from four miles out who stands in front of it with her mouth open, and the town walks round her.

I had three errands: a quarter of tea, a paper of needles, and a word with the man who sells clocks about a mainspring. I did the tea and the needles and I was standing at the clock man’s plank, which was two barrels and a board, when I heard the noise behind me.

The clock man was not there. His boy was, and the boy said his father had gone to see a man about a spring, and that the spring was a mainspring, and that if I wanted to know the price I should come back at two, and that he was not allowed to say the price, and that he did not know it anyway.

Behind the board there was a glass case with six small bottles in it, and the bottles had labels, and the labels said things like Fine, with showers, and Changeable, and Sharp, and A settled week, no wind.

“You sell weather,” I said.

“We sell the forecast,” the boy said. “The weather is free. It is the knowing that costs.”

The noise a crowd makes when it has found something to look at is not loud. It is a sort of settling, like birds going down into a field.

I turned round.

Mrs. Nunn was standing in front of the butter table with her basket on her arm and a man in a good coat standing over her, and the man had his hand on the basket.

“I’ll want the paper,” he said.

“I’ve got no paper.”

“Then I’ll want the basket.”

“You’ll not have my basket.”

The man in the good coat was the market clerk. I did not know his name then. I know it now: his name is Bell, and he has been the market clerk for eleven years, and he has a bad hip, and he goes home at three.

“There is a rule,” he said, in the voice of a man who has said the same sentence four hundred times. “A person selling in this market must produce a certificate from their parish that they are a person in good standing. No certificate, no basket.”

“I’ve sold here eleven years.”

“And for eleven years you have had a certificate, and this year you have not.” He held out his hand. “The basket.”

I was already moving.

I want to put down why, because it was not courage. It was arithmetic. A basket of eggs is four shillings. Four shillings is a quarter’s coal allowance for a woman in the eleventh class. And the reason Mrs. Nunn had no certificate was not that she had lost it. It was that her husband’s office had been marked diminished in the Michaelmas copy, and a diminished office does not get a certificate, and I knew that because I had copied the word diminished myself, in October, in my father’s hand, in a warm room, without thinking about it.

“Mr. Bell,” I said.

He looked at me.

“Miss Ashby.”

“You know my father.”

“I know your father.”

“Then you know that I copy his book, and that a copy is a thing that can be checked.” I put my hand on the basket, which was a mistake, and I will come to that. “The certificate for Nunn was issued in October and withdrawn in January. The withdrawal is in the Michaelmas copy and the reason written beside it is office diminished, and there is no note of what the office was diminished to.”

Bell looked at me for a while.

“That is a matter for the vestry,” he said.

“It is a matter for the certificate. A certificate is withdrawn when an office is diminished and the diminution is entered. If the diminution is not entered, the withdrawal is not lawful, and a certificate that is not lawfully withdrawn is a certificate that is still in force.” I said it slowly, the way I had said it to myself in the cart for fourteen miles. “You have not got a lawful withdrawal. You have got a piece of paper with a word on it.”

The crowd had gone quiet in the way a crowd goes quiet when it is enjoying itself.

Bell took his hand off the basket.

“You are very sure of yourself,” he said.

“I am sure of the column.”

“Then I will write to the vestry,” he said, “and the vestry will write to me, and in the meantime the woman can sell her eggs.” He stepped back. “But I will tell you a thing, Miss Ashby, and you may take it as a kindness. There are men in that office at Carrow who do not like a hand that is quicker than theirs. You have made yourself known this morning.”

“I have been known in this parish since I was born.”

“You have been known as a clerk’s daughter,” he said. “That is not the same as being known.”

✦

That is when it happened, and I have gone over it a hundred times since, and I cannot make it come out any way but the way it came out.

My hand was on the basket. Mrs. Nunn’s basket, with a linen cloth over the top and eleven eggs under it and a broken handle that had been mended with wire.

And the market went away.

Not the sound. The sound stayed, and that is the part that frightens me, because in the other times it had always been quiet. This time I could hear the crowd and the hens and a man calling out parsnips, and over all of it, close to my ear, a woman’s voice saying, Put it under the cloth, put it under, she’ll not look under the cloth.

A hand went into the basket. Not mine. A hand with a chapped knuckle and a wedding ring worn thin, and it took something out of the basket and put something else in, and then the cloth went back over it, and the cloth was smoothed flat with the flat of the hand, twice, the way a person smooths a thing they want to look untouched.

Then the market came back, and I was standing in it with my hand on a basket that was warm, and Mrs. Nunn was looking at me with her mouth open.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Whose eggs are these?”

“What?”

“Whose eggs are these?” I had the cloth off before she could stop me. Eleven eggs. Brown, and one of them pale.

Mrs. Nunn’s hens were brown. All of them. I had seen them in her yard in October.

“These are not yours.”

“They are mine.”

“Your hens are brown. This one is white.” I held it up. “And there is a woman’s voice in this basket saying put it under the cloth, and she has a ring worn thin on the left hand, and she is not you.”

The crowd made a noise.

Mrs. Nunn’s face did something complicated.

“It’s Bell’s wife,” she said. “She puts her eggs in with mine and I sell them and we split it. She can’t sell her own, she’s the clerk’s wife, it wouldn’t be decent.”

I put the egg down.

The whole square was looking at me. Not at the egg. At me.

And near the parsnips, at the edge of the crowd, with a basket of his own under his arm and a pencil behind his ear, was the auditor.

He was not looking at the egg. He was not looking at Mrs. Nunn. He was looking at me, and he had been doing it long enough to have decided something, and when I met his eye he did the smallest thing a man can do with his head, which is to move it a quarter of an inch to one side.

Then he bought a bunch of parsnips, which I have never got over, and went up the street with them under his arm like a man who had come to Carrow for that and nothing else.

✦

Mrs. Nunn caught me at the corner by the pump.

She had her basket on her arm with the cloth over it and she was not crying, which was worse.

“You’ll not tell the vestry,” she said.

“I have told the vestry nothing.”

“You told that man.”

“He is the auditor. He was standing in front of a butter table with a basket of parsnips.”

“He is Carrow.” She said it with a small space after it, the way people in this parish say that word, as though it needed room. “I have four children and one of them is a girl of twelve, and if they strike him out I will have four children and a girl of twelve in a house with a bad roof.” She shifted the basket. “I am not asking you to lie. I am asking you to be slow.”

“Everybody in this parish wants me to be slow.”

“That is because we have all seen what happens to the quick ones,” she said, and went off toward the carrier’s yard.

✦

I did not buy the mainspring.

I went and sat on the step of the assembly rooms with my hands in my lap and waited for the cart, and after a while Mrs. Gow came and sat down beside me with a pie in a cloth, and did not offer me any of it, which was her way of being kind.

“You went somewhere,” she said.

“I was thinking.”

“You were not thinking. Your eyes went out.” She took a bite of the pie. “I have seen a woman do that once before. My aunt, when the fits came on her. She would go out for a moment and come back and not know what she had been at.”

“It was not a fit.”

“I did not say it was.” Mrs. Gow chewed. “I said I have seen it once before. I did not say what it was.”

We sat there.

“Mrs. Gow.”

“Mm.”

“Has anyone else in this parish got it?”

She was quiet for long enough that a cart went past us and turned the corner.

“Your mother,” she said, “used to walk twelve miles to see a woman nobody would name, and come back with a face like a woman who had been to a funeral, and she never once told me why, and I never once asked her, and we have been neighbours for twenty-two years.” She finished the pie. “That is the only strange thing I know about your family, and I have known it for twenty years, and I have never said it out loud until this morning, and I am saying it now because you have just done something in a market in front of two hundred people and you will need somebody to have said it to you first.”

I sat very still.

“Twelve miles,” I said.

“Twelve miles. Tuesdays, mostly.”

“That is where she is today.”

Mrs. Gow looked at me.

“Your mother is at home with a shirt in her lap,” she said. “I saw her at the window as we came past.”

I stood up.

The cart was loading. The pig was screaming. And in my head, in a voice that was not mine, a woman was saying, put it under the cloth, put it under, she’ll not look under the cloth, and I understood that I had heard a thing that had happened in a basket in a market, and that the basket had told me because a basket remembers being packed, and that the same was true of every object in this parish, and that I had been given a gift that would let me see everything and prove nothing.

✦

We came back in the dark. I sat at the back with my knees against the barrel and I did not speak for fourteen miles.

There were six of us going back, and the pig had been sold, so there was room, and nobody spoke. That is what happens after a market. The day has used up all the talking there was in everybody, and the cart goes home full of people who have spent their words.

Mrs. Gow slept with her chin on her chest, woke at the bridge and said, “Have we passed the water?”, and was asleep again before anybody answered.

The rooks went over at the halfway house, going the other way, and there were a great many of them, and the sky behind them was the colour of a duck’s egg, and the light was going out of it from the bottom up.

I thought about a glass case with six bottles of weather in it and a boy who knew the price of knowing. There is a man somewhere in that town who has made a business out of the one thing nobody can refuse, which is being told what is coming. I have thought about him more than once since, and never kindly.

At the gate my father was waiting with a lamp.

“The auditor’s been,” he said.

“Rooke?”

“He came at four and he asked for the ninety-one book, and I told him it was at the church, and he asked me who had been at the church on Tuesday, and I told him my daughter.”

I stood in the gate with the tea and the needles in my hands.

“What did he say?”

My father lifted the lamp a little, so that I could see his face, and his face was the face of a man who has been asked a question he has been dreading for twenty years and has just heard the answer.

“He said, good.”

5

Two Householders

Hettie Fenn came at six in the morning, which is not a time for calling, and she came to the back door and not the front, which is not a thing a woman does in this parish unless she has decided that she is not a visitor.

I was at the fire with the kettle. She stood in the doorway with her shawl over her head and her hands already out of it, and she did not say good morning.

She had walked from the lane end and there had been a frost, and there was frost on the shoulders of the shawl that had not melted. Her boots were too big and had been her mother’s, and I knew that because I had seen them on her mother, in this kitchen, in November.

“Sit down.”

“I’ll stand.”

“Sit down, Hettie.”

She sat on the edge of the chair with her shawl still on.

“It’s gone,” she said.

“What’s gone?”

“The washing.” Her voice was flat. “It’s gone to Mrs. Bell.”

I put the kettle down.

“Bell the market clerk?”

“His wife. They put her in on Saturday.”

“Who put her in?”

“The vestry. There was a meeting on Saturday.”

Mr. Rooke had been in the room the day before for four hours and had said eleven words to me, and I had counted them.

That is not a joke and it is not a complaint. He came at nine with the third volume under his arm and he read it standing at the window, because the light is better there and because he does not sit down in this house unless my father is in it, and at the end of it he said, “That is a fair copy and it is correct,” which is nine, and then he said, “Good morning,” and “Good evening,” and that was the rest of them, and he went.

I have thought about those eleven words a great deal. I have thought about them more than I have thought about some whole conversations. At the time I took it for dislike. I know better now, and I have known better since the ninth of March, and I am putting it down here because a woman who leaves out the part where she was wrong has not written anything at all.

✦

I want to put down how it works, because people who have not lived in a parish think that a thing is either written or not written, and it is not like that at all.

I had seen the page. I went up to the church on the Monday morning and asked the nephew’s leave to look at the copy, and he gave it, because he is nineteen, and because a person who cannot spell diminished does not think anybody wants to look at a page except to admire it.

The page was fair. That is what made me stand in the porch afterwards with my hand on the wall. There was no erasure on it, no scraping, no knife. The column was ruled, the entries were in order, and where Hettie Fenn should have been there was her mother’s name and a space and a rule, and the rule was drawn the full width of the column with a ruler, and it was the straightest line on the page.

An error is a thing you can point at. There was nothing on that page to point at.

The vestry cannot change a name in the parish book. What the vestry can do is declare that the fair copy is not fair, and when the copy is not fair the clerk must make it again, and when the clerk’s throat is bad the copy is made by a hand approved by the vestry, and the approved hand is Mr. Pike’s nephew, who is nineteen and cannot spell diminished and who does exactly what he is told with a ruled line and a wet pen.

So on Saturday they had declared my copy unfair, and on Sunday the nephew had come with my uncle’s day book, and on Monday morning the washing was Mrs. Bell’s.

“They didn’t strike it out,” Hettie said. “That’s the thing I can’t get my head round. They didn’t strike it out. They wrote it again, and it isn’t there.”

“They copied the page from the old book.”

“And the old book hasn’t got me.”

“The old book has got your mother.”

“My mother’s dead.”

“Then the old book has got a space and a rule, and the rule has been copied, and the space has been left.” I sat down. “It is not a lie. That is what I cannot make anyone understand. A lie can be caught. This is a page with no error in it.”

My father knew. He had known since Sunday, when the nephew came for the day book, and he had said nothing. He had sat in his chair with his hands on his knees and let me find it out from a girl at the back door.

That was the first time I understood that his silence was a thing he did, and not a thing that happened to him.

Hettie stood there with her shawl in her hands.

“So what do I do?”

“You appeal.”

“To who?”

“To the board at Carrow. But you cannot appeal as a person. You appeal as a household, and a household needs two ratepaying householders of good standing to stand for you.”

She looked at me.

“Two.”

“Two.”

“Who?”

“That,” I said, “is the whole of the difficulty.”

✦

Mrs. Gow said yes before I had finished the sentence, which surprised me, and then she explained why, which did not.

The mill was going while we talked. It goes from six and it stops at six and the whole house walks to its rhythm, and there is a crack in Mrs. Gow’s table that opens and closes with the wheel, and I have watched that crack all my life and never once seen it at rest.

She poured me a cup and did not put sugar in it and did not ask, which is how I take it, and which she has known for nineteen years.

“I’ll sign,” she said, “because it is not right, and because I have been in this parish thirty-one years and nobody has ever asked me to sign anything, and because I am sixty-one and there is nothing left that they can do to me except bury me.” She wiped her hands on her apron. “But I will tell you the other thing too, and you will not like it.”

“Say it.”

“If I sign, they will remember it at Michaelmas when the mill wants the road mended. My husband will not say a word to me about it and he will think about it every day until he dies.” She looked at Hettie. “I am not doing it for you. I am doing it because a woman in this parish ought to be able to do one thing in her life that costs her something.”

Hettie said thank you, and Mrs. Gow said, “Don’t. Thank me in eleven years when you are the one being asked.”

✦

The second one took the whole day.

We walked to the Fenns’ neighbour’s together and it took eleven minutes, and in those eleven minutes Hettie Fenn told me the whole of her life, which is what a person does when she has decided to trust you and has not yet learned how much to tell.

Her father had died in the house at Collow when she was nine and she had been in the room. Her mother had washed for the parish for nineteen years and had never once been paid in money, only in bread and coal and the use of the copper. She could read, which nobody knew, because her mother had taught her out of a Bible that had been left in a house they washed. She could not write.

“I can do sums,” she said. “In my head. Mother used to say it was a pity I was not a boy, and I used to say it was a pity I was not a duck, and she would laugh.”

“Did she.”

“Only at that.” Hettie shifted the shawl. “She laughed at that every time, for eleven years, and it was a bad joke, and she laughed at it every time.”

I have thought about that a great deal since. A woman in the eleventh class, with the washing of a whole parish on her back, and one joke, and she laughed at it for eleven years.

The lane was full of the ordinary that afternoon, which is the thing nobody puts in a book. A man mending a gate with wire. Two children carrying a bucket between them, stopping every six steps to argue about who was carrying more. A cat on a wall that watched me go by and then, when I was ten yards on, jumped down and followed me as far as the corner for no reason at all, and then stopped as though it had remembered an appointment.

We went to the Fenns’ neighbour first, a widow with four rooms who keeps hens and pays her rates and has done for nineteen years, and she stood in her door with her hands folded and said no.

“I’ve nothing against the girl,” she said.

“Then sign.”

“I’ve nothing against the girl and I’ve nothing to gain.” She looked past me at Hettie. “Your mother was a good woman and she got the washing because she was the best washer in the parish and because she had a husband with an office. You have not got a husband and you have not got an office and you have not got a mother, and if I sign for you I will be the woman who signed for you, and there are eleven people in this lane who will remember it and one of them will be the overseer.”

“Nobody would know,” Hettie said.

“Everybody would know. That is what a signature is.” She went inside and shut the door, and then opened it again. “Try the Nunns.”

“Nunn’s office is diminished.”

“Try them anyway.” She shut it.

✦

The second refusal was worse, because it was kind.

Mr. Sparrow kept the shop and the post office and had known my father since they were boys. He listened to the whole of it standing behind his counter with a lead pencil in his hand, and when I had finished he put the pencil down and said, “I would sign it tomorrow.”

“But.”

“But I hold the post office, and the post office is a licence, and a licence is a thing the vestry can write to Carrow about.” He looked at Hettie and then away. “I have a boy at the grammar school in Carrow. He is thirteen. If the licence is questioned, the boy comes home.”

“Mr. Sparrow.”

“Don’t.” He picked the pencil up. “I have been in this shop thirty years and I have never once refused a person a thing that cost me nothing. This is the first thing that has cost me something, and I have found out what I am, and I would rather not have found out at this hour of the morning.”

✦

We tried the Nunns.

The yard smelled of cold iron and of the ditch, and there was a goose in it that had one leg and did not get out of the way.

Nunn was in the yard with a hurdle and a hammer and he put both down before he had finished hearing it.

“I’ll sign.”

“You can’t.”

“I’m a vestryman.”

“Your office is diminished. A diminished office is not good standing.”

He stood there with the hammer in his hand and something went across his face that I have seen on men in yards all my life and have never once seen written down.

“Nine years,” he said. “I’ve been on that vestry nine years and I have voted on every road and every rate and every office in this parish, and I have never once been asked for a thing until today, and now I am told that what I am is diminished.”

“Nunn.”

“I’ll go with you. I’ll stand in the room and I’ll say it in front of them.”

“It won’t be received. It has to be a ratepaying householder in good standing.”

“Then it’s a pretty sort of rule,” he said, “that lets a man vote and will not let him sign.”

He picked the hammer up and hit the hurdle, once, hard enough that the wood split, and then he stood looking at the split and did not say anything else.

✦

We got the second one at four in the afternoon, and it was Mr. Pike’s own sister, who is a widow and keeps the school and has no reason on this earth to love her brother.

The school is one room with a stove in it and forty children, and she keeps it in an order that would shame a bank. There were slates on the desks in rows and every slate was clean, and there were seven maps on the wall and not one of them was of this parish.

“I will sign,” she said, “and I will tell you why, and then we will not speak of it again.”

“Yes.”
