“Because when I was twenty-two I wanted to keep the school, and the vestry said a woman could not keep a school, and I asked my brother to speak for me, and he prayed about it for a week and then he told me that he could not in conscience.” She put her spectacles on. “I have kept the school for nineteen years. Nobody has ever explained to me how that works. But I have never forgotten the week.”

She signed, and she signed with her own name and not with a cross, in a hand that was better than her brother’s.

✦

I wrote the petition on a half sheet of foolscap at the kitchen table with the day book shut and pushed to one side, and I wrote it twice. The first I wrote in the flowing hand I use for the fair copy, and I looked at it, and it was the hand of a clerk, and a petition in the hand of a clerk is a petition from the clerk. So I tore it up and wrote it again, slower, with the pen held further up, and it came out looking like a woman’s writing, which is a thing I had spent eleven years learning not to do.

Hettie and I stood at the gate at five o’clock with the paper in my hand and the two names on it, and for about a minute we were happy, and I want to put that down, because I have been accused since of never having been happy in that year.

“It might work,” Hettie said.

“It might work.”

“Two names.”

“Two names.”

She looked at the paper and then at me.

“You wrote it out.”

“I wrote it out.”

“You’re not allowed to write.”

“I am not allowed to write the fair copy.” I folded the paper. “Nobody has said a word to me about a petition.”

Hettie laughed, and it was the first time I had heard her laugh, and it was a good laugh, and it was not like her mother’s, and I thought: there, that is a thing I have done that the other life did not do. In the other life she went to service at twelve miles and I never heard her laugh at all.

“Take it to the church tomorrow,” I said. “Give it to Craddock’s clerk. Do not give it to Craddock.”

“Why not?”

“Because Craddock will read it before it is entered, and Craddock is an honest man, and an honest man with a paper he does not like is the most dangerous thing in a parish.”

✦

There was a paper under the salt when I came back from the Fenns’.

It was a quarter sheet, folded twice, and it had three lines on it in a hand I had been reading sideways for a month.

The petition will be received. The note will go with it. I have read the note.

Under that, smaller: Do not answer this.

I put it in the fire and watched it take, and then I took it out again with the tongs, because a thing I have burned is a thing I cannot read twice, and I had a feeling I would want to.

✦

She came back at eight that night.

I was at the table with the day book open and my father asleep in the chair, and she came to the back door again and this time she came in, and she stood in the middle of the kitchen with her hands at her sides. Her hands were red to the wrist and the skin was broken across three knuckles, and she held them as though she did not want them looked at.

“It’s done,” she said.

“Done how?”

“I went to the church and the clerk wasn’t there, and Mr. Craddock was, and he read it, and he said it was in order.”

I stood up.

“He said it was in order.”

“He said the two names were in order and the petition was in order and it would go up with the Lady Day returns.” She swallowed. “And then he said there was one more thing, and it would cost me nothing, and it would make the whole business certain.”

The fire took a stick.

“Hettie.”

“He said there was a question about how the fair copy came to be made, and whether the hand that made it had ever altered a figure, and if I could say what I had seen in that copy on the Thursday morning, he would put a note with the petition, and a petition with a note from the overseer is a petition that does not get lost.”

I sat down slowly.

“And what did you say?”

Hettie looked at me.

“I said I didn’t see anything,” she said. “I said I was at the door and you wrote a name and I never saw the book.”

The clock in the passage struck eight.

“Mercy,” she said. “He didn’t believe me.”

“I know.”

“He didn’t believe me and he wrote something down anyway.”

6

The Bread Count

The parish loaves are given out at the church door on the second Thursday of the month, and there were forty-one on the table on the twelfth of March, and there should have been forty-four.

I knew that before I counted, because I had counted them in my sleep four nights running.

The twelfth of March was a Thursday, and it was cold the way March is cold, which is not the cold of winter but the cold of a thing that has been promised and not delivered.

We set the table up at the church door at half past eight. The table is a plank on two trestles with a white cloth over it, and the cloth is the same cloth every month and it has a hole in it that has been turned to the back for eleven years.

The bread came up from the mill in a cart with a sack over it. Forty-one loaves, still warm, and the smell came off the cart and went down the lane, and by nine o’clock there were people at the gate who had been standing there since half past eight.

That is the thing about bread. It does not have to be called.

The queue goes along the wall on the north side, out of the wind, and it is not one queue. It is three, and everybody knows which one they are in. The first is the old, who come early and stand at the front and do not talk. The second is the women with children, who come at nine and talk the whole time and are told to be quiet twice. The third is the men who have come because there is nothing at home, and they come last, and they stand apart from each other, and they do not look at the bread.

The bread is baked by the miller’s wife and paid for out of the eleventh class, which is the class for warmth, which is the class my mother is in, and the money for it is a bequest. A woman in the eighteenth century left four pounds a year to be spent on bread for the poor of this parish, and a condition, which was that the bread be given at the church door and not at the houses, so that the giving should be seen.

I have thought about that woman a great deal. She wanted the giving to be seen. She did not think about who would be standing at the door doing the seeing.

✦

There were twenty-nine people in the queue and forty-one loaves, which sounds like enough until you know how it is worked.

A household in the first four classes gets one loaf for each two persons, which is not written down anywhere and which everybody knows. A household in the fifth to the ninth gets one loaf. A household in the tenth and eleventh gets one loaf and a half, which is given as one loaf and a piece, and the piece is cut on the table with a knife by the man giving it out, and the size of the piece depends on the man.

Mr. Pike gives out the bread. He has done it for nineteen years. He does it in his second-best coat and he says a word to each person, and the word is always the same word, which is there, and he says it in a voice that suggests he is the one being generous.

He is not a cruel man. I want that written down, because it would be easier if he were, and he is not. He visits the sick. He buried my grandmother. He has given away more of his own coal than the vestry has given away of the parish’s, and he does it quietly, and he does not like to be thanked.

That is the whole difficulty with Pike. He is a good man, and the system has found a use for good men.

I stood at the end of the table with the list, because the list has to be read out, and the list is a copy of the column, and the column is mine.

“Ashby, Mary Ann.” That is my mother. I gave her the loaf and the piece myself, and she took it with both hands and said thank you, and did not meet my eye, which is what a woman does when her daughter is standing behind a table.

“Dunnet, Sarah.”

An old woman came up with a shawl over her head and a bag in her hand, and she had the sort of face that has been worn down to the good part. She took her loaf and her piece and said, “Thank you, sir,” to Pike, and then she looked at me and said, quietly, “You have a look of your grandmother.”

“I never knew her.”

“No,” Mrs. Dunnet said. “She was gone before you came. She was a woman who could count.” And she went off down the path with her bag.

Then down the list.

“Fenn, Hettie.”

Hettie came up. Pike looked at the list, and then at her, and then he did a thing I have seen him do four hundred times and had never once seen for what it was.

He turned the leaf over.

“Fenn,” he said. “Tabitha Fenn.”

“My mother’s dead, sir.”

“Tabitha Fenn is on the list.” He turned the leaf back. “You are not.”

“There’s a petition gone up.”

“There is a petition gone up and it will be considered at the Lady Day board, and until it is considered the list is the list.” He held out the loaf. “You are not on the list, so I cannot give you the loaf. But I can give you the piece.”

He cut the piece.

It was the smallest piece I have ever seen a man cut with a knife. It was the size of a thumb.

Hettie stood there with her hand out.

“That’s a piece,” she said.

“That is a piece.”

“For a household of two.”

“For a person,” Pike said, “who is not on the list.”

✦

I want to be exact about what happened in me, because it is the hinge of everything after.

I did not think: this is unjust. I have known it was unjust since I was nine. Every person in that queue knew it was unjust, and had known it for longer than I had, and had built their lives around it the way a man builds a wall around a well.

What I thought was: forty-one.

Forty-one loaves on the table and forty-four on the list. Three loaves missing. Three loaves is nine persons’ bread for a fortnight. And in the same week, in the same parish, a page had come back from the vestry with a rule on it and no name on it, and a basket of eggs in Carrow market had gone to a woman who was not on any list at all.

I put my hand on the bread table.

Not on a loaf. On the table. Oak, scrubbed, forty years of flour in the grain.

And the church door went away, and the queue went away, and I was standing at the same table on a different morning.

A man was counting. Loaves into a basket, out of a basket, counting in a low voice, and the voice was Pike’s voice and it was younger.

Forty-four. Forty-four. Forty-one.

Three for the office.

Then a second voice, a woman’s, which said something I could not hear, and Pike’s voice said, quite clearly, they will not miss three out of forty-four, and if they do, they will miss them at the bottom of the list.

The table came back.

I was holding the edge of it with both hands.

My ears were ringing. That had not happened before. It was not the three seconds that had changed. It was that I had gone into a thing that had been done in that place and had come back with it, and there was a taste in my mouth like a penny.

The queue was still there. Twenty-nine people, and the wind had got into them, and they were standing with their shoulders up, and not one of them was looking at the table.

“Miss Ashby,” Pike said. “Are you ill?”

✦

I have been asked since why I did not simply tell the queue what I had heard. The answer is that I had learned, in four days, what a person who says a thing in a market gets, and it is not the thing they wanted.

So I did the other thing.

I picked up the list.

“Sir,” I said. “There is an error in this list.”

“There is no error in that list. It was copied on Sunday.”

“It was copied on Sunday from the old book, and the old book has forty-four names in the eleventh class and this list has forty-one, and the three that are missing are Dunnet, Fenn, and Craddock’s own cowman.” I did not look up. “I am not asking you to put them back. I am telling you that the list is a copy, and a copy that does not match the book is not a list, and if the board at Carrow asks me to swear to it on Lady Day, I will have to say what I have said to you.”

The queue had gone quiet.

Pike looked at me for a long time.

“Give them the bread,” he said at last.

“Sir?”

“Give them the bread, and put the three back on the list, and if the vestry asks who did it, you will say that the list was found to be in error.” He set the knife down. “And then you will come and see me, and we will discuss what a copy is.”

Pike’s hands were shaking. That is the thing I remember. He was not angry and he was not ashamed. He was frightened, and he was frightened of me, and I have never been so careful with a man in my life as I was careful with him in the four minutes after that.

✦

I gave out forty-four loaves at the church door on the twelfth of March, 1831, in front of twenty-nine people, three of whom were Dunnet, Fenn, and a cowman who took his loaf without a word.

Hettie stayed to help me take the table down.

She did not say anything for a while. She folded the cloth, which has a hole in it, and she turned the hole to the front, and I did not tell her that it had been turned to the back on purpose for eleven years.

“You gave them the bread,” she said.

“I gave them what was on the book.”

“You gave them the bread.” She put the cloth under her arm. “I want to say a thing and then I will go home. When I was a girl I thought the people at the table were the ones who had the bread and the people in the queue were the ones who had not. That is what it looks like from the queue. It has taken me until this morning to see that the people at the table have not got the bread either. They have got the table.”

And afterwards I sat on the church step with the empty basket and did the arithmetic I had been avoiding since the market.

Three loaves is nine persons for a fortnight. Nine persons for a fortnight, twelve times a year, is a great deal of bread.

But the bread is not the money. The bread is bought with the bequest, and the bequest is four pounds a year, and four pounds a year buys four hundred and sixteen loaves at twopence, which is eight a week, which is more than forty-four a fortnight, and that is the thing that had been sitting at the back of my head since October without a name.

The bequest buys more bread than the parish gives out.

Somewhere between the woman who left the money in 1788 and the man who cuts the piece on the table, eight loaves a week had stopped being bread and had become something else, and whatever it had become, it had a name in the book, and the name was in a column at the bottom of the page where nothing was written except a figure, and the figure was carried forward every quarter, and the column was called unapplied.

The lane going home was full of people carrying loaves. That is a thing you see once a month and never think about: twenty-nine people going up a lane in the same direction with a loaf under an arm, and every one of them walking a little faster than usual, and none of them looking at each other’s bread.

✦

The audit began at two.

The fire had been made up. It had not been made up in that room in eleven years, and somebody had done it properly, with coal at the back and wood in front, and it had taken, and the room was warm in a way that made the cold outside feel like a thing that had happened to other people.

I went in with a pencil, as I had been told. Rooke had the ninety-one volume open and the ninety-four beside it and the current book at his elbow, and he had taken his coat off, and his sleeves were turned back twice, as they had been on the first morning.

“How long did that take you?” I said.

“What?”

“The fire.”

“I put a shilling of my own on it,” he said. “A man cannot audit in a coat.”

“Sit,” he said.

I sat.

He turned the ninety-one volume round so that it faced me, which is a thing he did every time, and which I have since understood was the only courtesy he knew how to make.

“Read me the eleventh class from ninety-one.”

“Ashby, Mary Ann,” I said. “Wait.”

He looked up.

“There is no Ashby in ninety-one.”

“No.”

“Then why did you ask me to read it?”

“Because I wanted to see your face when you found out,” Rooke said, “and I have seen it, and now we can begin.”

I read for an hour. It is slow work, reading an old book aloud, because the hand changes every few years and the ink changes and the spelling changes, and half of what you are doing is not reading at all but deciding what a word was meant to be.

“Stop.”

“Dunnet, Sarah, widow, one life.”

“One what.”

“One life.”

“There is no such entry.”

“There is in ninety-one. It says one life, and then it says and no more, and the man who wrote it had a bad pen.”

He came round the table and looked at the page over my shoulder. He was close enough that I could smell the coal on him, and the oil in his hair, and the cold coming off his coat, and I could hear him breathing, which is a thing you do not hear a man do unless he is that near or that tired. I did not move. The page was in front of me and my finger was on the line and I kept my finger on the line, and for four seconds I was the most attentive reader in the county.

He stayed four seconds. Then he went back round the table and said, “Read on.”

I read on. I could not have told you one word of it.

“Your mother is in the eleventh class.”

“She is.”

“Has she ever been in another.”

“Not since I have been alive.”

He wrote something. “And before that.”

“I do not know what was before that.”

“No,” he said. “Neither do I. That is the difficulty.”

I did not answer him.

“Ninety-one has no Ashby,” he said. “Ninety-four has one. Ninety-eight has two. By eighteen hundred and one it has three, and by eighteen hundred and six it has three and a line drawn under them, and after that it has your mother and nobody else, and the line is still there.”

“Then my family came into the book in ninety-four.”

“Your family came into the book in ninety-four,” he agreed, “and in ninety-four there is a hand at the bottom of the page that is not the clerk’s, and I have been looking at it for nine days, and I do not know it.”

He turned the volume round and pushed it across the table to me.

“Miss Ashby. Whose hand is that?”

I looked at it for a long time. It was a good hand. It had been taught, and it was small, and it was steady, and it had made the letters the way a person makes letters who has been shown once and has never forgotten.

I had seen my mother’s hand. My mother did not write.

“My grandmother’s,” I said. “She could count.”

Rooke sat back in his chair.

“Then there are two women in this parish who could do a thing that no office allowed them to do,” he said, “and one of them is in the ground, and the other one is sitting in my chair.”

“Which one am I?”

“The one in the chair,” he said, “and the chair is mine, and I have just put a shilling of my own on the fire, and I would like that noted.”

“Noted.”

“Good.” He closed the ninety-one volume. “Now read me ninety-eight, and this time do not stop at the interesting part.”

7

A Blank Leaf

The parish chest is iron and it stands against the north wall of the little room, and the lock has been broken since before I was born, and everybody in this parish knows it, and nothing has ever been taken out of it. That tells you something about this parish that I have never been able to get into a sentence.

I took the ninety-nine volume out on the fifteenth of March at seven in the morning.

It is heavier than the others. Not because it has more in it. The paper is a different paper: the mill at Collow made it and it has a grey cast to it and it holds the damp. The first twenty leaves of that book are thicker at the bottom than at the top, the way a thing gets when it has been breathed on for thirty years.

I put it on the table under the crooked window and I opened it at April.

The eleventh class in ninety-one has eleven names. In ninety-four it has thirteen. In ninety-eight it has thirteen. In ninety-nine it has fourteen.

I had copied those numbers a hundred times. I had never once put them side by side and looked at them the way you look at a row of trees to see which one is not a tree.

The fourteenth name is my mother’s. The hand is my father’s. The ink is the ink he used from ninety-six to eighteen hundred and four, which is a browner black than the one he uses now, and I know that because I have been mixing his ink since I was fourteen and I know what a year smells like.

Under my mother’s name there is a line ruled across the column.

It is the only ruled line on the page.

✦

I sat with it for a while. The fire was not lit and the room was cold and there was a smell of cold iron off the chest, and outside a blackbird was doing the thing blackbirds do in March, which is to sing as if it were being paid by the note.

Then I went and got my father.

He was in the yard with the hurdle he had been mending since February, and he had his coat on over his shirt and no collar, and his hands were the colour of the wood.

“Come to the church,” I said.

“Now?”

“Now.”

He looked at the hurdle.

“It will still be broken when I come back,” he said, and put down the hammer.

✦

We walked up the lane without talking. That is nine minutes and I have never in my life walked nine minutes with my father in silence without counting them.

There is a thing that happens to a man’s walk when he knows where he is going and does not want to arrive. His stride gets shorter and it does not get slower, so that he takes more steps for the same road, and if you are walking beside him you find yourself taking them too, and by the time you get there you are both of you walking like men in a procession.

At the church gate he stopped.

“Which book,” he said.

“You know which book.”

“Yes,” he said. “I know which book.”

✦

I had it open at April.

He stood on the other side of the table and looked at it, and he did not touch it, and he did not sit down, and after a while he put his hands behind his back, which is what he does when he is being a clerk, and I understood that he had decided to be a clerk.

“That is my hand,” he said.

“It is your hand.”

“And that is a line I ruled.”

“Was it.”

“It is a ruled line and I ruled it.” He looked up. “You want me to say the rest and I am going to say it, and I am going to say it once, so get your pencil.”

I got my pencil.

“On the ninth of April, 1799, a man came to this house from Carrow. He came in a hired chaise, which is a thing nobody in this parish had seen before, and he had a leather case, and in the case was a slip of paper with the parish and the class and the office already written on it, and a space at the bottom for a name, and a space under that for a witness.

“He said the office had been created by the commissioners in London for the relief of persons in this parish who were in a condition that the existing classes did not provide for. He said the endowment was already made and would be paid by the office at Carrow quarterly. He said the appointment was in my gift as clerk, and that the formality was a signature, and that if I did not sign it the office would lapse, and the money would go back, and there were eleven persons in this parish who would not get it.

“He had the paper in his hand and his thumb over the bottom of it.

“And I signed it.”

The blackbird had stopped. That is a thing I have noticed about blackbirds: they stop at exactly the wrong moment.

“Did you read it,” I said.

“I read the top.”

“Did you read the bottom.”

“There was nothing at the bottom. There was a space for a name and a space for a witness. I read the top and I signed the bottom and he folded it and put it in the case and drank a glass of my beer and went away, and it took him eleven minutes.”

“And the name in the space.”

“The name in the space was your mother’s.”

I put the pencil down.

“Then why the line,” I said.

“Because a name in the office column without a line under it is a name that can be added to,” he said. “A line is a boundary. A line says: this far and no further. I ruled it that night, in this room, with the door shut, and I told your mother in the morning.”

“And she said.”

“She said, then we had better be worth it.”

✦

I want to put down what I understood in that room, because I did not understand it all at once and it is not a thing that can be put in a single sentence.

I had spent eleven years thinking that my mother’s name in the eleventh class was a small fraud for a small pension. Eleven pounds four a year and a coal allowance, and a woman who sewed, and a family that ate.

It was not that. The office had been made in London. There was an endowment. The endowment had been paid quarterly for thirty-two years. And the amount of it, whatever it was, had been going somewhere since 1799, and it had not been going to us, because we had eleven pounds four a year and a coal allowance and no more, and I had counted the coal myself since I was fourteen.

Somebody had been drawing it.

“That is not what I asked you about,” my father said.

“You asked me nothing.”

“I am asking you now.” He put both hands flat on the table on either side of the book. “You have got the book and you have got the numbers and you have got a man from Carrow with nothing to lose, and you are going to pull this out of the ground. And when you have pulled it out, the first thing that will come up with it is the name at the bottom of the slip, and the name is mine, and I signed it, and I did not read it.”

“You read the top.”

“I read the top,” he said, “and I have been a clerk for thirty-one years, and a clerk who reads the top of a thing and signs the bottom of it is a clerk who has been paid for a signature and not for a reading. That is the story they will tell.”

“Who will tell it.”

“The board. The commissioners if it comes to that.” He straightened. “And they will be right.”

✦

Rooke came in at half past eight without knocking, which he had begun to do, and which I have never decided whether to be angry about.

He had a folder under his arm and he had been out in the wind and his hair was standing up at the back, and he put the folder down and looked at the two of us and the open book and did not ask a single question, which is the most useful thing I have ever seen a man do in a doorway.

Then he came and looked at the page.

“April,” he said.

“April.”

“Whose hand.”

“Mine,” my father said.

Rooke looked at him for four seconds.

“Mr. Ashby,” he said, “I am going to say a thing to you and I would like you to hear it in the way I mean it, which is as a fact and not as an accusation. The signature at the bottom of this entry is the only piece of evidence in this parish that has a chance of being accepted at Carrow. It is accepted because it is a clerk’s signature and a clerk’s signature is a thing the board believes. And the reason it will be accepted is that it is yours.”

“And.”

“And it proves that you did it,” Rooke said. “It does not prove that you were told to.”

My father stood there with his hands behind his back.

“Then it proves nothing,” he said.

“It proves there was a paper,” Rooke said. “A paper with an office written on it and a space for a name. Papers with spaces in them do not write themselves, and they do not come from London addressed to a parish clerk in a hired chaise, and they do not have a thumb held over the bottom of them.”

“You are very sure of the thumb.”

“I have been a clerk for thirty years,” Rooke said. “I have held my thumb over a great many things.”

The room went quiet in a way that had a shape to it.

✦

I want to write down what my father did next, because it is the thing about him that I have never been able to put anywhere.

He went to the chest and opened the second drawer, which is the drawer that has the returns in it, and he took out a folded paper, and he brought it back and put it on the table and unfolded it and smoothed it with the side of his hand.

It was a quarter sheet. It had been folded so many times that the folds had gone white and the paper had begun to part along one of them.

“This is the copy I made,” he said. “The night after. I made it in this room with the door shut, and I made it because a man who has signed a thing he did not read has a choice: he can forget it, or he can keep a copy of it and not forget it. I have never been able to do the first one.”

Rooke picked it up.

He read it the way he reads everything, which is from the bottom up.

“This is not the slip,” he said.

“No.”

“This is a return. For the quarter ending Lady Day, 1799.” He turned it over. “And there is a figure at the bottom of it. One hundred and four pounds.”

“That is the endowment,” my father said. “That is what the office was endowed with. One hundred and four a year, out of the commissioners’ fund, payable to the clerk of this parish, to be applied to the office.”

“To be applied,” Rooke said. “Not paid to the holder.”

“To be applied.”

“Then somebody had to apply it.”

“Yes,” my father said. “Somebody had to apply it. And every quarter for thirty-two years somebody has signed a return saying it was applied, and the returns are in that drawer, and the signature on every one of them is mine.”

✦

I sat down on the stool by the window because my legs had begun to do the thing they do.

Rooke put the quarter sheet down on the table and put one finger on the figure.

“Mr. Ashby,” he said. “In ninety-nine, when the money came in, what did you do with it.”

“I entered it.”

“In what column.”

“In the column it came in under. The column for offices with an endowment.” My father’s voice had gone very flat. “And then I carried it forward at the bottom of the page, because that is what you do with money that has not been spent. You carry it forward.”

“And the next quarter.”

“The same.”

“And the next.”

“The same, and the next, and the next, and I have carried it forward every quarter since, and it is in the book, and anybody who can read a page can see it, and in thirty-two years not one man has asked me a question about it.”

Rooke straightened up.

“Except one,” he said.

“Except one,” my father said. “Craddock has been asking for nine years. He asks in writing. He asks the board. He asks the rector. He has never once asked me.”

“Why not.”

“Because he does not want the answer,” my father said. “He wants the office. There is a difference.”

✦

I had the pencil in my hand and I had not written anything for a quarter of an hour and the point had gone blunt.

“There is a thing I do not understand,” I said.

“Only one,” Rooke said.

“Only one that matters.” I turned the book round so that it faced me and put my finger on the ruled line. “My mother had eleven and four out of it and a coal allowance, and I have checked it, and it is exactly right.”

“And.”

“And the rest of it. Where has the hundred and four been for thirty-two years.”

Rooke looked at my father.

My father looked at the window.

“It has been applied,” he said.

“To what.”

“To the parish.” He said it slowly, the way a man says a thing he has said to himself a great many times in order to make it sound like a fact. “There is no rate in this parish that has not been short since I was a young man. The roads. The school. The pump. The bridge at the ford, which has been down twice in my time. The money has gone into the parish, quarter by quarter, and there is not one penny of it in my pocket, and there is not one penny of it in that book with a name beside it, and that is the whole of the trouble, because a thing that has been spent on the parish is a thing that nobody can point at and say there.”

The fire took a stick.

“That is what the column is,” I said.

“That is what the column is.”

“An unapplied balance that has been applied.”

“Yes.”

“Then there is no fraud,” I said. “There is only a column with a bad name.”

My father looked at me then, and his face did a thing I had seen once before, on the day my mother’s mother was buried, which is that it went completely still.

“There is a fraud,” he said. “It is not the money. The money went where it was needed and I would do it again and I would not be able to prove a penny of it.

“The fraud is the office. There was no office. There was a name in a column and a class that was made up in ninety-four to hold it, and a line ruled under it to keep it from being added to, and a woman in this parish who has been paid eleven pounds four a year for thirty-two years to be a thing that does not exist.

“And I made her that. I signed the paper. In this room. In eleven minutes.”

✦

I did not say anything.

There is a kind of silence that a person can break and a kind that he cannot, and this was the second kind, and it lasted long enough that the church clock struck nine.

Rooke was the one who broke it. He did it the way he does everything, which is without any warning at all.

“Mr. Ashby,” he said. “Where was your wife on the ninth of April, 1799.”

My father did not move.

“She was at her sister’s,” he said.

“Which sister.”

“Her younger sister. At Collow.”

“Nether Collow.”

“Nether Collow.”

Rooke took the folder out from under his arm and opened it, and there was one sheet in it, and it was not a parish paper, and I could see from where I sat that the hand on it was not any hand in this parish.

“And on the tenth,” he said. “Where was she on the tenth.”

My father looked at the paper in Rooke’s hand.

“I do not know,” he said. “She came back on the eleventh. She walked. She had been gone two nights and she came back with her boots in her hand.”

“Her boots in her hand.”

“The road was bad,” my father said, “and she had walked it in the dark, and she came in at the back door at six in the morning with her boots in her hand so that nobody would hear her on the flags.”

Rooke put the sheet down on the table and turned it round so that it faced me.

It was a bridge receipt. Fourpence, paid at the ford, dated the tenth of April, 1799, and in the space for the name of the traveller, in a toll-keeper’s hand, there were three words.

A woman, alone.

“There is a book at that bridge,” Rooke said, “and it goes back to seventeen hundred and forty, and I have read every page of the last forty years of it, and in that forty years there is exactly one person who crossed that bridge more than two hundred times and whose name was never once written down.”

I looked at the three words.

“Her,” I said.

“Her.” He closed the folder. “And the reason I asked your father where she was on the ninth is that the man who came here in the chaise did not come from Carrow that morning. He came from Collow, and he came over that bridge, and he paid fourpence, and he gave a name, and the name is not a name I know.”

“Whose name.”

“A man called Vane,” Rooke said. “And there is no Vane in the registry at Carrow, and there has not been for thirty years, and I have looked.”

✦

My father sat down then, for the first time, on the stool by the chest, and he put his hands on his knees.

“Mercy,” he said.

“Mm.”

“Your mother has been walking to that house every Tuesday for twenty years and I have never once asked her why, and I want you to understand that it was not because I did not want to know. It was because I had made a bargain with a paper in eleven minutes, and a man who has made a bargain like that does not go asking his wife questions, because he is afraid of what she will ask him back.”

He looked up.

“Now you have asked,” he said, “and I find I do not have an answer, and I would like to have one before I die, and I am not going to get it from her, because she has decided not to give it to me, and she has a right.”

I put the pencil in my pocket.

“Then I will get it,” I said.

“You will not be able to.”

“Why not.”

“Because she has been carrying it for thirty-two years,” my father said, “and a person who has carried a thing for thirty-two years does not put it down because her daughter asks nicely. She puts it down when it gets too heavy, and it will get too heavy, and when it does you will not be ready for it.

“And there is one more thing, and then I am going to go and finish that hurdle.

“The paper I signed in ninety-nine had a space for a witness. I have been telling myself for thirty-two years that the space was filled by the man from Carrow, because that is what I remember. But I have been wrong about what I remember before, and I want you to find out who signed it, and I want to know before the board meets, because if it was the rector then this parish has been lying in a way I did not know about, and if it was nobody then there is a forged witness on a parish paper and that is a hanging matter in law, and it is in my handwriting either way.”

He went out.

The door did not bang. He is not a man who bangs doors.

✦

I stood at the window and watched him go down the path with his hands behind his back and his coat too thin for the wind, and behind me Rooke was putting the quarter sheet back into the folder with the care of a man who has been handling other people’s paper for thirty years.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mm.”

“Your father has just told us that he is the one who will hang,” he said, “and he told us in eleven words and then he went out to mend a hurdle. I have been in this trade a long time and I have not often seen that.”

“He is not going to hang.”

“No,” Rooke said. “He is not, because the witness was not forged, and I know that because a forged witness would have been written in the same ink and it would have been written badly, and whoever filled that space did it with a pen that had been wiped and a hand that was not hurrying.” He put his hat on. “I have not seen the slip. But I have seen thirty-two years of returns, and the witness on every one of them is the same, and it is a woman’s hand, and it is not your mother’s.”

I turned round.

“Whose.”

“I do not know yet,” he said. “But I know where it is written down, and I am not going to get it today, because the file is at Carrow and the man who keeps it is a man I have been avoiding for eleven years.”

“Who.”

He got as far as the door.

“His name is Quill,” he said. “Ambrose Quill. He is the deputy registrar, and he was the clerk to the commission in 1806, and he is the only man alive who can tell us what was in the file before three pages of it went missing.

“And Miss Ashby, when you meet him, do not let him see that you can read a thing by touching it, because he will smile at you and he will offer you tea and he will write down the exact hour you left, and I have seen him do it to better people than either of us.”

8

Eleven Entries

By the sixteenth of March the little room had stopped being a room and had become a thing with a shape.

Rooke had done it. He had taken the four volumes out of the chest and put them on the table in order, and between them he had laid the loose returns in eleven piles, and each pile had a slip of paper on top with a year on it in his hand, and the slips were all the same size because he had cut them with a knife against a rule.

He had also brought in a second chair.

The room had a smell by the fourth day that it had never had before: paper warmed through, and coal, and the particular smell of a man’s coat hung too near a fire. It is not a bad smell. It is the smell of a thing being taken apart carefully.

I noticed the second chair before I noticed anything else, and I did not say anything about it, and neither did he, and it stood at the end of the table where the light was best.

“Nine o’clock,” he said, when I came in. “You are late.”

“The pump was frozen.”

“The pump was frozen yesterday.”

“Then it was frozen again.”

He did not look up.

That is the whole of our conversation before nine in the morning, most days. It is not much, and I have thought about it more than I have thought about most conversations I have had.

✦

We worked the returns first.

A return is a single sheet. It has the parish at the top and the quarter and the class, and then a column of figures, and then a total, and then a signature. There is nothing on it that a person could not read in a minute.

There were a hundred and twenty-eight of them.

“Read the totals,” Rooke said.

“Ninety-nine, Lady Day. One hundred and four pounds.”

“Next.”

“Ninety-nine, Midsummer. One hundred and four.”

“Next.”

We did that for an hour and a half. He wrote the figures in a column on a sheet of foolscap and I read them out, and the fire went down twice and he built it up twice, and at half past ten he put the pen down and turned the sheet round and pushed it across to me.

“Add it,” he said.

I added it.

“Three thousand three hundred and twenty-eight pounds,” I said.

“Which is.”

“One hundred and four a year for thirty-two years.”

“Which is.”

“Which is the endowment, paid in full, every quarter, for thirty-two years, and the last one was paid on the twenty-fifth of December last, and it came into this parish eleven days after my mother’s name went into the book, and it has never once been late.”

Rooke sat back.

“Now the other side,” he said.

✦

The other side was harder, because the other side was not in one place.

The office money went out of the book in the column at the bottom of the page, and the column at the bottom of the page said only carried forward, and the carrying forward was a figure, and the figure did not say what it had been carried forward into.

So we had to find it in the parish accounts.

The parish accounts are not one book. There is the churchwardens’ book, and the overseers’ book, and the surveyors’ book, and the constable’s book, and the school book, and a book that is only about the bridge, which has been kept since 1740 by a man who could not spell arch.

We went through them in the order Rooke gave, and he gave the order from the top of his head, which is a thing I have only seen two men do.

“Churchwardens,” he said. “1799 to 1804.”

“Nothing.”

“Overseers, same years.”

“There is a hundred and eighty pounds in 1799 under repairs, roads and bridge.”

“Note it.”

“Surveyors, 1800.”

“There will be nothing.”

“There is nothing.”

“Constable, 1801.”

“There is four pounds eleven in 1801 under removal of a family to Collow, and I know that entry, I copied it, and the family was the Dunnet family, and they came back in 1803.”

“Note it and go on.”

✦

We found two thousand three hundred and nine pounds.

It took us four days.

It was in the bridge book and the school book and the overseers’ book and once, in 1817, in a book that I had never opened, which was the book of the parish coal yard, and there it was under a heading that said coals for the poor, sundry, and the sundry was eighty-one pounds.

Two thousand three hundred and nine pounds of roads and bridges and school slates and coal, spread over thirty-two years, in eleven different hands, and not one line anywhere in any of it said where the money had come from.

And the difference was six hundred and fifty-nine pounds.

I did that sum three times. I did it with the pencil he had given me and I did it with a pen and I did it on the back of a return, and it came out the same every time.

The pencil was a bad pencil by then. I had sharpened it four times on the underside of the table, and the wood was down to the lead and the lead was down to a stub, and I did the last sum with a stub because I did not want to ask him for another one.

Six hundred and fifty-nine pounds, that had come in every quarter for thirty-two years and had gone out of the column and had not gone into any book in this parish.

✦

“Then it is somewhere,” I said.

“It is somewhere.”

“Then somebody has it.”

“Or somebody spent it,” Rooke said, “and did not write it down, and a thing that is spent and not written down is a thing that is gone.”

He had his coat off and his sleeves turned back twice and there was a smudge of ink on the side of his right hand that had been there since Tuesday.

“There is a third possibility,” he said.

“Say it.”

“That it was applied to a purpose that is not a parish purpose.” He turned a leaf. “An endowment for an office is not parish money. It is money held in trust for a particular use, and if the use does not exist, the money does not become the parish’s. It goes back.”

“To whom.”

“To the commissioners. Or to the Crown. Or to whoever the commissioners sold it to in 1806, which is a thing they did, and which is a thing I have seen in a file at Carrow with my own eyes.”

The fire took a stick.

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Mm.”

“Six hundred and fifty-nine pounds.”

“Yes.”

“That is not a small sum.”

“No.”

“That is a house.” I put the pencil down. “That is a house and four acres and a cow, in this parish, at the prices my father quotes. That is a man’s whole life, put by, in a column, for thirty-two years, and nobody has touched it.”

Rooke looked at me for a while.

“You are thinking that it is still there,” he said.

“I am thinking that a sum that nobody has touched is a sum that somebody is saving.”

✦

Craddock came at noon with his hat in his hand and his gloves on, which is how he comes when he has decided to be formal.

He stood in the doorway and looked at the table.

“You have been busy,” he said.

“The auditor has been busy.”

“The auditor.” Craddock came in and put his hat on the chest and did not sit down. “Miss Ashby. I am going to ask you a question and I want you to think before you answer, because the answer will go into a deposition and a deposition is read out at the board.”

“Ask it.”

“Did your father sign a paper in 1799 that placed your mother in the eleventh class.”

I looked at him.

“Yes,” I said.

Rooke had not moved. He was standing at the window with his back to us and his hands behind him, and he might have been a man looking at weather.

“Did he read it.”

“He read the top.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It is the answer he gave me.”

Craddock put his gloves on the chest beside his hat, one on top of the other, and squared them with two fingers.

“Then I will tell you what will happen,” he said. “At the Lady Day board I will present a case. The case will say that an office was created in ninety-four for which there was no warrant, that a paper was signed in ninety-nine by the clerk of this parish, that the paper was not read, that the office has been held for thirty-two years by a woman who is not qualified to hold it, and that the endowment has been drawn quarterly and applied to parish purposes without authority, which is a misapplication, which is a matter for the commissioners and possibly for the courts.”

“That is four things,” I said.

“It is four things.”

“And the fourth is the only one that is true.”

Craddock’s face did not change.

“The first three are true as well,” he said. “That is what makes the fourth one dangerous.”

✦

I have thought about that sentence for a long time.

He was right, and he knew he was right, and there was no pleasure in his face at all.

“Mr. Craddock.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“You have been asking for nine years where the fourteenth name came from.”

“I have.”

“And now you know.”

“And now I know.” He picked up his gloves. “I would rather not have known. I want you to understand that. For nine years I have been able to say that the book does not add up and that somebody should look at it. Now I have looked at it, and the somebody is a man with a bad throat who mends hurdles in his yard, and the woman is the woman who has been giving my wife bread at the church door for eleven years.”

“You are sorry for us.”

“I am not sorry for you,” Craddock said. “I am sorry for the book. There is a difference and you are clever enough to know it.”

“Then what do you want from me.”

He put his gloves on.

“I want you to sign a statement,” he said. “It will say what you saw and when you saw it and that you told me of your own accord. It will not say that your father is a criminal, because I do not think he is one. It will say that the office was irregular, and that the irregularity began before you were born, and that you have cooperated.”

“And if I sign it.”

“Then when the board strikes the office out, it will strike it out of the book and not out of your father. He will lose the office at Michaelmas. He will keep his pension, because a pension is not an office and I have checked.” He paused. “And your mother will keep her coal until Lady Day, which is nine days from the board, and after that she will be in the eleventh class as a pauper and not as a holder, and the difference is eleven pounds four a year and a coal allowance, and I am telling you the figure because I do not want you to be able to say afterwards that nobody told you.”

There was no good answer to that.

“And if I do not sign it.”

“Then I will present the case without your statement,” Craddock said, “and the board will ask why the clerk’s daughter, who copied the book and who found the fourteenth name, did not come forward. And there will be an answer to that question and it will not be a good one, and the man who gives it will be your father.”

✦

Rooke turned round from the window.

“Mr. Craddock.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“You have forgotten a thing.”

“I doubt it.”

“You have forgotten that the office is not the only irregularity in this parish.” Rooke came over to the table and put one finger on the foolscap sheet with the column of figures on it. “I have spent four days adding up thirty-two years of returns. The endowment came in at one hundred and four a year and it was applied, in eleven hands, to roads and bridges and coal and school slates, and there are receipts for two thousand three hundred and nine pounds of it, and two thousand three hundred and nine pounds of roads and coal in a parish that has been telling the board for thirty years that it cannot afford to mend its own bridge.”

Craddock looked at the sheet.

“Where is the rest,” he said.

“Six hundred and fifty-nine pounds is unaccounted for.”

“Then find it.”

“I intend to.” Rooke straightened. “But you should understand what you are doing. You are bringing a case about a paper signed by a clerk in ninety-nine. The moment that case is opened, the board will want the accounts, and the accounts are the thing this parish has been hiding, and they are not hiding a woman in the eleventh class. They are hiding six hundred and fifty-nine pounds.”

The room was very quiet.

“You are telling me,” Craddock said slowly, “that if I bring this case, I will ruin the parish.”

“I am telling you that if you bring this case, the parish will be examined,” Rooke said, “and I have been examining parishes for thirty years, and I have never yet examined one that came out of it clean. You are an honest man and you have been nine years on a book that does not add up, and I am telling you that the book does not add up in a place you have not looked.”

Craddock stood there with his hat in his hand.

“And the woman,” he said.

“The woman stays in the class until the board rules,” Rooke said. “That is the law, and the law is not changed by a case being prepared.”

✦

Craddock went out.

I heard his boots on the flags and then on the path and then nothing.

“He will bring it anyway,” I said.

“Yes.”

“Then why tell him.”

“Because he will bring it differently.” Rooke sat down in the second chair, the one he had brought in, and rubbed his eyes with the heel of his hand. “A man who is certain he is right comes into a room with a case prepared. A man who is not certain comes into a room and asks questions. I would rather be examined by a man who is not certain.”

“He is certain.”

“He was certain when he came in,” Rooke said. “He is less certain now. It will not stop him and it will make him slower, and slower is what your father needs, because your father has nine days and a bad throat and a hurdle to mend.”

I looked at the sheet of foolscap.

“Six hundred and fifty-nine pounds,” I said.

“You are not to go looking for it.”

“I am not going to promise that.”

“I did not ask you to promise it. I asked you not to do it.” He picked up his pen. “There is a difference, and it is the same difference as the one between a copy that is fair and a copy that is true, and you are the only person in this parish who knows what I mean, and you are going to ignore me.”

“Probably.”

“Then at least tell me where you are going.”

I got my coat.

“To look at what the money bought,” I said.

✦

I walked the parish that afternoon and looked at what thirty-two years of an office that did not exist had paid for.

The bridge at the ford, which has three arches and a stone in the middle one with the date 1799 on it, and the date has been cut deep and the mason did it well, and it is the best piece of work in this parish and nobody has ever thought to ask who paid for it.

The school, built in 1811, with a stove in it and forty children and seven maps.

The coal yard, which has a wall round it that was built in 1822, and the wall has a gate in it and the gate has a lock, and the lock is the only lock in this parish that works.

I stood at the coal yard gate for a while.

A woman came out with a sack and said good afternoon, and I said good afternoon, and she went off down the lane with the sack on her shoulder, and I stood there looking at a wall that had been paid for by a woman in a class that did not exist, out of an endowment created by a commission in London, and every stone of it was in the right place.

The light was going when I came down the last lane. There is a point on that lane where the whole parish is in front of you at once: church, mill, school roof, four chimneys with smoke, and the reservoir beyond them lying flat and grey like a plate. In March the sun goes down behind the reservoir and the water takes the light and throws it back up at the sky, and for about four minutes the whole parish is lit from underneath.

I stood at that point for the four minutes.

Then I went home and my mother was at the window.

“Where have you been,” she said.

“Looking at walls.”

She stood there with her hands on the sill.

“You have been looking at the wrong walls,” she said. “Those are the ones the money built. You want the one it did not.”

9

Three Times in One Night

The house has three rooms and a passage and a stair with nine steps, and at night that stair makes a noise on the fourth step and on the seventh, and everybody in the house knows it, and we all walk on the edges.

I waited until the clock had struck eleven and then I waited another quarter of an hour to be certain, and then I came down with my boots in my hand, because a person who is going to do a thing she should not do might as well be quiet about it.

My father sleeps in the chair now. He has slept in the chair since the winter, and the reason is the stair, and the reason is not the stair.

He was in it with his head back and his mouth open and his hands on the arms, and there was a fire down to three coals and a lamp turned so low that the wick was only a red line.

My mother’s boots were by the door under the coat.

✦

I want to put down how the reading works, because I have been careless about it in this account and a careless account is worse than none.

A thing will tell you what it was made for. That is the first thing, and it is not the same as saying that a thing remembers. A thing does not remember. A thing is a record, and the record is of the making and the using, and the using is the part that hurts.

When I put my hand on a thing I go away for three seconds. Not three seconds by the clock. Three seconds by the thing: it is as long as the thing has to say, and no thing has ever said more than three seconds’ worth, and I have tried. I have put my hand on a chair and stayed and stayed and it will not give more than the three, and the rest is like a room you can see through a keyhole and cannot get into.

Afterwards there is a price. Not always the same price. A cup is nothing. A wall is a headache. A thing that has been used hard and long enough costs more, and the price is paid at the time or it is paid at four in the morning and there is no asking which.

Dead things are honest. I will say that once, here, and then not again, because it is the whole of my religion and a religion does not want repeating.

✦

The boots were cold and stiff and the leather at the heel was worn down on the outside edge, which is the way a person wears a boot who walks with her weight on the outside of her foot, which my mother does, and which I had never once thought about.

I put both hands on them.

The kitchen went away.

✦

The first was the road.

Not a picture of the road. The road itself, from the height of a woman walking it, with the boots going down on it one after the other. There was a gate at the ford that she opened with her hip and not her hand, and the hinge made a noise like a bird being stood on, and she did not hurry past the noise, which is what a person does who is afraid of being heard.

Three seconds. The kitchen came back with the fire at three coals.

I sat on the floor with my back against the dresser and let my hands shake.

Then I did it again.

✦

The second was the water.

There is a place on that road, past the ford, where the ground goes down and holds the wet, and there is a board laid over it and the board is older than my father. She stepped on it and it gave, and the water came up over the boot to the ankle, and she stopped.

She stopped for four seconds. Not because of the cold. Because a person who has walked a road two hundred times and knows every board on it has stepped on that board on purpose, and I have thought about that a great deal since.

Then she went on.

And under the water going over the boot there was a second thing, which was a sound, and the sound was singing. Low. Not words. The sort of singing a woman does when she is doing something with her hands and has forgotten that anybody can hear.

My mother does not sing in this house. I have lived eighteen years in this house and I have never heard her sing.

✦

I put the boots down and I sat and looked at them for a while.

The fire had gone to two coals. The lamp had begun to smell.

The third reading is the one I have never told anybody the whole of, and I am going to put it down here because the account is not worth having if it is a careful one.

✦

The third was a door.

Not the road. Not the water. A door, at the end of a passage, with a light under it, and a hand on the latch. Her hand. The hand was old by then, or older, and there was a ring on it that I had never seen, and the ring was iron and it was on the third finger of the left hand and it was too big and it had been turned round so that the flat of it faced out.

She lifted the latch.

The door opened into a room with a bed in it and a woman in the bed, and the woman in the bed turned her head, and I saw her face for one quarter of a second before the kitchen came back and I was on the floor with my head down and blood coming out of my nose onto the flags.

✦

I have thought about that face for a long time.

It was my mother’s face.

Not my mother. My mother was asleep upstairs with a shirt on her lap and her mouth open. But it was her face, in the way that a face is her face, and it was older, and it was thinner, and it was dying, and it looked at my mother in the doorway the way a person looks at somebody who has come a long way.

I sat on the floor and held my nose and counted to sixty.

✦

The fourth step made its noise at half past twelve.

I had the boots in my hands and no time to put them back, so I put them behind me and sat up straight, which is what a person does when she has been caught and has decided to be caught honourably.

My mother came down the last five steps with her hand on the wall.

She had her shawl on over her nightdress and her hair was down and she looked, for a moment, about forty, which is the age she was when I was small, and which I had forgotten.

“You have had them,” she said.

“Yes.”

“Both of them.”

“Yes.”

She came and sat down in my father’s chair, which is a thing she never does, and put her hands in her lap.

“Then say what you saw,” she said, “and I will tell you what is true and what is only the boot.”

✦

I told her.

I told her about the stone a mile out and the hedge that leaned and the gate she opened with her hip. I told her about the board that gave and the water over the ankle and the four seconds. I told her about the singing, and she looked at the fire when I said the singing.

I did not tell her about the door.

When I had finished she sat for a while with her hands in her lap.

“The stone,” she said. “That is real. It is a bad stone and I have stepped over it since I was a girl and I have never once stepped on it. The hedge is real. The gate is real, and the hinge wants grease, and I have asked your father for grease for eleven years and he has not got round to it, and that is the whole of my marriage in one sentence, and you will not put that in the book.”

“The singing.”

“The singing is mine,” she said. “That is the thing about a boot. A boot will tell you what a woman did and it will not tell you why. I sing on that road because there is nobody on it and because the singing keeps the walking from being the only thing in my head.”

“Then why do you do the walking.”

She looked at me.

“Because she is there,” she said. “Because she is there and she is my sister and she has been dying for eleven years and there is not one other person in this parish who will go up that lane, and if I do not go, then she is in a room at the end of a passage with a light under the door and nobody at all.”

✦

I sat very still.

“My sister,” I said.

“Your aunt. Ellen. She married a Rooke in 1804 and he died in 1809 and she has been at Collow since.”

“Rooke.”

“Rooke is a common name in this county.” My mother’s voice had gone flat. “It is not that Rooke. It is not any Rooke you are thinking of. It is a man who kept bees and died of a fall and she has been a widow for twenty-two years and she has a house with four rooms and a garden and she has not been out of the gate since Michaelmas.”

“Does she know I exist.”

“She knows everything about you,” my mother said. “She has known since the day you were born. She has a cloth she was making for you that she has never finished because she cannot see well enough now, and she asks me every Tuesday what you are doing, and I tell her, and she says the same thing every week, which is she should not come here.”

“Why not.”

“Because she is the last of it,” my mother said, “and a thing that is the last of something is a thing that people come and look at, and she does not want to be looked at.

“And because there is a thing in that house that I have been carrying back and forth on that road for twenty years, and if you come into it, you will put your hand on it, because that is what you do, and it will tell you everything, and then you will be in it and there will be no getting you out.”

✦

The clock in the passage struck one.

“The thimble,” my mother said.

“What thimble.”

“The one in the drawer in the bedroom. The one I keep the pins in.” She had her eyes on the fire. “I want you to have it. I have wanted you to have it since you were fourteen and I have not been able to give it to you, and I am going to tell you why, and then you will understand what kind of a thing it is that I have been doing every Tuesday for twenty years.”

She put her hand into the pocket of her nightdress and took out a small thing and held it in her palm.

It was a thimble. Silver, worn to the copper at the tip, and there was a mark on it that had been rubbed almost away.

“That was hers,” she said. “Not mine. I have never used it. I have kept pins in it and I have kept it in a drawer and I have not once put it on my finger, because it does not fit me and because it is not mine to wear.”

“Whose.”

My mother looked at me.

“Put your hand on it,” she said, “and then tell me, and I will know whether you are ready for the rest.”

✦

I put my hand on it.

Three seconds.

A woman was sitting at a table with her hands in her lap and a lamp beside her, and she was not sewing. The thimble was on her finger and there was a heap of something in front of her on the table, and she was not looking at it. She was looking at the door.

She was waiting for somebody, and she had been waiting a long time, and she was not afraid, and the not being afraid was the thing that came through.

Then the kitchen came back.

“It is a woman waiting,” I said. “At a table. For somebody. She is not afraid.”

My mother closed her hand on the thimble.

“Yes,” she said. “That is what it says. It has said that to me every time and I have put my hand on it perhaps forty times in twenty years, and it always says the same, and it has never once said who came through the door.”

“It is a thimble. It would not know.”

“No,” my mother said. “But I have always thought that a thing which remembers a woman waiting would remember the door.”

✦

She stood up.

“Take it,” she said. “It is yours. It has been yours since you were fourteen and I have been keeping it because I am a coward, and I am not going to be a coward about a thimble at one o’clock in the morning.”

I took it.

It was warm. It should not have been warm. It had been in a drawer in a cold room for twenty years and it was warm in my hand the way a thing is warm when it has just been put down.

“There is one more thing,” my mother said, “and then we are going to bed and we are not going to speak of this again before breakfast, because your father will hear it in the chair and he has enough.”

“Say it.”

“Your aunt has a book.” She had her hand on the stair rail. “It is not a parish book. It is a book she made herself, and it is in that house, and it has been in that house since 1806, and there are things written in it that are not in any book at Carrow, and one of them is a name.

“I have never read it. She offered it to me in 1811 and I said no, and I have thought about that no every Tuesday for twenty years.

“If you go up that lane, and you will go, because you are what you are, then you will read it, and when you have read it you will know what your grandmother did and what I have been paying back a loaf at a time, and you will not be able to be eighteen any more.

“So I am asking you to wait. Not to not go. To wait until I am dead, which will be in ten months, and which I have known since November, and which is the reason I have been doing the walking while I still can.”

She went up the stairs.

On the fourth step she stopped.

“And Mercy,” she said. “The thimble was hers. Not mine. Do not let anybody tell you I was the one who started it.”

She went up, and the seventh step made its noise, and then the door of the room above, and then nothing.

✦

I sat on the floor with a thimble in my hand and my nose still tasting of blood and the fire out.

There was a paper on the table under the salt.

It had not been there when I came down.

I unfolded it. Three lines in a hand I had been reading sideways for a fortnight, and one word under them, and the word was not do not answer this, because that had been the last one and he is not a man who writes the same sentence twice.

The file is not at Carrow. It was moved in 1807 to the diocesan office at Ely, and the man who moved it is dead, and the man who received it is not.

If your mother is going to tell you the truth, let her tell it in her own words and do not correct her.

She has been keeping it for thirty-two years and she will get it wrong in the small places and right in the large ones.

10

What I Wrote at Two in the Morning

The Nunn house is the last but one in the lane. It has two rooms and a lean-to, and there were five people in it when I got there at eleven at night, and there was a fire in the grate that had no coal in it, only wood that was too green.

The whole house smelled of that wood. It is a smell that gets into the back of your throat and stays there. I have never been able to be in a room with it since.

Nunn met me at the door with a candle and his boots off.

“She is not well,” he said.

“I know.”

“Mrs. Gow came at eight and she has gone home and she said to come back if. She said to come back if.” He stopped. “I have forgotten what she said to come back if.”

I went in.

✦

The child was in the bed in the corner, and she was two years old, and her name was Hannah, and she had been crying for six hours and now she was not crying, which is worse.

Her breathing had a sound in it. Not a rattle. A pull. The skin under her ribs went in when she breathed, and the skin at the base of her throat went in, and she was hot in a way that a person is hot when the heat has got past being a fever and become the thing itself.

Mrs. Nunn was sitting on the edge of the bed with her hand on the child’s forehead and she did not look up when I came in.

“There is no coal,” she said, in a flat voice, twice, and I understood that she was not telling me a fact. She was telling me the reason. She had been holding the reason since October and had nowhere to put it.

Nunn stood in the corner with his hands hanging and did not come near the bed, and I have seen that in men before. It is not that they do not love the child. It is that they have been told all their lives that a thing in a bed is a woman’s business, and they have come to believe it, and now they are standing in a corner with their hands hanging and there is nothing to do with them.

“Mr. Nunn.”

“Ma’am.”

“Go and break up that hurdle in the yard and bring it in. All of it. Not the top rails. The whole of it.”

“It is a good hurdle.”

“It is a hurdle,” I said, “and it is wood, and she is two.”

“The doctor is four miles,” she said.

“The doctor is four miles and he will not come for a child in a lean-to at eleven at night,” I said. “I am not going to fetch him. I am going to tell you what to do and then I am going to go and do the other thing.”

✦

I did the thing with the water and the cloth and the steam, which is what my mother did for the Rye children in the bad winter, and which is not medicine, and which is the only thing there is.

Then I went out and stood in the lane and looked at the house and understood the whole of it in about four seconds.

The Nunns had no coal.

They had no coal because Nunn’s office had been marked diminished in the Michaelmas copy. A diminished office is not a vacant office, and it is not a full office, and the parish pays half of what it paid before, and the coal allowance goes with the half that is gone.

And the word diminished was in the book because I had written it there. In October. In my father’s hand. In a warm room. Without thinking about it for one second.

I had written it on a page, and a page had taken a coal allowance off a house with a two-year-old in it, and the two-year-old was now breathing with a pull in her ribs.

That is not a figure of speech. That is the mechanism. I want it written down exactly, because people who have not lived in a parish think a book is a record of things, and a book is not a record of things, a book is a machine, and it has a crank, and the crank was in my hand.

✦

The church is nine minutes from the Nunns’ door. I did it in six.

The little room was not locked. It has never been locked. I have thought since that if it had been locked I might have gone home, and I want to be honest about that, and I am not sure I do.

I lit the lamp with the tinder box and I took down the current book and I opened it at the Michaelmas quarter and I found Nunn.

Nunn, Thomas. Office: thatcher. Diminished.

The word was mine. The hand was my father’s. There is a whole philosophy in that and I did not have time for it.

I took the penknife out of the drawer and I scraped at the d.

✦

I want to say what I did and what I did not do, because there is a difference and it matters.

I did not change the word. If I had changed diminished to full there would have been a scrape, and a scrape is a thing that a man who has been at this trade for thirty years can see through the cover of a book, and my father would have seen it before the board did and he would have known it was me.

What I did was this. I scraped the d, and I scraped the i, and I scraped the m, and I wrote over the top of it, in the same ink, in my father’s hand, the word reduced.

Reduced is not in the schedule. There is no such category. But a word that is not in the schedule is not a diminution, and a man who is not diminished has a coal allowance, and a coal allowance is six hundredweight of coal a quarter, and six hundredweight of coal is a fire in a grate in a house with a two-year-old in it.

It took me four minutes and it is the best piece of writing I have ever done.

Then I signed it.

✦

The signing is the part I have gone over most.

My father signs with a loop on the J that he has never been able to get out of his hand since he was a boy, and a y that goes down and comes back up through itself, and a pressure at the end of the surname that puts a dent in the paper. I have watched him sign eleven thousand times. I have copied his signature on the fair copy more times than I can count, and I have never once been asked to, and I have never once been told not to.

I did it in one movement.

My hand did not shake. That is the thing I want to put down. My hand had shaken for a quarter of an hour at the bread table over a word I had already written. My hand did not shake at all over a signature.

When I had done it I blotted it, which is what a person does when they have practiced.

✦

I put the book back. I put the penknife in the drawer with the blade closed. I put the lamp out and I stood in the dark in that room for a moment with my hands on the edge of the table.

There is a particular silence in a church at two in the morning. It is not the silence of an empty room. It is the silence of a place that has been full all day and is not full now, and it has a pressure in it, like a held breath.

I said, out loud, to nobody: “It is only coal.”

The church did not answer, which was decent of it.

✦

Rooke was in the little room when I came at eight.

He had the current book open.

I saw it from the door and I stopped in the doorway, and my whole body did the thing it does, which is that the blood went out of my hands and left them cold and the back of my neck went wet.

“Sit down,” he said.

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Sit down.”

I sat down.

He turned the book round so that it faced me, and put his finger on the entry, and did not say anything for long enough that I heard a cart go past the churchyard wall and turn up the lane.

“Read it,” he said.

“Nunn, Thomas. Office: thatcher. Reduced.”

“Reduced.” He took his finger off. “That is a very good word.”

“Sir.”

“It is not a word in the schedule. It is not in the schedule of 1794, and it is not in the amendments of 1811, and it is not in the standing orders. It is a word that a person would use if they wanted a thing to stop being a diminution without saying that it was a full office, and it is a word I have never once seen in a book in thirty years.”

I said nothing.

“Somebody wrote that word in the Michaelmas quarter,” Rooke said. “The Michaelmas quarter was copied in October and the copy was signed by me in November, and I read the copy in November, and it did not say reduced.”

“Then somebody has altered it since November.”

“Yes.”

“And a person who alters a parish book.”

“A person who alters a parish book is a person who has committed a felony,” Rooke said, “and the penalty is transportation, and it is not a penalty that has been imposed in this county in my lifetime, and it is a penalty that is on the statute book, and there is no discretion in it. There is not a fine. There is not a warning. There is transportation.”

The fire had gone down.

I sat with my hands in my lap and looked at the word.

“Are you going to report it,” I said.

Rooke got up and went to the window and stood with his back to me, and the crooked light came down onto his shoulder in the shape of a broken kite.

“No,” he said.

“May I ask why.”

“Because the book is mine.” He said it without turning round. “I signed that copy in November. It is my signature at the bottom of the page and my signature is the thing that makes the page lawful. If there is an alteration in a copy I have signed, then the fault is mine before it is anybody else’s, and I am not going to transport a girl to save my own name.”

“That is not the reason.”

“It is part of the reason.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

He turned round.

“The other part,” he said, “is that the child is better this morning, and Mrs. Gow has been down the lane telling everybody that the clerk’s daughter came at eleven and did the steam and the cloth, and that the child is better, and that there is coal in the Nunns’ grate this morning which nobody can account for.

“And I have been in this parish for a month and I have seen a girl write a name in a book and lose her copy for it, and stand in a market and give away a thing about herself that she cannot get back, and sit in a vestry and be told that her hand is not fit to make a copy, and I have not once seen her do a thing for herself.

“So when I found that word this morning I sat down and I thought about what I would have done if I had been eighteen and there was a child with a pull in her ribs and the word in the book was mine.

“And then I closed the book and I waited for you to come in.”

✦

I do not know what my face did.

“There is a condition,” he said.

“Name it.”

“You will not do it again.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“I am not asking. I am telling you the condition.” He came back to the table. “You have done a thing that cannot be undone. That page will go to Carrow at Lady Day and it will be bound into a volume and it will be there in a hundred years, and a man in a hundred years will read reduced and will not know that it was a lie, and that is the whole horror of it, and it is the same horror that is on the page in ninety-nine with your mother’s name on it.

“You are now the third person in this parish to do it. Your father did it in ninety-nine. Somebody did it in ninety-four. And you did it on Tuesday night with a penknife.

“And I am the man who is going to have to decide, at Lady Day, whether to send all three of you to Carrow in a bundle.”

“You would not.”

“I would not,” he said. “But I want you to know that the reason I would not is not that it is not my duty. The reason is that I have been thirty years in this trade and I have never once seen a thing made right by a rule, and I have seen a great many things made worse. So I am going to do the thing I have always done, which is to write a note in the margin and let the next man deal with it.”

He picked up his pen.

“What note,” I said.

“Examined. Word found to be irregular. Referred.” He wrote it. “That is all. Three words and a date. It does not say who. It does not say when. It says a thing that is true, which is that the word is irregular, and it says a thing that is also true, which is that it has been referred, and it has been referred, to me, and I am going to sit on it.”

“Until when.”

“Until I know what else is in this parish.” He blotted it. “Which will be soon, because I am going to Ely, and I am going to take the bridge book, and I am going to come back with a file that has three pages missing out of four.”

✦

He put his pen down and stood for a moment with his hand flat on the book.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Sir.”

“Your father’s signature has a dent in it.”

I looked at him.

“The pen he uses has a bad nib and he leans on the last stroke of the surname, and it puts a dent in the paper that you can feel with the back of a thumbnail.” He closed the book. “Yours does not.”

I sat very still.

“You did it with the nib turned,” he said. “Which is clever, because the line is thinner and the loop is better. But the dent is in the paper and not in the ink, and a man who signs eleven thousand times puts a dent in the paper without knowing he is doing it, and a woman who copies a signature once does not.
