“Do not do it again. Not because it is wrong. Because you are better than that, and a person who is better than that should not be caught by a dent.”

✦

He went out.

At the door he stopped and looked back at me, and there was something in his face that I had not seen there before, and it took me a year to be able to name it, and the name is not fear.

“If I am right about what is in that file,” he said, “then I am going to have to ask you to do a thing that is worse than altering a word in a book, and I am going to ask you in front of people, and you will not be able to refuse, and I want you to know now that I will not enjoy it.”

“Ask me now.”

“No.” He put his hat on. “Ask me why nobody in this parish has ever said to your face that your mother is not in the class she is in.”

“Why has nobody.”

“Because they have all been eating the bread,” he said, “and I am the only man in this parish who has not, and that is not a virtue, it is an accident of where I was born, and it is the reason you should not trust me as far as you can throw me.”

✦

I sat in the little room for a while after he had gone.

There was a dent in the paper. I put the back of my thumbnail on it and felt it, and then I did the thing I should not have done, which is that I put my hand flat on the page.

Three seconds. A man in a coat with his sleeve on the table to keep the ink off it, signing fast and once and not going back, and then stopping and looking at the wall.

My father. In October. Signing what I had written.

I took my hand off.

That is the thing about a copy. It is not one hand. It is every hand that has been over it, and they are all in the paper, and a person who can read a page can read all of them, and there is no such thing as a secret in a book.

11

As Far as the Bridge

The ford is a mile and a quarter out of the village and the bridge is over it, and the bridge is the boundary of the parish, and there is a stone on the far side with the parish name cut into it and a line under the name, and the line is the boundary, and it goes down into the water and comes out on the other side and goes up the bank.

I had been to that stone eleven times in my life.

I had never once been past it.

✦

I went out on the eighteenth of March, which was a Friday, and I went in the afternoon because the afternoon is when a woman may be out of the house without being asked where she is going, and I took nothing with me but the thimble in my pocket and a piece of bread.

The lane to the ford was dry on top and wet underneath, which is what March is, and there were lambs in the field on the left, and they were doing the thing lambs do, which is to run in a group for no reason and then stop and look at a person as though she had asked them a question.

At the last gate there is a house, and the house is the toll house, and the toll house has a window in the gable that faces the bridge, and the window has four panes and one of them is cracked and has been cracked since 1819.

Nobody has lived in the toll house since 1827. The bridge is free now, and it has been free since the Turnpike Act, and the book is still in the house because nobody has ever taken it away.

✦

The door was not locked. That is the third unlocked door I have written down in this account and I would like it understood that this is not carelessness, it is a parish, and a parish is a place where a thing is left alone because everybody knows whose it is.

There is one room downstairs and a ladder to a loft. There was a table and a chair and a settle and a dead fire and a smell of cold soot. The book was on the table with a stone on it to keep the wind from taking the leaves.

I sat down in the chair.

✦

I want to put down what I did, because what I did was not reading.

I opened the book at the back and I looked at the last page that had anything on it, and then I turned back a year, and then five years, and then twenty.

It is a toll book. It has a column for the date and a column for the name of the traveller and a column for what they were, whether on foot or on horse or with a cart, and a column for the toll, and a column that was barely used, which is the column for remarks.

Nobody writes a remark in a toll book. It is not that kind of book. But there is a man who kept it from 1794 to 1827 and he wrote remarks, and his remarks were mostly about the weather, and once, in 1802, a sow, very unwilling.

I read four hundred pages of that man’s remarks in an hour and a quarter.

And in the years after 1806 there is a thing that happens on Tuesdays.

✦

I found it first by the shape.

There is a hand that writes a name in a toll book for twenty years and after a while the name has a shape, and the shape is not the letters, it is the pressure of the pen and the little turn at the end of the last stroke. This hand has a turn at the end of the last stroke. It goes up.

In 1807 there are four Tuesdays with a turn at the end.

In 1812 there are twelve.

In 1819 there are twenty-seven, because the man had begun to write a woman and then stopped and written nothing, and a stopped word has a shape too.

By 1826 there is nothing at all on Tuesdays. The column is empty on every Tuesday for that whole year, and the man’s remarks stop in the September of that year, and there is a note at the bottom of the last page in a different hand, which says: Toll abolished. Book closed.

Twenty years of Tuesdays. Not one name.

There is one entry I have never been able to leave alone. It is the fourteenth of February, 1827, and the traveller is a woman, and the toll is fourpence, and in the remarks column the man wrote: valentine, and she would not take it.

I do not know what to do with that. It is the only line in four hundred pages where he wrote something that was not about the weather. It is about a woman who came over his bridge in February and would not take a thing he was offering, and it is the last thing he wrote in the book before the toll was abolished.

I sat with my hands flat on the table.

Twenty years of Tuesdays. Not one name.

✦

I went out and stood on the bridge.

The water was high and it was the colour of a cup of tea with too much milk in it, and it came down under the arches in a way that made the stones look like they were doing the work, and there was a branch turning over and over in the pool below the second arch and it had been turning there for as long as I watched it.

The far bank is a field and then a hedge and then a lane, and the lane goes up, and there is a house at the top of the lane with a roof that needs work, and there was smoke coming out of the chimney of that house.

One chimney. One thin line of smoke, going up and then bending east because the wind was from the west.

I stood on the bridge for a while and looked at that smoke.

It is nine hundred yards from the middle arch to that chimney. I have measured it since on the map at Carrow. It is nine hundred yards and it took me eleven minutes to decide not to walk it, and I want to put that down too, because a person who reads this account will want to know why a girl who had gone into a vestry and a market and a book would stand on a bridge and turn round.

The answer is that a bridge is not a market. In a market you can be brave for four minutes and then go home. On the far side of a bridge there is a woman in a bed who has been waiting for twenty years, and the moment you walk up that lane you are not brave, you are something, and it is a thing you cannot put down again.

✦

Rooke came up the lane at four with his hat in his hand.

He had walked. His boots were grey to the ankle.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“I have been to your house and your mother told me where you were, and she told me in a way that made it clear that she did not want to tell me and equally clear that she had decided it was better than the alternative.”

“Which was.”

“Which was you standing on a bridge alone at dusk with a thimble in your pocket.” He put his hat on. “The book is in there.”

“It is.”

“And you have read it.”

“I have read four hundred pages of a man’s opinions about the weather, and I have found twenty years of Tuesdays and not one name, and the reason there is no name is that the man who kept this book knew her and would not write her down.”

Rooke looked at the toll house and then at the water and then at me.

“Then he was a better man than most,” he said.

“He was a coward.”

“He was a man who had one book and one living and a woman who came over his bridge every Tuesday for twenty years,” Rooke said. “I have known a great many cowards and they do not usually cost themselves anything. That man cost himself the only thing he had, which was a tidy book.”

✦

I had not thought of it that way.

“The file is not at Carrow,” I said.

“No.”

“Your note said Ely.”

“My note said Ely because the diocesan office at Ely has the archive of the commission, and because the commission sat at Ely in 1806, and because a man who wants to hide a page does not hide it in the place where the person who wants it will look first.” He took a paper out of his coat. “I have the letter.”

“What letter.”

“The letter that went with the file. It is a covering letter and it is one page and it was written by the clerk to the commission and it is the only thing in the whole business that has a date on it that nobody has tried to hide.”

He held it out.

I did not take it.

“I will read it with my hand,” I said.

“I know.”

“And then I will know what happened in that room in 1806 from the man who wrote it, and it will be a thing that no book at Carrow will ever be able to contradict, and if it comes out in a board room I will not be able to say I did not know.”

“Yes.”

“Are you asking me to do it.”

“I am giving you the choice,” Rooke said. “Which is a thing that nobody in this parish has given you since the fifth of March, and which is the only thing I have that is worth anything.”

✦

I took the letter.

It was one page. The paper was good and it had been folded in three and the folds were soft. The hand was small and upright and it had been written by a man who was in a hurry and did not like being in one.

To the Secretary of the Commissioners.

I am directed to return the file of the office at Nether Collow, in the county of, called the Letter of the Dead, together with the schedule of suppressed offices and the inventory of the same.

The office was suppressed by the commission on the fourth of November instant, and the land and the endowment are vested in the commissioners, and the commissioners have directed that the endowment be applied to the relief of the poor of the several parishes named in the schedule.

I am further directed to report that the file as returned consists of four pages, and that the second and third pages are wanting, and that the first page has been endorsed by the commissioner and the fourth page has been endorsed by the clerk to the commission, and that no person now living can supply what was on the second and third pages, as the persons who wrote them are dead.

I have the honour to be, sir, your obedient servant,

A. Quill.

✦

I sat down on the parapet of the bridge, which is a thing I should not have done, and Rooke sat down beside me, which is a thing he should not have done.

“Three pages,” I said.

“Two pages wanting and the first and fourth endorsed. Which is four pages, of which two are gone.”

“You said three.”

“I said three because there are three pages I cannot read. The second and the third are gone and the first is an endorsement and the fourth is an endorsement, and an endorsement is not a page, it is a signature on the back of a page.” He looked at the water. “I was not being exact. I am being exact now.”

“Who is A. Quill.”

“The man who is the deputy registrar at Carrow now,” Rooke said. “He was twenty-six in 1806 and he was the clerk to that commission, and he wrote that letter, and he is the only man alive who was in the room.”

“And he is the man you have been avoiding for eleven years.”

“Yes.”

“Why.”

Rooke was quiet for a while. The branch came round in the pool and went under and came up again.

“Because he taught me the trade,” he said. “Because when I was nineteen I came into that office as a boy to copy schedules, and my father had the chair then, and Quill was the only man in the building who would speak to a boy who could not read the hand he was given. He taught me how to fold a return and how to carry a file and how to write a note in a margin that says a thing without saying it.

“And because in 1820 I found a discrepancy in a return from a parish in the north of this county, and I took it to him, and he looked at it for a while and then he said, the office is not a place for a man who wants to be right. It is a place for a man who wants things to be in order, and he put the return in the fire.

“I have thought about that sentence every day for eleven years.”

✦

“Then why go now,” I said.

“Because I have a girl in front of me who has altered a parish book for a child with a pull in her ribs, and a clerk with a bad throat who signed a paper in eleven minutes and has been paying for it for thirty-two years, and a woman who has walked twelve miles a week for twenty years to sit with her sister, and none of them are in order, and all of them are right.”

He stood up.

“And because I am forty-four years old and I have signed four thousand papers and every one of them was correct, and I have never once signed a thing that was true and incorrect at the same time, and I would like to know what happens.”

✦

I stood up too.

“There is a thing I have to say,” I said, “and I am going to say it here, because on the other side of that stone I will not be able to.”

“Say it.”

“If I go up that lane I will read a book that my aunt made, and the book will have things in it that are not in any book at Carrow, and one of them is a name. And when I have read it I will not be able to be a person who has not read it.

“And I am going to go up that lane. Not tonight. But I am going to go, and when I go, I am going to need a man at Carrow who can take a page out of a file and put it in front of a board and say what it is.

“So I am asking you now, before I go, whether you are going to be that man, or whether you are going to be the man who puts the return in the fire.”

Rooke looked at me for a long time.

“I do not know,” he said.

“That is an honest answer.”

“It is the only honest answer I have.” He put his hat on. “Ask me again on the day.”

✦

We walked back together as far as the last gate and then he went on to the church and I went home, and neither of us said anything for the last half mile, and the lambs had stopped running and were standing in a group in the middle of the field looking at nothing.

At the gate my mother was waiting with the lamp.

“Not tonight,” she said.

“Not tonight.”

She looked at my face for a while.

“You stood on the bridge,” she said.

“I stood on the bridge.”

“And you turned round.”

“I turned round.”

She nodded, once, the way a woman nods when a thing has gone the way she thought and she is not glad about it.

“Good,” she said. “Then you are not a fool. Now come in and eat something, because tomorrow you are going to Carrow and you will need to have eaten.”

✦

I went up and sat on the edge of the bed with my boots off and the thimble in my hand.

There was a noise in the passage. My father, going down, on the fourth step and the seventh.

He had not gone to bed. He had been in the chair with his eyes shut and he had not been asleep, and he had heard me come in, and he had heard what my mother said about Carrow, and he had said nothing, because that is what he does.

I put the thimble on my finger. It was too big, and it went on to the second joint and stopped there.

Then I took it off and put it under the pillow and lay down with my eyes open, and the last thing I thought before I slept was that a man who could not read the bottom of a paper in 1799 had heard his daughter say she was going to Carrow, and had gone to bed without asking her what for, and that this was either the greatest kindness of his life or the last piece of cowardice, and that I would not know which until it was too late to ask.

12

The Fourth Page

I had been to Carrow eleven times in my life and every one of them had been a market.

That is the thing about a town you do not live in. You know it by one street. You know the market square and the butter table and the man who sells parsnips and the step of the assembly rooms where you sit and wait for the cart, and you go home and you say you have been to Carrow, and what you have been to is a square.

I went on the nineteenth of March, which was a Saturday, and I went in the carrier’s cart as far as the turnpike and then I walked, because a woman who arrives in a cart arrives as a person who has been in a cart, and a woman who walks arrives as a person.

✦

The first thing is the sound.

Carrow has a sound that a village does not have, and it is not the people. It is the ground. There is iron under the streets in Carrow. It is not laid in the road, it is laid in the pavement at the edge, in strips, and the cart wheels go over it and the boots go over it, and the whole town rings, low, all the time, like a bell that has been struck and has not stopped.

The second thing is the light.

There are lamp posts in Carrow. They are iron and they are eleven feet high and they have a glass box on the top with four panes in it, and a man comes round at dusk with a pole and a hook and opens the box and lights the gas, and there are a hundred and forty of them, and there is not one in my parish and there never will be.

I stood under the first one for a while on the Saturday morning, which was stupid, because it was not lit.

✦

The registry office is at the top of the square and it is the largest building I have ever been inside.

It has four floors and a dome, and the dome is glass and lead and it has a thing on the top of it that is either a bird or a flame and nobody I have asked knows which. The steps are stone and there are eleven of them, and the eleventh has a hollow in the middle of it that has been worn by feet since 1764.

The door is oak and it is nine feet high and the handle is brass and the brass has been worn to gold in the place where hands go, and there is a man in a brown coat sitting on a stool inside the door whose entire employment is to ask people what they want.

They call him the porter. He is sixty and he has a wooden leg and he has been at that door since 1809.

“Miss.”

“Good morning. I want the archive.”

“The archive is not open to the public.”

“I am not the public. I am the clerk’s daughter from the parish of St. Cuthbert’s, and I have come about a file that was returned to the diocesan office in 1806 with pages wanting, and I have a letter here that was written by the clerk to the commission that suppressed the office, and I would like to see the endorsement on the fourth page.”

The porter looked at me for a while.

“You are the clerk’s daughter,” he said.

“I am.”

“And you have walked.”

“I have walked from the turnpike.”

“Then sit down on that bench,” the porter said, “and I will send for Mr. Bellamy, and I will tell you now that he will not see you, and that you will sit on that bench for an hour and a half and then he will see you, and that is the way it is done here, and there is no use in being clever about it.”

✦

I sat on the bench for an hour and a half.

I want to put down what I saw in that hour and a half, because it is the hour in which I stopped being a person from a parish and became a person from a county.

There were clerks. There were eleven of them that I counted, and they came down the stair and went up the stair and none of them looked at me, and all of them had white cuffs on over their coat sleeves, and the cuffs were not the same white. Some were bleached and some were only washed.

There were two men in good coats arguing about a valuation in a voice that carried, and one of them said, it is not the figure that is wrong, it is the principle, and the other one said, the principle is the figure, and they went out into the square still arguing.

There was a woman with a basket and a summons, and she was crying, and she was not crying in the way that a woman cries in a lane. She was crying the way a person cries in a public building, which is with her face completely still and the water coming out of it as though it were a separate arrangement.

And at eleven o’clock a bell rang somewhere above us and forty people stopped what they were doing and went to a room, and the door shut, and for four minutes the whole building was quiet except for the ringing of the boots on the iron in the street outside.

That is a government. Not the board, not the commissioners. Forty people going into a room when a bell rings.

✦

Mr. Bellamy was sixty-three and he had the archive in four rooms on the second floor and he had been in charge of it since 1809 and he did not stand up when I came in.

“You are Ashby’s girl,” he said.

“I am.”

“Your father I know. He writes a fair hand and he has never once sent me a return I had to send back.” He put his pen down. “That is the highest thing I can say about a man in this trade and I do not say it often. Now. What is this about a file.”

I put the letter on the desk.

He read it the way a man reads a thing he wrote himself thirty years ago, which is quickly, and then again slowly.

“Quill,” he said.

“Yes.”

“You have been to see Quill.”

“I have not. I have the letter from the diocesan office at Ely and I have come to you because the fourth page of the file is here and the first page is here and the second and third are wanting, and a man who wants to know what was on a missing page should look at the back of the page before it.”

Bellamy sat back.

“That is a very good sentence,” he said. “Did you think of it yourself?”

“I have been thinking about it for a week.”

“Then you are wasted in a parish.” He stood up. “Come with me. And do not touch anything, and do not read anything with your hand, because I have heard about you from a man at the Corn Exchange and I do not want my archive read by a girl with a thimble.”

✦

The archive is four rooms and the fourth room is the one they keep the suppressed offices in, and it is not a large room, and it has one window with a grille on it and no fire and the damp comes up through the floor.

There are two hundred and eleven boxes in that room. Two hundred and eleven offices that were suppressed between 1790 and 1830, and every one of them has a box, and every box has a file, and every file has a number.

Bellamy took down a box from the third shelf and put it on the table and opened it.

“Here,” he said. “The file of the office at Nether Collow. Four pages, of which two are wanting. The first page is the petition. The fourth page is the endorsement.”

I looked at it.

The first page is a petition and it has fourteen signatures on it, and I knew one of them before I had read down the list, because I have been reading that hand on the fair copy since I was fourteen.

Josiah Ashby, clerk.

“Your father signed the petition,” Bellamy said.

“My father signed the petition.”

“He was one of fourteen men of that parish who petitioned the commissioners for the suppression of the office, and the petition was granted, and the land and the endowment went to the commissioners, and the commissioners applied it to the relief of the poor of the parishes named in the schedule.”

I sat down on the stool by the table.

“He told me he signed a paper in ninety-nine,” I said. “He did not tell me he signed this.”

“Ninety-nine is the other thing,” Bellamy said. “Ninety-nine is the endowment of a new office. This is eighteen hundred and six and it is the end of an old one. They are the same business at the two ends of it, and a man can be at one end and not the other, and your father was at both.”

✦

The fourth page is the endorsement.

It is a single sheet, folded, with three lines on it, and it is the back of the page that would have been the fourth page of the report if the report had been whole.

The three lines are in a hand that is old and very good, and they say:

Report of the Commissioners for the Suppression of Superfluous Offices in the County of, 1806.

Office: the Letter of the Dead, at Nether Collow.

Received and entered. A. Quill, clerk to the commission.

“There is nothing else,” I said.

“There is nothing else on the fourth page.”

“Then the file is nothing. The file is a petition and an endorsement and two missing pages, and a man cannot be hanged on a petition.”

“No,” Bellamy said, “but a man can be ruined on one. And a county can be.” He turned the sheet over. “Look at the back.”

I turned it over.

On the back of the fourth page, in the same old good hand, there is one line more, and it is not an endorsement, and it is not signed.

The inventory of the office was returned separately and is in the box of the office at Ely.

✦

“The box at Ely,” I said.

“The box at Ely is the diocesan archive and it is not the same as this one and it has never been in this building.” Bellamy sat down opposite me. “There is a thing you should know before you go to Ely, and I am going to tell you because you are Ashby’s girl and because I have been in this room for twenty-two years and I have never once said it out loud.”

“Say it.”

“Your father’s petition was not the beginning.” He put his hand on the box. “There was an office in this county called the Letter of the Dead, and it was held by a woman at Nether Collow, and it was a real office, with a real endowment, and it did a real thing. And in 1806 a commission suppressed it, and the commission had a clerk, and the clerk was twenty-six years old and his name was Ambrose Quill.

“And there was a man in this building who copied the schedule of suppressed offices for that commission. He was nineteen. He could not read the schedule he was made to copy, because the schedule was in a hand he had never seen and in a form he did not know, and he copied it anyway, because that is what a clerk does.

“He copied it for four days and on the fourth day he came to this room and he asked me a question, and the question was: sir, what is a letter of the dead?

“And I told him it was a formality.

“He has had the office for twenty-five years. He sits in that chair. He has signed four thousand papers. And he has been in this building since he was nineteen years old.”

The room was very quiet. There was water running somewhere in the wall.

“Rooke,” I said.

“Rooke,” Bellamy said. “The auditor of your parish is the boy who copied the schedule for that commission, and he has been in this building since he was nineteen years old, and he has never once asked me what a letter of the dead is.

“I have known for six years what was in that file. I have thought about telling him perhaps four hundred times. I have not, and I am not going to, and I will tell you why, and then we will talk about what you are going to do.”

“Why.”

“Because a man who finds out that he copied the paper that abolished an office will do one of two things. He will put it in the fire and go on being an auditor, or he will take it to the board and lose everything.” Bellamy closed the box. “I have watched him for twenty-five years. He is not the first kind. So if he finds out, he will lose everything, and this county will lose the only man in it who reads a page to the bottom.

“And you are going to make him find out.”

“I am.”

“Then you will do it in front of the board and not in a room,” Bellamy said, “because a man who loses everything in a room loses it alone, and a man who loses it in front of a board loses it in front of people who will have to decide what to do about it, and that is the only way anything in this county has ever been changed.”

✦

He wrote a note and sanded it and folded it and gave it to me.

“That is for the porter,” he said. “You will want the box at Ely and you will not get it without a note from this office, and Quill will not give you the time of day, and the porter at Ely is a man who respects a seal.”

I put the note in my pocket.

“Mr. Bellamy.”

“Mm.”

“Why are you doing this.”

He looked at me for a while.

“Because I am sixty-three,” he said, “and I have been in this room for twenty-two years, and the whole of my work has been to keep other people’s paper in order, and there is not one thing in these two hundred and eleven boxes that has ever done anybody any good.

“And because in 1806 I told a boy of nineteen that a letter of the dead was a formality, and it was not a formality, and he has been carrying it for twenty-five years without knowing what he was carrying.

“That is my account. It is not a good one and I am not asking you to think it is.”

✦

I went down the eleven steps and out into the square.

It was one o’clock and the market was finishing and the men were taking down the boards and there was a smell of wet straw and oranges and the gas was not lit and would not be lit for five hours.

I stood at the bottom of the steps with the note in my pocket and looked up at the dome.

Then I went and found the carrier’s yard and asked when the cart went back, and the man said four, and I said I would be there, and he said, you will be lucky, and I said, I know.

✦

And then, because I had four hours and because I had been in a parish all my life and had never once been in a street that sold nothing but things a person did not need, I went into the Quarter.

The Quarter is four streets behind the assembly rooms and it is not a market and it is not a shop. It is a place where people make things and sell them to each other.

There is a man in the first street who makes eyes for dolls, and he makes them in eleven colours, and he has a card in his window with the colours on it, and the card has been in that window since before I was born and has faded to a kind of pink.

There is a woman in the second street who sells maps, and every map in her window is of a place that is not Carrow, and there is one of the county that has a hole in it where a parish should be, and when I asked her about the hole she said, that is where the surveyor would not go.

And in the third street there is a shop with a green door and no sign, and the window is full of bottles, and the bottles have labels, and the labels say things like for a bad memory, and for a good one, and for the second week in March.

I stood in front of that window for a long time.

A woman came out of the shop with a parcel and looked at me and said, “You are not from here.”

“No.”

“Then do not buy the third one,” she said. “It works and you will not like what it works on.” And she went off up the street.

✦

I got the cart at four and I was home by seven and my mother was at the window and my father was in the chair and neither of them asked me a single question, which is how I knew that they had talked about it while I was gone and had decided between them what they would do.

I went up and put the note in the drawer with the thimble and sat on the edge of the bed.

Three pages. One petition with my father’s name on it. One endorsement with the name of a man who was twenty-six in 1806 and is fifty-one now. One line on the back that sends me to Ely.

And a man in a room with two hundred and eleven boxes who has known for six years and has said nothing, and who told me to break it in front of a board and not in a room.

I lay down.

Downstairs the clock struck seven and then struck seven again four minutes later, because it gains four minutes a day, and nobody has ever thought it worth stopping.

13

The Note

Ely is nineteen miles and it takes a day.

I went on the Monday, on the carrier’s cart as far as the crossroads and then on the mail coach, which cost four shillings and which I had never been on before, and I sat on the outside because the inside was full of a farmer and his wife and a dog, and the outside was cold but it was the top of the world.

The road to Ely is straight for eleven miles, and a straight road does not look long, and then you are four hours into it and the cathedral is exactly the same size it was when you started.

The cathedral is the size of a hill. That is the only useful thing I can say about it.

✦

The diocesan office is in the precinct and it is a low building with a slate roof and a door with a brass plate, and the brass plate says: Registry of the Consistory Court. Not open before ten.

I was there at nine.

I walked round the precinct three times.

There is a green in front of the cathedral and there were men on it with barrows, and a boy selling something out of a basket, and a woman with a baby on her hip who looked at me the way women with babies look at women without them, which is with a kind of pity that has no patronising in it at all.

At a quarter to ten I stood at the door.

At ten the door was opened by a man of about sixty with a cast in one eye, and I gave him Bellamy’s note, and he read it and looked at me and read it again.

“Wait,” he said, and went in.

I waited forty minutes.

✦

The room I was taken to was not the archive.

It was on the first floor at the front, and it had two windows onto the green, and it had a fire and a carpet and a desk and a chair on each side of the desk. There was a shelf behind the desk with twenty or thirty small objects on it, and the objects were all metal, and they were all birds.

There were eleven birds on that shelf. Some of them were silver and some were brass and one was enamelled in blue. They were all standing, and none of them was in a cage, and there was not one of them that was not beautifully made.

I was looking at them when the door opened.

“Miss Ashby.”

He was five feet nine and he was fifty-one and he was the best-dressed man I had ever been in a room with. His coat was dark green and his linen was white and his hands were the hands of a man who has never lifted anything.

“Mr. Quill.”

“Sit down.” He went round the desk. “You have come from St. Cuthbert’s. That is nineteen miles. You will want tea.”

“No, thank you.”

“You will want tea,” he said again, and rang a bell on the desk, and a boy came, and he ordered tea, and the boy went, and the whole of that took about eleven seconds and I had not said a word.

✦

He sat down and put his hands on the desk and looked at me.

“You are Mary Ann’s girl,” he said.

I did not answer.

“You have her chin,” he said. “I knew her in 1799. She came into this office with her sister and she stood exactly where you are sitting and she did not sit down when she was asked, and she had her boots in her hand.”

“She had her boots in her hand in 1799?”

“Her sister’s boots. She had walked in them and they were too big and she had taken them off at the door because they were covered in the road, and she stood in this office in her stocking feet and asked me a question.” He straightened a pen on the desk. “The question was: if a thing is not written down, is it still there?

“I have thought about that question for thirty-two years. It is the best question anybody has ever asked me in this room.”

✦

The tea came on a tray with two cups and a plate of seed cake.

I did not touch it.

“You have come about the box,” Quill said.

“I have come about the box.”

“The box is the inventory of the office at Nether Collow. It is one sheet. It lists a table, four chairs, a bed, a chest, a looking glass, a clock, and a book. It is the most disappointing document in this building and I have known that for thirty years.”

“A book.”

“A book.” He poured the tea. “You are thinking it is the book your aunt made. It is not. The inventory is dated the fourth of November, 1806, and it lists the contents of the house at the moment of suppression, and there was one book in that house and it was a Bible, and it went with the rest of the goods to the commissioners.”

“Then where is it.”

“The Bible?”

“The book.”

“I do not know,” Quill said. “And I want you to notice that I have answered you honestly, because in a minute I am going to say a thing that will make you angry, and I would like it on the record that I answered the small question first.”

✦

He drank his tea.

“Miss Ashby,” he said. “Your mother has ten months.”

The room did not change. The fire did not go down. The birds stood on the shelf.

“How do you know that.”

“Because I am the deputy registrar and the register of this county comes through this office, and because in November a return came in from your parish and it had a note in the margin in your father’s hand, and the note said infirm, no expectation of recovery, and it was in the column for the eleventh class.” He put the cup down. “I read every return that comes through this office. That is what I am for. I am not a spy and I do not apologise for reading the paper that is sent to me.”

“And you remembered it.”

“I remembered it because I have been waiting for it for thirty-two years,” Quill said. “I have known since 1799 that there was a woman in that parish sitting in an office that the Crown had abolished, and I have known that the moment she died the thing would come to the surface, because a thing that has been held in one hand for thirty-two years is a thing that falls when the hand opens.

“Your mother is the hand. When she dies, the office dies with her, and the endowment is free, and the file comes back to this building, and it will be entered and closed and nobody will ever read it again.

“That is the good outcome. I want you to understand that. That is the outcome where nobody goes to prison.”

✦

I waited.

“And the bad outcome,” I said.

“The bad outcome is that somebody finds the second and third pages of that report and takes them to a board.”

“Somebody has.”

“Yes,” Quill said. “You have. Or you are about to, which is the same thing to me.”

He stood up and went to the window and stood with his hands behind his back, and the light came through the glass and lay on the carpet in two long shapes.

“I am going to tell you what those pages said,” he said. “I am going to tell you because you will find them, and because when you find them you will think you have found a crime, and I would rather you heard the whole of it from me than from a piece of paper written by a man who is dead.

“The office at Nether Collow was called the Letter of the Dead. It was created in 1711 by a private Act. It was held by a woman, and the woman was called the letter, and the duty was this: when a person died in that parish or in any of the four parishes named in the Act, and there was a question that only the dead could answer, the letter was to go to the body and put her hand on it and answer the question.

“There were four such offices in England. Three were suppressed before 1750. The fourth was suppressed in 1806 by the Commission for the Suppression of Superfluous Offices, which was a commission set up by the Crown to abolish posts that did no work and took money for it.

“That was not what the office at Collow was. The office at Collow did work. But the commission had a schedule, and the schedule had four hundred and eleven offices on it, and the office at Collow was on it because a clerk in London read letter of the dead and did not know what it meant, and put it in the column for superfluous because he had nowhere else to put it.

“And I was the clerk to that commission, and I did not correct him.”

✦

He turned round.

“That is the crime,” he said. “Not the suppression. The suppression was lawful. The crime is that I knew what the office was and I let it go on the schedule, because to take it off the schedule I would have had to explain what it was, and to explain what it was I would have had to write the word body in a report to the Treasury, and in 1806 there was no man in that building who would have signed a report with that word in it.

“So I let it go. And the office was suppressed. And the woman who held it lost her house and her land and her living, and the land went to fourteen men of that parish who had petitioned for the suppression, and one of the fourteen was your father.

“And I have been in this building for thirty-two years, and I have read every return that came through it, and I have known every quarter when the endowment was paid and every quarter when it was not applied, and I have done nothing at all.”

✦

“Why are you telling me this,” I said.

“Because you are going to find the pages,” Quill said, “and because I have decided what I am going to do about it, and I would like you to know before you do whatever you are going to do.”

“Which is what.”

“I am going to close the file,” he said. “On the day your mother dies, the office lapses, the endowment returns to the Crown, the land is confirmed in the fourteen men who hold it, and the file is closed. And I am going to write, in my own hand, on the fourth page, a note that says what the office was.

“One line. That is all. An office for the answering of questions by the dead, suppressed in error, and not restored.

“And that line will sit in a box in this building for a hundred years and nobody will read it, and in a hundred years a man will open the box and read it and will not know what to do with it, and that is the entirety of what I can do, and I have thought about it for eleven years.”

✦

I stood up.

“That is not enough,” I said.

“No.”

“You took her house.”

“I did.”

“You took her land and gave it to fourteen men and one of them was my father, and my father has been paying it back a loaf at a time for thirty-two years, and my mother has been walking twelve miles a week to sit with a woman in a bed, and you are going to write one line in a box.”

Quill looked at me, and his face did not change at all, and that was the worst thing about him.

“Yes,” he said. “And if I write two lines, the file is reopened, and a reopened file goes to the Treasury, and a Treasury file goes to the newspapers, and a newspaper in 1831 is a thing that will print the words letter of the dead next to the words the Bishop of Ely, and this office will be investigated by men who do not know what a parish is, and the four parishes named in the Act will lose their endowments entirely, and the poor of those parishes will lose the bread.

“That is the arithmetic. I have done it every year since 1806. It does not come out any other way.”

✦

I went to the door.

“Miss Ashby.”

I stopped.

“You have your mother’s chin and your father’s hand,” Quill said. “Your father is a good clerk. He has never sent me a return I had to send back. That is not nothing in a county where half the returns are wrong.

“And I am going to give you a piece of advice, and it is not a threat, and I want you to hear the difference.

“Whatever you find, do not take it to a board in a parish. A parish board is fourteen men who have been eating the endowment for thirty-two years, and they will not save you, they will save themselves, and they will do it by saying that a girl with a thimble read a page with her hand.

“Take it to this building. Take it to me. And I will do the one thing I have left, which is to write a line that is true.”

✦

I went down the stairs and out into the green.

It had begun to rain. Not hard. The sort of rain that does not fall so much as hang.

Rooke was standing under the arch of the precinct gate with his hat off and his coat over his arm, and he had been there long enough that his hair was wet.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“I have been to the box,” he said. “The box at Ely. It is one sheet of paper and it lists a table and four chairs.”

“I know.”

“And there is a book in it and it is a Bible.”

“I know.”

He looked at me.

“You have been in to see him,” he said.

“I have.”

“And he told you.”

“He told me the whole of it,” I said, “and he told me that he is going to close the file on the day my mother dies and write one line in a box, and he told me that if I take it to a board, the fourteen men will say that a girl with a thimble read a page with her hand.

“And he told me that the man who copied the schedule in 1806 was nineteen and could not read it, and he did not say the name, and I did not ask him, and I am not going to tell you what I know until you ask me.”

Rooke stood in the rain with his coat over his arm.

“Ask me,” he said.

“Not here.”

“Then where.”

“At the board,” I said. “In front of fourteen men. On Lady Day.”

✦

He put his hat on.

“You are asking me to lose my office,” he said.

“I am asking you to read a page to the bottom.”

The rain came down.

“That is the second time you have said that to me,” Rooke said. “The first time was in a market and you were talking about a basket of eggs. I have thought about it every day since.”

“And.”

“And I am forty-four years old and I have a chair that was my father’s and a room that has my name on the door and a pension at the end of it, and I have never once in my life done a thing that was not in order.

He put his coat on.

“Ask me on Lady Day,” he said, “and I will tell you the truth on Lady Day, and I will not know what it is until you ask me, and that is the only honest thing I can give you.”

The rain had not stopped and his coat was over my shoulders before I had decided to be cold. It was warm from him and it smelled of ink and wet wool, and I walked down to the turnpike wearing an auditor’s coat in full view of Ely, and I did not think once about what anybody would say. I thought about it eleven times. But not once did I take it off.

14

Lady Day at the Barn

Lady Day is the twenty-fifth of March and it is the first of the four quarter days and it is the day the rents are paid.

Mr. Barrow’s rents are paid in the long barn, which is ninety feet and has a threshing floor at one end and a stone floor at the other and a roof that was rebuilt in 1812. There is a table at the stone end, and behind the table there is a chair, and the chair has arms and the chair is the only chair in the barn.

For nineteen years the clerk has sat at a small table at the side, with the parish book and the copies and the ink.

For nineteen years that had been my father.

✦

I went at nine in the morning because I had been sent for, and I was sent for by a note that said: The clerk being indisposed, his daughter will attend and keep the book. Barrow.

Will attend.

Not may. Not is asked to. A man who writes will attend is a man who has decided that a thing is going to happen and does not want to be argued with, and I have noticed that men who write like that are usually in a hurry.

✦

The barn at nine in the morning was cold and smelled of last year’s straw and of the tar that has been put on the beams to keep the woodworm out.

Riddick the steward was there with the rent roll and the box. He is fifty and he has one eye that does not move and one that does not stop, and he has been steward for eleven years and he has never once said a word to me that was not about paper.

He put the box on the small table and opened it.

“Fair copy,” he said. “Foul copy. Blotter. Two pens. Ink. Sand.”

“I know what is in a box.”

“Then you will not need me to tell you.” He shut it. “He will be here at ten. The tenants come at ten and they go at four and there are forty-one of them. You will enter the payments in the roll as they are made and you will not speak.”

“I will enter the payments.”

“And you will not speak.”

“I heard you the first time.”

✦

Barrow came at ten and stood at the table and the whole barn stood up, which is a thing I had never seen in a barn before and have seen many times since, and it is not respect, it is the thing that happens when a man owns four hundred acres and everybody in the room works on them.

He is sixty-two. He has a face like a boot. He has been the largest ratepayer in this parish for thirty years and he has been the chairman of the vestry for nineteen and he has a way of standing with his hands behind his back and his chin up that makes a room feel that it has been arranged wrong and is about to be put right.

“Sit,” he said.

Forty-one people sat down on benches.

✦

The rents took six hours.

A tenant comes to the table with his hat in his hand. Riddick reads out the holding: the name of the field, the acreage, the rent. The tenant puts the money on the table. Riddick counts it and calls the figure and I write it in the roll. Then the tenant stands there for a moment, because there is one more thing, and the one more thing is different for every man.

One asks for a gate. One asks for a drain. One says his roof has gone at the north end and he has put a tarpaulin on it and would the steward come and look. One says nothing at all and stands there until Barrow says, that will do, and then he goes, and the next one comes.

Barrow said yes to the gate. He said no to the drain. He said he would send a man to look at the roof and everybody in the barn knew he would not.

At three o’clock there were eleven tenants left and Barrow stood up and stretched his back, and Riddick poured him a glass of something from a bottle under the table.

“Miss Ashby,” Barrow said. “You have a fair hand.”

“I have my father’s hand.”

“You have a fairer hand than your father,” he said, and drank. “That is not a compliment to him. It is a fact about a pen.”

✦

The last eleven tenants were the small ones, and they went quickly, and at twenty past three the barn was empty except for Barrow and Riddick and me and a boy at the door.

Barrow sat down.

“Riddick,” he said. “The book.”

Riddick brought the parish book, which was not mine and had never been mine, and put it on the big table, and opened it at the letter.

“The accounts of the parish,” Barrow said. “I have been chairman nineteen years and I have signed the accounts every year and I have never once read them, and that is not a thing I am proud of, and it is a thing I am going to do something about this afternoon, because there is a man at Carrow who has been writing to the board for nine years and the board has been putting his letters in a drawer.

“So. Read me the eleventh class.”

I stood up.

“The eleventh class,” I said, “is the class for warmth. It has fourteen names. The first is Ashby, Mary Ann.”

“And the money.”

“The money is eleven pounds four a year and a coal allowance of six hundredweight a quarter.”

“And where does the money come from.”

“From the endowment of the office.”

“Which office.”

“An office created in 1794 and endowed in 1799,” I said. “There is no name for it in the book. The column says only office, and the class says only eleventh, and my father has written the same three words in the same place every quarter for thirty-two years.”

Barrow looked up.

“Say that again.”

“There is no name for the office.”

The barn was quiet. The boy at the door had stopped moving.

“Then what is it,” Barrow said.

“It is a line,” I said. “It is a line ruled under a name in a column in a book, and it has been paid for thirty-two years out of an endowment of one hundred and four pounds a year, and eleven pounds four of it has come to my mother, and six hundredweight of coal a quarter has come to my mother, and the rest of it has come into this parish and has been spent on roads and bridges and coal and school slates and there are receipts for two thousand three hundred and nine pounds of it.

“And six hundred and fifty-nine pounds of it is not in any book at all.”

✦

Barrow put his glass down.

“Riddick,” he said. “Shut the door.”

Riddick shut the door.

“Now,” Barrow said, “you will tell me where you got those figures, and you will tell me as though I were a bench of magistrates, because that is what I am going to have to be before this is over.”

“The figures are the parish accounts,” I said. “The bridge book, the school book, the overseers’ book, the coal yard book. They are in this parish and anybody may read them.

“The endowment is the returns at Carrow. There are a hundred and twenty-eight of them and they are signed by my father every quarter and I have read all of them.

“And the man who added them up is the auditor of this parish, and he is Carrow’s, and he has been at it for a fortnight, and he will swear to every figure in front of any board in this county.”

Barrow sat back.

“The auditor,” he said. “Rooke.”

“Rooke.”

“Rooke is a man I have known since he was a boy in this parish.” Barrow turned his glass round on the table. “His father was a clerk at Carrow and his grandfather was a clerk at Carrow before that, and the family has been in that building for a hundred years, and I have never once known one of them to say a thing that was not in a paper.”

“Then you will not mind hearing it from him.”

“I will mind it very much,” Barrow said. “But I will hear it.”

✦

He stood up and walked to the end of the barn and came back, which is what a man does when he has something to say and is deciding how to say it.

“Miss Ashby,” he said. “In 1794 I paid for a school.”

“I know. There is a stone over the door with your initials on it.”

“I paid for it myself and I would not let the parish pay a penny and I have never mentioned it in the vestry and I do not want it mentioned now.” He stopped at the table. “In 1799 a man came to this house with a slip of paper and asked me to witness it, and I witnessed it. The man was from Carrow and he had a leather case and he had a manner that made you want to agree with him, and the slip had a space at the bottom for a witness and I signed my name in it.

“In 1806 there was a commission at Ely and a petition from this parish, and I signed the petition, and I was one of fourteen, and the office at Collow was suppressed and the land came to the fourteen.

“And from 1806 to 1817 I took a hundred and four pounds a year as a rent charge on that land.”

✦

The fire in the brazier had gone out.

“Eleven years,” Barrow said. “A hundred and four a year, and I took it, and it came in with the rents and it went into the estate account and I did not ask what it was, because I knew what it was, and a man who knows what a thing is and does not ask is a man who has decided.

“And in 1817 I found out what the office was. Not from a paper. From a woman. She came to this house and stood where you are standing and told me what her sister had been, and she told me what the office did, and she asked me for the rent charge.”

He looked at me.

“I gave her nothing,” he said. “I told her the rent charge was on the land and the land was mine and the office was abolished and there was no arrangement of words that would make it hers. And she stood there and looked at me and then she went out, and she has walked past this gate every Tuesday for fourteen years since, and she has never once looked at the house.”

“Her name is Ellen,” I said.

“Her name is Ellen Rooke,” Barrow said, “and she is your mother’s sister, and she is the last woman of that line, and I have known that for fourteen years.

“Now. You have come here with figures and a man from Carrow and a fair hand, and you have not yet said the thing you came to say. So say it, and let us have it in the open, because I have been carrying this for fourteen years and I am sixty-two and I would rather be ruined this afternoon than carry it another winter.”

✦

I put both hands on the table.

“Mr. Barrow,” I said, “in 1799 you witnessed a slip that put my mother into an office that had been abolished seven years before she was put into it.

“In 1806 you signed a petition that abolished the office that office was copied from, and you took the land, and you took a hundred and four pounds a year for eleven years.

“In 1817 you were told by the woman whose office it was that the money was not yours, and you kept it fourteen years more, and you have kept the land for twenty-five.

“And in 1831 you are the chairman of a vestry that is going to meet at Lady Day and rule on whether my mother’s name is to stay in a book.

“Which of those four do you want to explain to the board first?”

✦

Barrow looked at me for a long time.

Then he did a thing I did not expect.

He laughed.

It was not a kind laugh. It was the laugh of a man who has been carrying something for thirty-two years and has just been told that somebody else will take an end of it.

“Riddick,” he said.

“Sir.”

“Bring the tenants back in.”

Riddick’s one eye moved.

“Sir, they have gone home.”

“Then go and get them,” Barrow said. “Go to the lane and the yard and the four cottages and tell them the rent day is not finished and there is a thing they should hear, and if they will not come for me, tell them there is a woman in the barn with a book and she is going to read it out loud.”

He sat down in the chair with the arms.

“And bring a chair for the clerk’s daughter,” he said, “because she is going to be here until four.”

✦

They came back.

Forty-one tenants and eleven women and a boy who had been sent to the wrong end of the lane and came in last, out of breath, and stood at the back.

Barrow stood up.

“I am going to say a thing,” he said, “and then I am going to say it again in the vestry on the twenty-ninth, and then I am going to say it to a board at Carrow, and I would rather you heard it here first from me than in a lane from somebody who has got it wrong.

“In 1806 this parish petitioned for the suppression of an office at Nether Collow. I signed the petition. The office was suppressed and the land came to fourteen of us and there was a rent charge of a hundred and four pounds a year on it, and I took that money for eleven years.

“In 1799 a paper was signed in this parish putting a woman into an office that had no warrant. I witnessed that paper. Her name is Mary Ann Ashby and she is in the eleventh class and she has been in it for thirty-two years, and the endowment that has paid for this parish’s roads and this parish’s school and this parish’s coal for thirty-two years is the endowment of the office we suppressed.

“So there is not one fraud in this parish. There are two, and they are the same fraud, and it has been going on since before most of you were born, and I have been at the middle of it, and I have taken money out of it, and I am not going to stand here and say I did not know.

“Now.” He turned. “Miss Ashby. Read them the eleventh class.”

✦

I read them the eleventh class.

Fourteen names. My mother’s last. And when I had read it I read the column at the bottom of the page, which says only carried forward, and then I read the figure, and the figure was six hundred and fifty-nine pounds.

“There is money in this parish that nobody has touched for thirty-two years,” I said. “It is in a column with no name on it and it is not in anybody’s pocket and it is not in the estate account and it is not in the road book. It is in the book and it is not anywhere else.

“Somebody has to decide what to do with it. I have been in this parish eighteen years and I have never once been asked to decide anything, and I am not asking to decide it now, because a person who decides where money goes is a person who has to answer for it, and I have watched what happens to people who answer for things in this parish.

“But I will tell you what I would do. I would take the six hundred and fifty-nine pounds and I would put it in a book with the name of the office at the top of the page, and I would pay the office out of it, and I would pay Ellen Rooke what her sister was owed for twenty-five years, and then I would take the rest and I would put a stone over the door of the school with the date on it and I would write on the stone the word restitution, because that is what it is, and a parish that has been eating out of a column for thirty-two years should have to look at the word every time it goes to school.”

Nobody said anything.

Then a woman at the back said, “How much is twenty-five years?”

“Two thousand six hundred pounds,” I said, “at a hundred and four, and there is not that much, so she would have to have the land instead.”

The woman thought about it.

“That seems fair,” she said.

✦

Barrow sat down.

“Riddick,” he said.

“Sir.”

“Take this down.”

Riddick took out a pencil.

“At the rent day of Lady Day, 1831, at the long barn, the chairman of the vestry did declare...” Barrow stopped. “No. Not that. Take this instead.

“The vestry is asked to consider the state of the eleventh class, and the endowment of the office at Nether Collow, and the claim of Ellen Rooke of that parish, and the sum of six hundred and fifty-nine pounds carried forward, and the chairman will support any resolution that is lawful and will not oppose any that is honest.

“Read it back to me.”

Riddick read it back.

“That will do.” Barrow stood up and picked up his hat. “Miss Ashby. Walk with me to the gate.”

✦

We walked to the gate.

The sun had come out and the yard was full of that yellow light that comes at five in March and makes everything look like a thing that has been polished.

“You will lose your mother,” Barrow said.

“I know.”

“Ten months the doctor says and doctors are wrong about the month and right about the year.” He stopped at the gate. “I am not going to say I am sorry. I am sorry, and I am not going to say it, because if I say it you will have to say something back and neither of us has anything to say.

“I am going to say this instead. At the board there will be a man from Carrow who will want to make it about a girl with a thimble. He will say the whole thing rests on a woman’s reading of a page and that a woman’s reading is not evidence.

“And there is only one answer to that, and you had better have it ready.

“You have to make it about the figures. Not the reading. The figures are in eleven books in this parish and any man may add them up and get the same answer, and if they make it about the thimble, you make it about the figures, and you make it about them until they are sick of hearing it.”

He opened the gate and my boot went off the wet step, and his hand was under my elbow before I knew I was falling. He put me right and let go at once, and neither of us said a word about it.

I thought about his hand on my elbow for two miles.

“And Miss Ashby. There is one more thing and then I will let you go home.

“The man who came in 1799 with the slip. He is not dead. He is at Ely, and his name is Quill, and I have written to him four times in thirty years and he has answered three times, and every answer was one line.

“The last one said: the file is closed.”

He went in and shut the gate and I stood in the lane with the fair copy under my arm and the light going and the rooks coming over.

15

My Mother’s Sister

There were two plates on the table when I came in.

The cloth on the table was the one we use for funerals. That is not a figure of speech either. There is a cloth in this house that is kept in the chest and comes out for burial teas, and it is darned at one corner, and my mother had put it on.

My father was not at the table. He was in the yard, in the dark, doing something to the gate that did not need doing, and he had been out there since six.

“Sit down,” my mother said.

“Who is coming.”

“Sit down.”

I sat down.

She put the pot on the table and spooned out the barley, and she did it carefully, the way she does everything, and then she sat down opposite me and put her hands flat on the table on either side of her plate, which is a thing she does when she has decided something.

She had her hands on the table and she did not move them while she talked, and I watched them. There was a wedding ring on the left one that had worn so thin at the back that the gold was a line.

The lamp was lit and stood still in the still air, and its light went along the funeral cloth and into the darned corner and back, and it was the warmest that room had looked in a year, which was its own kind of wrong.

✦

“Her name is Ellen,” she said. “She was born in 1781 and I was born in 1783 and there was a brother between us who died at four months, and there was a sister after me who died at two years, and then there was nobody else, and my mother used to say that she had been given four children and had been allowed to keep two, and that she did not know whether that was a mercy or a debt.

“Ellen was the eldest. She had the office from our mother in 1801, when our mother could not do the walking any more, and she held it for five years.

“She married in 1804. His name was Rooke and he kept bees and he was a quiet man and he was not clever and he was kind to her, and he died in 1809 of a fall from a ladder, and she was twenty-eight, and there was no child.”

“Then the office.”

“Then the office.” My mother turned her plate round. “In 1806 the commission came. It sat at Ely and it had a schedule and the schedule had four hundred and eleven offices on it, and ours was the four hundred and something, and it was suppressed, and the land went to fourteen men of this parish, and one of the fourteen was your father, and one was Barrow.

“And Ellen was told that the office no longer existed, and that the house was the property of the commissioners, and that she might stay in it as a tenant at the pleasure of the commissioners, and she has stayed in it for twenty-five years at the pleasure of the commissioners, and she has never paid a penny of rent and she has never been asked for one, and that is the whole of what the law has done for her.”

I put my spoon down.

“Why has nobody.” I stopped.

“Say it.”

“Why has nobody in this parish done anything.”

My mother looked at me.

“Because they got the land,” she said. “Fourteen men, and the land was worth a hundred and four pounds a year, and it was divided, and the fourteen men were the men who paid the rates and sat on the vestry and stood at the front at church. And a thing that has been divided among fourteen men is a thing that fourteen families will not talk about, and in twenty-five years fourteen families is most of a parish.”

“And you.”

“I married your father in 1806,” my mother said, “and he had signed the petition, and he had been one of the fourteen, and I did not know it when I married him and I found out in the November.

“And I have been in this house for twenty-five years and I have not once said the word petition to him, and he has not once said the word sister, and we have been married twenty-five years and we have each been carrying a thing and neither of us has put it down.”

✦

The fire ticked.

“Then in 1799,” I said.

“Then in 1799.” She got up and went to the dresser and came back with a paper in her hand. “That is the other end of it and I am going to tell you the whole of it and then I am going to give you this, and after that we are going to have to decide what to do about a man who is coming at nine.”

“Who is coming at nine.”

“Sit down.”

“I am sitting down.”

My mother sat down.

“In 1799 a man came to this house from Carrow,” she said. “He had a leather case and a manner and he came in the evening, when your father was at the vestry, and he sat in that chair, and he said that there was an office in the book with no name against it, and that the commissioners had endowed it, and that it was in your father’s gift, and that if we did not take it the money would go back to London and be lost to the parish.

“And I said to him: what does the office do?

“And he said: it is a formality.

“And I asked him a second question, which was: then why is there money in it?

“And he looked at me for a while, and then he said a thing I have never forgotten, and I have gone over it perhaps a thousand times in twenty-five years.

“He said: there is money in a great many things that do not do anything. That is what a country is.”

✦

The fire took a stick.

“And you took it,” I said.

“I took it.” My mother’s voice did not change. “I took it because there was eleven pounds four a year in it and a coal allowance, and because I had a child of four and a husband with a bad chest and there was no money in this house in 1799 that was not spoken for, and because the man said it was in my husband’s gift and my husband was at the vestry and would be home at nine.

“And because I had been Ellen’s sister all my life, and I had watched her hold an office that everybody in this parish knew about and nobody in this parish said out loud, and I had thought, in the way that a young woman thinks a thing she will be ashamed of later: if there is a place, it is mine as much as hers.

“I did not say that out loud. I have never said it out loud until now. But it was in me and it was the reason I did not wake your father and ask him to read the bottom of the paper.

“So the next day I walked to Collow, because if a thing is going to be done it should be done by the person who is going to do it, and I told Ellen. I told her the whole of it. And she listened, and she did not say anything for a long time, and then she said:

“Take it and be careful, and when they come for it, do not fight them, because they will take the land as well.”

✦

I sat very still.

I had a pain in my jaw and I did not know why, and then I understood that I had been holding my teeth together since the barley.

“She knew,” I said.

“She knew in 1799 that it would end the way it has ended, and she has known it for thirty-two years, and she has never once said I told you.” My mother put her hands in her lap. “That is the whole of my sister. She has been dying in a bed at Collow for eleven years and she has never once said the thing that would have made it easier for her.”

“And the walking.”

“She stopped.

“Say the rest.”

“The rest is a road.” She turned her cup round. “It is twelve miles and it goes over the ford and up the hill and past a barn that fell down in 1811, and there is a man at the top of the hill who keeps geese and has never once said good morning to me in twenty years, and there is a gate at the end of the lane that opens inward because the ground falls away, and I have opened that gate a thousand and forty times.

“I have counted. That is what a person does on a road. She counts.”

She went on. “In the summer I go at six and I am there by eight, and the light is on the hill behind the house and it is the best hour of my week. In the winter I go at eleven and I am back by four and the last mile is in the dark, and I have done that mile in the dark eleven hundred times and I know every stone of it.”

“Why Tuesdays?”

“Because Tuesday is the day the carrier does not go to Collow and the lane is empty,” my mother said, “and because a woman who is seen on a road every Tuesday is a woman about whom a story is told, and a woman who is seen on a road on different days is a woman about whom no story is told at all.”

“The walking is mine,” my mother said. “You do not get to have the walking. I have walked twelve miles a week for twenty years to sit with a woman I ruined, and it has cost me my knees and most of my shoes, and it is the only thing I have ever done that was not a bargain.”

✦

She pushed the paper across the table.

I unfolded it.

It was a piece of linen, not paper, and it had been washed so many times that it had gone soft as a hand. It was about the size of a handkerchief and it had been worked on one corner and not the rest, and the work was a sprig with three leaves and a bud, and the stitching was the smallest I have ever seen. The thread was silk, not cotton, which is what you use for a thing that is not going to be used. The bud had been left with one stitch out.

“That is for you,” my mother said. “She started it the week you were born. She has been at it for eighteen years and she cannot see well enough now, and she has never finished it because she has never finished anything she was making for a person she had not met.

“Take it up with you and put it in the drawer and do not say anything about it tomorrow, because she will ask whether you have it and she will pretend she does not care about the answer.”

I folded the linen and put it in my pocket with the thimble.

✦

“Now,” my mother said. “The man at nine.”

“Rooke.”

“I sent word with the boy at four o’clock, before you came in.”

I put my hands flat on the table.

“You did what.”

“I sent word with the boy at four o’clock,” my mother said. “He is coming here at nine, and he is coming to this house, and he is going to sit in that chair, and I am going to tell him the whole of it, and you are going to sit in that chair and be quiet.”

“Mother.”

“I have been quiet for thirty-two years,” she said, “and I am better at it than you. You have been at this a fortnight and you have been in a market and a vestry and a barn and you have done well, and I have watched you do it and I have been proud of you in a way I have not been able to say.

“But there is a thing you do not know how to do, and it is the thing this parish has been doing since before you were born, and it is to sit in a chair and be quiet and let a man from Carrow talk himself into a corner.

“You talk. You have always talked. You talked when you were four and you have not stopped. And a person who talks gives a man from Carrow a thing to answer, and a man from Carrow who has a thing to answer will answer it for an hour, and at the end of the hour everybody will have forgotten what the question was.

“So tonight you will sit in that chair and you will say nothing, and when he has finished I will say one thing, and the one thing will be the thing that matters, and it will be a thing I have been keeping for twenty-five years, and I am not going to give it to you before I give it to him.”

✦

She got up and began to clear the plates.

I sat at the table.

“There is a thing I want to ask,” I said.

“Ask it.”

“You said the office was in father’s gift.”

“It was.”

“Then in 1799 the office was in his gift, and the paper had a space for a name, and the name written in it was yours.” I looked up. “Whose hand wrote your name?”

My mother stood at the dresser with her back to me and the plates in her hands.

“His,” she said.

“Father’s.”

“Your father’s.” She put the plates down. “He came home at nine and I told him what I had done, and he sat down at that table and he took the pen and he wrote my name in a space on a paper that a man from Carrow had left with him, and he did it in his own hand, in this kitchen, at nine o’clock at night, and he did it without reading the bottom of the paper.

“And then he ruled a line under it, and he said: there. Now it is a boundary.

“And I said: what is on the other side of it?

“And he said: nothing. That is the point.”

✦

The clock struck eight.

My mother went up and I heard her on the fourth step and the seventh and then the door of the room above.

I sat in the kitchen with the two plates and the linen in my pocket and the fire going down.

Then I got up and put the second chair at the table, and moved the lamp to the middle, and swept the hearth, which did not need sweeping, and stood at the window.

✦

He came at nine exactly.

I heard him on the path before I saw him. His boots are heavy and he does not walk on the edges of anything.

I opened the door before he knocked.

He had his hat in his hand and his coat was dark at the shoulders and his hair was flat with the wet, and he had walked from the church in the rain, and he looked at me and did not say good evening.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Your mother sent for me.”

“She did.”

“At four o’clock, with a boy, and the boy said: the mistress says to come at nine and to come to the house and not to the church, and to come alone.” He turned his hat over in his hands. “I have been at this trade for twenty-five years and I have had three such messages in my life and every one of them was the beginning of the worst week of somebody’s year.”

“Come in.”

He came in.

My mother was already on the stairs.

She came down with her shawl round her and her hair up and she did not look at me, and she went to the table and stood with her hand on the back of the chair, and Rooke stood in the middle of the kitchen with his hat in his hand.

“Mrs. Ashby.”

“Sit down, Mr. Rooke.”

“I would rather stand.”

“You will sit down,” my mother said, “because what I am going to tell you will take an hour, and because you are going to want to write some of it down, and a man who is writing is a man who is not deciding yet, and I would like you to have an hour before you decide.”

Rooke sat down.

He put his hat on the floor beside the chair and took out a pencil and a folded sheet of paper, and then he looked at my mother and put the pencil down again.

“No,” my mother said. “Write. I have had it in my head for twenty-five years and I would like it to be somewhere else.”

He picked the pencil up.

My mother sat down opposite him.

And I sat in the chair by the fire with my hands in my lap and said nothing at all, which is the hardest thing I have ever done, and which I did, and which is the reason the thing that happened next happened at all.

16

What My Mother Asked Him

He wrote for an hour.

My mother talked and he wrote, and I sat in the chair by the fire with my hands in my lap, and the fire went down and I did not get up to put anything on it, because getting up would have been a thing to look at and I had been told to be quiet.

Once the coal settled and the fire threw a fan of sparks up the chimney, and for a second the whole room was orange, the plates and the cloth and my mother’s face, and then it went back to being a room where a thing was being decided.

She told it in order. She was better at it than I would have been.

And then the last part, which I had not heard, and which she had been keeping for twenty-five years.

✦

“In 1807,” she said, “a man came to this house from the commissioners. He came in the spring, and he had a schedule, and he said that the office had been suppressed and that the endowment was vested in the commissioners and that the payments would cease.

“Your father told him that the office in this parish was a different office, with a different endowment, and he showed him the book.

“And the man looked at the book for a long time, and then he said a thing that I have thought about every day since.

“He said: I have no instruction about this parish.

“And he went away. And the payments did not cease. And they have not ceased for twenty-four years.”

Rooke stopped writing.

“He found it,” he said. “In 1807, a clerk from the commission found a suppressed office still being paid in a parish, and he wrote I have no instruction about this parish, and he closed his book.”

“Yes.”

“That is not an accident,” Rooke said. “That is a decision.”

“It is a decision that kept a roof on this house,” my mother said. “And I have never known whether to be grateful for it, and I have prayed about it, and I have come to no conclusion, and I am not going to come to one now.”

✦

Rooke put the pencil down and looked at the page he had written and then at my mother.

“Mrs. Ashby,” he said. “I have a thing to tell you and I am going to tell it badly, because I have never told it to anybody and I have been not telling it for twenty-five years.

“In 1806 I was nineteen years old and I was a boy in the registry at Carrow, and I was given a schedule to copy. It was four pages. It was in a hand I had never seen, and it had four hundred and eleven offices on it, and I could not read it.

“I copied it anyway. I copied the words the Letter of the Dead, at Nether Collow and I did not know what they meant, and I did not ask, because a boy who asks questions in his first month does not get a second month.

“On the fourth day I asked Mr. Bellamy in the archive what a letter of the dead was, and he told me it was a formality, and I went back up the stair and finished copying it, and the commission suppressed the office on the fourth of November, 1806.
