“And I have been in that building for twenty-five years, and I have read every schedule that came through my hands since, and I have never once read the words letter of the dead on another one, because there has never been another one, because I copied the only one there was.

“That is my account. It is worse than yours.”

✦

My mother sat with her hands on the table.

“You have known since 1806,” she said.

“I have known that I copied a schedule. I did not know what I had done until this morning, when I went to Ely and a man in that archive told me the truth about a formality.”

“And now you know.”

“Now I know.”

“Then what are you going to do.”

Rooke looked at her.

“I do not know,” he said. “I am going to sit here for a minute and then I am going to tell you what I think, and you should not trust it, because I have wanted to be a good man for twenty-five years and I have found out this morning that I have been the instrument of the worst thing in this county, and a man who has just found that out is not a man whose judgment is worth anything.”

✦

I did not say anything.

I want that written down, because it was the hardest hour of my life up to that point, and because my mother was right and I have never told her so.

She let him sit.

She got up and put a stick on the fire and came back and sat down, and she did not fill the silence with anything, and the silence went on for four minutes by the clock, and Rooke sat in it with his hands on his knees.

Then he looked up.

“Mrs. Ashby,” he said. “I am going to ask you a question and I want the true answer and not the kind one.”

“Ask it.”

“Did you know that the office had been suppressed when you took it?”

“No.”

“Did you suspect.”

My mother was quiet for a moment.

“I knew that a thing with money in it and no name on it was not a thing that came from God,” she said. “I did not know it had been abolished. I knew it was wrong and I did not know what kind of wrong it was, and I have told myself for twenty-five years that not knowing the kind is a kind of innocence, and it is not, and I have known that for a long time.”

Rooke nodded slowly.

“Then there is one more question,” he said. “And this is the one that matters.”

“Ask it.”

“What do you want?”

✦

My mother looked at him.

Nobody had asked her that. I want to put down that nobody in twenty-five years had asked her that, and that when he asked it her face did a thing I had never seen it do, which was that it came apart and then put itself back together in about two seconds.

“I want my sister to have her house,” she said.

“She has her house. She has it at the pleasure of the commissioners and she has been in it for twenty-five years and there is no man in this county who will turn a dying woman of fifty out of a house she has lived in all her life.” Rooke’s voice was flat. “That is not the answer. Try again.”

“Then I want the office out of the book.”

“Why.”

“Because it is a lie.”

“Why does that matter, at your age, with ten months?”

My mother put both hands flat on the table.

“Because I have been a lie in a book for thirty-two years,” she said, “and I have been a good woman in this parish for thirty-two years, and I have been the woman who does the bread and the woman who sits with the dying and the woman whose daughter has a fair hand, and every one of those things has been paid for out of a column that does not exist.

“And when I am dead my daughter will be the woman in the eleventh class whose mother was a fraud, and she will have to live in this parish and hold her head up in a lane where everybody knows.

“So I want the office out of the book. Not because it is a lie. Because a lie in a book is a thing that a person has to carry, and I am not going to leave it on her.”

✦

Rooke sat back.

“Then I am going to tell you what I think,” he said, “and it is not what you have asked for, and it will take a minute.”

He took the folded sheet he had written on and turned it over and drew a line on the back.

“Here is the office,” he said. “It was created in 1711 by a private Act. It was held by four women in a hundred years. It was suppressed in 1806 by a commission that did not know what it was, on a schedule copied by a boy who could not read it.

“Here is your class. It was made in 1794 to hold a name. It has fourteen names in it and eleven pounds four a year and a coal allowance, and it is a lie, and you are right that it is a lie, and it must come out.

“And here is the thing that nobody has said in this room.

“There are two ways to take it out.

“The first is to strike your name out of the class and close the class and let the endowment go back to the Crown. That is the lawful way. It takes one resolution at Lady Day and it is done, and in the summer there will be no eleventh class in this parish, and it will not be the end of a fraud, it will be the end of a book.

“The second is to restore the office. Not your class. The office. The Letter of the Dead. To put it back in the book of this county as an office that exists, and to say that the suppression of 1806 was made in error, and to ask the board to say so.

“That is not the lawful way. There is no procedure for it. There has not been a restoration of a suppressed office in this county since the Reformation, and if I propose it, the board will look at me the way a bench of farmers looks at a man who has taken his hat off in a field.

“And if it goes through, your mother’s class is not a lie any more. It is a holding. It is the office at the bottom of a line that goes back to 1711, and the woman in it is the letter, and her daughter after her, and there is nothing in the book that has to be carried by anybody.”

✦

Nobody said anything for a while.

“You would lose your office,” my mother said.

“I would lose my office.”

“And your pension.”

“And my pension.”

“And your name in that building, which is the only name you have.”

Rooke turned the paper over.

“I have signed four thousand papers,” he said. “Every one of them was correct. I have never once signed a thing that was true and incorrect at the same time, and I told your daughter in April that I wanted to know what happens, and she has been holding me to it ever since, and I have been avoiding the question for a fortnight, and I am not going to avoid it in this kitchen.

“There is a thing I have to say first, and it is not to you.”

He turned to me.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Your mother has asked me for the office out of the book. You have asked me for nothing. I have noticed that and I have thought about it, and I think it is because you know that a thing which is given to you by a man in a kitchen is not the same as a thing you have taken in a board room.

“So I am going to ask you a question instead, and I want you to answer it as though you were not eighteen.

“If I put that motion at Lady Day, the board will want evidence. They will want the file at Ely, which is four pages with two wanting. They will want the returns, which are in my name and signed by your father. They will want the bridge book and the toll book and the coal yard book, which are in this parish and can be produced.

“And they will want a living witness who can say what the office was, because a paper from 1711 will not do it.

“Your aunt is dying in a bed at Collow and she is the last woman who held it and she is the only person alive who can tell a board what the office did.

“Are you going to ask her?”

✦

I did not answer that.

“She is fifty,” I said. “She has been in a bed for eleven years. She has not been out of the gate since Michaelmas.”

“I know.”

“And you are asking me to walk up that lane and ask her to come to a board room at Carrow and say out loud, in front of fourteen men, that she put her hand on the dead.”

“Yes.”

“Why me.”

“Because she is your mother’s sister,” Rooke said, “and because you are the only person in this county who can do what she did, and because when she sees you she will know that the thing is not going to die with her, and a person who knows that will do a thing she would not do for a board or a bishop or a man from Carrow.

“And because I cannot ask her. I am the man who copied the schedule. If I walk up that lane she will see the boy who abolished her office, and she will be polite, and she will say no.”

✦

My mother stood up.

“Mr. Rooke,” she said. “There is a thing I have not said and I am going to say it now, and then I am going to bed, because it is eleven o’clock and I have been up since five.

“I have asked you for the office out of the book and you have told me it will cost you your office and your pension and your name, and you have not once asked me what I would give.

“So I will tell you. I have ten months and I am not going to spend them in a chair. I am going to walk that lane until I cannot, and I am going to take my daughter with me, and I am going to sit in that room while she reads that book, and I am going to be there when Ellen says what she is going to say.

“And on the day the board meets, I am going to be in that room. Not in the gallery. In the room. And when they ask who the woman is in the eleventh class, I am going to stand up and say: I am, and I am going to say what I did in 1799 and what I have taken since.

“And then they will have to decide about me before they decide about the office, and a board that has just ruined a dying woman will find it very hard to ruin the man who told the truth about her.

“That is what I have to give. It is not much. It is the only thing I have got.”

✦

Rooke stood up and put his pencil in his pocket and picked up his hat.

“Mrs. Ashby,” he said. “I have been in this trade for twenty-five years and I have met a great many people who wanted a thing put right.

“You are the first one who has offered to be the evidence.”

He went to the door and stopped.

“Lady Day is the twenty-fifth,” he said. “The board sits on the twenty-ninth at Carrow. That is four days after the rents are paid and it is the day the county presents its accounts, and there will be eleven parishes there and a great many men who have come to hear about drains.

“Your daughter has until the twenty-ninth to walk up that lane and come back with a witness.”

He put his hat on.

“And Miss Ashby.”

“Sir.”

“Your mother is right about one thing and I want you to hear it from me as well as from her.

“Do not talk at that board. I have watched you for a fortnight and you are quicker than any man in that building and it will do you no good at all. A board is not a market and it is not a barn. A board is fourteen men who have been in the same room for eleven hours and want their dinner, and the way to move them is not to be clever.

“The way to move them is to put one page in front of them, and then another page, and then to stop.”

✦

He went out.

I heard his boots on the path and then on the lane, and then nothing.

My mother stood at the table with her hand on the back of the chair he had been sitting in.

“Mercy.”

“Mm.”

“Tomorrow you will go up that lane,” she said, “and I will go with you as far as the bridge, and I will not go over it, because I have never gone over it with you and I am not going to start now.

“And when you come back, you will tell me what she says, all of it, and you will not leave out the parts that are hard for me to hear.”

“Yes.”

She went up the stairs.

On the fourth step she stopped.

“Put the second plate away before you come up,” she said. “I do not want to see it in the morning.”

17

The Lane

My mother walked with me as far as the bridge and stopped at the stone.

That was the arrangement and I had agreed to it and I had not expected it to be hard, and it was hard.

“Go on,” she said.

“Come with me.”

“No.”

“Mother.”

“No.” She had her shawl up over her head and her hands inside it and she looked at the far bank the way a person looks at a thing she has decided not to have. “I have walked that lane a thousand and forty times and I have never once walked it with anybody, and I am not going to start today. If I am there when you knock she will look at me first and she will not look at you, and she has been looking at me for twenty-five years and she has had enough of it.

“Go on. And Mercy.”

“Mm.”

“Whatever she says about me, do not defend me. She has earned the right and I have earned the listening.”

✦

The bridge is forty feet and I have never counted anything more slowly.

The stone with the parish name is at the far end and there is a line cut under the name, and the line goes down into the water. I stepped over it and put my foot down on the other side and stood there for a moment like a person who has come out of a house into cold air.

Nothing happened. That is the thing nobody tells you about a boundary. You cross it and your feet are still your feet.

✦

The lane on the far side goes up between two hedges for three hundred yards and then it opens out and there is a field and then the house.

It is a good house. I had not expected that. It is stone with a slate roof and four windows on the front and a chimney at each end, and there is a garden in front of it with a wall round it and a gate in the wall, and the gate opens inward because the ground falls away.

My mother had said that. She had said it in the kitchen, in the list of things she knew about a road, and I had not understood that she was telling me about a house.

There were daffodils in the garden. Somebody had planted them in rows and somebody had kept the rows weeded, and there were eleven out and the rest still closed, and there was a bench against the wall with a cushion on it, and the cushion had been rained on and had not been brought in.

The roof had a place at the north end where the slates had gone and somebody had put a tarpaulin over it and lashed it down with rope, and the tarpaulin was old and had been there long enough to grow a green line along the fold.

That is a thing about a house with a sick woman in it. The front of it can be as neat as a church and the back of it is a list of things nobody has had the strength to finish.

✦

I knocked.

Nobody came.

I knocked again and stood back and looked at the windows and there was no face in any of them and no curtain moved.

Then I went round the side.

There is a back door with a step and a boot scraper, and the boot scraper is iron and it is worn down in the middle, which means a great many boots have been scraped on it, which means that at some time this house had a great many people going in and out of it.

I knocked at the back door.

Nothing.

I sat down on the step.

From it you could see the whole of the garden, and the light was coming low across it, and the daffodils by the wall had opened since the morning and each one had its shadow, and a bee was working the currant flowers with a sound like a small saw. It was the most orderly beautiful thing I had seen in a parish that did not know it was keeping any.

✦

I want to put down the hour I spent on that step, because it is the hour in which I stopped being afraid of the house.

The garden had a pear tree in it that had been espaliered against the wall, and the espalier had been done properly, with the branches trained flat and tied with list, and the list had been renewed at some time and not recently. There were buds on it.

There was a hen house with the door open and no hens.

There was a bench and a cushion and a rope and a tarpaulin, and a bucket upside down by the door, and a hoe leaning against the wall with the blade clean.

Somebody was keeping this garden. Somebody was keeping it the way a person keeps a thing they are not able to do any more: not well, and not badly, and every single day, and with the tools wiped.

At about eleven o’clock a woman came round the corner of the house with a basket on her arm and stopped dead.

“Who are you?”

“Mercy Ashby.”

She was about fifty and she had the arms of a woman who carries things and she did not put the basket down.

“Ashby.”

“Mary Ann’s daughter.”

The woman looked at me for a long time.

“She said you would come,” she said. “She said it in January. She has been saying it since January and I have been telling her she is an old fool and there is nothing coming up that lane but the wind.”

She came and put the basket down and opened the back door with her hip.

“My name is Trewin,” she said. “I come at seven and I go at four and I do not do the nights and there is nobody does the nights. You will find her awake and she will not be pleased to see you and you are not to take it personally, because she is not pleased to see anybody, and it has been eleven years.”

✦

The passage was clean and cold and it had a chair in it and a table with a basin on it and a row of hooks with nothing on them but the marks where things had hung. There was a clock on the wall that had stopped at four, and a shelf with three books on it, and a barometer that had not worked since before I was born.

Mrs. Trewin stopped at the door at the end.

“Two things,” she said. “She cannot lift her left arm and she will not be helped with her hair, so do not offer. And she will tell you she is dying and she is, and she will use it, and she has earned the right to use it.”

She opened the door.

“Ellen,” she said. “There is a girl here says she is Mary Ann’s.”

✦

The room had a bed and a window and a fire and a chair by the fire, and the window was open two inches at the bottom and there was a smell of wet earth and daffodils. There was a shelf beside the bed with a comb on it and a glass and a book with a marker in it and a small brass box. There was a work basket on the floor with a shirt in it and the needle still in the shirt.

She was sitting up in the bed with a shawl round her shoulders and her hair done, and the hair had been done properly, in a way that takes two hands and a mirror, and she had done it herself with one.

She was fifty and she looked sixty and she had the face of a woman who has been in a bed for eleven years and has decided not to look like it.

Her eyes were the thing. They were my mother’s eyes, exactly, and they were not friendly.

“You took your time,” she said.

✦

I stood in the doorway.

“Mrs. Rooke.”

“Do not call me that. I was married to a man for five years and I have been a widow for twenty-two, and I am not going to be called by a name I had for five years when I have had my own for fifty.” She shifted her shoulder against the pillow. “Sit down. Not that chair. That chair has a bad leg. The other one.”

I sat in the other one.

She looked at me.

“Stand up.”

I stood up.

“Turn round.”

I turned round.

“Sit down,” she said. “You have her chin and you have his hands and you have the shoulders of my mother, and I have not seen those shoulders on anybody for thirty years.”

“You knew my grandmother.”

“I had her for twenty-three years,” Ellen said. “She was my mother and she was the letter before me and she put her hand on the dead for eleven years, and I have never once in my life met anybody else who could do it, and neither will you, and that is the whole of what I have to tell you and you could have had it in a letter.”

✦

Mrs. Trewin brought tea and went out.

Ellen did not touch hers.

“Now,” she said. “You have come up my lane and you have come on a Tuesday and my sister did not come with you, and I know what that means, so we will not waste an hour being polite.

“Ask me the thing you came to ask.”

I put my hands on my knees.

“Was the office real?”

The room was quiet.

“That is a good question,” Ellen said. “That is a better question than the one I expected. I expected what is it, and can I do it, and is it worth money. Not one of those is a question. Yours is a question.”

She sat forward.

“Yes,” she said. “It was real. It was created by an Act in 1711, and it was endowed with land, and it was held by four women in a hundred years, and it did a thing that no other office in England did, and there is not one piece of paper anywhere in this county that says what the thing was, because the men who wrote the Act could not bring themselves to write the words.

“So they gave it a name that sounds like a clerk’s job. The Letter of the Dead. A letter is a thing you send and a dead person is a thing you send it to, and if you put those two words together in a schedule in London it sounds like a formality. That is how it got abolished. That is how it got past fourteen men in this parish who knew exactly what they were voting for and did not want to say it out loud.”

✦

“Did you do it,” I said.

“I did it for five years.”

“What did it feel like.”

Ellen looked at me.

“Ask me the other question first,” she said. “The one you are not asking, because you are very polite and because you have been told to be quiet by two different people this week.

“You want to know whether it works.”

I did not say anything.

“It works,” she said. “You put your hand on a thing and the thing tells you what it was made for and what was done with it. A cup tells you about the hands that held it. A wall tells you about the men who built it. A knife tells you about the hand that held it, and a ring tells you, if you are unlucky, about a promise.

“And a body tells you the last thing that happened to it.”

The fire took a stick.

“That is the office,” she said. “That is what the county abolished in 1806, because a clerk in London did not know what the words meant and a commission did not want to write the word body in a report to the Treasury.

“And it is the reason I have not been out of this room since Michaelmas, because a person who has done it for five years does not stop doing it, and a person who cannot go to the body any more lies in a bed and reads the bed, and the bed tells her about every night she has spent in it, and there have been four thousand and eleven of them, and I know all of them.”

The fire had burned down to a red shape and the light in the room had changed, the way it does at noon in March, when the sun goes behind a cloud and comes out and the walls go from grey to yellow and back.

I looked at the chair I was sitting in and I did not put my hand on it.

That is the first thing she taught me and I have never forgotten it, and it is the reason I have been able to live in a house since.

✦

I sat very still.

“How do you bear it,” I said.

“I do not read the bed,” Ellen said. “I have not put my hand on this bed in eleven years and I will not, and if you put your hand on that chair you are sitting in, I will ask you to leave.

“That is the first thing you have to learn and it is the only thing anybody can teach you. You choose. You do not have to read a thing just because your hand is on it. A woman who reads everything is a woman who is not fit to live in a house.

“Now.” She sat back. “You have had your question. I have one.”

“Ask it.”

“How long has your mother got?”

“Ten months.”

“Ten months.” She looked at the window. “She has been coming up that lane for twenty-five years and she has never once told me. She has told me about you, and about your father’s throat, and about a cat that got into the church in 1821, and she has not told me that.

“Ten months. And she will spend nine of them walking twelve miles a week to sit with me, and she will not tell me why she is tired, and she will die in the spring, and I will hear about it from Mrs. Trewin.”

She put her hand flat on the blanket.

“Give me the thimble,” she said.

✦

I took it out of my pocket and gave it to her.

She did not put her hand on it as a reader. She put it on her finger, on the third finger of the right hand, and held it up.

It fitted her. It had never fitted me.

“That was my mother’s and it is mine and it will be yours,” she said, “and it is not a magic thing, and it never was, and do not let anybody in a board room tell you that it is, because a woman who is described as having a magic thimble is a woman who can be dismissed.

“It is a thimble. It is a good one. She wore it for eleven years and the tip is worn to the copper, and that is not because she sewed. It is because she was a woman who put her hand on the dead, and a thimble is what you wear when you are going to put your hand into something that will cut you.

“Now take it back and put it in your pocket and give me the other thing.”

“What other thing.”

“The thing in your other pocket.”

I took out the folded linen.

Ellen unfolded it on the blanket and looked at it for a long time.

“Eighteen years,” she said. “I have been at this since the week you were born and I have got three leaves and half a bud.

“And it is not a present. I want that understood. It is not a present and it is not a christening cloth and it is not a thing you will put on a chair.

“It is a pattern. It is the same pattern that is on the collar of the shirt your mother was sewing the day you came into the kitchen, and it is on the hem of the shift my mother was buried in, and it is on the front of a book in that chest, and it is not in any pattern book in England.

“You will find it in the book. And when you find it you will know that a thing can go down four women in a family and not be written anywhere, and that is not a superstition. That is a way of keeping a thing out of the hands of a county.”

✦

She folded the linen and gave it back.

“Now,” she said. “You came here with a thing to ask me and you have not asked it, and it is nearly twelve and Mrs. Trewin will bring the dinner at one and I do not want to be asked it while I am eating.

“So ask it.”

I put the linen in my pocket.

“There is a board at Carrow on the twenty-ninth,” I said. “There is a man who is going to propose that the office be restored. To do that he needs a living witness who held it and can say what it did.

“And I have come up your lane to ask you to come down it.”

✦

Ellen did not answer.

She looked at the window for a long time. There were daffodils out there and the light coming through them and the tarpaulin on the roof and the four thousand and eleven nights.

“Nineteen miles,” she said. “In a cart. On a road I have not been down since Michaelmas.

“To a room full of men who will look at me the way they looked at me in 1806. One of them will be a man who took my land. One will be a man who wrote my name in a schedule. And one will be a clerk from Ely who will ask me how I do it.

“And I will have to sit in a chair in front of them and say: I put my hand on the dead and they answered me.

“And there will not be one man in that room who believes me, and there will be eleven who will write it down as a woman’s fancy, and the newspapers will have it, and in a hundred years there will be a book that says there was a woman at Collow in 1831 who thought she could talk to the dead.

“Why would I do that?”

“Because it is true,” I said.

“Truth is not a reason.”

“Because if you do not, it dies with you.”

“It has been dead since 1806,” Ellen said. “It has been dead twenty-five years. I am the only thing that is not dead yet.”

✦

I did not move.

“No,” I said.

She looked at me.

“You are not the only thing,” I said. “I turned eighteen on the fourth of this month. I have done it four times and one of them put me on the floor with a nosebleed, and I do not know what it is, and I have been in a barn and a vestry and a market and a registry office in the last fortnight, and the whole of it has been run by men who are trying to decide what I am.

“And if you do not come to that board, the answer will be what they decide it is. Not what it is. Not what your mother did for eleven years. What fourteen men in a room decide.

“And they will decide it is a girl with a thimble, and that is worse than it dying. A thing that dies is gone. A thing that is decided is written down.”

✦

Ellen sat with her hand on the blanket.

The fire moved.

“You have the chin,” she said, “and the shoulders, and a temper I do not remember in my sister.”

She looked at me.

“Get Mrs. Trewin,” she said. “I am going to need a dress and I am going to need it let out at the shoulder, and I am going to need a chair with arms, and I am going to need a man with a cart, and I am going to need the whole of that arranged by the twenty-eighth and not the twenty-ninth, because I am not going to arrive at a board in a cart on the morning of the board and be looked at.

“And Mercy.”

“Mrs. Rooke.”

“Ellen.” She said it the way a person puts a thing down. “There is a book in that chest. It has been in that chest since 1806 and I have not opened it since 1809 and I am not going to open it now.

“Take it home with you. Read it. And when you have read it, come back, and then we will talk about what we are going to say.”

✦

The chest was at the foot of the bed and it was oak and it had a lock and the key was in the lock.

“Take the key,” Ellen said. “It has been in that lock since 1809 and I have not turned it.”

I turned it.

Mrs. Trewin came in with her sleeves rolled and looked at Ellen and then at me and then at Ellen again.

“You are going to Carrow,” she said.

“I am going to Carrow.”

“Then I am coming.”

“You are not.”

“I am coming,” Mrs. Trewin said, and that was the end of it.

✦

I got as far as the door.

“Mercy.”

I turned round.

“Your mother is at the stone,” Ellen said. “She has been at the stone since nine. She will not come up and she will not go home, and when you get down there she will not say anything for a while.

“Let her not say anything. She has been not saying it for twenty-five years and she will need to do it in her own time.”

✦

She was at the stone.

She had been there for four hours and she had not gone home, and there was a basket at her feet with bread in it that she had brought for me and not for Ellen, and she had not eaten any of it.

“Well?”

“She is coming.”

My mother put her hand on the parapet.

“Say it again.”

“She is coming to the board. She has a dress to be let out at the shoulder and a chair with arms and a man with a cart, and she wants it arranged by the twenty-eighth.”

My mother stood there with her hand on the parapet for a long time.

Then she said, “Walk home with me,” and she took my arm, which she had not done since I was a child, and we walked the mile and a quarter with the light going and neither of us said a word.

18

The Book She Made

I did not open it that night.

I want to put that down because it is the only piece of self-control I showed in the whole of that spring, and because I have been given credit since for a great many things I did not do.

I put it on the table and I sat and looked at it and I went to bed.

✦

It is not a book in the way a parish book is a book.

It is a thing a woman made. The boards are covered in a piece of dark cloth that has been folded over at the edges and stitched down, and the stitching is the same stitch as the linen in my pocket, and the cloth is a coat. I know it is a coat because there is a buttonhole in the middle of the front board with the button still in it, and the button is bone, and it has been sewn on with a thread that does not match.

The paper is not paper. It is the backs of receipts, cut to size and sewn in, and every leaf has a figure on the back of it in a clerk’s hand. Somebody in this family has been making a book out of another book’s leavings for a hundred and twenty years.

There are forty leaves. There are seventy-six lines that have been written on.

The candle moved and the figures behind the writing moved with it, faint, the way fish stand under ice, and I sat with my hand an inch off the cover for longer than I want to write down.

The leaves are not all the same size and the sewing is not even, and where the paper is thin you can see the figure on the back of it through the writing. On the inside of the front board, in pencil, in a hand that is not any of the four, there is a figure: 4. I have never found out what it is.

✦

I opened it at nine in the morning with the door shut and my mother in the room, because I had decided on the bridge that she had a right to be there and that if I read it alone I would get it wrong.

She sat on the other side of the table with her hands in her lap and did not touch it.

“Read it out,” she said.

✦

The first line is 1711.

1711. March. Hannah Trewin. A man at the ford. Q: whether he was sorry. A: he was not.

I stopped.

“Trewin,” I said.

“There are Trewins in this parish yet,” my mother said. “Mrs. Trewin at Collow is one and her husband was one, and there were Trewins here in 1711 or I am a Dutchman.”

“Then she is in the book.”

“Then she is in the book.” My mother’s voice was flat. “Read on.”

✦

I read for two hours.

At the eleventh line my mother got up and went to the window and stood there with her back to me, and I went on reading. At the twentieth she came back and sat down, and she did not say anything about it and I did not ask.

Three times while I read, I put my finger on a name and took it off again. It is a thing I have had to learn, the way a person learns not to put her hand on a hot iron. The wanting does not go away. It stops being a thing you do.

Seventy-six lines. I am going to put down the ones that matter and not the rest, because the rest are the same shape as the first, and a person who reads out seventy-six of them will lose the thing that is in them.

1743. November. A child, unnamed, of the parish of St. Cuthbert’s. Q: whether it was baptized. A: it was not, and it is not lost.

1764. June. The wife of the miller. Q: whether he did it. A: he did.

1788. February. A man from Carrow, name not given. Q: what the office is worth. A: it is worth nothing to a man who asks that question.

1797. October. Sarah Dunnet. Q: whether my sister is with God. A: she is with the child.

My mother put her hand over her mouth.

“That is old Sarah Dunnet,” she said. “She died in 1822 and she was ninety. She used to say she had a sister who died in a fire in 1796 and there was no child.”

“There is a child in this line.”

“Then there was a child,” my mother said, “and nobody in this parish knew it, and Sarah Dunnet knew it, and she went to my grandmother in October of 1797 and asked, and got an answer, and never told a living soul for twenty-five years.”

She took her hand away from her mouth.

“That is what it was for,” she said. “That is what the office was. It is not for the dead. It is for the living, and it is not for the living to know the future. It is for the living to know the one thing they cannot ask anybody else in the world.”

✦

The lines are not all in the same hand.

There are four hands in that book and I found them all before noon.

The first is small and upright and it has a way of making an e that looks like an i with a lid on it, and it is there from 1711 to 1753.

The second is larger and it slants and it has a strong down stroke on the h, and it is there from 1753 to 1788, and there are two entries in 1754 in a different ink that are corrections of the second hand’s spelling, and the corrections are in the first hand again, only older.

The third is my grandmother’s. I know it is my grandmother’s because there is a line at the back of the book in the same hand that says for E., when she is able, and the E is Ellen.

The fourth is Ellen’s, and it is the smallest of the four, and it goes from 1801 to 1806.

And there is a fifth thing, which is not a hand.

✦

The fifth thing is a stitch.

There is one line in that book that has been sewn through. Not crossed out, not inked over. Sewn, with the same thread as the binding, in three stitches across the paper, and the stitches go through the writing so that a person could not read the line without cutting them.

It is on the leaf for 1799.

I put my finger on it and my mother said, “Do not.”

“Read it with my hand.”

“No.”

“Mother.”

“Do not,” she said. “You have read a page in a book and a boot and a thimble and a basket, and every one of them gave you three seconds of a thing that was over. That is a line that somebody sewed shut. A person who sews a line shut is not hiding it from a board. She is hiding it from herself.

“And if you put your hand on it you will know what is under it and you will not have cut the stitches, and then you will be a woman who knows a thing she is not supposed to know and has not earned it, and there is no living with that.

“Cut them or leave it.”

I got the scissors out of the work basket.

✦

The thread was old and it came apart at the second stitch, and the paper under it had been folded once, along the line, and the fold had been pressed flat with a thumbnail.

I opened it out.

1799. April. A man from Carrow, name of Vane. Q: what he wanted. A: he wanted the office taken out of the book and given to a woman who could not use it, so that it would be there when he came back for it.

Nobody said anything for a while.

Then my mother said, “Read it again.”

I read it again.

✦

“Vane,” I said.

“Vane.”

“Rooke told me that name. In March. He said the man who came here in 1799 with the slip was called Vane and that there is no Vane in the registry at Carrow and there has not been for thirty years.”

My mother was looking at the book.

“There is no Vane in the registry,” she said, “because he was never in the registry. Read the line again. He wanted the office taken out of the book and given to a woman who could not use it, so that it would be there when he came back for it.”

“He wanted a place to put it.”

“He wanted a place to park it,” my mother said, “the way a man parks a cart. And he found a woman with a child of four and a husband with a bad chest, and he came to her in the evening when her husband was at the vestry, and he told her it was a formality, and he paid her eleven pounds four a year to keep his cart in her yard for thirty-two years.”

✦

I got up and walked to the window and stood there.

“He is not dead,” I said.

“Who says he is dead.”

“Rooke said there is no Vane in the registry.”

“There is no Vane in the registry,” my mother said, “which is a thing a man arranges. And there is a man at Ely who has been in that building since 1806 and who has signed every return that came through it, and who wrote in 1799 that the office was a formality.

“What was his name?”

“Quill.”

“Quill.” My mother said it slowly. “And what did the line say he was called?”

“Vane.”

The fire took a stick.

“Then Vane is a name a man gives a clerk when he does not want the clerk to have his own,” my mother said. “And there is one man in this county who has been in the middle of this since before I was married, and he has been in the middle of it under two names, and one of them is on a line my sister sewed shut in 1799.”

✦

I read the last page at four in the afternoon.

I had gone through the whole book by then. Seventy-six lines. Two hundred and four names of people who had come to a house at Collow and asked a question that nobody else in the world could answer, and the answers are not prophecies. They are facts. They are things that had already happened and were hidden.

The last page has one line on it.

1806. April the ninth. Q: what becomes of it.

Underneath, there is nothing.

The paper is clean. The ink is Ellen’s and the line is the last thing in the book, and under it there is an inch and a half of blank paper and then the end board.

My mother sat with her hands in her lap and looked at it.

“She asked,” she said, “and she did not get an answer.”

“She got an answer. She did not write it.”

“How do you know.”

“Because she sewed the other one shut,” I said. “A woman who sews a thing shut is a woman who has decided not to look at it. And a woman who asks a question of the dead on the day the office is taken away and does not write the answer is a woman who has been told something she could not put on paper.”

✦

My father came in at five.

He had been in the yard all day and he came in with his coat on and stood in the doorway and looked at the book on the table and did not come any further.

“That is Ellen’s,” he said.

“It is.”

“I have not seen it since 1799.”

“Then you have seen it.”

He came in and sat down heavily.

“Once,” he said. “In this kitchen. In April of 1799. She brought it here and put it on that table and opened it and showed me the line, and asked me to read it, and I read it, and I told her it was a thing a man had written to frighten her, and I told her to sew it shut and not to speak of it.

“And she sewed it shut in front of me, with a needle out of that basket, and she did not say one word to me while she did it, and when she had finished she folded the paper flat with her thumbnail and closed the book and went out.

“And I have known for thirty-two years what is under those stitches and I have never once asked her, and I have never once told your mother.”

My mother did not look at him.

“You have told her now,” she said.

✦

I want to put down what happened next, because it is the only time in my life I have seen my father go down.

He did not cry. He sat in the chair with his hands on his knees and his shoulders went down about an inch and stayed down, and he said:

“There is a thing I have to say and I am going to say it once.

“In 1799 I signed a paper I did not read, and it put your mother in an office, and I have told myself for thirty-two years that I did it for eleven pounds four a year and a coal allowance and a child of four.

“That is not true. I did it because the man from Carrow told me the office was in my gift, and that a man who does not use a thing that is in his gift is not fit to have a gift.

“And I have been a clerk for thirty-one years and I have never once been offered a thing in my gift, and I took it in eleven minutes, and I did not read the bottom, and there is not a day since that I have not known it.”

He looked up.

“Now. There is a man at Ely who has been in the middle of it under two names, and I am the only person in this parish who has seen him twice. Once in 1799, when he came with the slip. And once in 1807, when he came back and stood in this kitchen and told me there was no instruction about this parish.

“He was a different man in 1807. He had a different coat and he had let his side-whiskers grow and he gave a different name, and I knew him the moment he came in the door, and he knew that I knew, and he stood there and looked at me and said:

“There is no instruction about this parish.

“And then he said: and there is no reason for anybody to be instructed.”

✦

Nobody said anything.

Then my mother stood up and went to the dresser and came back with the ink and a pen and put them on the table in front of me.

“Write the answer,” she said.

“Mother.”

“It is a page with a question on it and no answer, and there is one person in this county who can answer it, and she is sitting at this table.” My mother put the pen down. “You have been to a vestry and a barn in a fortnight and you have come home every time with a thing written on a page. Write the answer.”

I picked up the pen.

“What do I write?”

“Whatever is true,” my mother said. “That is what the book is for.”

✦

I sat with the pen in my hand for a long time.

Then I wrote, under the line, in the smallest hand I could make:

1831. March. Q: what became of it. A: it came back, and it is in a girl who has to be taught, and there is nobody left to teach her but a woman in a bed and a book.

I blotted it.

My father got up and put his hat on and went out to the yard.

My mother sat and looked at the line.

“That is not an answer,” she said.

“It is the true one.”

“It is not an answer to the question she asked.” My mother closed the book. “She asked what becomes of it. Not who holds it. What becomes of it.

“And you have written that it came back, and that there is nobody to teach you, which is a fact about you and not an answer about the thing.

“So you will have to find the answer, and you will have to find it before the twenty-ninth, because a board is not going to want to hear a girl say she does not know.”

✦

I sat in the kitchen after they had both gone up.

The fire was down and the lamp was low and the book was on the table with the pen still beside it, and the answer I had written was wet for about a minute and then it was dry and then it was only ink.

I thought about a hundred and twenty years of women making a book out of other people’s leavings. A woman in 1711 who wrote down that a man at a ford was not sorry. A woman in 1797 who told Sarah Dunnet that her sister was with the child. My grandmother writing for E., when she is able. Ellen sewing a line shut in a kitchen with a man watching her do it.

That is a line of women who kept a thing alive by not writing it down anywhere. And I had just written an answer in it, in ink, and blotted it, and it was the first time in a hundred and twenty years that anybody had put a thing in that book for a board to read.

I do not know whether that was the right thing to do.

I have never known.

19

His Hand

He came to the church on the Wednesday and he came at seven in the morning, which is the hour he always came, and I was already in the little room.

I had the book on the table in front of me with the boards closed and my hands flat on it, the way a person sits who has decided to wait.

He stopped in the doorway.

“That is not a parish book,” he said.

“No.”

“That is Ellen Rooke’s book.”

“Yes.”

He came in and put his hat on the chest and stood looking at it.

“I have heard about that book for twenty-five years,” he said. “I have heard about it from a man at Ely who called it a woman’s nonsense, and I have heard about it from a woman at Collow who called it nothing at all, and I have never once been in a room with it.”

“Read it.”

“I am not going to read it standing up in a coat.”

He took his coat off and hung it on the back of the chair and turned his sleeves back twice and sat down, and I opened the book at 1711 and pushed it across to him.

✦

He read for an hour and a half.

I did not talk. That is the thing about him I have never been able to explain to anybody: he reads a page the way a man drinks water after a day in the field, which is without any pleasure in his face at all.

He read with one finger moving under the line, which is a thing clerks do and which I have never seen a gentleman do. Twice he stopped and went back a line. Once he laughed, at the entry for 1764, and it was a short laugh and it was over before it started, and I have thought about it since.

At the line for 1797 he stopped.

“Sarah Dunnet,” he said.

“You know her.”

“She died in 1822 and she was in the eleventh class for the last eleven years of her life and I signed her burial entry.” He turned the page. “She is in this book.”

“Everybody in this parish is in this book.”

“No,” he said. “Not everybody. Two hundred and four people in a hundred and twenty years, in four parishes, and one of them is a woman I put in the ground nine years ago, and there is not one word in this parish about any of it.”

✦

He read to the end and then he turned back and read the sewn leaf and then he read the last page.

He sat looking at the blank half-inch under Ellen’s question for a long time.

“She did not write the answer,” he said.

“She got one.”

“You think so.”

“I think a woman who asks a question of the dead on the morning the office is taken out of the book and then sews a different line shut is a woman who was told something she could not write down.”

Rooke put his finger on the line and then took it off.

“What did you write?”

I turned the book round and showed him.

He read it twice.

“It came back,” he said, “and it is in a girl who has to be taught, and there is nobody left to teach her but a woman in a bed and a book.” He sat back. “That is not an answer to a board.”

“I know.”

“It is better than one,” he said. “A board would not understand it and it would go into the minutes and in a hundred years it would read as a girl being modest. But it is the true thing and it is the only true thing that has been written in that book since 1806.”

✦

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Mm.”

“Read the sewn line again.”

He did.

“Vane,” he said.

“Vane.”

“Who came to this parish in 1799 with a slip in a leather case and a manner that made people agree with him.” He closed the book. “And I have been looking for a Vane for a month and there is no Vane in the registry and there is no Vane in the commission’s list and there is no Vane in the poll book of this county for 1806 or 1810 or 1818.

“Now I know why. Vane is not a man. It is a name a man used, and it is the name he used when he came to this parish in 1799, and it is the name he used when he came back in 1807 with side-whiskers and a different coat.”

“Then who is he.”

Rooke looked at me.

“You know who he is,” he said.

“Say it.”

“Ambrose Quill.”

✦

The fire took a stick.

“He was twenty-six in 1806 and he was the clerk to the commission,” Rooke said. “In 1799 he was nineteen and he was not yet in the commission, and he was a clerk in the registry at Carrow with a bad salary and a good coat.

“And in 1799 somebody sent a young clerk from Carrow into a parish with a slip of paper and a leather case, and that clerk found a woman with a child of four and a husband with a bad chest, and he told her a lie in a kitchen in the evening, and then he went away and let the paper do its work for seven years.

“And then in 1806 he was the clerk to the commission that abolished the thing he had parked. And he wrote in a letter that two pages were wanting.”

“That is the whole of it.”

“That is the whole of it,” Rooke said, “and it has taken him thirty-two years and a great deal of paper to do a thing that could have been done in an afternoon by a man who was willing to say what he was doing.”

✦

I stood up and went to the window.

The crooked light was on the floor in the shape of a broken kite, and there was a blackbird on the wall outside doing the thing blackbirds do in spring.

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“There is a thing I have been wanting to ask you for a fortnight and I have not asked it, because it is not a question a person asks a man from Carrow.”

“Ask it.”

I turned round.

“Take your glove off.”

He did not move.

“I beg your pardon.”

“Your glove. Take it off and put it on the table.”

He looked at me for four seconds, and then he took his right glove off and put it on the table, and put both hands in his lap.

The glove was grey wool, and the fingers were worn through at the tips, and the leather across the palm had been patched with a piece of black cloth, and the stitching on the patch was very fine.

“That is a good repair,” I said.

“My wife did it.”

“You are married.”

“I was married,” Rooke said. “She died in 1826. There was no child.”

✦

I sat down and looked at the glove.

“I am going to put my hand on it,” I said, “and I am going to be away for three seconds, and when I come back I am going to know a thing about you that you have not told me, and you will not be able to take it back.

“So I am asking first.”

Rooke sat with his hands in his lap.

“Ask,” he said.

✦

I put my hand on the glove.

Three seconds.

✦

A room with a high window and a table too big for it, and a boy at the table with his sleeve on the paper to keep the ink off it. He is nineteen. His coat is too big at the shoulders because it was his father’s and it has been turned.

The paper in front of him is a schedule and it has four columns and the fourth column says remarks, and the remarks are in a hand he cannot read, and he has been copying for four days.

He is not writing. He is sitting with the pen in his hand and his eyes shut, and he is doing the thing a boy does when he has been given a task he cannot do and has decided to do it anyway. He is counting. He has counted the lines. There are four hundred and eleven.

Then he opens his eyes and writes.

✦

The room came back.

I had my hand on a grey wool glove and there was a fire and a crooked window and a man of forty-four sitting across the table with his hands in his lap.

I took my hand off.

“You counted,” I said.

Rooke did not move.

“Four hundred and eleven lines,” I said. “You counted them before you copied them. You were nineteen and your coat was your father’s and it had been turned, and you sat with your eyes shut and counted the lines, and then you opened your eyes and wrote.”

“Say the rest,” he said.

“There is no rest.”

“There is always a rest.”

“Not this time.” I put my hands in my lap. “You did not know what the words meant. That is the whole of what I saw. A boy counting lines and then writing them, because it was the only thing he could do.”

✦

Rooke sat looking at the table for a long time.

Then he said, “I am going to tell you what I did in 1806 and I have never told it to a living person, and I am going to tell it now because you have had your hand on my glove and I would rather you had it from me.

“When the schedule was sent to the commission there was a covering note with it, written by the senior clerk: the schedule is returned complete and the offices therein have been examined.

“And the offices therein had not been examined. Not one of them. The examining consisted of a man reading the list and saying, that one sounds like a formality.

“And I was nineteen and I knew it, and I did not say one word, because I had been in that building for four days and I wanted to keep my chair.”

✦

He had not finished and I knew it.

“You have kept your chair for twenty-five years,” I said.

“I have kept my chair for twenty-five years.”

“And now you are going to lose it.”

“And now I am going to lose it,” Rooke said. “And I find that the thing I am afraid of is not losing it. The thing I am afraid of is that I will stand up at that board and say the words and they will not believe me, and I will have lost the chair for nothing, and the office will still be out of the book, and I will have to go home and sit in a room in Carrow and know that I did the right thing and it did not work.”

✦

I got up and went round the table and stood beside him.

I want to put down what I did next, because I have gone over it a great many times.

I put my hand on his hand.

Not on a glove. On his hand, on the back of it, on the place where the veins are, and it was warm and it was dry and it was the first time in my life I had touched a man who was not my father.

I did not read it. I want that understood. I did not go away for three seconds. I did not see anything. I put my hand on the back of a man’s hand because it was there, and because he had told me the worst thing he had ever done and had not asked me for anything.

He did not move his hand. He sat completely still, and he looked at our hands on the table, and he said:

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mercy.”

He looked up.

“Mercy,” he said.

And then he took his hand away and put it in his lap, and he did it slowly, the way a man takes his hand away from a stove that he has found to be hot.

“That is not a thing I can have,” he said.

“Why not.”

“Because I am the man who is going to stand up at that board and swear to a thing about your mother,” he said, “and if there is a thing between us, then everything I say will be a man saying a thing about a woman he wants, and there is not a board in England that will not see it.

“And because you are eighteen and I am forty-four and your mother has ten months, and a man in my position who takes a girl’s hand in a cold room in March is a man who has taken an advantage, whatever he tells himself afterwards.”

✦

I sat down in my chair.

“Then I will ask you again on the twenty-ninth,” I said.

“On the twenty-ninth I will be an auditor giving evidence.”

“On the twenty-ninth,” I said, “you will be a man who has lost his chair. And I will ask you again after.”

Rooke looked at me.

There was a thing in his face that I could not name then and can name now, and the name is not desire and it is not fear. It is the face of a man who has been alone for five years and has arranged his life so carefully that nothing can get into it, and who has just watched something get in.

“You have an answer for everything,” he said.

“I am sure of the page.”

“That is not the same thing.”

“No,” I said. “But it is the thing I have got.”

✦

He put his glove on.

“The board sits on the twenty-ninth,” he said. “That is four days.

“On the twenty-seventh I am going to Carrow to lodge the papers, and on the twenty-eighth I am going to Ely to bring Quill, because a board will not accept a letter from a man who is not there, and because if he is not in the room he will write to the board on the thirtieth and undo the whole of it.

“And on the twenty-ninth, at ten in the morning, I am going to stand up in front of eleven parishes and read out a page, and then another page, and then I am going to stop.”

“That is what you told me to do.”

“It is what I am going to do,” he said. “It is easier to tell a person than to do it.”

✦

He went to the door.

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Mm.”

“There is one thing I have not told you and I am going to tell you now, because you are going to Ely on the twenty-eighth and you will be in a room with that man.”

He turned round.

“Say it.”

“Vane is a name he used in this parish in 1799,” I said. “And I have been thinking about why a man uses a name. He does not use it to hide from the people he lies to, because they never see him again. He uses it to hide from the people he works with.”

Rooke stood very still.

“So when you are in that room,” I said, “and he tells you he has never been to this parish in his life, you will know that he is not lying to you.

“He is lying to the man he was in 1799. And that is a different thing, and it is a thing you can use, because a man who is lying to himself will make a mistake, and the mistake will be that he will be kind to you, because he taught you the trade and he remembers a boy in his father’s coat.”

✦

Rooke put his hat on.

“Mercy,” he said.

“Mr. Rooke.”

“If I come back from Ely on the twenty-eighth,” he said, “I will come to this room at seven in the morning and I will tell you what he said, and I will not tell you the parts that are not fit to be told, and you will not ask me for them.

“And if I do not come back, it will be because he has offered me the chair.”

He went out.

I sat in the little room with the book and the fire and the crooked light, and outside on the wall the blackbird had stopped.

I did not move for a while.

There was a place on the back of my hand where his hand had been, and it was not warm and it was not cold, and I sat looking at it as though a mark might come up on it.

Then I put both hands flat on the book and did the thing I had been avoiding since the bridge. Not the book. The room. The table, the chair he had sat in, the place on the floor where the crooked light fell.

Three seconds. Two men at that table, one in a good coat and one in a work coat, and the one in the good coat had his hand flat on the book and was talking, and the other one was listening and writing nothing down.

I took my hand off.

That was not a thing I had any right to. I have never told my father.

20

The Ring

It rained on the Friday and it rained on the Saturday, and on the Sunday it stopped and the wind came round and the roads were so bad that the carrier would not go to Collow, so I walked.

Twelve miles in the wind on a road that had been a river the day before. I want to put that down, because I have been told since that I was carried to Collow in a cart by a man who was in love with me, and it is not true, and a thing that is not true in a story will be believed in a hundred years and I would rather it were not.

Mr. Rooke walked the first three miles with me.

That is where the story comes from and I may as well say so. Somebody on the top road saw us go up together and by the Monday it was a cart, and a cart is a better story than a walk, and it will still be a cart in a hundred years.

He was at the gate at six with his hat pulled down and no gloves on, and he said, “I am going to walk as far as the top.”

“You are not.”

“I am going to walk as far as the top and come back down. I am not going to discuss it. I am not going to carry anything for you, and I am not going to offer you a cart, because I do not have one and because you would refuse it.”

“I would refuse it.”

“I know.”

So we went up.

The first mile neither of us said anything, and that is a thing I did not expect. Two people who have only ever talked across a table do not know what to do on a road. There is no book to look at. There is no page to put a finger on. There is a hedge going past and a person beside you with a wet sleeve, and you cannot pretend that you are working.

At the second mile he said, “Ask me.”

“Not yet.”

“In March I told you to ask me on the twenty-ninth.”

“It is not the twenty-ninth.”

“Ask me on any other day and I will tell you that I have not decided, and you will know that it is a lie, and then we will both know that we know, and that is a worse position than the one we are in now.”

I said, “That is the most you have ever said about it.”

“It is the most there is.”

At the top he stopped. From there you can see the tower at Carrow and, on a clear day, the water beyond it. It was not a clear day.

“You have eleven miles,” he said. “The wind is behind you for six of them and against you for five. It will be dark at seven and you want to be off the top before then.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“Why are you here.”

He looked at the road going down into Collow. His coat was wet on the shoulder where the rain had come back the second time.

“If you break your leg on that road there is nobody on it until Wendon,” he said. “That is the reason and I know that it is not a good one.”

Then he turned round and went back down the way we had come, and I stood and watched him for longer than I needed to, and I have never told anybody that either.

The road to Collow goes up and then along the top and then down, and the rain came back twice on the way, and I got there at two.

My boots were finished. Mrs. Trewin took them away and put them by the fire without saying anything, and brought me a pair of her husband’s, which were too big, and a basin of water, and I sat in the passage with my feet in the basin and my skirt steaming, and heard Ellen say, through the door, “Is she drowned?”

“She is wet.”

“Then she is not drowned. Bring her in.”

✦

There is a path round the house and it is paved with bricks laid on edge, and the bricks have been there a long time and the moss has come up between them, and somebody has been scraping the moss out with a knife and has got about a third of the way round.

She was in the chair by the fire.

That was the first time I had seen her out of the bed, and it was worse. In the bed she was a woman who had been ill. In the chair she was a woman who had been carried, and a woman who has been carried has a look about the shoulders.

“Come in and sit down and do not say anything about the chair,” she said. “I have been in it since Thursday and I am going to be in it until the twenty-eighth, because if I am to be in a cart on the twenty-ninth I will not be in a bed on the twenty-eighth.

“Now. You have read the book.”

“I have read the book.”

“All of it.”

“Seventy-six lines. Two hundred and four names. Four hands and a stitch.”

Ellen sat back.

“Then you know more about my family than I do,” she said, “because I have never read the whole of it. I have read the part that is mine and the part that is my mother’s, and I have not once read 1711 to 1753, because a woman who is going to be the letter does not want to know what the letters before her were afraid of.”

✦

Mrs. Trewin brought tea and went out and shut the door.

There was a square of sun on the floor by the bed that had come through the horn pane in the casement, and it sat there the whole visit and never moved onto the coverlet, and I watched it instead of crying, which is a use for a square of sun.

Ellen put her cup down.

“Give me your hand,” she said.

I gave her my hand.

She turned it over and looked at the palm and then at the fingers, and then she let it go.

“Nothing,” she said. “That is what I wanted to know. There is a line in a hand that does a thing for a long time, and you have not got it yet, and you will get it, and when you get it you will be able to see it on yourself and you will not like it.”

“What line?”

“Here.” She put her thumb across my palm below the little finger. “It comes from holding a thing that does not want to be held. My mother had it. I have it. You have not.”

✦

She reached into the pocket of her skirt and took out a thing and put it on the arm of the chair.

It was a ring. Iron, thick, black with age, and too big for any finger I have ever seen, and it had a line of cord wound round the back of it to make it sit.

“That is not my husband’s,” she said. “I want that understood before you touch it, because everybody in this parish has decided for twenty-five years that I am a widow with a ring, and I am a widow and I have a ring, and they are two different things.

“My husband’s ring is in the box with his razor and I have not looked at it since 1809.

“That is the ring of the office. It was made in 1711 when the Act was passed, and it was made for a hand that was not a woman’s, because the man who paid for it did not know who was going to hold the office, and it has been too big for four women in a hundred and twenty years.

“Put it on.”

✦

I put it on.

It went over the thumb and sat there, loose, and I had to hold my hand flat to keep it from falling.

“Read it,” Ellen said. “And not with your hand. Look inside.”

I turned it to the light.

Inside the band, cut deep and worn at the edges but not worn away, there are six words.

ASK, AND BE ANSWERED. AND THEN BE QUIET.

✦

“Be quiet,” I said.

“That is the part everybody forgets,” Ellen said. “There are four words in the first half and they are the ones in the book, and there are four more in the second half and they are the ones that keep a woman alive.

“Ask, and be answered, and then be quiet. Not because it is a secret. Because a person who goes about telling what the dead said is a person who will be told to stop, and a person who is told to stop by a parish is a person who stops being able to do it.

“That is a thing I know and my mother knew and her mother knew, and it is not written anywhere except inside a ring that nobody has read since 1809.”

✦

I took it off and held it in my palm.

“May I,” I said.

“May you what.”

“Read it.”

Ellen looked at me for a while.

“There are two ways to read a thing,” she said. “There is the way you have been doing, which is to put your hand on it and go away for three seconds and come back with a picture. And there is the other way, which is to ask it a question first.

“Ask it a question.”

“What question.”

“That is for you to decide,” Ellen said, “and it is the whole of the difference between a woman who is a letter and a girl who can read a cup. A cup tells you what it wants to tell you. A ring will answer a question, and if you have not got a question, do not put your hand on it, because it will answer the question you are carrying and you will not have chosen it.”

✦

I sat with the ring in my palm for a long time.

Then I said, “Who wore it last before you.”

I closed my hand on it.

Three seconds.

✦

A room with a low ceiling and a fire and a table and a woman at the table with her sleeves rolled, and she is not young and she is not old and she is not anybody I know. She has the ring on her thumb. There is a basin on the table and a cloth and a man’s coat over the back of a chair, and she is not working and she is not waiting. She is sitting with her hands in her lap looking at the fire, and she is doing the thing people do when they have finished a day and there is nothing left in it.

Then she takes the ring off and puts it on the table and goes to the door, and the door opens onto a yard with washing in it, and there is a child in the yard, and the child is a girl of about six, and the woman says a thing I cannot hear and the girl laughs.

✦

The room came back.

I had the ring in my fist and my fist against my chest and my heart going.

“Who was that,” I said.

“What did you see.”

“A woman at a table with her sleeves rolled and a basin, and she took the ring off and went out to a child in a yard, and the child laughed.”

Ellen sat very still.

“My mother,” she said. “In 1788. That is the year she had the office, and I was five and my brother was three and the brother died the year after.

“And you asked who wore it last before me, and it gave you my mother, and it gave you a thing that had nothing to do with the office at all.”

“It gave me a day.”

“It gave you a day,” Ellen said. “That is what the ring does. It does not give you the history of the office. It gives you a day of the woman who wore it, and the day it chooses is not the day you would choose, and that is the whole of it, and I have never understood it and neither will you.”

✦

I sat for a while with the ring in my hand.

There is a thing that happens when you read a thing that has been read before. You get the day, and the day is somebody else’s, and then it is yours, and you cannot give it back. I have four of them now. A man signing a thing he did not agree with. A woman walking a road in the dark and singing. A girl waiting at a table with a ring on her thumb. A boy counting four hundred and eleven lines with his eyes shut.

None of them are mine and all of them are in me, and there is no arrangement of words that makes that a comfortable thing.

She held out her hand.

I gave her the ring.

She put it on her thumb and held it up and looked at it.

“Now,” she said. “You have read the ring and you have read the book and you have been to Carrow and Ely and come back, and there is one thing left in this house that I have not given you, and I am going to give it to you now, and then I am going to sleep for an hour, because I am fifty and I have been up since five.

“It is the answer.”

✦

I sat forward.

“On the ninth of April, 1806,” Ellen said, “the commission sat at Ely and the schedule was read and the office was put in the column for offices that are superfluous. I know the date because it is in my book and because it is the date on which I stopped being the letter.

“I had known it was coming for six months. There had been a letter in the October and a man had come in the February, and they had asked me questions and written down the answers, and not one of the questions was about the office. They asked how many rooms there were and what the land was worth.

“And on the ninth of April I went into the room where my mother died, and I put my hand on the bed, and I asked a question.”

She looked at the fire.

“I have not told this to a living person. My sister knows that I asked and does not know what I was told, and she has never asked me, and I have been grateful for that every Tuesday for twenty-five years, and I am not grateful for it now.

“I asked: what becomes of it.

“And the answer was: it comes back in the third generation, and it is a girl, and the water does not take her.”

✦

The fire took a stick.

“Third generation,” I said.

“My mother was the letter. I was the letter. You are the third.”

“Then I am the third generation.”

“You are the third generation,” Ellen said, “and you are a girl, and the first two parts of it are true, and I have been sitting in this chair since Thursday thinking about the third part, and I do not know what it means, and I am not going to pretend that I do.

“But I know this. There is a thing in this county that a girl can be taken by, and it is not water. It is a board. It is a room with eleven men in it and a clerk with a pen, and it takes a girl and it writes her down as a woman with a fancy, and after that she is not a woman any more, she is a note in a margin.

“So when you stand up in that room on the twenty-ninth, remember that a bed at Collow told me in 1806 that the water does not take you. And ask yourself what the water is.”

✦

I did not answer straight away.

“Mrs. Trewin says you have not been out of the gate since Michaelmas,” I said.

“Mrs. Trewin says a great many things.”

“Are you able to do it? The cart, the nineteen miles, the room.”

Ellen looked at me.

“No,” she said. “I am not able. I will be able on the twenty-ninth for about four hours and then I will not be, and I have arranged with Mrs. Trewin that if I am not able before four o’clock she is to say that I am tired and take me out, and nobody in that room will argue with a woman of fifty being tired.

“That is what I have got. Four hours. I have been saving them since Thursday.”

✦

She closed her eyes.

“Now go home,” she said, “and come back on the twenty-eighth with the cart, and bring your mother, because she is not going to want to come and she is going to come.”

“Why is she going to come.”

“Because I am going to send her a note,” Ellen said, “and it is going to say four words, and I have been keeping those four words for twenty-five years, and I am not going to tell you what they are.

“Go home. My boots are by the door. Take them. Yours are finished and mine are better and I am not going to need them again before the twenty-eighth.”

I got the boots and put them on.

They fitted.

✦

I went out through the garden and stopped at the gate and looked back at the house.

The tarpaulin was still on the north end of the roof. The daffodils were nearly over. Mrs. Trewin was in the garden with a basket, doing something to the pear tree with a knife, and she had her sleeves rolled and her arms were the arms of a woman who carries things.

I walked back the twelve miles in Ellen’s boots, and it took me four hours, and I was home by eight, and my mother was at the window.

“She has given you her boots,” she said.

“She has.”

My mother stood in the doorway and looked at them for a long time.

“Then she is coming,” she said, and went in and put the kettle on, and did not say another word about it.

✦

I sat up that night with the book open at the last page and the pen in my hand and I did not write anything.

Downstairs my mother was moving about the kitchen, which she does not do at night, and once she stopped and I heard her say something to my father that I could not make out, and he said something back, and then the cupboard opened and shut.

At eleven the clock struck, and then struck again four minutes later.

I did not write anything. The page is still half blank and the answer I put on it in March is still the only answer on it, and I have never been able to think of a better one.

21

Idle

Rooke went to Carrow on the twenty-fifth and did not come back on the twenty-fifth, and on the twenty-sixth he was in Ely, and on the twenty-sixth at seven in the morning I was in the little room with the door open, waiting, and he did not come.

I want to put down that I was not afraid. That would be a lie and I have been trying not to tell them.

I was afraid for about an hour, and in that hour I swept the hearth and put coal on the fire and moved the second chair to the window and moved it back. I opened the window and shut it. I read the first page of the ninety-one book four times and could not have told anybody what was on it.

At half past eight I stopped, because there is a limit to what a woman can do with a room before she is doing it for the sake of the doing, and I have a horror of that. My mother does it. She has been doing it since November. She moves a thing from one end of a shelf to the other and back and calls it keeping house.

So I got the office book down off the shelf and put it on the table and began to work, because a woman who is afraid and has nothing to do will make herself worse, and a woman who is afraid and has a book in front of her will make herself useful.

✦

The office book is not a parish book.

It is a thin book, bound in green cloth that has gone brown at the spine, and it has a ribbon in it that is not a marker, it is the ribbon off a hat. The leaves are ruled and the entries are in four hands, and the fourth is mine.

It lists every office in the parish, what it is worth, who holds it, and what the holder is required to do. It has been kept since 1794 and there are nine offices in it.

Nine offices and fourteen duties.

That is the thing about a parish. The offices were made in a century when a parish needed a man to do a thing, and the thing was written down, and the office was given a piece of land or a share of the tithe to pay for it, and then the century changed and the land did not.

I had copied that book four times. I had never once read it as a list of work.

✦

The office of the clerk. Worth: eleven pounds four and a house. Duty: to keep the books of the parish, to make the fair copy, to attend the vestry and the board, and to enter all births, deaths, and offices.

That is my father.

The office of the sexton. Worth: four pounds. Duty: to dig graves, to keep the churchyard, and to ring the bell at eight, at noon, and at eight.

That is Dunnet’s father, and Dunnet after him, and Dunnet is dead and the office has not been filled since 1829 and the four pounds has been paid every year to a woman in the lane who is Dunnet’s widow and who does not dig graves.

The office of the parish pump. Worth: two pounds. Duty: to keep the pump in order and the well covered.

That is Hobbs, who is a smith and does it and does not need the two pounds and takes it anyway.

I put my pencil down.

✦

The office of the bell.

That is a different thing from the sexton. The sexton rings the bell for the services. The office of the bell is a separate office, created in 1701, worth six pounds a year, and the duty is: to ring the great bell at the hour of death of any person in this parish, and to ring it again at the hour of the burial.

Six pounds a year.

Holder: Craddock, William, by grant of 1812, on the death of Craddock, Thomas, his father.

Remarks: Idle.

✦

I sat and looked at that word for a long time.

Idle is not a thing the book says. The book has a column for remarks and the column is mostly empty, and where it is not empty it says things like granted, or by purchase, or see the return of 1818.

Idle is a word somebody wrote in 1819 and nobody has written anything after it.

I got up and went out into the churchyard.

✦

The churchyard is on the south side and it has a yew in it that is older than the church, and the yew is where the men stand after a funeral, and there is a bench under it that was put there in 1804 and has been mended twice.

Craddock was sitting on the bench.

He had his hat off and his hands on his knees and he was looking at a grave about eleven feet away, and I knew whose grave it was before I got close, because there is a stone with a bell cut on it.

He had been sitting there long enough for the cold to have got into him. There was a leaf on his shoulder and he had not brushed it off, which is a thing I had never seen on him in nine years. He brushes things off. He is a man who squares his gloves on a chest.

“Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Craddock.”

“Sit down.”

I sat down.

We sat there for a while. The yew makes a noise in the wind that is not like any other tree, and there were rooks in it, and one of them said something and the others agreed.

✦

“You have found the office of the bell,” he said.

“I have found the word Idle.”

“Then you have found the office.” He did not look at me. “My father had it for thirty-one years and he rang that bell for every death in this parish, and he rang it at eight in the evening and he rang it again at the burial, and he did it in all weathers, and he did it for six pounds a year and a bad back.

“And in 1812 he died and the office came to me by grant, because the vestry gave it to me, and I was twenty-six, and I did not ring it.”

“Why not.”
