“Because I was the overseer of the poor by then and an overseer does not ring a bell.” He turned his hat over in his hands. “And because I did not want to. That is the whole of it. There is no better reason and I have looked for one for nineteen years.

“So the bell was rung by Dunnet, who was sexton, and Dunnet did not ask for the money and I did not offer it, and for nineteen years the six pounds has come to me every Lady Day out of the parish accounts, and for nineteen years the bell has been rung by a man who was not the holder of the office, and in 1819 somebody wrote Idle in the remark column and nobody has asked me about it since.”

✦

I took my time about it.

“Six pounds a year for nineteen years,” I said.

“A hundred and fourteen pounds.”

“That is a house.”

“That is a house,” Craddock said, “and there is not a man in this parish who would not say that I have done more for it than a hundred and fourteen pounds’ worth. I have been overseer nine years and I have given away my own coal every winter and I have sat on that vestry and I have kept the rates down and there are eleven families in this parish today who are not in the workhouse because of a thing I did in a room with nobody watching.

“And none of that is the point. I know that. I have known it since 1819.”

✦

“Why are you telling me,” I said.

“Because you are going to Carrow on the twenty-ninth,” Craddock said, “and you are going to stand up in front of eleven parishes with a book and a witness and a man from Ely, and you are going to pull a thread out of this parish.

“And when you pull it, the whole of the vestry is going to come with it, and one of the things that will come is a word written in a remark column in 1819 in my own hand, next to my own name, in a book that I have signed every year.

“So I am going to stand up before you do.”

✦

I turned and looked at him.

“You are going to what.”

“I am going to stand up at that board and I am going to say what I did.” He put his hat on his knee. “Not because I have found God in a churchyard. I have not. Because there is a difference between a man who is found out and a man who says it, and I have been nine years on a book that does not add up and I have been the only man in this parish asking questions about it, and it will be a very pretty thing for the board to discover that the man asking the questions was eating six pounds a year out of the same column.

“So I am going to say it first, and then I am going to say the rest, and then I am going to sit down, and after that there is nothing that anybody in that room can do to me that I have not already done to myself.”

✦

I did not help him.

“Mr. Craddock.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“You have been against me since the seventh of March.”

“I have been against your copy,” Craddock said. “I have never been against you and I want that put down, because in nine years nobody has given me credit for a distinction and I am not going to get any at Carrow.

“Your copy was not fair. It is still not fair. A girl wrote a name in a column before the vacancy was declared and the fact that she was right to do it does not make the copy fair, and if you go into that board room and say the copy was fair you will lose, because eleven parishes will hear a girl saying that a rule does not apply to her.

“What you say is: the copy was not fair, and the rule that made it not fair was made in 1794 to cover a fraud, and I am not asking the board to excuse a bad copy, I am asking it to remove the reason the copy was bad.

“That is the difference between a girl with a grievance and a clerk with a case. And if you do not know the difference by the twenty-ninth, then everything your mother has done and everything that woman at Collow is going to do in a cart will be for nothing.”

✦

The rooks went up out of the yew all at once, for no reason, the way they do.

“There is one more thing,” Craddock said, “and then I will go and see to the grave.

“The board at Carrow is not a court. It is eleven parishes and a chairman and a clerk, and it sits four times a year, and it decides things by a show of hands. It is not going to want to hear about an office that was abolished in 1806, because every man in that room has an office in his own parish that was abolished in 1806 and was never told, and if it can happen at Collow it can happen at Wexbridge, and there is not one of them who wants to go home and look.

“So they will try to make it about you. Whether you are a fit person. Whether your hand is a hand. Whether the thing you do is a thing that a board can take notice of.

“And the only way to beat that is to make it about a number. Not a principle. A number. Eleven parishes can understand a number and no board in England has ever understood a principle.”

“Which number.”

“Six hundred and fifty-nine pounds,” Craddock said. “It is in the book. It is in eleven books. Any man in that room can add it up and get the same answer, and there is not one of them who will be able to say that a girl with a thimble made him do it.”

✦

He stood up and put his hat on.

“Mr. Craddock.”

“Mm.”

“Why did you write Idle.”

He stood there with his back to me.

“Because I wanted somebody to ask,” he said. “Nineteen years. Every year I have signed that book and every year I have come to that page and seen what I wrote in 1819, and I have waited for a man to look at it and say: what is this?

“Not one has. Not the rector, not the chairman, not the auditor, not the board at Carrow. Nineteen years and eleven signatures a year and nobody has ever read the remark column of a parish office book.

“And then a girl of eighteen reads it in an hour.”

He went off down the path between the graves with his hat in his hand.

✦

I sat on the bench under the yew for a while.

Then I went back into the little room and opened the office book at the page and looked at the word again.

Idle.

It is a good word. It is the best word in the book, and it has been sitting there in the remark column since 1819, and there is not one man in this parish who has read it, and it was written by the only honest man on the vestry, and he wrote it himself, about himself, and then waited nineteen years for somebody to notice.

I got my pencil out and wrote in the margin, in the smallest hand I could make:

Read. 26 March, 1831.

And then, under that, because I could not help it:

Not idle. Nobody rang it.

✦

Mr. Rooke came back at nine that night.

I heard him in the lane first, because a man walking on a road in the dark makes a different noise from a man walking on a road in daylight, and I had been listening for it since seven.

He had come from the turnpike on foot because the carrier would not take him, and he was wet to the knee and he had not been to bed, and he sat down in the second chair, the one he had brought into the little room in March, and he did not take his coat off.

He took the papers out of it first. The file from Ely came out from inside his shirt, where it had been carried against his skin, and it was dry, every page of it, while the man himself was wet through and his lips were grey with cold. The dip past the ford was under water and the low road with it, and he had walked it anyway with the bundle held up over his head in the wind, because the dry road round by the ridge was an hour longer and he had decided that the hour belonged to the paper.

“Paper does not mend,” he said, when he saw me looking at the file. “Cold does.”

I got him tea. He held the cup with both hands and did not drink it.

“You have been to Ely,” I said.

“Yesterday. And Carrow the day before. And I have not slept since Friday and I am not going to tell you that I am well, because I am not, and I am not going to tell you that it was worth it, because I do not know yet.”

The parish book was open on the table with the pencil lying in it. He looked down at the page and saw what I had written in the margin.

Not idle. Nobody rang it.

He read it twice.

“You have written in a parish book,” he said.

“In the margin.”

“In a parish book.”

“Somebody has to.”

He put the cup down.

“There are eleven parishes in this board,” he said. “I have looked at the remark column of nine of them and there are thirty-one offices marked idle, and I am going to tell you now that I do not know what to do with that, and that it is the first thing in twenty-five years that I have not known what to do with.”

He sat with his head back against the chair and shut his eyes.

I could have read him. I know that I could have. Three seconds and I would have had Ely in it and the box and whatever he had been told in that building, and I would not have had to sit there not knowing.

I put my hand on the sleeve of his coat instead. It was wet.

“Go to bed,” I said.

“I have to be at Carrow by eight.”

“Then go to bed and I will wake you.”

He opened his eyes.

“You are not going to read me,” he said.

“No.”

“Because of the rule.”

“Because of the rule,” I said. “And because I would rather hear it from you.”

He looked at me for a while, and then he stood up and put his coat back on properly, which is the sort of thing he does when he has decided something, and he said, “Tomorrow,” and went out into the lane, and I heard him all the way to the turn.

✦

I went home at noon and my mother had the cart arranged.

She had been at it since seven. There was a basket with a cloth over it and a stone bottle and four blankets, and she had been to the inn and spoken to the man with the cart, and she had been to the church and got the cushion off the rector’s chair, which is the softest cushion in this parish, and she had not asked for it, she had taken it.

“Mother.”

“It is for Ellen.”

“The rector will miss it.”

“The rector will sit on a board for three hours and not know what he is sitting on,” my mother said. “Ellen will sit on a board for four hours and it will be the difference between her being able to speak and not.”

She put the cushion in the basket and put the cloth over it.

“There is a thing I have not told you,” she said, “and I am going to tell you now, because after tomorrow there will be no time.

“When your father signed that paper in 1799 I was in the room. I stood at the dresser with my back to him and I watched him in the glass. He had the pen in his hand for a long time before he wrote, and he looked at the bottom of the paper, and he looked at it for so long that I thought he was going to read it.

“And then he wrote.”

I stood in the kitchen with my hands at my sides.

“You watched him,” I said.

“I watched him in the glass and I did not say one word, and I could have, and it would have taken four words.” My mother folded the cloth. “So when you stand up in that room tomorrow and they ask you who knew, you will say my mother knew.

“Not because it is true in law. Because it is true.”

22

What He Would Do

My father was in the kitchen when I came down at nine with a lamp turned up and the fire built to a height it had not been built to since Christmas.

There was a pot on the table and a bowl and a spoon and a jug, and there was a paper on the table in front of him with a stone on it, and the paper was covered in figures.

He had put on his second-best shirt for this. That is a thing I noticed and did not say. There was no meeting, there was nobody in the house but the two of us, and he had put on his second-best shirt and brushed his hair.

The broth had barley in it and a piece of carrot, and it was the first hot thing I had eaten at that table since November that had not been made out of a shilling.

“Sit down,” he said.

“Father.”

“Sit down and eat that, because you will not eat tomorrow and I know what a person is like at a board on an empty stomach, and it is not a good thing to be.”

I sat down and ate it.

It was a mutton broth and there was more meat in it than there had been in that house since February, and I did not ask where it came from.

✦

When I had finished he took the stone off the paper and turned it round.

It was a sheet of foolscap, ruled, and the ruling was his, done in pencil with a rule. There were four columns and eleven rows, and every row had a year in it and a figure and a word.

“That is not a parish paper,” I said.

“No.”

“That is not in the chest.”

“No,” my father said. “It has been under the drawer in the chest since 1817, and before that it was in the lining of my coat, and before that it was in my head, and it has been in my head since 1799 and there is a limit to what a man can keep in his head before it begins to change the shape of the rest of him.”

The paper was good paper. It had been folded twice and the folds were worn and there was a mark on the back where a cup had stood, and the pencil ruling had been done with a rule, and the rule had slipped at the bottom of the third column.

His hands were on the table and they were the hands of a man of sixty-one who has held a pen for thirty-one years: the middle finger bent at the top joint from pressure, the nails cut square and short, the skin over the back of them going thin the way it does.

✦

I read it.

1799. 104 0 0. Received. Applied: 11 4 0 office. 92 16 0 carried.

1800. 104 0 0. Received. Applied: 11 4 0 office. 41 0 0 bridge. 51 16 0 carried.

1801. 104 0 0. Received. Applied: 11 4 0 office. 18 0 0 school slates. 74 16 0 carried.

Down to 1817, where the figures change, and then to 1830.

At the bottom, a total, and beside it a note in his hand: carried 659 0 0. unaccounted: nothing. Every penny is in this paper and no penny of it is in the book.

Except that it is not quite every penny, and he told me so before I could ask.

“There is one pound twelve shillings,” he said, “that is neither in that paper nor in the book. It went in 1824 to the burial of a child at Marle whose father had nothing, and I put it in the plate on the Sunday and did not write it anywhere, and I have never written it down until tonight.

“That is the whole of the discrepancy and you may check the arithmetic, and you will find that it comes out to a pound twelve, and I would rather you found it yourself than that I told you, but I am sixty-one and I have stopped being able to wait for people to find things.”

“You have kept a second account,” I said.

“I have kept the true one.”

“You could have shown this to a board in 1806 and been done with it.”

My father put his hands flat on the table.

“I could have,” he said. “And they would have asked me where the money went, and I would have said: the bridge, the school, the school slates, the coal, the pump, the road at the ford. And they would have asked me for the receipts, and I would have produced the receipts, and they would have looked at them and seen that the receipts are all made out to the parish and not to the office.

“And then they would have asked me: by what authority did you apply the endowment of an office to the general purposes of a parish?

“And I would have had no answer, because there is no answer, because it is not lawful, and it never was, and I have known it since 1800.

“So I have kept the true account and I have kept it under a drawer for fourteen years and I have never shown it to a living soul, and it has not done one single person in this parish one single day of good.”

✦

He got up and went to the fire and stood with his back to it.

“There is a thing I have to tell you and then we will talk about the paper,” he said. “It will take a while.”

“Say it.”

“I have been to that house four times.”

I put the spoon down.

“Four times,” he said. “Not to the gate. To the door. Once in 1807 and once in 1811 and once in 1819 and once in 1826.

“1807 was the year after the commission, and I went because I had heard that Ellen had been told the office was gone and I had it in my head that I would go and say a thing to her. I got as far as the door and I heard a woman’s voice inside, singing, and I stood there with my hand up and I could not do it, and I went home.

“1811 was the year the school was built, and I went because there was money in the column and I had a thought that I could take some of it to her and it would be a kind of restitution. I got to the door and I had the money in my pocket in a purse and I stood there for a quarter of an hour, and then I went round the side and put the purse on the step and went away.

“She found it. She never said a word about it to anybody. And in the February of 1812 there was a parcel left at our door with a pair of gloves in it that she had knitted, and no note, and I have never worn them and I have never thrown them away.”

✦

The fire took a stick.

“1819,” my father said. “That was the year of the pump, and it was a bad year, and I went up that lane in the November with the intention of telling her the whole of it. I had it worked out in my head, the way a man works out a speech in a lane. I was going to say: the money went to the parish and I did it and there is no arrangement of words that will make it right, and I am not asking you to forgive me, I am asking you to tell me what to do.

“And I got to the gate and there was a child in the garden. A girl of about nine. And she looked at me the way a child looks at a man she does not know, and I turned round and walked home.

“I have thought about that child for twelve years. There is no child at that house. There has never been a child at that house. Mrs. Trewin had a niece who stayed for a year in 1819 and that is who it was, and I have known that since 1821, and it has made no difference at all.”

“And 1826.”

“1826 was the year your mother’s knees went,” he said, “and I went because I had decided that if I could not do it for Ellen I could do it for her, and I would go up that lane and offer to take the walking over. One Tuesday in four. That was the offer.

“And I got to the gate and I saw the cart from Collow go past on the road above, and I stood there and I thought: a man who offers to walk a road for a woman is a man who has decided that the walking is the whole of it.

“And it is not the whole of it. The walking is nothing. The walking is the one part of it that costs nobody anything, and I stood at that gate and I understood that, and I went home and I never went again.”

✦

He came back and sat down.

“Four times,” he said. “And I want to say the thing that I have never said to anybody, including your mother, and I am going to say it now because you are going to a board in the morning and there is a chance that a man on that board will ask you what your father did.

“I did not go up that lane because I was afraid of Ellen. I went up that lane four times in nineteen years and I did not go in because I was afraid of what she would say, and the reason I was afraid of what she would say is that I knew what she would say.

“She would have said: sit down and have some tea.

“That is all. That is the whole of it. She is that kind of woman and she would have said sit down and have some tea, and she would have asked me about your mother’s knees, and she would not have mentioned the land once.

“And I could not bear it. I could bear a woman who cursed me. I could have gone up that lane a thousand times for a woman who cursed me, because a curse is a thing a man can carry, it is a shape he can put down at night.

“I could not bear to be forgiven by her, because a forgiven man has to live.”

I have known a great many people in this parish who were sorry for a thing they had done. It is the commonest thing in a village. It is worth nothing, and it is usually a way of asking to be let off.

That was the first time in my life I had heard a man say he could not bear to be forgiven.”

✦

He was waiting for me to be shocked and I was not.

The fire made the noise a fire makes when it is settling.

“There is one more thing in the chest,” my father said. “It is under the drawer with the paper. Go and get it.”

I got up and went to the chest in the corner of the room, which is not the parish chest, it is our chest, and I lifted out the drawer and put my hand under it.

There was a bundle in brown paper, tied with string, and the string had been tied and untied so many times that it was soft.

I brought it to the table.

“Open it,” my father said.

✦

Inside the paper there were four things.

A purse, empty, with the drawstring gone.

A pair of gloves, knitted, grey, never worn, with the paper label still on them and the label in Ellen’s hand and the label says for J. A. and no thanks.

A receipt, dated November 1811, for four pounds, in the hand of a shopkeeper at Collow, for one ton of coal, delivered to the house at the end of the lane.

And a letter, folded in three, addressed to my father, and the seal on it is broken and the letter is not opened.

I thought about the three of them in a room in 1799. A man with a leather case and a lie. A woman with a child of four and a coal allowance. A clerk with a pen who did not read the bottom of the paper.

Three people, and not one of them a villain, and the whole of it happened in eleven minutes in a kitchen, and thirty-two years later there is a girl of eighteen going to a board with a piece of foolscap.

✦

I looked at the letter.

“That is from her,” I said.

“It came in the parcel with the gloves in 1812.”

“And you have not opened it.”

“No.”

“Father.”

“I have carried it for nineteen years,” my father said. “It is in my coat and it has been in my coat every day since 1812 and it has gone soft in the folds and I have never opened it, and I am not going to open it tonight.

“You will open it. Not because I am giving it to you. Because a man who has carried a letter for nineteen years has forfeited the right to read it, and because whatever is in it is not for me.

“And there is one thing I want understood before you do it. If it says what I think it says, then I have been wrong about the whole of my life in a way that is worse than the fraud, and you will not be able to tell me, and I will not ask.”

✦

He stood up.

“Now,” he said. “The paper. The true account. What do you want me to do with it?”

I looked at the sheet of foolscap with the four columns and the eleven rows and the figures in my father’s hand.

“You sign it,” I said.

“And then.”

“And then you come with me tomorrow and you stand up and you say what it is, and you give it to the board, and you do not say one word about the bridge or the school or the coal or the eleven things this parish has had out of that column for thirty-two years.”

My father stood very still.

“You are asking me to go into a room and confess to a misapplication of an endowment in front of eleven parishes,” he said, “and to say nothing at all about the fact that not one penny of it came to me.”

“Yes.”

“That is the worst thing you could ask a man to do.”

“It is the only thing that will work,” I said. “Because if you go in there and say I took it but I spent it on the parish, they will hear the first half and they will not hear the second half, and every man in that room has spent forty years learning how to hear the first half of a sentence and not the second.

“And if you go in there and say I misapplied it and I have kept the account and here it is, they will have to look at the account. And the account is the thing that shows the six hundred and fifty-nine pounds, and the six hundred and fifty-nine pounds is the thing that will make them sit up, because it is money that has not been spent and there is not a board in England that has ever been able to leave money alone.”

✦

My father sat down slowly.

“You have been thinking about this,” he said.

“I have been thinking about nothing else for a fortnight.”

“Then say the rest of it. You have not said the rest of it. There is a thing you are not asking me and it is the only thing that matters.”

I put my hands on the table.

“If you sign that paper and go into that room,” I said, “you will lose the office. Not at Michaelmas. At the board. They will take it the same day, and you will be a man of sixty-one with a bad throat and no office and no house, because the house goes with the office.

“And I am asking you to do it anyway.”

✦

My father sat with his hands on his knees.

Then he did a thing I had never seen him do.

He laughed.

Not a big laugh. A short one, and it had no bitterness in it at all, and it was the laugh of a man who has been waiting for a question for thirty-two years.

“Sixty-one,” he said. “I have been clerk of this parish since I was thirty, and my father was clerk before me, and his father before him, and there has been an Ashby in that room since 1750.

“And I have been a good clerk. I want that in the minutes, whatever else goes in. I have made a fair copy every quarter for thirty-one years and there is not one page of it that a man could find a fault in, and there is not one person in this parish who has come to that door with a paper and gone away without an answer.

“That is the thing I have got. It is not much and it is not a defence and I am not offering it as one.

“Bring me the pen.”

✦

I brought him the pen and the ink and he signed the sheet of foolscap at the bottom, and then he blotted it, and then he sat looking at his own name for a while.

“There is one more thing,” he said, “and then I am going up.

“Tomorrow, when they ask you what the office was for, do not answer them with a principle. Answer them with a person. There is not a man in that room who can follow a principle for four minutes, and every one of them has sat with somebody who was dying.

“Tell them about Sarah Dunnet. 1797. A woman of sixty-five who wanted to know whether her sister was with the child.

“Tell them that, and then tell them the date, and then tell them that the woman who answered her was sitting at that table this afternoon in a chair with a cushion, and that she is fifty years old and she has four hours in her, and that after those four hours there will be nobody in this county who can answer that question again.

“That is the case. Not the six hundred and fifty-nine pounds. The pounds are what will make them listen. Sarah Dunnet is what will make them vote.”

✦

He picked up the lamp.

“Father.”

“Mm.”

“The letter. From Ellen. What do you think it says?”

My father stood on the bottom step with the lamp in his hand.

“I think it says four words,” he said, “and I have thought so for nineteen years, and I am not going to say them out loud in this kitchen, because if I say them out loud I will have to believe them.”

He went up.

The fourth step made its noise, and then the seventh.

✦

There was a knock at ten.

Not a knock at the door. A knock on the window, which is what he does, and I have never asked him why, and I think it is because a knock on a window is a thing you can pretend you did not hear.

Mr. Rooke was standing in the yard with no hat and no coat and the rain coming down straight.

I went out to the step and shut the door behind me.

“You have not slept,” I said.

“No.”

“Come in.”

“Your father is upstairs.”

“He knows.”

“He does not.”

“He has known since 1799,” I said. “He has known every day since 1799 and so has my mother, and the only thing either of them did about it was not say it, and that is the whole of what is wrong in this house.”

He stood in the rain and looked at the door.

Then he said, “I have made a list.”

“Of what.”

“Of every page I have signed without reading it to the bottom.” He took a folded paper out of his coat and held it out. “There are nine. The first is in 1812 and the last is in November of last year, and I wrote it out this evening at a table in the George with a pot of porter I did not drink, and I have carried it about for six hours trying to decide whether to give it to you.”

I took it.

“Why me,” I said.

“Because you are the only person who will know what it is,” he said. “Your father has just given you his true account. That is mine. It is nine lines and it is worse than his, because his was a thing he did to keep a parish alive and mine is a thing I did to keep a chair.”

I had the paper in my hand. It was wet at the corner where his coat had been.

“What am I to do with it,” I said.

“Nothing. Keep it. Or put it on the fire. I am not giving it to you to use. I am giving it to you because a man who has carried a thing for nineteen years and has never once said it out loud is a man who has started to think he is the only one who knows, and I would like there to be one other person, and there is nobody else it could be.”

The rain came down between us and neither of us moved out of it.

“Mr. Rooke. Why the window and not the door.”

“Because I came to give you a thing and not to ask for one, and a man who knocks at a door has to be let in.”

I stepped off the step into the rain.

I did not know I was going to do it until I had done it, which is how the important things happen to me. It put us close, closer than a step and a yard have any business putting two people. I put my hand flat on his chest, on the wet shirt, and his heart was going hard and uneven, like something with a crack in it, and he did not move away, and I turned my face up.

He looked at my mouth. I watched him do it. Then he closed his eyes, and when he opened them he was a man again, and it cost him, and I saw what it cost him, and I have never told anybody what that was worth to me.

He took my hand off his chest. Both of his hands round the one of mine, the paper between us, wet at the corner. He held it, and he said, “No.”

Not to me. To himself.

“Not tonight. Tomorrow I sit at a table in Carrow with a paper in front of me that says whether your father keeps his office and his house, and I am the auditor until the board rises, and a man who kisses a woman the night before he can ruin her spends the rest of his life proving that the two things were never the same thing. I will not spend it proving that.”

“Nobody would say it.”

“I would say it. Every day, in the room where nothing is proved. And you would hear it, and I would hear you hearing it.”

He put my hand down gently on the step rail, the way you set a thing down that you mean to pick up again, and stood back from me.

I want it written down that I was not sent away. He sent himself, and he did it for my father, and I have thought about it every day since, and on the whole I am glad of it, and on some days I am not, and both of those are true.

He looked at the door.

“Will you read it,” I said.

“Not yet.”

“On the twenty-ninth.”

“On the twenty-ninth,” he said.

He went back across the yard and I stood on the step until I could not hear him any more, and then I went in, and the kitchen was warm and the lamp was up and the paper was wet in my hand and I did not put it on the fire.

✦

I sat at the table for a long time after he had gone up.

Then I untied the string.

The letter was two pages and the hand was Ellen’s and it was written in 1812, and it began with a date and a place and then went straight into the middle of the thing without any of the four sentences a person writes at the beginning of a letter.

I read it three times.

Then I folded it and put it back in the envelope and put the envelope in the paper and tied the string and put the whole of it back under the drawer.

I am not going to put it down here. Not yet. There is one person who has to read it first, and it is not me.

Upstairs my father’s boots came off and hit the floor, one and then the other.

23

The Lie

My father would not come into the building.

He got as far as the steps and stopped and looked up at the door, and the porter with the wooden leg looked at him and said, “Morning,” and my father said, “Morning,” and then he took his hat off and held it in both hands and did not go up.

“Father.”

“I will be in the yard.”

“It is a public building. There is nothing in it that can hurt you.”

“There is a great deal in it that can hurt me,” my father said, “and the worst of it is a man I have known for thirty-two years who will be very glad to see me, and I am not able to be glad back, and I have not got the face for it this morning.

“Go up. Lodge the paper. I will be in the yard by the wall and I will not go home.”

✦

The registry at ten in the morning is a machine that is running.

The gas brackets were lit in broad daylight, which they are every day, because the hall is deep and the daylight does not reach the middle of it, and the flames stood white and even in their glass, fourteen of them, and the dome took the light of all fourteen and gave back a hum. It is the best-lit room in the county and it is a room where nothing that matters is ever said out loud.

There were fourteen people in the hall and three of them were clerks going up with papers under their arms and eleven were the public, and the public had the look that the public has in that building, which is the look of people who have dressed for a thing and are not sure they have dressed for the right thing.

I had the sheet of foolscap in a cardboard case and the office book in a cloth bag and Ellen’s book in my pocket, and I went up the eleven steps and across the hall and up the stair to the second floor, and I was four doors from the archive when a door opened on my left and a man came out of it.

“Miss Ashby.”

I stopped.

He was five feet nine and he was fifty-one and he was in a dark grey coat and he had a paper in his hand that he had been reading and he closed it.

“Mr. Quill.”

“You have come to lodge a paper.”

“I have.”

“Then you will want the petitions clerk and the petitions clerk is at the end of that corridor and he will not see you before eleven.” He looked at the case under my arm. “May I.”

“No.”

“Then I will walk with you.”

✦

He walked with me.

That is a thing I have thought about since. He did not take me into a room and he did not offer me tea. He walked beside me down a corridor with his hands behind his back, and he talked, and the whole of it took four minutes.

“I have been expecting you since the twenty-first,” he said. “There is a man at Ely who writes to me, and his last letter said that the auditor of your parish had been in the archive asking for the box of the office at Collow, and had asked for it twice, and had been very civil about it, which is the thing I notice about him and have always noticed.

“So I know what you are going to lodge and I know what is in it, and I want to say four things to you before you do, and then I will leave you at the end of the corridor and you may do as you like.”

✦

“The first is that I have never been to your parish in my life.

“The second is that I do not know the name Vane. There is no Vane in the registry and there is no Vane in the commission’s list and there is no Vane in the poll book, and a name that is in no book is not a name, it is a noise.

“The third is that the file of the office at Collow was returned to the diocesan office in 1806 complete, and if two pages are missing now they were mislaid at Ely and not at Carrow, and I have a letter from the secretary at Ely in 1811 that says the file is complete, and I will produce it.

“And the fourth is that your mother has my sympathy.”

✦

We had come to the end of the corridor.

“There is the petitions clerk,” Quill said. “He is a good man and he will take your paper and he will stamp it and it will go into a bundle and the bundle will go to the board on the twenty-ninth, and the board will look at it for as long as a board looks at a thing, which is about four minutes.

“Good morning, Miss Ashby.”

“Mr. Quill.”

He went back down the corridor.

✦

I did not go to the petitions clerk.

I went out into the yard.

My father was by the wall with his hat in his hands, standing in the sun where it came over the roof, and he saw my face and did not say anything, which is the thing he is best at in the world.

I sat down on the mounting block and got out the pencil and a piece of paper and wrote down the four things while they were still in my head, and then I looked at them.

✦

1. Never been to your parish.

2. Do not know the name Vane.

3. The file was returned complete.

4. Your mother has my sympathy.

✦

I sat on the mounting block in the yard of the registry at Carrow with the sun on my hands and did the thing that I have done all my life, which is to look at a list and find the one that does not belong.

The first is a lie. I know it is a lie because my father saw him in the kitchen in 1807 and knew him in a different coat.

The second is a lie. I know it is a lie because a woman sewed a line shut in 1799 with a man watching her do it, and the line has a name on it.

The third is a lie. I know it is a lie because there is a letter in a box at Ely in his own hand that says two pages are wanting, and a man who writes that a file is complete in 1811 when he wrote in 1806 that it was not is a man who has gone back and written a second letter to cover the first.

The fourth.

The fourth is true.

✦

My father came and stood by the mounting block.

“You have found something,” he said.

“Four things. Three lies.” I put the paper in his hand. “And one that is true, and it is the one he should not have said.”

My father read it.

“Sympathy,” he said.

“He does not have sympathy. He has a thing that looks like it from the outside, and he has been carrying it for thirty-two years, and it has made him careful and correct and it has never once made him kind.” I got up. “A man who tells three lies and then tells the truth does it because the truth is the thing he cannot leave alone. It is the only true thing he has said in this building in twenty-five years and he said it to a girl in a corridor because he could not help it.

“And that means he is not finished. A man who is finished does not tell the truth. A man who is finished tells four lies and goes to his dinner.”

✦

I went back up the eleven steps.

The porter with the wooden leg looked at me and said, “You again.”

“Me again.”

“He will not see you.”

“He will see me,” I said. “Tell him I have read the fourth thing.”

✦

He saw me.

The room was the same room: two windows on the green, a fire, a carpet, a desk, and eleven birds on a shelf, and one of them, the blue enamelled one, had been moved four inches to the left since March.

I noticed that and I have never known what to do with it.

“Sit down,” Quill said.

“I will stand.”

“You will sit down, because I am going to say a thing to you that will take four minutes, and I would like you to hear it sitting down.” He went round the desk. “You have read the fourth thing.”

“I have read the fourth thing.”

“And you have come back to tell me that it is true.”

✦

I sat down.

“You do not have sympathy,” I said. “You have a thing that has been in you since 1799 and it is not remorse and it is not grief, and I have been looking at it for a fortnight and I did not know what it was until I stood in a corridor and heard you say the word.

“It is the thing a man feels when he has been careful for thirty-two years and it has not worked.

“You have kept every paper correct. You have kept the file at four pages. You have kept a name off a list and a letter in a box and a man in a different coat. And the thing you were keeping correct has gone wrong anyway, because a girl read a page with her hand and a clerk signed a true account and a woman in a bed at Collow is coming to Carrow in a cart on the twenty-ninth.

“And what you feel about that is not sympathy for my mother. It is the thing a man feels about a house he has kept clean for thirty-two years when somebody opens a window.”

✦

Quill stood behind his desk.

“That is a very good speech,” he said.

“It is not a speech. It is the fourth thing.”

“It is a guess.”

“It is the fourth thing,” I said, “and you know it is, because you have not sat down.”

✦

He sat down.

I want to put that down, because it was the only moment in the whole of that spring when I saw him do a thing that was not calculated, and it lasted about two seconds, and then it was gone.

“Miss Ashby,” he said. “I am going to tell you what I am going to do, and then you may go.

“On the twenty-ninth the board will sit. Your paper will be in the bundle. I will be in the room, because the deputy registrar attends the board, and I will be asked whether there is any objection from this office, and I will say: the file is complete.

“And then the auditor will stand up, and he will say what he has to say, and he will produce the letter I wrote in 1806, and the board will look at it, and one of them will ask me whether the letter is mine, and I will say that it is.

“And then I will say the rest of it, which is that the letter is a covering letter and that the two pages referred to in it are the inventory and the schedule, and that both of those were supplied separately and are in the box at Ely, and that the file is therefore complete in law.

“And that will be true. It is true now. I have seen to it that it is true, and I saw to it in 1811, and I have been seeing to it for twenty years, and it is the only thing I have ever done that I am not ashamed of.”

✦

I had the answer ready and did not want to give it.

“You have replaced the pages,” I said.

“I have supplied what was missing.”

“You have written them yourself.”

“I have written a schedule and an inventory,” Quill said, “and they are correct, and they are in the box at Ely, and they have been in that box since 1811, and there is not a man in England who can tell them from the originals because there are no originals. The originals were burnt in the fire at the diocesan office in 1809.”

The fire took a stick.

“There was a fire,” I said.

“There was a fire,” Quill said, “and it took four rooms and eleven boxes and the whole of the 1806 commission, and I have thanked God for that fire every day for twenty-two years, and I am not going to pretend otherwise in front of a girl who has just told me the truth about my own face.”

✦

I stood up.

“Then there is nothing in the file,” I said. “There never was. There is a petition with fourteen names and an endorsement and two pages you wrote yourself in 1811, and the only thing in that box that is real is a letter that says two pages are wanting.”

“That is correct.”

“Then why are you telling me.”

“Because you are going to stand up at that board and say that a file is missing two pages,” Quill said, “and the auditor is going to say it, and the board is going to ask me, and I am going to say what I have just told you, and it will be in the minutes, and a man in a hundred years will read it and will know that the deputy registrar supplied a document after a fire and called it a file.

“That is the worst thing that can happen to me. Not the loss of the office. Not a prosecution. That sentence in the minutes.”

✦

He stood up.

“So I am going to make you an offer,” he said, “and it is a real one, and you should hear it before you decide.

“Withdraw the paper. Take your mother out of the class quietly at Lady Day. Let the office lapse with her, which it will do in ten months, and let me close the file, and let me write in it the one line I told you about in March.

“And in exchange I will do a thing that no deputy registrar has done in this county in twenty years. I will find the office. Not restore it. Find it. There is a way, under the Act of 1818, for a board to declare that an office was suppressed in error and to refer it to the commissioners, and the commissioners may regrant it, and it takes two years and it costs a hundred pounds and it has been done four times since 1818 for officers of the court.

“I will do that for your daughter. Not this year. In 1833, when the file is closed and the fire is twenty-four years behind us and there is nobody left who remembers 1806.

“She will be twenty. She will have a patent under the seal. And no board in England will ever be able to say that she is a girl with a thimble.”

✦

I stood by the chair with my hands on the back of it.

“Mr. Quill.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“In 1833 my mother will have been dead two years.”

“Yes.”

“And you will be fifty-three, and the file will be closed, and the fire will be twenty-four years behind you, and there will be nobody left who remembers 1806.” I looked at him. “Including you. That is the whole of the offer. You are not offering me a patent. You are offering me a wait, and the wait is the thing you have been using on this parish since 1799.”

Quill did not move.

“You are a very clever girl,” he said, “and you are going to lose.”

“I know.”

“Then why.”

“Because my father is in the yard,” I said, “and he has been standing by that wall since nine o’clock with his hat in his hands, and he is going to come into that board room on the twenty-ninth and say that he misapplied an endowment for thirty-two years for which he is going to lose his office and his house. And if I withdraw the paper he will have done it for nothing.

“And because a woman of fifty is coming nineteen miles in a cart, and she has four hours in her, and she is going to spend them in a room full of men who will not believe her.”

I picked up the case.

“You said in March that you would write one line in a box and it would sit there for a hundred years. That is the saddest thing anybody has ever said to me, and I have thought about it a great deal, and I have decided that it is not enough.

“Not for her. Not for my mother. Not for the two pages you wrote yourself in 1811.

“And not for you, Mr. Quill. You have been keeping a thing correct for thirty-two years and it has made you the loneliest man I have ever been in a room with, and I do not think you want one line in a box. I think you want somebody to make you say it out loud.”

✦

I went to the door.

“Miss Ashby.”

I stopped.

“There is a thing about the board that nobody has told you,” Quill said, “and I am going to tell you, because I am fifty-one and I have watched eleven parishes vote for twenty-five years and I know what they do.

“They will not vote on the office. They will vote on you. Whether you are a fit person to be heard. It is the first item on every agenda and it is decided in four minutes and it is never written down.

“And you are a girl of eighteen with no office, no husband, no father in office after tomorrow, and a book that a woman made out of a coat. You will be heard because the chairman is a fair man. And then he will ask whether there is any member who wishes to speak against, and there will be.

“And the member who speaks against will not be me. I am the deputy registrar and I do not speak. It will be a man from a parish who has never heard of you and does not care, and he will say the thing that ends it, and he will say it in eleven words, and I have heard it four times in twenty-five years and it has never once failed.”

“What eleven words.”

“This is a matter for the parish and not for the board.”

✦

He sat down and picked up his pen.

“Good morning, Miss Ashby,” he said. “I will see you on the twenty-ninth.”

I went out and down the stair and across the hall and out of the door, and my father was by the wall with his hat in his hands, and he looked at my face and said:

“Nothing.”

“Nothing,” I said. “And everything. Get me a pencil. I have eleven words to kill.”

✦

Rooke was at the foot of the stair when I came out, and he was not alone.

Quill had followed me to the head of the stair, and he stood on the top step with the case under his arm, and he said, in a voice that carried down the whole well of it: “Miss Ashby. A word of advice, since you will not take a warning. Men who stand in yards do not always stay in yards. Your father has been in that yard since nine o’clock, and a man who waits that long is a man who is deciding something.”

Rooke came back up three steps and stopped on the one below him, so that the two of them were a stair apart and level, and he said, quietly, in the voice he used for reading a page to the bottom:

“Mr. Quill. If you have business with the clerk of Nether Collow or his daughter, it is business with the audit, and the audit is mine, and I am standing here. Say it to me or say it another day.”

They looked at each other for the length of four seconds. Twenty-five years of the same building were in the look, and neither of them blinked, and it was the first time in my life I had seen Mr. Quill take his eyes off a person first.

He went up.

Rooke came down past me without a word, and his hand touched my elbow once at the turn, to take my weight off the worn step, and was gone before I could thank him, and the stair went on creaking behind him all the way to the hall.

24

Twelve Miles

The twenty-eighth of March was a Monday and it was fine, and I was up at four in the morning with the oven.

The oven is in the wall of the kitchen and it takes two hours to heat and it has been in that wall since 1764 and it has a fault: the flue draws too well and the bread at the back catches before the bread at the front is set. My mother knows the fault the way a person knows a limp. She puts a slate in front of the flue and takes it out at the half hour.

She was not up. That was the arrangement. I had made it with her the night before and it had taken a quarter of an hour and she had agreed at the end of it in the way she agrees to things, which is to say nothing and then do as she likes.

So I did it on my own with her slate and her peel and her cloth, and I made four loaves and one of them I made badly on purpose.

✦

That is the part I want to put down.

Ellen has been in a bed at Collow for eleven years and she has eaten parish bread for twenty-five of them because her sister brought it on Tuesdays and the bread at Collow is not the same bread, and there is a thing about a person who has been eating a thing for twenty-five years.

You cannot give her a good loaf.

A good loaf, on the twenty-eighth of March, on the day before a board, is a loaf that says: this is the last one. It says: we do not expect to be doing this in April. A person who is being taken to a board on the twenty-ninth does not want a good loaf.

So the fourth one has too much salt and it is a little low on the left side because I did not turn it, and it is the loaf I took.

✦

My mother came down at six and looked at the four of them on the table and did not say anything for a while.

“Which one.”

“The bad one.”

“Why.”

“Because a good loaf is a thing you give at the end,” I said.

My mother stood in the middle of the kitchen with her shawl round her.

“You are learning,” she said.

✦

I wrapped it in the blue cloth.

I want to put down about the blue cloth, because it has been in this account twice and both times at the edge of it and a person reading this will have noticed and will not have been told.

There is a piece of blue cloth in this house. It is about a yard square and it is old and it is the blue that cloth goes when it has been dyed with woad and washed for forty years, and it is in the chest with the funeral cloth, and it came out of that chest in November, and in November it went out of the house and I did not know where.

My mother had it in her hand at six in the morning on the twenty-eighth of March.

“Where has it been,” I said.

“At Collow.”

“Since November.”

“Since the fifth of November,” my mother said. “I took it up the lane in the November and I sat in that room and I told her what the doctor had said, and she asked me for it, and I gave it to her, and she has had it on the bed since.”

“And now.”

“And now she has sent it back,” my mother said, “with a note that says four words, and the four words are not for you, and I am not going to say them in this kitchen.”

✦

We put the bread in the basket and the basket in the cart, and the cart was at the gate at seven.

It was Barrow’s cart. That is a thing I have not put down and should have. It was Barrow’s cart and Barrow’s man driving it, and when I had said at the barn that I would need a cart on the twenty-eighth, Barrow had said nothing at all, and the cart had arrived at seven in the morning with a cushion in it.

My mother got up into it with her hand on the wheel and her lips pressed together, and she sat down on the cushion and arranged her skirt and said, “Well?”

“Nothing.”

“Then get up.”

✦

The road to Collow on a fine March morning with two women in a cart.

We went over the bridge at half past seven and my mother did not look at the stone.

We went up the hill and along the top and there were lambs in the fields and the light was the light you get in March when the sun has come up but has not yet got any heat in it, and everything is a colour that is too bright for the thing it is on.

At the top of the hill there is a place where you can see eleven miles and there was a man ploughing in the field below us with two horses and he was going away from us and the gulls were behind the plough.

My mother said, “I have walked this road a thousand and forty times and I have never once seen it from a cart.”

“Is it different?”

“It is shorter,” she said, “and it is not better.”

✦

We were at Collow by nine.

Mrs. Trewin had the door open before the cart stopped.

She had Ellen’s dress over her arm and a brush in her hand and she was in her own good coat, and she looked at me and said:

“She has been up since five and she has had her hair done twice and she has eaten nothing and she has asked me four times whether the cart has come.”

“And?”

“And the fourth time she asked, I told her that if she asked a fifth time I would put her in the cart in her nightdress.”

✦

Ellen was in the chair.

She had the dress on. It was grey and it had been good in about 1810 and it had been altered twice and the shoulder had been let out with a piece of the same grey that did not quite match, and she had a lace collar on it that was not from this century and a brooch at the throat.

Her hair was up and it was done properly and it had been done with one hand.

“You are late,” she said.

“We are early. It is nine.”

“Then you are early and I have been ready since five, which is worse.” She looked at the basket. “Is that bread.”

“It is.”

“Give it to Mrs. Trewin and sit down and do not tell me it is a bad loaf. I know it is a bad loaf. You have made it badly on purpose and I know why you have made it badly on purpose, and I am not going to have it discussed in this room.”

✦

I sat down.

Mrs. Trewin took the bread out and Ellen waited until the door was shut.

“Now,” she said. “Tomorrow.”

“Tomorrow.”

“You will not read one thing in that room.”

I looked at her.

“Not a chair,” Ellen said. “Not a pen. Not a man’s coat. Not the table. Not the door. Not the book they will put in front of you, and they will put a book in front of you, because a board always puts a book in front of a person it is examining, and it is a test.

“If you put your hand on one thing in that room and they see you do it, they will have you. Not the office. You. And it will not be a prosecution and it will not be a charge. It will be a word. A word in a minute, in a clerk’s hand, and after that word is written there will not be a parish in this county that will let you in the door.

“So you will go in there and you will sit on your hands if you have to, and you will be a girl who has read a great many books and has a father who is a clerk and a witness who is fifty years old, and that is all you will be in that room.

“Do you understand me?”

“Yes.”

“Say it back.”

“I will not read anything in that room.”

“Say the rest.”

I had it ready and I did not want to say it.

“If I read anything in that room,” I said, “I will be the girl with the thimble, and the office will be a woman’s fancy, and my mother will have died in the eleventh class and my father will have lost his house for nothing.”

✦

Ellen sat back.

“Good,” she said. “Now the other thing.

“You have been in that room before. The vestry. The barn. The registry at Carrow. And every time you have gone in with a thing that nobody else had, and you have been quick, and it has worked.

“Tomorrow you will not be quick.

“Tomorrow you will sit in a chair and you will let your father speak first, and then you will let the auditor speak, and then you will let them ask you a great many stupid questions, and you will answer every one of them as though it were the first intelligent question you had ever been asked.

“And when they have finished, and the room has gone quiet, and the chairman looks at the paper in front of him and thinks that it is nearly dinner, you will say the one thing you have come to say.

“One thing. Not four. Not a speech. One thing, and then you will stop, and you will not answer the man who speaks against it, because the man who speaks against it wants you to answer him, and if you answer him you will be a girl arguing and he will be a man being argued with, and there is only one end to that.”

✦

“What is the one thing,” I said.

“That is for you to decide,” Ellen said, “and I have been thinking about it since Thursday, and I have got four, and none of them are right, and I am not going to give you one of mine.

“A thing that is given to you by an old woman in a chair is not yours and it will come out of your mouth in her voice, and there will be fourteen men in that room who have spent their lives hearing old women in chairs and not listening to them.”

✦

She shifted her shoulder against the cushion.

“There is one more thing and then we are going to have dinner,” she said.

“Say it.”

“Your mother has asked me a question every Tuesday for twenty-five years and I have never answered it. She has never said it out loud. She has said it by coming up that lane and sitting in that chair and not asking, and it is the loudest question I have ever been asked by anybody.

“Tomorrow I am going to answer it, and I am going to answer it in that room, in front of eleven parishes, and I want you to be ready for it, because it is going to be worse for her than anything that man from Ely can do.”

“What is the question?”

“You will hear it,” Ellen said. “And so will she, and she will know it is hers, and she will not be able to do anything about it, because it will be in the minutes.”

✦

We had dinner at one.

Mrs. Trewin had made a pie and there was a bottle of something and Ellen ate half of a slice of pie and drank a glass of the something and said it was the first thing she had tasted since Thursday.

My mother sat on the other side of the bed and did not eat much and the two of them talked about a cat.

That is the thing I have never got over. Two women who had not spoken about the thing between them for twenty-five years sat with a plate on their knees and talked about a cat that got into the church in 1821 for a quarter of an hour, and both of them laughed twice, and it was not a performance.

At three Mrs. Trewin took the plates away and Ellen said, “Sleep. Both of you. I will be asleep in ten minutes and if I hear anybody talking in this house after four I will have something to say about it.”

✦

I could not sleep.

I went out into the garden at four.

The pear tree had come out while I was not looking and there were bees in it, and the tarpaulin on the north end of the roof had been lashed down again with a new piece of rope, and somebody had been at the moss on the path with a knife and had got nearly half way round.

I sat on the bench with the cushion that had been rained on.

My mother came out at half past four and sat down beside me and we did not say anything for a while.

“Mercy.”

“Mm.”

“There is a thing I want to say to you and I am going to say it now, because tomorrow there will be a cart and a room and eleven parishes and no time.

“When you were born the midwife said: she has got her grandmother’s hands. And I lay in that bed and I looked at them and I thought: no. Not the hands. Anything but the hands.

“And I have spent eighteen years watching you write with those hands. And I have been proud of them and I have been afraid of them and there has not been one day when I did not know that they were the thing that would take you out of this parish or bury you in it.”

“Mother.”

“Do not say anything,” she said. “I have not finished and if you say anything I will not finish.

“Tomorrow, when they ask you what you are, you will not say I am the clerk’s daughter and you will not say I am a girl who reads things. You will say: I am the letter.

“Not because you have a patent. Because there is a woman in a chair in that house who is the letter and there is a woman in the ground at Collow who was the letter before her, and there has been one in that family for a hundred and twenty years, and tomorrow you are going to be the third, and the paper does not make it true.

“Say it once in that room. Say it out loud. And then let them do what they like.”

✦

I sat on the bench in the garden at Collow with my mother’s hand in mine and the light going and the bees in the pear tree.

“Mother,” I said.

“Mm.”

“I am not afraid.”

That was the only lie I told that day.

She looked at me for a while and she did not believe it and she did not say so, and she said, “Good,” and she got up and went in.

✦

Mr. Rooke came at seven, on a horse he had hired at Wendon, with fourteen miles on it and mud up to the horse’s belly.

Mrs. Trewin heard him in the lane and came to the door with her hands under her apron and said, “There is a man,” as though a man were a thing that arrived in that lane about once a century, which in fairness it did.

He did not come in. He stood in the yard with the reins over his arm and asked for me, and I went out, and my mother watched us from the window and did not come to the door, and I have never asked her about that either.

He had ridden like a man who had argued with the horse the whole way and won on points. The horse was lathered to the ears and standing with its head down, and there was a wet line across his shoulder where a branch had come back at him out of the hedge on the Collow lane, and he did not look at any of it.

“I have seen the agenda,” he said.

“You have seen it.”

“I was in the building for an hour on another matter and it was on the table in the clerk’s room with the door open, and I do not know whether the door was left open for me and I am not going to ask.”

He took a paper out of his coat.

“The fourth item. Ellen Rooke of Collow, calling herself the Letter of the Dead, to be heard touching the nineteenth class.”

I read it twice.

“Calling herself,” I said.

“Calling herself.”

“Who wrote it.”

“The clerk of the board writes the agenda. The clerk of the board is not Mr. Quill.”

“Then who.”

“A man at Carrow who has never met her,” he said. “That is the whole of it. He has a name and a column and a space that is four inches wide, and he has put her in it.”

He folded the paper and put it back.

“You could have sent it,” I said.

“Because she should know what they have called her before she walks into the room. Not so that she can prepare an answer. So that she is not surprised, because a woman who is surprised in a room like that says the wrong thing, and she has four hours and I am not going to have one of them spent on being surprised.”

I stood in the yard with him.

“You rode fourteen miles,” I said.

“I did.”

“On a hired horse.”

“It was the only one left at Wendon and it has a mouth like iron and I am going to be stiff tomorrow and I do not care.”

Mrs. Trewin brought the basin out without being asked. I took his right hand and turned it over, and the rein had opened the skin at the base of two fingers, and I cleaned it and bound it with a strip of the blue cloth, and he watched me do it and did not pull away once, and neither of us spoke, and the speaking was happening somewhere else entirely.

I want to put down what my hand was doing while it did that, because I did not understand it until much later.

My hand was on a living man. A cup is a record. A wall is a record. A living hand is not a record, it is a person in the middle of happening, and there is nothing in it to read and nothing in it to take. I held his hand open in mine for the whole of the binding and nothing happened, and nothing has never been so loud.

He looked at the house.

“Is she in the chair,” he said.

“She is in the chair.”

He stood with the reins in his hand and did not move for a while. He had not once in his life asked to see her, and he did not ask then. I understood why, and I think he knew that I understood, and neither of us said it.

“Tomorrow at ten,” he said.

“Tomorrow at ten.”

He got up on the horse and turned it in the lane and went, and the mud came off its legs onto the wall, and there was a print of a shoe on the stone there for two days.

✦

I sat out there until it was dark.

Then I went in and stood in the passage outside Ellen’s door and listened, and there was nothing, and I went up to the room they had made for me and lay down on the bed in my clothes with the thimble in my hand.

At nine the clock downstairs struck, and then struck again four minutes later, because it had been gaining four minutes a day since 1790 and nobody at Collow had ever thought it worth stopping, and the sound of it came up through the floor of a strange house and it was the most familiar thing in the world.

I lay there with my eyes open.

Tomorrow at ten o’clock a board would sit in Carrow, and there would be a bell and forty people going into a room and a clerk with a pen, and a man with eleven words, and a girl of eighteen who was not going to read anything at all.

25

Four Minutes

The bell at Carrow is not a church bell. It hangs on a bracket over the market cross on four iron straps, and a man is paid four pounds a year to ring it, and it is rung for three things: a fire, a death in a house of substance, and a board.

It rang at ten.

I heard it from the top of Pump Street with the cart still moving, and I have thought since that the man who rang it did not know what he was ringing for. He pulled a rope. Four pounds a year. Somewhere in the town a woman looked up at the sound and thought somebody had died, and she was not wrong, and she was not right either.

✦

The market was half down. It opens at six and by ten it is a wet floor and a smell and men carrying hurdles. There were peas on one stall, early peas in a basket, and a woman selling them had her shawl pinned at the throat with a brooch that had lost its stones. I saw all of that in the time it takes a cart to cross a square, and I have never been able to stop seeing it, and today of all days I could not stop.

The George is on the north side. It has a yard you can get a coach and four into, and a gallery round the yard on iron posts, and the long room is on the first floor because a long room on the ground floor is a barn.

There are fourteen steps up to it and the seventh has a dish worn in it the depth of a plate, and the stair carpet is held down with brass rods, and one rod is missing and the gap has been mended with a strip of tin cut from a can.

Ellen came up those steps in a chair.

Four men carried her. Two were the George’s porters and two were out of the yard and had been found by Mr. Barrow’s man and promised a shilling each, and they carried her the way men carry a thing they have been told is worth a shilling, which is to say carefully and without looking at her.

She had a blanket over her knees and the gray dress under it and her hands on the arms of the chair. Mrs. Trewin walked beside her with the other blanket folded over one arm in case. My father walked behind with his hat in his hand. I went up first because Ellen had told me on the road that she wanted somebody at the top she knew.

At the eleventh step she said, “Stop.”

They stopped.

“I will walk in,” she said.

“Ellen,” Mrs. Trewin said.

“I will walk in. There are four steps left. If they carry me over the door they will spend the whole morning looking at the chair, and I would rather they looked at me.”

So she got up out of the chair at the eleventh step of the stair at the George with two men holding her elbows, and she stood, and she put her hand on the rail, and she walked the last four steps and in at the door on her own feet, and then she sat down in the chair that had been carried round and put in the second row by the wall.

It took her a quarter of an hour to do it and she was gray to the lips at the end and she did not say one word about it.

I have thought about those four steps more than I have thought about anything else that happened that morning.

✦

The long room has three windows on the market and a fire at the far end and a table of plain deal with a green cloth on it. The cloth is too short at the bottom end and you can see the deal, and somebody has written on the deal with a penknife, and the writing is a date, 1809, and initials, and nothing else.

There is a pike in a glass case over the door. It was caught in the navigation in 1802 and it is the only thing in the room that anybody looks at.

The room smelled of last night. Cold gravy, and tobacco, and a lemon somebody had squeezed over something and left on a plate. The fire had been lit at nine and had not yet decided to draw, and there was a smell of coal smoke on top of the gravy.

We came in at five minutes before ten and the room was empty except for the clerk.

The clerk was at a side table with the minute book open and a ruler laid across it to keep the line, and he was sharpening a pen with a penknife and he had three sharpened ones already laid in a row. He looked up when we came in, and he looked at Ellen in the chair, and he looked back at his pens.

Then Mr. Quill came in.

He came in with his hat under his arm and he put it on the side table and he stood at his place at the bottom of the table and he did not sit, and when Ellen came in he stood up straighter, and when she sat down he sat down.

That is the whole of it. A man in a room in Carrow stood up for a woman who had walked four steps. He had abolished her office and taken her house and he stood up.

I did not know what to do with that and I still do not.

✦

They came in between ten and a quarter past. Eleven of them, one for each parish, and Mr. Gedge last.

Mr. Gedge is a maltster and he has been chairman eleven years and he is sixty-three. He is a heavy man in a good coat that has been let out at the back and he came in carrying a small glass of water which he put down in front of his place and did not drink and did not offer to anybody.

Mr. Ottley came in with his hands folded, which he did with them all day.

Mr. Deverell came in and looked at Ellen in the chair for a moment longer than the others did, and then sat down.

Mr. Craddock came in and sat down at the table on the side nearest the window and put his hands flat on the green cloth and looked at them.

And the others came in: a farmer from Marle, a man from Ashby Parva with a wooden hand, two men I did not know at all, and a thin man with a bad cough who sat nearest the fire and coughed into a handkerchief all morning and nobody looked at him.

Mr. Rooke came in last of the officers and sat at the end of the side table with the auditor’s book, and he set the book square to the edge of the table, and he did not look at me.

He did not look at me once in the first hour. He looked at the book and at the chairman and at the clerk’s pen. And at one point when Mr. Ottley was talking he looked at me for the length of a breath and then went back to the book.

I have been in rooms where a man looked at me for an hour and I have been in a room where a man looked at me once and I know which of those I would rather have.

✦

“Minutes of the twenty-fifth of December,” Mr. Gedge said. “Read.”

The clerk read them. They were three-quarters of an hour long and they were about a drain.

Then the accounts of the several parishes were laid. Then a letter from the clerk to the board about the price of paper. Then a man from Marle stood up and said that the bridge at Marle was going and had been going for four years and that he had said so at Christmas and would say so again at midsummer. Mr. Gedge said the board noted it, and the clerk wrote it, and the man from Marle sat down.

I want to put that down because it is the thing about a board that nobody tells you. Nobody was cruel. Nobody was in a hurry. A man stood up and said his bridge was going and the board noted it, and that is what happens to a thing when it comes into that room. I understood, sitting there with my hands in my lap, that Ellen’s office was going to be a bridge at Marle.

“Petitions,” Mr. Gedge said. “Nether Collow.”

The clerk turned to a page and read.

He read it badly. He read it the way a man reads a thing he has read forty times, and he put the stress on the wrong words, and when he came to the end he said, “Ellen Rooke, of Collow, spinster, and Mercy Ashby, of Collow, spinster.”

“I am not a spinster,” Ellen said from the second row. “I am a widow. My husband was Thomas Rooke and he died in 1809 and it is in the register of this parish in that gentleman’s hand.” She did not point. She did not need to.

The clerk’s pen stopped. He looked at the paper. He looked at Mr. Gedge.

“The paper says spinster,” he said.

“Then enter what the paper says,” Ellen said. “I am used to it.”

Mr. Gedge turned his glass a quarter turn.

That was the first time he did it and by four o’clock I knew what it meant and by four o’clock it was too late to be any use.

✦

“Before the board hears the petition,” Mr. Gedge said, “it must satisfy itself as to the petitioner. Miss Ashby. You have four minutes.”

Four minutes. I had known it was four minutes. Mr. Quill had told me in a corridor and I had counted on it and prepared for it, and when the man said the words out loud in a room with a pike over the door, the four minutes came off me like a coat.

I stood up.

I had a paper in my hand. It had the six hundred and fifty-nine pounds on it and the two thousand three hundred and nine and the endowment of one hundred and four a year and the dates, and I had written it out twice the night before and once in the cart, and I stood up and started to read it.

“Miss Ashby.”

I stopped.

“The board has not asked you about money,” Mr. Gedge said. “The board has asked whether you are a person fit to be heard. There are eleven men at this table who have sat here for a great many years and have heard a great many petitions, and every one of them has been brought by somebody who believed what they were saying. Belief is not in question. What is in question is whether this board should spend its morning on you. Four minutes.”

I stood there with the paper in my hand.

I want to put down what that was like, because I had thought I was ready and I was not ready, and the reason I was not ready is that I had spent a month preparing to argue and nobody had asked me to argue.

He had asked me to be worth listening to.

I folded the paper and put it in my pocket.

✦

“I am eighteen,” I said. “I have lived in this parish all my life. I can read and write and I have copied the register of this parish since I was fourteen years old, in my father’s hand and under his eye, and there is not a page of it in the last four years that is not mine.”

“You are the clerk’s daughter,” Mr. Ottley said pleasantly.

“I am the clerk’s daughter.”

“Then you will understand,” Mr. Ottley said, “that a board cannot hear a clerk’s daughter on a matter concerning her own father’s accounts, any more than it could hear a man on his own debt.”

“I understand it.”

“Good.” He folded his hands. “I am sure Miss Ashby is entirely sincere.”

There it was. Not the words. The words came next. But the thing that did the work was sincere, said pleasantly, by a clergyman, with his hands folded. I have heard a great many things said about me since and nothing has ever been said with a knife in it like that.

“But this,” Mr. Ottley said, “is a matter for the parish and not for the board.”

“Mr. Gedge.”

Rooke was on his feet at the end of the side table with the auditor’s book in his hand, and he did not raise his voice. He had sat through the whole morning without moving and without once looking at me, and now he was up, and he was not looking at me now either.

“The petitioner has not finished her minutes. Mr. Ottley has spent the better part of one of them. The standing orders of this board require that a petitioner entered on the agenda be heard before she is disposed of, and I am the auditor of this union, and if she is not heard I will minute the fact and the refusal, and the minute will go to Carrow under my signature, and Carrow will ask this board why.”

Mr. Ottley looked at him for a while. Then he looked at his own hands, which had been folded and now were not.

“Continue, Miss Ashby,” Mr. Gedge said.

✦

I have gone over that sentence a great many times.

Eleven words. Mr. Quill had told me the night before that there were eleven words and that they had been used four times in twenty-five years and had never failed, and he had been right about the number and right about the record, and none of that helped, because a thing you have been warned about is not the same as a thing that has been said to your face.

I did not answer him.

That was the one thing I had been given, from three separate people, and it was the only one I used. Do not answer the man who opposes you. He wants an answer. An answer makes you a girl arguing and makes him a man being argued with, and there is only one end to that.

So I stood there and let it sit in the room, and the room was very quiet, and the fire had finally decided to draw and there was a sound from it like paper.

Then I said, “Yes.”

Mr. Ottley looked up.

“Yes,” I said. “It is a matter for the parish. You are right, and I am not going to pretend you are wrong, because you are not wrong, and if I stood here for four minutes telling you that you were wrong I would be wasting the four minutes and you would be right about that as well.

“It is a matter for the parish. The parish is in this room.”

I turned round. Not far. There was not far to turn.

“The clerk of this parish is in this room, and he has kept its books for thirty-one years, and he is sixty-one years old and he is standing at the back with his hat in his hand and he will lose his office before dinner. The overseer of this parish is in this room and he is sitting at that table because this parish put him there. There is a woman in the second row who held an office in this parish for twenty-five years and she walked the last four steps of that stair on her own feet this morning so that you would not spend the morning looking at a chair.

“And there is a girl of eighteen who has copied every page of the register of Nether Collow since she was fourteen. That is the parish. We are the parish. All of us are here, in this room, at ten o’clock in the morning, and we have come nineteen miles and one of us has four hours of strength in her and has spent two of them on that staircase.

“So when you say it is a matter for the parish, I agree with you. And then I ask you the only question I have, which is: if the parish brings a matter to the board, and every person in the parish who can speak is standing in front of you, what is the board for?”

Nobody said anything.

Mr. Gedge did not turn his glass.

✦

“I have one more thing,” I said, “and then I will sit down, and it is not about money and it is not about the law and it is not about my father.”

“Go on,” Mr. Gedge said.

“In 1797 a woman of this parish went to that office.” I did not look at Ellen. If I had looked at Ellen I would have stopped. “Her name was Sarah Dunnet. She was sixty-five years old and she had one question and she had carried it for a year, because there was nobody in this world she could ask it of. Her sister had died in a fire in 1796 and there had been a child in that house and the child was not in the burial register and nobody in this parish would say the word out loud.
