“So she went down that lane and she asked, and the woman who held the office put her hand on a thing that had belonged to the dead woman and she answered her. The answer was: she is with her child.

“That is the whole of it. That is what an office is. A woman of sixty-five walked down a lane and asked whether her sister was alone, and was told she was not, and went home.

“There is no receipt for that. It is not in the accounts of this parish. It is not in the eleven hundred pages my father has written in thirty-one years. There is no column in any book at Carrow for a woman being told her sister is not alone, and that is why the office was marked superfluous in 1806 by a commission that had never been in this county, and it is why it is not in front of you today as a sum of money but as a girl of eighteen asking to be listened to for four minutes.

“The woman who answered Sarah Dunnet is in the second row of this room. She is fifty years old. She has four hours of strength in a day and she has spent two of them getting up a staircase, and when those four hours are gone there is nobody in this county who can answer that question, and there will not be anybody after her, and there has not been anybody but her since 1806.

“That is the case. Not the six hundred and fifty-nine pounds. That is what the money is for. The case is Sarah Dunnet, and the year, and the answer, and the woman in the chair.

“I have nothing else.”

I sat down.

✦

My hands were shaking and I put them under the table and held them together.

Ellen did not look at me. Mrs. Trewin did, and Mrs. Trewin’s mouth was doing something that I think was not crying but was related to it.

My father was at the back with his hat in his hand, and he had stopped holding it in front of him and had begun holding it against his chest.

Mr. Gedge looked at the table for a while. Then he picked up his glass of water and turned it a quarter turn and put it down and said, “The board will hear the petition. Mr. Craddock. You are the overseer of Nether Collow and you will speak to the accounts.”

And Mr. Craddock said, “Mr. Chairman. Before the accounts, I have a thing of my own to say, and I would like it minuted.”

The clerk’s pen came up.

Mr. Quill, at the bottom of the table, closed his eyes.

I saw him do it. Nobody else was looking at him. He closed his eyes for about the length of a breath, the way a man does when the thing he has been expecting for twenty-five years finally arrives and turns out to be on time.

26

Out Loud

“There is an office in this parish,” Mr. Craddock said, “called the office of the bell. It was made in 1701 and it pays six pounds a year. The holder rings the great bell of Nether Collow when anybody in the parish dies, and rings it again at the burial.

“My father held it for thirty-one years. He rang it for every death in that parish for thirty-one years, and when he died in 1812 the office came to me by grant, and I have held it nineteen years, and in nineteen years I have not rung it once.

“The six pounds has been paid every Lady Day. It was paid to me this Lady Day, six days ago. In 1819 a man wrote the word idle in the remark column of the book of offices and then the book was put away, and in twelve years nobody has opened that book and read the remark column, and I know that because I have signed the book myself every year and looked at my own word.

“One hundred and fourteen pounds. That is what I have taken for a thing I did not do.

“I am the overseer of this parish and I am sitting at this table because this parish put me here, and I am telling this board a thing that will be in the minutes in my own parish’s hand, and I am doing it because a girl of eighteen stood up in front of me ten minutes ago and told this board what she did in October, and I have been in this county forty-five years and I have never once heard a person do that.

“I have a motion.”

Mr. Gedge had not moved since Craddock began. He said, “State it.”

“That at midsummer this board require the remark column of the book of offices of every parish in this union to be read aloud, and that every office marked idle be examined, and that the money paid on it be stated.”

Nobody said anything at all.

The man with the cough stopped coughing. I noticed that and I noticed the fire and I noticed that the clerk’s pen had not moved for a quarter of a minute, and a clerk’s pen not moving is the loudest thing in a room.

“Mr. Craddock,” Mr. Ottley said. “You are asking this board to open eleven books.”

“I am asking it to open eleven books at a table it already sits at.”

“You are asking it to open eleven books in a year in which every parish in this county is behind on its rates.”

“I am asking it to read a column,” Mr. Craddock said. “It is one column. It is four inches wide.”

Mr. Gedge picked up his glass of water and turned it a quarter turn and put it down and said, “The motion will be taken at the end. Mr. Craddock, the accounts of Nether Collow.”

✦

The accounts of Nether Collow took forty minutes.

I will not put them all down. They were read by the clerk in the flat voice a man uses for a thing he does not understand and does not need to, and they were the ordinary accounts of a parish: the rates, the surveyor, the constable, the churchwardens, the coal and the bread and the doctor and the man who cleaned the pump.

Then the clerk came to the foot of the page and said, “Carried forward, six hundred and fifty-nine pounds.”

Mr. Gedge looked up. “Carried from what?”

“From the account of the endowment of the office at Nether Collow, sir. It has been carried since 1799.”

“Carried by whom.”

The clerk looked at the paper. Then he looked at the back of the room, where my father was standing with his hat against his chest.

“By the clerk of that parish, sir.”

✦

My father came forward.

He had put on his second-best shirt that morning and shaved in cold water and combed his hair, and he had done all of that for a room that was going to take his office off him, and there was something about the way he walked up that room that I have not got words for. He was sixty-one years old and his voice was going and he had been the clerk of Nether Collow for thirty-one years, and he walked up the side of a long room in an inn past eleven men and put a paper on the green cloth and stood back from it.

“That is the account,” he said. “It has four columns and thirty-two lines and it is in my hand. Every penny of the money that has come into that parish out of the endowment since 1799 is in that paper, and what it was spent on is in that paper, and the total carried is six hundred and fifty-nine pounds, and there is not one penny of that six hundred and fifty-nine pounds that I cannot tell you the use of.

“And not one penny of it is in the book.

“I have kept two accounts for thirty-two years. One of them is the parish account and it is correct in every particular and it balances, and the other is that paper, and the difference between them is the thing this board has been reading out for forty minutes without seeing it.

“I have no defence. I am not going to offer one. I will say one thing, which is that the money in that paper went on roads and on a bridge and on a school and on coal, and it did not come to me, and that is not a defence, because a clerk who decides which of two books a sum of money goes into has taken the decision out of the hands of the men whose business it is.

“I am sixty-one. I have a wife who is ill and a daughter in this room. I know what happens next and I am not going to stand here and make it longer.”

He put his hands behind his back.

Mr. Gedge read the paper. He read it the way a man reads a thing he has not expected to be able to read, which is slowly, with one finger.

Then he said, “Mr. Ashby, will you answer a question.”

“Sir.”

“Why is the six hundred and fifty-nine pounds carried and not applied?”

My father stood there.

“Because if I applied it,” he said, “it would have had to be applied to the name of the office, and the office does not exist, and a sum applied to an office that does not exist is a summons to this board to ask why this parish is paying money out of an office that was abolished in 1806. So I carried it. A carried sum is a sum that has not been decided on. I have been not deciding it for thirty-two years.”

“And in the meantime the money went on roads.”

“It went on roads, sir.”

“That is not what I asked.”

“No, sir. But it is the truth, and I have found that the truth about a thing like this is generally two sentences long and the second one is the one that hangs you.”

Somebody at the far end of the table laughed. One syllable. Then stopped.

Mr. Gedge put the paper down and squared it to the edge of the cloth.

“This board,” he said, “will not dismiss a clerk for keeping a better account than the board keeps. Mr. Ashby, sit down at the side and do not leave the room.”

✦

“Mrs. Rooke,” Mr. Gedge said. “You may speak from where you are.”

“I would rather stand,” Ellen said.

“Mrs. Trewin, will you assist”

“I have her,” Mrs. Trewin said.

She stood up out of the chair. It took a while and nobody helped her and nobody said anything while it took a while, and when she was up she put one hand on the back of the chair and the other on Mrs. Trewin’s arm, and she stood.

She was fifty years old and she was wearing a gray dress that had been good about 1810 and had been altered twice, and the alteration at the shoulder did not match, and there was a lace collar at her throat that was not of this century, and a brooch. Her hair had been done with one hand.

“My name is Ellen Rooke,” she said, “and I held that office from 1801 to 1806, and I have been in a bed at Collow for eleven years, and I have four hours of strength in a day, and I have spent two of them on that staircase, so I will be short.

“I do not want the office back. I am fifty and I have four hours and I would not know what to do with an office.

“I want the book to say what happened.”

She stopped. She had to. Mrs. Trewin moved half an inch closer.

“In 1806 a man came to my house. He was a young man in a good coat and he sat in my kitchen and he read my book, all of it, for above an hour. And then he stood up and he said: I have no instruction about this parish. And he went out and got on his horse and went away.

“And the money came in the next quarter. And the quarter after that. And every quarter for twenty-five years.

“Nobody ever wrote to me. Nobody ever came. The money came four times a year for twenty-five years and it came to the parish and not to me and nobody ever asked me to stop, and I want to ask the gentleman who came to my house in 1806 why the payments did not stop.”

She turned her head. It took her a long time to turn her head. “Because a thing that is abolished is abolished, and I have thought about it for twenty-five years and I cannot make it work. If it was abolished, the money should have stopped. If the money did not stop, then the thing was not abolished, and somebody in this county has been paying a hundred and four pounds a year for twenty-five years to a thing he says does not exist.

“Those are the only two. I have had twenty-five years and those are the only two I can find.”

She sat down.

It took longer to sit down than it had to stand up and Mrs. Trewin’s face was wet and neither of them said anything about it.

✦

Mr. Gedge looked at the bottom of the table.

“Mr. Quill.”

Mr. Quill stood up.

He had been sitting with his hands on the table for two hours and he had not written anything and he had not spoken. He stood up and put his hands behind his back and he was fifty-one years old and there was nothing in his face at all.

“I went to that house in 1806,” he said. “The lady is correct in every particular, including the coat.

“I was twenty-six years old and I was the clerk to the commission for the suppression of superfluous offices, and I had four hundred and eleven offices on a schedule in a hand I did not write and could not always read, and I had been in that employment for four months.

“The office at Nether Collow was on the schedule. It was entered as the Letter of the Dead, at Nether Collow, and against it, in the column for the reason, were the words superfluous, no return made since 1794.

“There had been no return made since 1794 because nobody had ever asked for one. That is not the same thing and I knew at the time that it was not the same thing, and I did not go to Collow to inquire. I went to Collow to satisfy myself that the office was a formality so that I could go home.

“I read the book. It is a book of questions and answers, and the questions are from people in four parishes, and there are two hundred and four of them, and I read above an hour of them in that kitchen and I understood what they were.

“And I said what the lady says I said, and I got on my horse.

“I did not report that I had read the book. I reported that I had visited the parish and found the office vacant in effect. That is the first thing I have to say and it is the least of them.

“The second is this. In 1809 the diocesan office at Ely burned, and it took four rooms and eleven boxes and the whole of the papers of the 1806 commission, and the four pages of that schedule with it.

“In 1811 the bishop’s court asked this office for the file of the office at Nether Collow, because a question had been raised about the rent charge. There was no file. There was a fire.

“So I wrote a schedule and I wrote an inventory and I put them in the box at Ely, and they were correct in every particular, and they were not copies of anything, because there was nothing left to copy. And on the fourth page I entered the endorsement and on the back I entered the line that says the inventory of the office was returned separately and is in the box of the office at Ely.

“I made a file. I made it out of what I remembered and what I could establish, and I made it correctly, and for twenty-two years it has been the file, and there is no man in England who can tell it from an original, because there are no originals.

“That is the second thing. The third is the one the lady has asked, and it is the only one that matters.

“The payments did not stop because I did not stop them.

“Abolishing an office and stopping the money are two acts. The schedule abolished the office. Stopping the money required a letter from this office to the paymaster, and a letter is a document, and a document has to say on its face why the money is stopping. And to write that letter I would have had to write the words the holder is a woman of this parish who answers questions for the dead, because that is what the office was, and the paymaster’s clerk would have read it, and it would have gone into the account, and in 1806 there were men in London who had spent nine years making the county accounts fit to be read by a committee.

“I did not write the letter. I let the money run. I told myself that a payment that was not stopped was an administrative matter and would be dealt with by somebody whose business it was, and there was nobody whose business it was, and I knew that.

“And then the money ran for twenty-five years and every quarter I saw the return come through this office with the figure on it, and every quarter I could have written the letter.

“Twenty-five years is a hundred letters.”

He stopped.

Mr. Gedge said, “Is that all.”

“No.” Mr. Quill put his hands on the table. “I have a thing to ask of this board, and I am going to ask it in a way that cannot be refused.

“If this board petitions the bishop’s court, the file goes up. When the file goes up, the fire goes up, and the schedule of 1811 goes up, and there will be a man in that court who does what I have done for twenty-five years, which is to read a page to the bottom. And he will find that a document was supplied in 1811 out of nothing and was entered as a file.

“I want that in the minutes. Not the petition. That. I want it written down in this room, in this hand, in this book, that the deputy registrar of this county stated to the board that the file of the office at Nether Collow is defective and that he supplied the deficiency himself in 1811.

“I have spent twenty-five years keeping the register of this county correct. It is not correct. I am obliged to say so, and I am obliged to say it in a place where it will be written, because a thing said in a room is a thing that can be denied tomorrow, and a thing written is not.”

The clerk’s pen was going. It had been going the whole time. I could hear it and I think everybody could hear it and nobody looked at it, and it went on for a while after he stopped.

✦

“Mr. Ottley,” Mr. Gedge said. “You have the objection.”

“I have,” Mr. Ottley said, “and I am going to withdraw it.”

He said it into the table. He had his hands folded and he said it into the table and then he lifted his head and said it again, louder, to the room.

“The objection was that this is a matter for the parish and not for the board. Miss Ashby answered it better than I have heard it answered in nineteen years, and Mr. Quill has answered the rest of it, and I am not going to be the man who stands between this board and a thing that three people have now said out loud in front of a clerk with a pen.

“I will say one thing for the record and then I will sit down, and it is not an objection, it is a warning. If this board petitions the bishop’s court about a suppression made in 1806 on a schedule that this board has never seen, then this board is asking a court to look at the way this county has been administered since 1806. There are eleven parishes in this union. There is not one of them that will come out of that examination with its book in the state it was in this morning.

“I am going to vote for it. I want it minuted that I said that first.”

✦

Mr. Gedge put the question.

“Mr. Craddock’s motion, that the remark column of the book of offices of every parish in this union be read at midsummer and every office marked idle be examined. Those in favour.”

Six hands went up.

Mr. Craddock’s was first. Then Mr. Deverell. Then the man with the wooden hand, Mr. Naseby, without any expression at all. Then Mr. Kett of Marle, who had said his bridge was going. Then Mr. Trant. Then Mr. Ottley, last, with his hands still folded, unfolding one of them.

“Against.”

Five hands.

“The ayes have it. The clerk will minute it.” Mr. Gedge turned his glass a quarter turn. “And the petition. Those in favour.”

The same six hands.

“Against.”

The same five.

“The ayes have it. It is to be drawn and laid before this board at the next sitting and transmitted to the bishop’s court.” He looked at the bottom of the table. “Mr. Quill, you will draw it.”

“I will draw it,” Mr. Quill said.

“You will draw it in a form that will not be thrown out.”

“I will draw it in a form that will not be thrown out,” Mr. Quill said, “and it will contain the paragraph I have asked for, and it will be true, and the court will throw it out anyway, and that is not my affair.”

Mr. Gedge looked at him for a moment.

“No,” he said. “It is not.”

He stood up.

“This board is adjourned to the twenty-fourth of June. The board will dine.”

✦

They went out. Twelve men and a clerk and a pike in a glass case, and the door shut behind them, and the room was suddenly a room in an inn again, with a fire that had been drawing for two hours for nobody and a smell of gravy.

Ellen had not moved. Mrs. Trewin had her hand.

My father came round the end of the table and stood in front of Ellen’s chair and did not say anything, and Ellen looked up at him and said, “Well, Josiah.”

“Ellen.”

“Thirty-two years.”

“Thirty-two years.”

“I am not going to say a thing to you in this room,” Ellen said, “because I have four hours and I have spent three of them, and I am going to need the last one for the stairs.”

And my father nodded, and stood back, and put his hat on.

Rooke was still at the side table. He had closed the auditor’s book and he had his hand flat on the cover, and he had not stood up, and he was looking at the table.

Mr. Quill came back into the room alone.

He came back in and shut the door and stood with his back to it.

“Mrs. Rooke,” he said. “I have one thing to say to you and it is not an apology, because an apology is a thing a man offers and a thing the other person may refuse, and I have no right to put you in the position of refusing anything today.

“I want to tell you what happened to your office, because you asked the board and the board did not answer you.

“Your office was not abolished because it was useless. It was abolished because it could not be described. There is a form at the Treasury, and the form has a column for the nature of the office, and there is no word in that column for a woman who answers for the dead. The commission was instructed to reduce four hundred and eleven offices to a schedule that a committee could read in a morning. Yours could not be reduced. So it was marked superfluous, which is the word the schedule uses for a thing that will not fit.

“That is what happened. Not a fraud and not a conspiracy. A column that was four inches wide.”

Ellen sat in her chair with her hands in her lap and looked at him for a long time.

“Then it was a very small thing,” she said, “to have taken a house for.”

“It was.”

“And you have carried it thirty-two years.”

“I have.”

“Well,” Ellen said. “I have carried it thirty-two years as well, and I have had a bed and a lane and a woman who comes at seven, and you have had a chair in a building in Carrow. I do not know which of us got the better of it and I am not going to sit here and work it out.”

She put her hand out. Not to shake. She put it out palm up on the arm of the chair, the way you put out a hand for a thing to be put into it.

Mr. Quill looked at it.

Then he came across the room and put his own hand in it, and she held it, and neither of them said anything for a while, and Rooke got up and went to the window and stood with his back to the room and looked down at the market.

I stood by the door and looked at the floor and counted the boards.

I have wondered since whether I should have read something in that moment. There was a great deal in that room. A woman’s hand and a man’s hand and thirty-two years and a chair with the arms worn pale. Three seconds and I would have known what it was to be either of them.

I did not. Ellen had told me the rule in her own house and I have tried to keep it. You choose. A woman who reads everything is not fit to live in a house.

✦

We went down the stair of the George in a queue, Mrs. Trewin first with Ellen’s arm, then my father with the cushions, then me.

Rooke came behind me.

On the half landing, where the stair turns and the window looks down into the yard, he put his hand out to steady me at the turn, the way a man does, and I took it, and I did not let go when the ground was level, and he did not take his back.

Below us the yard was full of the noise of the dinner and the pump going, and Ellen was on the bench with her eyes shut, and nobody in the world was looking at the landing.

“We won,” I said. Which was not the thing I meant.

“Yes,” he said. “We did.”

“The board has risen,” I said.

“It has,” he said.

“Then I am going to do a thing now,” I said, “and if you tell me no again I am going to hear it and I am going to keep hearing it, so you had better be sure.”

“I am sure,” he said.

I kissed him on the half landing of the George at Carrow with a horse complaining in the yard below and a board dining over our heads. It lasted about as long as the pump going twice. His hand came up and held the side of my face and his other hand held the rail above my head, and he kissed me back, and it was not a chaste thing and it was not a bold thing, it was a true thing, and in all my years of keeping accounts it was the first entirely true thing I had ever done in daylight.

Then I let go of his hand and went on down the stair, and he stood on the landing above me, and I did not look back, and I heard him follow, and his tread was not even, and I counted the steps and smiled at every one of them.

27

The Yard

The yard of the George has a pump in the middle of it. There is a mounting block against the wall and a gallery on three sides on iron posts. The posts are painted the color of a dead leaf. That is the color the George has always been painted and nobody remembers deciding.

We sat in the yard from a quarter past twelve until two.

The board dined in the room we had left. The windows were shut. The noise came out through them anyway. That is a thing about a room full of men at dinner that no glass will stop. Eleven chairs. A dozen knives. Somebody saying a thing that makes the others laugh. Twice, the same joke.

I have thought since that the worst moment of that day was not in the room. It was in the yard. The noise came out of the window above me and Ellen sat on a bench with her eyes shut and her hands in her lap and the smell of somebody else’s dinner came across the cobbles.

A girl came out with a bowl of broth on a tray and said the gentleman had ordered it. Ellen opened her eyes.

“Which gentleman.”

“The one with the wooden hand, ma’am.”

Ellen looked at the bowl for a while. Then she took it and drank the whole of it and gave it back and said, “Tell him thank you, and tell him it was too salt.”

The girl went away with the tray.

“It was not too salt,” Mrs. Trewin said.

“No,” Ellen said. “But a man who sends broth wants to be told about the broth. He does not want to be told thank you.”

✦

Mr. Barrow came out at half past twelve with his hat on and his stick in his hand and stood in front of the bench.

“Mrs. Rooke.”

“Mr. Barrow.”

“I am going to say a thing and then I am going to go back in and eat a chop, and I am not going to make a speech about it because I made a speech at the barn and it was a great deal longer than it needed to be.

“There is eleven hundred and forty-four pounds that came to me out of that rent charge between 1806 and 1817, and I knew where it came from by about 1809, and I took it for eight more years.

He put an envelope on the bench beside her. It was thick and it was folded over and tied with the same twine that goes round a solicitor’s bundle.

“There is two hundred pounds in that and it is the whole of what I have by me in the house. The rest is in land and in a mortgage at Marle and I cannot turn it this month. I will have it by June and I will not have it before June, and I am not going to promise you a thing I cannot do in a month, because a promise in a month is a thing a man makes so that he can leave the room.

“That is what I have. I am not asking you to say anything about it.”

Ellen looked at the envelope and did not pick it up.

“Mr. Barrow, I have been in a lane for twenty-five years and you have walked past the end of it every Tuesday and I have known it every Tuesday. That is the whole of what I have against you, and it is a small thing, and I have known for a long time that it is a small thing, and I have kept it because a small thing is better than nothing when you have not got much.

“Two hundred pounds is not a small thing. I am not going to pretend it is, and I am not going to be gracious about it either, because you have not been gracious to me and it would not be honest.

“Put it in my hand.”

He put it in her hand.

“Now go and eat your chop,” Ellen said.

✦

They came out one at a time.

Mr. Kett of Marle came out. He stood in front of the bench and took off his hat. “I was not on this board in 1806 and I never had a penny of it and I am not going to stand here and be thanked for a thing I did not do. But I have forty pounds in my pocket because I sold beasts this morning and I would rather it went to you than to the bank at Carrow.”

Mr. Deverell the surgeon came out and did not put money down at all. He sat on the mounting block and looked at Ellen and said, “May I,” and she said, “You may,” and he took her wrist and held it and looked at his watch, and then he put her hand back down on her knee.

“You have been like this since when?”

“Since the fourth step.”

“Since the fourth step.” He said it flatly, without any sympathy in it at all, which is the only way a thing like that can be said. “Mrs. Rooke, you have a heart that is doing what it can and it is not doing enough. You will not see June.”

“I know,” Ellen said.

“Do you.”

“I have known since Candlemas. I asked a man in Ely in November and he told me ten months and I have spent five of them.”

Mr. Deverell looked at her.

“Then I will tell you what I would tell my own sister,” he said. “You will get worse in steps and not in a slope and the steps will be shorter. You will have a good week and then a bad one and the good week will not come back at the same height. You are not to go up any more stairs. You are not to be carried up any more stairs, which is worse for you than walking, and I have watched two men carry you and I could see it in your face.

“And you are to have somebody in the house at night.”

“There is nobody at night now,” Mrs. Trewin said. “I come at seven and I go at four.”

“Then that changes this week,” Mr. Deverell said, and stood up, and put his hat on, and went back inside without another word.

Ellen sat with her hands in her lap.

“Well,” she said. “There is a man who does not waste a morning.”

✦

Mr. Naseby with the wooden hand came out. He put down four sovereigns. “I am the only man on that board who has never taken a penny out of a parish and I am not going to start explaining why I am giving this.”

Mr. Trant came out and gave nothing and shook Ellen’s hand and said, “My wife died in 1824 and there was nobody to ask. I did not know there was such an office. I have been on this board nine years and I did not know.”

Mr. Lubbock, the one with the cough, came out last of the five who had voted against. He stood at a distance and coughed into his handkerchief for a while.

“I voted against you,” he said.

“You did.”

“I am going to vote against you again in June. I have eleven parishes and nine of them are behind on their rates and I cannot have a court in Ely opening books, and if you had my books you would do the same, and I know that is not a reason and I am not offering it as one.

“But my sister kept a school at Wendon for thirty years and she died in 1828 and there is a thing I never asked anybody and now there is nobody to ask. So.” He put a folded note on the bench. “That is ten pounds. It is not for you. It is for the office. Do not spend it on bread.”

✦

Mr. Gedge came out at a quarter to two, when the noise in the room had changed from knives to bottles.

He came out without his hat and stood in the yard and looked at the pump for a moment.

“Mrs. Rooke. Miss Ashby. I am going to give you a half hour of my time and it is worth more than the money, and I want you to listen to it carefully, because I am sixty-three and I have chaired that board eleven years and after today I do not expect to chair it much longer.

“The petition will be thrown out.

“It will be thrown out on the first day and it will not be thrown out because the board is wrong or because the case is bad. It will be thrown out because a court does not answer a board. A court answers a party. There is a rule older than any of us: a man who wants a thing from a court must be a man who has been hurt by the thing he is complaining of, and he must be able to say this was done to me. Eleven men sitting at a table in an inn have not had anything done to them.

“So the petition is a piece of paper that will go to Ely and lie in a bundle and be read by a clerk and struck out in a morning, and every man in that room knows it, and they voted for it anyway, and that is what a board does when it has been ashamed of itself.”

“Then what is the use of it,” I said.

“The use of it is that it puts a paper in the bundle with the date on it, and it costs the court a morning to strike out, and a thing that costs a court a morning is a thing that a clerk remembers. That is all it is for. It is a nail. You do not build a house with a nail.

“Now. What you want is a memorial.”

“A memorial.”

“A memorial from a party aggrieved, sworn before a master in the court at Ely, in the name of the person the thing was done to, setting out what was done and what is asked. That is a thing a court must answer. It must be lodged in the registry and it must have a living party on the face of it.

“And that is your difficulty, and I am going to say it in plain words because you are both a great deal tougher than the men in that room think you are.

“A memorial lodged by a dead woman is a memorial with no party. The court will strike it out and it will not even be a decision, it will be a note by a clerk in the margin. Mrs. Rooke, if you die before that memorial is lodged and answered, this ends. Not in a hundred years. In a morning.”

Ellen sat on the bench with the twine-tied envelope in her hand.

“Then I had better not die,” she said.

“Mrs. Rooke, I have just been told by a surgeon that you will not see June.”

“Then it will have to be lodged before June.”

“It will have to be drawn first, and drawn by a man who knows the form, and there are four men in this county who know the form and one of them is in that room eating a chop and he is the only one who will do it for nothing.”

“Mr. Quill.”

“Mr. Quill,” Mr. Gedge said. “And he will do it, and he will do it well, and it will be the last thing he does in that building, and he knows that, and you should know that he knows it, because when a man draws a document that will end him he draws it very carefully.”

He put his hat on.

“Miss Ashby. A word.”

I walked with him to the arch.

“You did a thing in that room that I have not seen done,” he said. “You agreed with the man who was killing you. I have watched men try to beat that objection for eleven years and I have never seen anybody beat it by standing up and saying yes.

“Now I am going to tell you the other half of it, and then I am going to go and drink a bottle of Mr. Barrow’s wine and not think about any of this until tomorrow.

“The half you have not got is this. You won the room. You did not win the board. Six to five is not a majority, it is a Tuesday. If two of those men had been at a funeral this morning you would have lost, and next time there will be a next time and there will be a man at that table who has been told by somebody in Carrow what to do.

“So do not go home and think you have won a thing. Go home and get a memorial drawn and sworn and lodged, and get it done before the woman in that chair dies, and do not spend one hour of the next month being pleased with yourself.”

He put his hand out and I shook it.

“Mr. Gedge.”

“Mm.”

“Why are you telling me this.”

He looked at the market for a moment.

“Because I have sat in that chair eleven years,” he said, “and in eleven years I have never once been in that room when a thing was said that I did not already know. And this morning a girl of eighteen said a thing I did not know, and a man who has been in this county twenty-five years said a thing I did not know, and a woman was carried up a staircase to say a thing I did not know.

“I am sixty-three. I have been a maltster since I was twenty. Nobody has ever said a thing I did not know in a room I was chairing.

“I would like it to happen once more before I die, and I am not going to get it if you lose.”

✦

Rooke came out at ten past two with the auditor’s book under his arm.

He came across the yard and stood in front of me and he did not say anything for a moment.

“Your father has gone to see about the carriage,” he said.

“I know.”

“He is standing in the arch talking to a man about a horse and he has not mentioned this morning once and he will not mention it, and I have been watching him for a quarter of an hour and I want you to know that I have never seen a man do a harder thing than stand in an arch and talk about a horse.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“You said to ask you on the twenty-ninth.”

He looked at me.

“I did,” he said.

“It is the twenty-ninth.”

Somebody in the yard was pumping water. The handle went and the water came and the handle went again. It is a sound that fills a silence better than anything I know. I have never been so grateful to a piece of iron in my life.

“I have a thing to tell you and it is not the thing you want,” Rooke said, “and I am going to say it in this yard in the daylight because if I say it anywhere else I will say it worse.

“In November I signed a copy of the register of this parish. It was a fair copy and it was correct and I signed it, and there is a word in it that was altered afterwards, and I did not look for it because a man does not look for a thing in a page he has signed himself.

“When that memorial goes up, I am the auditor, and I will be asked about that copy, and I will have to say that I signed a page I did not read to the bottom.

“And then I will not be the auditor of this parish any more. Not because they will take it off me. Because an auditor who has signed a page he did not read is not an auditor, and there is no way back from that, and I am forty-four and it is the only thing I have ever been.

“So there is a thing I am going to do in June and it is going to cost me my place, and I am going to do it because it is true, and I want you to understand that I am not doing it for you. If I were doing it for you it would be worth less than nothing, and you would know it, and I would know it.”

“I know,” I said. “That is why I asked you on the twenty-ninth and not in March.”

He almost smiled.

“Now,” he said. “The other thing.”

He took off his right glove.

He folded it once, and put it in my hand, and closed my fingers over it with his own hand, and did not take his hand away.

We stood like that in the yard of the George with the pump going and the board dining over our heads and eleven men within a wall of us who would have made a day of it if they had seen.

“You did not say no on the landing,” I said.

“No,” he said. “I did not.”

“But you are not going to say yes here.”

“Here I am the auditor until the carriage goes through the turnpike,” he said. “You will have the whole of the rest of me somewhere else.”

Then he took his hand away, and it left the shape of itself behind, the way a ring leaves a mark in a glove.

“It is a glove with a hole in the finger and a patch my wife sewed in 1824,” he said. “It is not a ring. I know exactly what it is not. I am not going to stand in a yard and give you a thing and call it something it is not, and I am not going to give you a thing at all that could be mistaken for a promise in front of eleven men and a pump.

“But it is mine and it is in your hand, and it will be in your hand in June, and if in June I am a man of forty-four with no place and a house with four rooms, then I will come and stand in your mother’s kitchen and ask you a question, and I would like you to think about the answer between now and then so that I do not have to stand there in a cold kitchen being looked at.”

I held the glove.

He had given it bare-handed and I had taken it bare-handed, and the touch of them lasted about the length of a breath, and neither of us took the breath. That was the whole of what happened. It kept me awake half the night.

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“I have stopped keeping accounts of what things cost me,” I said. “I stopped in March. It was the only sensible thing I did all spring.”

And he laughed.

Once. Quietly. Not for long. It changed his whole face, and I have thought about it more than I have thought about the board.

✦

The carriage came at half past two and we put Ellen in it with four blankets and a stone bottle of hot water at her feet and Mrs. Trewin on the seat beside her, and she was asleep before the George’s arch was out of sight.

She slept the whole way. She did not wake at the turnpike and she did not wake at the bridge. At the bridge I looked down at the water. It was the color of a roof, and there was a man on the far bank digging.

My father sat opposite me with his hat on his knee and looked out of the window and said, at Wendon, “I have been the clerk of that parish for thirty-one years.”

“I know.”

“They will not take it off me. That is the thing I cannot get used to. I went in there this morning prepared to lose a house and I came out with a piece of paper in my pocket and a man telling me I keep a better account than the board keeps.”

“Papa.”

“Thirty-one years,” he said, “and the only thing I have ever done that anybody will remember is a paper I was not supposed to keep.”

He turned his hat over on his knee.

“I would like to have been hanged for it,” he said. “That is a wicked thing to say in front of my daughter and I am not going to take it back. A man who is punished knows where he is. I have been let off by eleven men who did not want to look at me, and now I have to go home and be a man who was let off.”

The carriage went over the last mile and neither of us said anything.

Ellen woke at the end of the lane and sat up and looked out and said, “There is my roof.”

“Yes.”

“There is a man on it.”

There was a man on it. He had a ladder against the north end where the tarpaulin had been. He was on the roof with a hammer. There were new laths on the ground in the yard and a boy was stacking them.

Ellen looked at that for a long time while the carriage came down the lane.

“Mrs. Trewin,” she said. “Who is that man.”

“I do not know, ma’am.”

“Stop the carriage.”

The carriage stopped. Ellen put her head out of the window.

“You. On the roof.”

The man stopped hammering and looked down and touched his cap.

“Who sent you?”

“Nobody sent me, ma’am. I was told there was a roof at the end of this lane that had been under a tarpaulin eleven years and I came and looked at it, and the tarpaulin has been holding water and two of the principals are gone.”

“Who told you.”

The man on the roof looked at the boy in the yard. The boy looked at the ground.

“A gentleman, ma’am. He come down the lane Tuesday and he says to me, there is a roof, and I says, whose roof, and he says, it is not mine.”

Ellen sat back.

“Get in,” she said. “It is cold.”

28

A Man at the End of the Lane

April came in green and wet and the parish went back to being a parish.

That is the thing nobody tells you about a great day. The day after it, there is a fire to light and bread to set and a woman in a lane who needs a roof over her. The board was on the twenty-ninth of March and on the thirtieth of March the rain came and the roof at the end of the lane was under a tarpaulin that had been holding water for eleven years, and the man on the roof worked through it and on the ninth of April he finished and was paid, and it was not us that paid him, and I never found out who did.

The north end of the house is straight now. You can see it from the bridge. Mrs. Trewin says the water comes off it in a pipe and into a butt and the butt is full and there is a boy who comes to fill a can for the yard.

Hettie went to Collow on the first of April with a bag and came back on the fourth.

She did not like it. She said so at the pump.

“There is nothing to do. She sleeps and then she is awake and there is nothing to do and she does not want to be talked at. I talked at her for two hours the first night and she said, Hettie, you are a good girl and you have talked at me for two hours, and then she said, now sit down and be quiet, and I sat down and was quiet and it was the longest hour of my life.

“And then she said, you can tell me about the duck.”

I stopped.

“What duck.”

“From the joke. The one about being a duck.” Hettie’s mouth did something. “She said, my sister tells me you have a joke about a duck and I have not heard a joke in a year. So I told her. And she laughed. She laughed until she had to hold her side and then she told me to stop, and then she made me tell it again.”

Hettie looked at the pump.

“I am going back on Monday,” she said. “She has a bad week coming and Mrs. Trewin says she does not want to be alone in the bad week and she does not want Mrs. Trewin in the room at night because Mrs. Trewin fusses. I do not fuss. I have never fussed at anybody in my life.”

✦

The man came on the eleventh of April at about eleven in the morning, in a hired gig with a boy driving it, and I saw the gig come down the lane from the churchyard wall and I knew it before I could see who was in it, because nobody comes down that lane in a gig.

He got out at the gate and stood in the road and looked at the pear tree.

He was in a dark coat and he had a case under his arm, and there was mud on his boots to the ankle and he looked at the mud and then he looked at the yard and then he looked at the pear tree again, and I understood that he did not know what to do about the mud and that this was the first time in twenty-five years that the question had come up.

“Mr. Quill.”

“Miss Ashby.” He took off his hat. “I have come to take your aunt’s answers. The court will not receive a memorial sworn at second hand, and she cannot come to Ely, so I have a commission to examine her here and to return her answers under my hand.”

“You could have sent a clerk.”

“I could have sent a clerk,” he said, and stood there with his hat in his hand.

I stood in the yard and looked at him.

“Mr. Quill,” I said. “There is a thing you should know before you go in that door, and I am telling you because nobody has told you and because I would rather you heard it from me in a yard than from my mother in a kitchen.”

“Go on.”

“In 1799 a man came to this house in the evening and sat in that kitchen and asked my mother what the office was for, and she told him, and he told her it was a formality, and she has had thirty-two years to think about that evening. That man called himself Vane.

“She knows who you are. She has known since November. She has not said your name once.”

Mr. Quill stood in the road with the mud on his boots.

“Then I will go in,” he said, “and she may say what she likes to me, and I will write it down, because I have come here with a pen and I am not going to come here twice.”

✦

My mother was at the table with the good cloth on it.

She had put the good cloth on. It was the one with the darn in the middle that she turned to the back, and she had turned the darn to the front this time, and I noticed that and I have never known what to do with it.

There were four cups. There was a plate of bread and there was butter and there was a pot of the damson that Mrs. Nunn had brought in February out of gratitude for a thing my father did with a form.

Mr. Quill came in and stood just inside the door with his hat in his hand.

“Mrs. Ashby.”

“Sit down,” my mother said.

He sat down.

“Now.” She poured the tea. She poured it the way she does, without asking, no sugar, and she put the cup in front of him and turned the handle to the right, and then she sat down opposite him with her own cup and her hands round it.

“You have come about Ellen.”

“I have come to take her answers.”

“Then you will want to be in the other room with her and not in this one with me,” my mother said, “and you will want to be there in an hour, when she has had her sleep, because she has a bad week and she will not be able to say a thing before noon and she will not be able to say a thing after three.”

“I am obliged to you.”

“You are not obliged to me. I am telling you because it is the shortest way to get you out of this kitchen.”

Mr. Quill did not lift his cup.

My mother looked at him over hers.

“You are not drinking your tea.”

“No.”

“Why.”

Mr. Quill sat with his hands on either side of the cup.

“Because in 1799 I sat in this kitchen,” he said, “and you gave me tea, and I told you that the office your sister held was a formality and that you should not trouble yourself about it, and then I got on a horse and went away, and I have thought about this room a great many times since and I have never once been able to remember what I said when you gave me the tea.

“I remember the tea. I remember the cup. I do not remember one word of what I said to you.

“And now I am in this room and there is a cup in front of me and I find that I cannot lift it, and I am fifty-one years old and I have never in my life been unable to lift a cup.”

My mother drank her tea.

“Drink it,” she said. “It is only tea.”

He drank it.

He drank the whole cup and put it down and my mother poured him another one without asking, and he drank that too, and then he said, “Mrs. Ashby, I have a question and it is not part of the examination and you may refuse it.

“In 1806 I asked a woman in a bed in that house what the office was for. I have had twenty-five years to think about the answer and I have decided that I asked the wrong question. So I am going to ask you instead.

“What was it for.”

My mother turned her cup a quarter turn on the cloth.

“It was for the thing that could not be said out loud in this parish,” she said.

“That is not an answer a court will take.”

“You did not ask me in a court. You asked me in my kitchen.” She looked at him. “There is a thing in this parish that everybody knows and nobody says. It has been like that since before I was born. A woman’s sister dies and there was a child in the house and the child is not in the register, and that woman will carry that until she dies and she will not say it to the minister and she will not say it to her husband, because a thing said out loud in a parish becomes a thing the parish owns, and she would have to live in it.

“So she goes down a lane and she asks a woman who has her hand on a thing, and she gets an answer, and she walks home, and she never says it to a living soul.

“That is what it was for. It was for the thing that could not be said out loud.

“And the reason the commission could not put it in a column, Mr. Quill, is not that the column was four inches wide. It is that the office existed in order that a thing should not be written down. You cannot abolish a thing by writing it in a book. You abolished it by writing it in a book, and that is why it went, and that is why it will be very hard to bring back, and I have thought about that for thirty-two years and I would like you to write it down.”

Mr. Quill had taken out his pen.

He wrote for a while.

“This is not evidence,” he said. “This is a woman’s opinion and the court will not read it.”

“Then put it at the bottom where nobody reads,” my mother said, “and in fifty years somebody will read the bottom.”

✦

Ellen was propped up with the blue cloth over the quilt and the book on the chair beside her and the window open four inches because she said a closed window in April was a thing only a well person could afford.

Mr. Quill sat down at the little table with the case open and the paper out and his pen and his ink and his sand, and he arranged them the way a man arranges a thing he has done ten thousand times, and then he said, “Mrs. Rooke, I am going to write down your answers in your own words and I am not going to correct you. If I put a thing in a way you did not say it, you are to stop me. That is not a courtesy. If a thing goes up to that court in my words and not yours, then the court will be reading me, and there are enough of me in that building already.”

“Begin,” Ellen said.

He began at the beginning and he was there two hours.

I sat at the end of the bed and I will put down what I can of it. Some of it I have from the paper, because he left a copy, and some of it is what was in the room.

✦

“State your name and your age and your residence.”

“Ellen Rooke. Fifty. The end of the lane, Nether Collow.”

“You held an office. State when it came to you and how.”

“It came to me in 1801 on the death of my mother, who held it before me, and it came the way it has come for a hundred and twenty years, which is that the woman who has it teaches the girl who will have it and then she dies and the girl takes it. There was no grant and no patent and no admission and no fee. There was a book and a hand and a woman to teach.”

“Was there an endowment?”

“Eleven pounds four a year and six hundredweight of coal a quarter. The endowment behind it was one hundred and four pounds a year, and the rest of it went into the parish and had gone into the parish since before I was born, and I knew that, and there is not a day since 1806 that I have not thought about it.”

“Did you make a return of the office to any officer of the Crown?”

“Never. Nobody ever asked me for one. A man came in 1806 and read my book for above an hour and went away, and no man ever came again.”

✦

“State what the office was.”

“It was the letter of the dead.”

“Use your own words.”

“That is my own words. It is what it is called. A letter is a thing you send and a dead person is who you send it to, and a man in a office in London put the two words together on a schedule and it sounded like a formality, and that is how it got onto a schedule in the first place.

“What it was, was this. A person comes to the door. They have a thing they cannot ask anywhere else. They bring a thing that belonged to the dead, or they do not, and it does not matter, because I do not need a thing. A thing helps. A cup is better than nothing because a cup was held.

“And I put my hand on the thing, or on the table, or on the bed, and I go away for three seconds, and I come back, and I answer the question.

“That is the whole office. There is nothing in it that a man with a pen can write in a column.”

“Three seconds.”

“Three seconds by the thing and not by the clock. Sometimes it is longer. I have had a wall take a quarter of an hour out of me.”

“And the price.”

“There is a price. It is paid at the time or it is paid at four in the morning. A cup is nothing. A wall is a headache for a day. A person is a nosebleed and a thing in the chest that stays a week.”

✦

“Were you taught rules?”

“I was taught four.”

“State them.”

“Never answer a question about money. A person who comes to a letter of the dead to be told where a thing is buried is not asking after the dead, they are asking after the living, and it is not the office.

“Never answer a question about who will die. Not because it cannot be answered. Because it can, and a woman who tells a man the day he will die has taken his life out of his hands and put it in hers, and there is no getting it back.

“Never take money for an answer. Not a penny, not a loaf, not a favor. The endowment pays the office and the office is not for sale, and the day it is for sale it is a trade and not an office and there is no difference in the world between a woman who reads a cup for sixpence and a woman who reads a cup for nothing except the sixpence, and the sixpence is the whole of it.

“And never ask for yourself.”

Mr. Quill’s pen stopped.

“Say that again.”

“Never ask for yourself. A letter of the dead answers the question that is brought to it. If the woman who holds the office brings her own question, then she is standing on both sides of the table, and a thing that answers its own question is not an office, it is a wish.”

“Did you keep them.”

Ellen looked at the window.

“Three of them,” she said.

✦

Mr. Quill wrote for a while.

“Mrs. Rooke. In 1806, after the commission had been to this parish, did you put a question to a thing.”

“I did.”

“What was the question.”

“What becomes of it.”

“And the answer.”

“It comes back in the third generation, and it is a girl, and the water does not take her.”

Mr. Quill’s pen did not move.

“Mrs. Rooke, I must tell you that a court will read that as a woman’s fancy, and it will do the case no good at all, and if it is in the memorial the whole of the memorial will be read in the light of it.”

“I know.”

“Then why have you said it.”

“Because you asked me what I did,” Ellen said, “and I did it, and I am not going to sit in this bed at fifty years old and give a court the answers I think will do the case good. That is what my father did with his life and it did not make him happy and it will not make me happy.

“Write it down. Put it in. And when they strike it out, they will have struck out a thing that was said, and there is a difference between a thing that was struck out and a thing that was never said.”

Mr. Quill wrote it down.

✦

“Mrs. Rooke. I have one more question and it is the one the court will ask, so I must ask it, and I would rather ask it in this room than have it asked in Ely by a man in a wig.

“The office was abolished in 1806. The payments did not stop. For twenty-five years this parish received one hundred and four pounds a year out of the endowment of an office that the Crown had abolished.

“Did you know that the payments had not stopped.”

“I knew.”

“Did you take any step to inform anybody.”

“I did not.”

“Why not.”

Ellen sat up a little.

“Because a woman in a lane does not write to a board,” she said. “I want you to write that down exactly. Because a woman in a lane does not write to a board. I have had twenty-five years and I have thought of the letter I would have written about four hundred times and I could never get past the first line, and the first line was always: I am the woman you abolished.

“And then what. Then a man in an office reads a letter from a woman who says she is the woman we abolished, and he puts it in a bundle, and the bundle goes into a box.

“So no. I did not write. I sat in a house at the end of a lane and the money came four times a year and nobody asked me to stop and I did not stop, and if the court wants to know why, that is why. Not because I was afraid. Because I could not see a way to say it that a man would read.”

✦

At about two o’clock Mr. Quill put his pen down and rubbed his hand.

“Mrs. Rooke. There is a thing I would like to do and it is not in the commission and you may refuse it.”

“Ask.”

“I would like to put my hand on your book.”

The room was quiet.

“You cannot read it,” Ellen said.

“No.”

“Then why.”

“Because I have spent twenty-five years,” Mr. Quill said, “keeping an account of a thing I have never touched, and I find that I would like to have touched it once.”

Ellen looked at him for a long time.

“It is on the chair,” she said. “Put your hand flat on the cover and keep it there and count to five, and when you have counted to five, take it off, and do not say anything clever.”

He got up and went round the bed.

He put his hand flat on the cover of the book. It is a dark cloth cover and the cloth was a coat and there is a buttonhole in the front of it and a bone button, and the thread that sews the button on is not the same color as the coat.

He counted to five. I know he counted to five because his lips moved.

Then he took his hand off and stood there.

“Nothing,” he said.

“Nothing,” Ellen said. “And there is a woman in this parish who has put her hand on that cover and gone away for three seconds and come back and told me a thing about my sister that nobody in this world knew. That is the whole of the difference between us, Mr. Quill, and it is not a difference of sense or of care or of how many years a man has sat in a chair. It is a thing that either happens to you or it does not.

“And the reason I am going to sign that memorial is not that I think a court will give me my office back. I am fifty and I have four hours and I do not want an office.

“It is that in a hundred years there will be a man in that building reading a box, and I would like him to read a page that says a woman in a lane said this out loud in 1831, and that she was not a formality.”

✦

He wrote until three and then Ellen’s voice went and Mrs. Trewin came in and said that was enough and Mr. Quill did not argue.

He packed the case. He sanded the last page and folded the paper and put it in the case and shut the case and stood in the yard with it under his arm.

“Miss Ashby. The memorial will be lodged in the registry at Ely before the twenty-fourth of May. I will draw it and I will swear it and I will lodge it, and there will be a hearing in June or there will not, and if there is not it will be because the court has decided a thing without a hearing, which it is entitled to do.

“There is one paragraph in it that I want you to read before it goes, because you are the only person in this county who will know what it costs.”

He took a page out of the case and gave it to me.

I read it standing in the yard with the pear tree behind me.

And your memorialist further states that the several payments made out of the endowment of the said office, from the year 1806 to the present year, were made by the officers of the registry at Carrow with the knowledge that the said office had been suppressed, and that the continuance of the said payments for the space of twenty-five years is a matter which your memorialist humbly submits cannot be reconciled with the suppression of the said office, and is evidence that the said suppression was not complete and was not intended to be complete.

And your memorialist further states that the file of the said office in the registry at Carrow is defective, and that the schedule and inventory now in the box of the office at Ely were made and deposited in the year 1811 by the deputy registrar of this county, Ambrose Quill, in the place of the papers destroyed by fire at Ely in 1809, and that the same are not copies of any original, and that the said Ambrose Quill has stated the same to the board of guardians at Carrow on the twenty-ninth of March 1831 in the presence of eleven persons.

I read it twice.

“Mr. Quill.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“This says that you made a file out of nothing.”

“It does.”

“And you drew it.”

“I drew it.”

“And you will swear it.”

“I will swear it.”

I stood in the yard with the page in my hand and I could not think of a thing to say that was not either stupid or cruel.

“Then why,” I said, “did you come here in a gig with mud on your boots, when you could have sent a clerk and had the answers in a week and drawn the memorial from them and never come down this lane at all?”

Mr. Quill looked at the pear tree.

“Because I have never been to your parish,” he said. “I said that to you in a corridor in March and it was true and it was the worst thing I have ever said out loud, and I have thought about it every day since.

“I have abolished an office in a parish I had never seen and I have kept the account of it for twenty-five years in a building in Carrow, and the whole of my work has been to make a thing that could be read by a man who was not there.

“I came down this lane because I wanted to be there once.”

He put his hat on.

The boy had the gig turned in the road. Mr. Quill got up into it and sat with the case on his knees and did not look back, and the gig went up the lane between the two walls, and at the top, where the lane comes out by the churchyard, he did look back.

He did not wave. He took his hat off and held it.

Then the gig went round the corner and the lane was empty and there was a hen in it.

I stood out there a long while.

Then I went in and my mother was at the table with the four cups and the plate and the darn in the cloth turned to the front, and she had not cleared any of it away.

“Did he drink his tea,” she said.

“He drank two cups.”

My mother nodded.

“Then he is not a fool,” she said. “A fool would have left the first one.”

29

One of Them Was a Dog

The memorial was lodged in the registry at Ely on the twentieth of May, and we knew because Mr. Quill wrote, and he wrote one line on a half sheet and did not put a compliment at the top of it.

Lodged at eleven this morning. The court sits in July. She should be told that it is lodged and not that it is heard.

I went to Collow on the twenty-first with the letter in my pocket.

Ellen was in the chair by the window. She had been in the chair by the window every day since the twenty-ninth of March, which was a thing Mr. Deverell had forbidden and which she had done anyway, and Mrs. Trewin had given up, and there was a cushion behind her that had come off the parson’s own chair and had never gone back.

I read her the letter.

She listened with her hands in her lap.

“Lodged,” she said.

“Lodged.”

“Not heard.”

“Not heard. In July.”

Ellen looked out of the window at the pear tree.

“Then I have got to June,” she said.

✦

The bad weeks came closer together after that.

I am going to put down what it was like, because nobody puts it down, and because in the summer I found that the thing I wanted most in the world was one page written by somebody who had been through it and had not made it beautiful.

She did not get worse in a line. She got worse in steps, the way Mr. Deverell said, and the steps were shorter than he had said and they came quicker.

The first step was the stairs. She stopped going up them in the middle of May and after that she slept in the little room at the back where the fire is, and Mrs. Trewin moved the bed and the chair and the table with the drawer into that one room, and after that there was nowhere in that house that Ellen could go that was not that room.

The second step was the sitting. She went from a chair with arms to a chair with arms and a cushion behind her to a chair with arms and two cushions and a stool, and then she stopped getting up in the morning and was washed where she lay.

Mr. Rooke came over from Wendon twice a week that spring, and he was the one who lifted her. He put his hands under her arms and took her whole weight like a man lifting a sack of feed, easy at the shoulders, and set her in the chair by the window, and Ellen, who did not thank anybody for anything in this life, said once, “You are stronger than you look,” and he said, “I look exactly as strong as I am,” and she laughed, and I stood in the door and saw the two of them at it and had to go back out to the garden for a minute, because there are things a person cannot watch and keep her face on.

The third step was her hands. They swelled. Mrs. Trewin said it was the heart and that it went to the feet first and the hands last, and I did not ask her how she knew.

The fourth step was that she stopped wanting the window open.

That was the one that frightened me. She had wanted the window open in April when the wind came off the water and she had wanted it open in the rain. On the second of June she asked for it shut and Mrs. Trewin shut it and looked at me across the bed and neither of us said anything.

The light through the curtain was still the light off the water, and it came into the room the color of the inside of a shell and moved on the wall all afternoon, and she lay with her back to it.

✦

She was clear in her head the whole way. That is the thing I did not expect. I had thought that a person going out of the world went out of it a bit at a time and that the head went early. Ellen’s head did not go at all. On the last day she was correcting Mrs. Trewin about the order of the sheets in the press.

She did sleep more. She slept about eighteen hours out of the twenty-four in the last week and she slept in the afternoons and she was awake at night, and Hettie sat with her at night and Hettie was the right person for it because Hettie could be quiet and Hettie could also be woken up at two in the morning to be told a thing about a person who died in 1804 and not mind.

“What is she like at night,” I asked Hettie on the first of June.

“She is like a woman who is going through a box,” Hettie said.

“Going through a box.”

“She says a thing and then she stops and then she says another thing and there is no order in it. She says, the woman from Ashby Parva had a green shawl and she would not put it down, and then she says, Mrs. Trewin, is there bread in the house, and then she says, the man with the wooden hand was not on that board in ninety-eight. It is like a person going through a box and taking out one thing at a time and looking at it and putting it down.”

“Does she talk to you?”

“She talks at me. It is not the same.” Hettie thought about it. “She asked me for the duck again.”

✦

On the third of June she sent everybody out.

She sent Mrs. Trewin out to see about the bread and she sent Hettie out to the yard to bring in the washing because there was rain coming, and she said to me, “Sit down,” and I sat down on the stool by the bed.

“Now,” she said. “I have a thing to ask you and I have been putting it off for a fortnight because it is not a thing a person asks, and I am going to ask it now while I have the breath for it.

“When I am dead. Before they come. Put your hand on me.”

The rain came on the window.

“Ellen.”

“Put your hand on me and ask it something.”

I sat on the stool and looked at her.

“That is the fourth rule,” I said. “That is the one you told me in March. Never ask for yourself.”

“I know what I told you.” She had her eyes shut. “I have kept three of the four for thirty years and I am going to break the fourth one at the end, and I am not going to apologize for it, and you are not to argue with me, because I have four hours in a day and I am not spending one of them being talked out of the only thing I have ever wanted for myself.

“Ask it: was I any good at it.”

I did not say anything.

“Not was it worth it. I know what it was worth. I have had twenty-five years to work out what it was worth and the answer is that it was worth exactly what it cost and no more, and a thing that is worth exactly what it costs is a fair trade and there is nothing to ask about a fair trade.

“Was I any good at it. That is the question. I did it five years and I was thirty-five when I stopped and I have never known. There is nobody who can tell me. My mother taught me and she died in 1801 and she would have said I was good because she was my mother.

“So you will put your hand on me and you will ask it, and whatever it says you will tell me nothing, because I will be dead. But you will know, and you will be the only person in this world who knows, and that is the whole of what I am asking.”

“Ellen, I have read a person once in my life and I was sick for a week.”

“Then you will be sick for a week.”

“And what if the answer is bad.”

“Then it is bad,” Ellen said, “and I will have been bad at it, and you will have to live with knowing that, and I am sorry for that, but I am not sorry enough to not ask.”

She opened her eyes.

“Say you will do it.”

“I will do it.”

“Say it properly.”

“I will put my hand on you and I will ask it whether you were any good at it, and I will not make it a pretty question.”

“Good.” She shut her eyes. “Now go and tell Hettie she can bring the washing in. She has been standing in the yard in the rain for a quarter of an hour because she thinks I cannot hear her feet.”

✦

She died on the fifth of June at about a quarter past four in the morning.

I was not there. Hettie was there and Mrs. Trewin was there and I was at home in my own bed because it was Hettie’s night and I had been at Collow for four days and my mother had made me come home and sleep.

Hettie came up the lane at six with her hair down and her boots in her hand.

She stood in the yard and said, “She is gone,” and then she sat down on the step and cried in the way that people cry who have not done much of it, in big surprised pieces, with her mouth open.

I have thought since that the thing I will remember about Ellen Rooke is that she got a girl who had never sat with a dying person to sit with a dying person, and that on the last night of her life she told that girl a joke about a duck.

✦

She had left instructions and they were in her own hand and they were on a half sheet and they were four lines long.

The blue cloth over me and not the black one.

Mr. Deverell is to be sent for and he is to say I died of the heart and not of anything else, and he is to say it to Mrs. Trewin and not to a committee.

Hettie is to have the chair with the arms and the cushion is to go back to the parson.

Nobody is to be let in before eleven.

I read the third line twice.

Hettie read it over my shoulder and went out into the yard and stayed there for a while.

✦

I did what she asked at about a quarter to six, before the light was properly up, with the door shut.

She was on the bed with the blue cloth over her and her hands outside the cloth because the swelling had gone down and Mrs. Trewin had folded them and they looked like hands again.

I sat on the stool.

I want to put down exactly what I did, because it was not reading, or it was reading and it was something else as well.

I put my right hand flat on the back of her hand.

I did not ask the question out loud first. I asked it first and then put my hand down, the way Ellen taught me in March with the ring, because the thing answers the question you bring and if you bring nothing it answers the question you are carrying.

Was she any good at it.

And I went away.

✦

It was not three seconds by the clock. I have no idea how long it was by the clock. Mrs. Trewin said afterwards that I was on the floor and that she had found me at twenty past six and that the stool was over and that my nose had bled onto the boards.

What I saw was a room.

It was not this room and it was not a room I have ever been in. It had a table and a window and a shelf with cups on it, and there was a woman at the table and there was no face on her, and there was a book open in front of her, and she was writing in it with a pen, slowly, and stopping, and going on.

And there was no sound in the room at all. That is the thing I cannot get anybody to understand. There was no sound. Not a low sound, not a far sound. Nothing. A room with a fire in it and no sound of a fire.

And then the thing that answers, which does not have a voice, put it into me the way a thing is put into you when you are read to as a child and you are nearly asleep, all at once and not one word after another.

Four hundred and eleven questions.

She answered four hundred and eleven and she did not refuse one and she did not take money for one.

She was wrong once.

It was the eleventh of October 1803 and the woman was asking about a dog.

And then, after a moment that was not a moment:

It has been entered.

✦

I came back on the floor with my cheek on the boards and the taste of pennies in my mouth and a pain in my chest like a hand closed on it, and Mrs. Trewin was saying my name from a long way off, and I could not move my right arm for a quarter of an hour.

The nosebleed took a long time to stop. Mrs. Trewin put a cloth under my head and another on my chest and said, “Do not sit up,” and I did not sit up because I could not.

She did not ask me what happened. That is the thing about Mrs. Trewin. She has been in a house with an office for eleven years and she has never once asked a question about it, and I have decided that this is not because she does not believe it but because she has decided that a thing she has not been told is a thing she cannot be made to give evidence about.

At about seven I could sit up. At half past seven I could stand.

My mother came at eight with the bread and found the two of us in that room with the cloth and the blood and she put the bread down and said, “What did you do.”

“I did what she asked.”

“And.”

“And I got an answer.”

My mother stood in the door of that room for a while.

“Was it a good one,” she said.

I sat on the edge of the bed with my hand wrapped in a cloth.

“It was an audit,” I said.

✦

I want to put down the rest of it before I say what I think, because a person reading this will want to know the plain things.

Four hundred and eleven questions.

I went home that night and got the book out and counted.

I counted every line in it. There are seventy-six lines, and forty of them are in the last hand, which is Ellen’s, and thirty-six of them are in the three hands before her, and the lines are not all questions, because some lines are two questions and some lines are a question and a note.

There are two hundred and four questions in that book.

Two hundred and four. That is the number Mr. Rooke said in this kitchen in March and it is the number in the book and it is the number of people in four parishes who have come down a lane in a hundred and twenty years.

Four hundred and eleven is not in that book anywhere.

So the thing that answered me was not counting out of Ellen’s book.

It was counting out of another one.

✦

I sat at that table until it got light.

There is a thing I have to say here and I am going to say it plainly because I have not said it to anybody, not to my mother and not to Mr. Rooke, and I have carried it since June.

I have never been afraid of the dead.

I have been afraid of a great many things in my life. I have been afraid of my father’s silence and of Mr. Craddock’s politeness and of a board of eleven men and of a lane with a house at the end of it. I was afraid in March that I would be a girl with a thimble and I am afraid now that I will be a woman with a patent.

But the dead have never frightened me. A cup tells you it was held. A wall tells you it was built. There is nothing in that but a record, and a record is a thing I have been reading since I was four.

What frightened me in that room on the fifth of June was not the dead.

It was that something had been keeping the account.

✦

My mother came down at six and found me at the table with the book open and the candle burnt out.

“Did you sleep.”

“No.”

“Come and eat something.”

“Mama.” I turned the book round. “There are two hundred and four questions in this book. That is a hundred and twenty years. That is one and a half a year.”

My mother looked at the page.

“Yes,” she said.

“Ellen answered four hundred and eleven.”

My mother sat down.

She sat down slowly and put her hands flat on the table, the way my father does, and she looked at the book for a long time.

“Say that again.”

“Four hundred and eleven questions. She was wrong once, in October 1803, and the woman was asking about a dog.”

“Who told you that.”

“It did.”

My mother sat at that table with her hands flat on it.

“Then there is another book,” she said.

“That is what I think.”

“There has been another book the whole time.” She was not asking. “A hundred and twenty years and four women and a book of seventy-six lines, and there has been another book the whole time, and none of them knew.”

“They knew,” I said. “Ellen knew. She asked it a question in 1806 and got an answer about the third generation and the water, and it was not in her book either. I looked. The page after it is blank.”

My mother got up and went to the window and stood with her back to me, and I sat at the table and looked at her back and did not say anything, because I have learned when not to say anything.

Then she said, “Your grandmother had a thing she said. I heard her say it twice and I did not understand it either time and I never asked her, and now there is nobody to ask.

“She said: we are the ones who are asked. We are not the ones who are counted.”

The sun came up over the roof of the barn and into the kitchen and my mother stood at the window in it.

“Write it down,” she said.

“Write what down.”

“All of it. The four hundred and eleven and the dog and the other book and what she said before she died.” She turned round. “You are the third and there is nobody left to teach you, and if you die in a bed at fifty there will be a fourth and she will have nothing but a book with seventy-six lines in it and a story about a dog that nobody can explain.

“So write it down. Not for a court. Not for a board. For her.”

✦

I got the book out that afternoon and I turned to the last page, which is the page after the blank one, and I wrote in it in ink, and my hand was steady.
