1831. June. Q: was she any good at it.

A: four hundred and eleven. She was wrong once. It was a dog.

And underneath, because I did not know what else to put and because a page with a question on it and an answer under it looks like a finished thing, I wrote:

Entered.

30

The Bell

They buried Ellen Rooke on the seventh of June, and the bell of Nether Collow rang for her at four in the morning and again at eleven.

The bell has rung for every death in that parish for three hundred years. It is in the tower and it is the third of three and the other two have not been rung since 1791 because the frame is rotten on that side, and the great bell hangs by itself in the dark with a rope coming down through a hole in the floor.

For nineteen years it has been rung by old Dunnet, who is seventy-one and has a bad shoulder, and who has never been paid a penny of the six pounds.

I did not know that until the fifth of June. What I knew, and what everybody in the parish knew, was that the bell rang when somebody died, and that it would ring for you when your turn came, and that this was the way the parish had of saying a thing without saying it.

I heard it at four in the morning of the seventh and I sat up in bed.

It is not a musical bell. It is cracked somewhere high up in the crown and it has a note that goes on after the strike like a man holding a vowel longer than he meant to. It fills the lane. It comes in through a window and it is in the room with you and there is nowhere in that parish to go to get away from it.

The whole parish heard it. There is not a house in Nether Collow where nobody woke up.

✦

The funeral was at eleven and the church was full and it was full of the wrong people.

I had expected the parish. I got the parish, and eleven people from Collow who had not spoken to Ellen in twenty-five years, and Mr. Barrow in a black coat with a hatband, and Mr. Craddock at the back standing up straight, and Mr. Naseby who came fourteen miles in a trap, and two women from Marle that I had never seen and who turned out to be Kett’s daughters.

Mrs. Nunn came. She stood at the very back with the child on her hip and she had put a clean apron on the child and not on herself.

And there was a man at the back on the left in a dark coat who came in after everybody was seated and stood by the font and did not sit down, and nobody knew who he was, and I did.

Mr. Quill stood at the back of that church with his hat in his hands for the whole of the service and left before the coffin came out.

✦

Mr. Tice took the service and he took it badly.

The east window of that church is old glass and nobody knows what the saint in the middle of it is holding, and at eleven in June the sun comes through it and puts a figure on the aisle in red and green and gold. The coffin went down the aisle through the middle of the figure, and it was the only thing that morning that was true.

He is sixty-eight and he has been the vicar of that parish since 1794 and he has buried four hundred people. He read the lesson and he read the psalm and he said the right things in the right order, and there was nothing in any of it that had anything to do with the woman in the coffin, and I stood in the second pew and listened to a man say that the deceased was now at rest and I thought: she is not at rest, she has been at rest for eleven years, and what she was doing before that was answering questions in a kitchen.

I am not fair to Mr. Tice. He did what he was taught to do and he did it in a cold church at eleven in the morning for a woman he had visited twice a year for twenty-five years and had never once asked a question.

But I will put this down because it is true and because it is the reason I did the thing I did in July.

When the coffin came out of the church, Mr. Tice stood at the door and shook hands with people, and when I came past he took my hand in both of his and said, “She is with her Savior, Miss Ashby.”

And I said, “She is with her sister.”

Mr. Tice looked at me.

“I beg your pardon?”

“Nothing,” I said. “It was a thing she said.”

✦

The grave is at the top of the churchyard against the wall, and there is a yew, and the yew is older than the church and has been cut back so hard on the lane side that it has a flat face.

There is no stone. Ellen did not order one and there was not money in the house for one and nobody thought of it, and I stood there while they filled it in and thought about a woman who had spent five years being asked about the dead and had not left a line for anybody to read.

That is going to be put right and I will come to it.

Mr. Craddock held one of the ropes.

I saw it and did not understand it for a moment, because a man in a good black coat does not hold a rope at a funeral unless he is family. And then I looked at the tower door and old Dunnet was standing in it with his hat off and his hands at his sides, and Mr. Craddock had the second rope and the sexton had the first.

Afterwards, in the lane, I asked him.

“Mr. Craddock. Why did you hold the rope.”

He had his hat in his hand and his gloves in the hat.

“Because I rang the bell,” he said.

“You did not.”

“I did. At four. I went up at half past three and Dunnet was at the rope and I took it out of his hands.”

“Mr. Craddock.”

“He said, it pulls to the left, you will want to lean.” Mr. Craddock looked at the churchyard wall. “That is all he said. Nineteen years that man has rung that bell for nothing and the only thing he had to say to me when I took it off him was how it pulls.”

He put his hat on.

“I have written to the board,” he said, “and I have asked them to take the office off me and give it to Dunnet with the six pounds, and I have asked them to do it at midsummer, and they will do it because they are eleven men who have just had a fright and a thing that costs six pounds is the cheapest way to feel better.

“That is not why I did it. I want that understood and I am not going to explain it in a lane.”

“Then when.”

“At the board,” he said. “I will explain it at the board, in the minutes, and then I will go home.”

✦

There was food in the house at the end of the lane, and there was a great deal of it, and none of it was ours.

That is how a parish does it. Nobody asks. The bread came from four houses and the ham came from the miller and there was a pie that Mrs. Nunn made and a jug of beer that a man put on the step and went away without knocking, and Mrs. Trewin and Hettie put it all out on the table in the little back room where Ellen had died four days before, and forty people stood in that house and ate and talked in low voices and looked at the roof.

I stood in the yard for a while because I could not be in that room.

Mr. Rooke was at the funeral. He stood at the back through the service, and he carried one corner of the coffin from the lych gate to the grave, which is not a thing an auditor does, and nobody stopped him, and he did not look at me once the whole time, which is how I knew he was keeping himself the way you keep a hand flat on a lid.

He found me in the yard.

He did not say anything. I did not say anything. Then I found that I was crying, for the first time since the fifth of June, in a yard, in front of a house with forty people in it, and he put his arm round my shoulders and turned me a quarter toward the wall so that the parish got my back and not my face, and he held me like that while I did it.

He did not say there, there. He said nothing at all. His coat smelled of the rain it had kept off me at Ely in March, and I stood in the one place in the county where I was not the Letter of the Dead or the clerk’s daughter or the girl with the thimble, which was the square yard of shadow under a man’s arm, and I stayed in it until I was done.

He stepped back when Mr. Barrow’s stick sounded on the flags.

Mr. Barrow came out and stood beside me and looked at the roof.

“That is a good job,” he said.

“It is.”

“Two of the principals were gone. It would have come down in another winter.”

“I know.”

Mr. Barrow turned his stick over in his hands.

“Miss Ashby. I am going to say a thing to you that I have been putting off for a week and it is not a pleasant thing and I would rather say it in a yard than in front of forty people eating ham.

“The house is not hers.”

I looked at him.

“She has been in it twenty-five years and she has never paid a rent and nobody has ever asked her for one, and that is because the house goes with the office, and the office was abolished in 1806, and there has never been a person in this county who wanted to be the one to write the letter.

“She is dead. A dead tenant is a letter.

“The commissioners will ask for it before Michaelmas. It will go to the Crown or it will go to whoever has the reversion, and I have spent a week trying to find out who has the reversion and I cannot, and that is not because it is a secret. It is because nobody has looked at it since 1806.”

I stood in the yard and looked at the roof that a man had mended for nothing at the end of March.

“Then we have until Michaelmas,” I said.

“Miss Ashby, I do not think you have until Michaelmas. I think you have until somebody in an office in London reads a return and notices that a house is occupied by nobody and has been for a quarter of a century, and that will happen when it happens and it will not be announced.”

He put his hat on.

“I will do what I can,” he said. “I am seventy-one and I have eleven hundred pounds to find and a reputation that is not worth what it was in March. But I will do what I can, and I want you to know that I am not doing it for her, because she is dead and it would be a strange thing to do for a dead woman.

“I am doing it because I have spent forty years being a man who signs things and I have found out what that is worth and I do not care for it.”

✦

The house emptied at about four.

Hettie washed the plates and Mrs. Trewin put the little room back the way it had been, and then they both stood in the yard with their bags and cried for a while and then went home, and Hettie took the chair with the arms and the cushion went back to the parson, and I watched her go up the lane with a chair on her back.

Then I sat down at my mother’s table with two things in front of me.

The first was the letter.

It is folded in three and it is addressed to my father and the wax is broken and it has never been opened. He gave it to me in March and told me to open it and I have carried it since March and not opened it, and I am not going to pretend that was delicacy. I did not want to know what was in it. There was a version of my father’s life in which that letter said what he thought it said and he had been right to stay away, and there was a version in which it did not, and I had a use for the first version and I did not want it taken away from me.

My father came down at half past four and sat down opposite me.

“You have got it out,” he said.

“I have got it out.”

“Open it.”

“Papa.”

“Open it,” he said. “I have carried it nineteen years and I have got as far as I am going to get.”

I opened it.

The paper is thin and it has been folded so long that the folds have gone soft and there is a small tear at the top where somebody’s thumb went through it. The hand is Ellen’s, which is a hard upright hand with the letters leaning a little at the end of a word, and she wrote it with a pen that was going.

There are four words and there is no date and there is no name at the top.

You may come in.

✦

I read it out loud because he asked me to read it out loud.

Then I put it on the table between us.

My father sat and looked at it.

He looked at it for a long time and I did not say anything, and my mother came to the door of the kitchen and looked at the two of us and went away again.

Then he laughed.

It was one sound and it was not a good sound and it is the only time in my life I have heard my father laugh like that.

“Nineteen years,” he said.

“Papa.”

“Nineteen years.” He put his hand over his mouth. “I have thought about that letter every day since 1812. Every day. I have thought about it in that chair and on that road and in the vestry and in this kitchen, and I have had a great many versions of it, and I have been afraid of every one of them.

“And it says you may come in.

“I could have gone in in 1812. In 1813. In 1819, when I stood at the top of that lane and turned round and went home because there was a child in the garden. In 1826 when I had the whole of the speech made. Nineteen years and a woman sitting in a bed at the end of a lane with the door open, and I did not go in, and there was nothing stopping me but a thing I had made up myself in 1812 and then carried around for nineteen years like a man carrying a chair.”

He picked the letter up.

“I would like to keep this,” he said.

“It is yours.”

He folded it and put it in his pocket.

Then he got up and went to the dresser and opened the bottom drawer and took out a parcel wrapped in brown paper, and he unwrapped it on the table.

The gloves.

Gray wool, and the paper tag still on them with the string through it, and the tag has gone brown at the edges and the writing on it is Ellen’s: for J. A. and no thanks.

He put them on.

They were a good fit. She had known his hands.

He stood in the middle of the kitchen with a pair of gray gloves on and his hands held a little away from his sides, the way a man holds his hands when he has just put gloves on and does not know what to do next.

“They are warm,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Nineteen years in a drawer and they are warm.”

He took them off and put them back in the drawer and shut it.

“I am going to Ely,” he said.

✦

Mr. Quill’s letter came the next morning and it was the second thing on the table.

Miss Ashby. I am required to inform you of a thing that will be in the Ely paper on Friday and that you should have from me first.

The registrar has moved the court to strike out the memorial of Ellen Rooke on the ground that the party is dead and the matter is therefore without a complainant. The motion will be heard on the eighth of July.

I am required to appear in support of the motion. I have been so required by the bishop’s secretary in writing and I have no choice in the matter, and I am telling you this because you will hear it from somebody else and it will be said in a way that makes it worse than it is.

I will appear. I will make the motion in the words the court requires. And I will make it badly.

There is one thing I am able to tell you and I have spent three days deciding whether I am able to tell it, and I have decided that I am, because a man who keeps a thing to himself in order to feel honest is not honest, he is vain.

When a court strikes out a memorial for want of a party, the matter is at an end. It cannot be brought again. The register is closed.

So the eighth of July is the whole of it. If nobody stands in that court and answers the motion, the office at Nether Collow is extinguished on the eighth of July 1831 and there will never be another question asked about it in any court in England.

I am not permitted to tell you who might stand. I am a party to the motion and I have already gone further than I am allowed to go.

I am sorry for the letter I have written. It is the most useful thing I have ever done and it is the worst thing I have ever done, and I do not expect to be forgiven for it and I would not accept it if it were offered.

A. Quill

✦

My father read it over my shoulder and put it down.

“Who can stand,” he said.

“That is the question.”

He sat down. “The party is dead. The party’s husband is dead. She had no children.”

“She had a sister.”

“Your mother is not a party. Your mother was never in that office. She is a woman who watched it happen in a kitchen and there is no writ in England that will make her a party to it.”

“Then there is nobody.”

“There is nobody,” my father said, “unless the thing that was done was done to something that is not a person.”

I looked at him.

He had his hands flat on the table and he was looking at the letter.

“Say that again.”

“A court will not hear a man for another man’s wrong,” he said. “But a court will hear a man for a wrong done to a body. A corporation. A parish. A charity. A thing that cannot speak for itself and must be spoken for by somebody. That is the whole of the law of the church and it is nine hundred years old and it is the only door in that wall.

“The office at Nether Collow was not Ellen Rooke’s. It was never hers. It was the parish’s, and she held it, and when they abolished it they did not take it off her, they took it out of the book. And the endowment is a charity of this parish and it has been paid for thirty-two years out of a rent charge on land in this parish.

“The parish has been wronged. Not her. The parish. And a parish can stand in a court through a man.”

He looked up.

“Which means the eighth of July is not about Ellen,” he said. “It was never about Ellen. It is about whether the parish of Nether Collow has been robbed, and that is a thing a court has been answering since before the Normans, and there is not a judge in England who will strike it out as a formality.”

I sat at that table and looked at my father.

“You have known this for a month,” I said.

“I have known it since March,” he said. “I have known it since the day I read that paper in the long room and I have not said it, because to say it means the parish must go to Ely and stand in a court and be a party to a suit against the registry, and the man who would have to stand there for the parish is the clerk of the parish, and the clerk of the parish is the man who kept two accounts for thirty-two years and will be asked about it under oath on the first morning.

“So I have been sitting on it,” he said. “I have been sitting on it for three months and I have watched you go up and down that lane and I have said nothing, and I am ashamed of it, and I am telling you now.”

He stood up.

“I am going to Ely,” he said. “Not to see about a horse.”

✦

My mother came in at that point and looked at the two of us and at the letter on the table.

“What has happened.”

“Nothing,” my father said. “Everything. Get me the good shirt.”

“The good shirt is in the wash.”

“Then get me the other good shirt.”

My mother stood in the door with her hands wet.

“Josiah.”

“Mary Ann.”

“You will lose the office.”

“I will lose the office,” my father said, “and I have been a clerk for thirty-one years and I have never once been able to say a thing in a room where it would cost me, and I would like to do it once before I die, and it appears that the only way a man in this county can get a thing done is to be willing to lose a house.

“Get me the shirt.”

She got him the shirt.

31

The Presentee

The board met at Carrow on the twenty-fourth of June and there were eleven books on the table.

They came in a crate. I saw it carried up the fourteen steps by two men. It was a good crate with a lid and a hasp. The books were stacked inside with paper between them. Each one had a label on the spine in the hand of the clerk of that parish.

Mr. Craddock read the report himself. He stood up and read it for an hour and a quarter and nobody interrupted him once.

Thirty-one offices in eleven parishes were marked idle in the remark column.

The office of the pound at Marle, three pounds a year, paid to a man whose job was to shut a gate on stray beasts, and the pound had been built over in 1819 and there was a cooper’s shed on the site.

The office of the swans at Wendon, fifteen shillings, for the marking of swans on the navigation, and there had been no swans marked on that water since 1804, and the money had been paid every Lady Day for twenty-seven years to a man whose father had held it before him.

The office of the clock at Ashby Parva, four pounds a year, paid to a man who had not wound a clock since the tower came down in 1823.

And twenty-eight more. A dole, a lamp, a well, a gate, a ferry, a seat in a church that had been taken out in 1811.

The money paid out on those thirty-one offices in the last twelve months was two hundred and forty-one pounds six shillings.

Mr. Craddock read the figure and put the paper down and stood there.

“That is not a fraud,” he said. “I want the board to hear me say that, because there is a word for a thing like this and it is not the word. Thirty-one men in eleven parishes have been paid for thirty-one things that stopped happening, and not one of them set out to rob anybody, and the greater part of them did not spend the money on themselves, and I know that because I am one of them and I did not.

“What it is, is thirty-one men being polite to each other for thirty years. Nobody would write the letter. Nobody would be the man who wrote to a widow at Wendon and told her that the swans were finished. So the money went on being paid and the book went on being signed and the remark column went on being written in and read by nobody, and the whole of it held together because every man at this table trusted every other man at this table not to look.”

He sat down.

The board took a quarter of an hour over it. Then it did the practical thing, which is what a board does when it has been given a number it cannot argue with. It ordered the thirty-one offices closed at Michaelmas and the money returned to the several parishes. It ordered the office of the bell at Nether Collow continued and granted to William Dunnet for his life at six pounds a year. And it ordered that the one hundred and fourteen pounds paid to Mr. Craddock on that office be repaid by him to the parish at twenty pounds a year.

Mr. Craddock voted for all three.

✦

Then Mr. Gedge said, “The book of Nether Collow. The clerk will read the remark column.”

My father stood up at the side table. He had been sitting there with the parish book open in front of him for an hour and a quarter. He did not read.

“Mr. Ashby.”

“Sir.” My father had his hand flat on the page. “There is an entry in this book that I have signed thirty-one times.”

“Read it.”

“The entry is in the hand of my father, who was clerk before me, and it is dated the tenth of April 1801, and it is the admission of Ellen Rooke to the office of the letter of the dead at Nether Collow on the death of her mother, by teaching, without fee, the endowment being eleven pounds four shillings a year and six hundredweight of coal a quarter.

“And against it, in the remark column, in my father’s hand, there is a line.”

“Read the line.”

My father read it.

“Suppressed by commission, 1806. Endowment carried to the parish account. No return required. Office not to be struck out of this book until the county register is corrected.”

Nobody said anything.

“That is the whole of it,” my father said. “It has been in this book since 1806. I have opened this book and signed the foot of the page four times a year for thirty-one years, and I have never read the remark column, because a remark column is not a thing a clerk reads. A clerk reads the entry. The remark is what somebody wrote for the next man, and the next man is always somebody else.

“My father wrote that line for me. He wrote it in 1806 and he died in 1812 and he never said a word to me about it, and I have signed the page under it eleven hundred times.

“The office was never struck out of this book. It is in this book now, with a woman’s name against it, and the woman is dead, and the office is still in the book.”

He shut it.

“That is my account,” he said. “I have nothing to add to it and I would like it minuted.”

✦

Mr. Barrow stood up at four o’clock with the light coming in flat off the market and said, “I have a motion and it is not a motion about money and I will be short.

“This parish has an interest in that office. It has had an interest in it since before any of us were born, and it has taken a hundred and four pounds a year out of it for thirty-two years, and a man who has taken the profits of a thing for thirty-two years cannot stand up in a court and say the thing does not exist.

“The eighth of July is a motion to strike out the memorial on the ground that the party is dead. The party is not dead. The party is this parish, and this parish can stand in that court through a man, and it can only stand there if it means to present somebody to the office if the office be held to exist.

“So I move that this parish do appear at Ely on the eighth of July by its vicar, and that it do present Mercy Ashby of this parish to the said office, and that the costs be borne by the parish and by me, in equal parts, and I will undertake the whole of them if the parish will not.”

Mr. Ottley asked whether the parish could lawfully present to an office that had been suppressed, and Mr. Gedge said that was a question for the court at Ely and not for a board in an inn, and Mr. Ottley said that was a very good answer and sat down.

Eight hands.

Three against.

“The ayes have it,” Mr. Gedge said. “The clerk will minute the presentation and the vicar will attend.”

He turned his glass a quarter turn.

“Miss Ashby. Stand up.”

I stood up.

“I have chaired this board eleven years,” Mr. Gedge said, “and I have presented two men to two offices in that time and both of them were men of forty and I said the same six words to both of them and I am going to say them to you, and I want the board to note that I am saying them in the same form, because a form is a thing that holds whether the person inside it is the right shape or not.

“It is your office to hold and to answer for.”

He sat down.

“Mr. Quill will draw the presentation,” he said. “The board will dine.”

✦

The consistory court at Ely sits in a room on the first floor of the bishop’s palace. It has three windows on the north side and a portrait of a bishop over the door.

The bishop in the portrait has a bad wig. It is not the painter’s fault. The bishop was sixty-eight when it was painted and had a wig that did not fit him. He had insisted on wearing it. The painter had painted what was there.

Dr. Merivale sat behind a table with a green cloth and a copy of the pleadings and a small glass of water. He was sixty and he had the post because he was the only clergyman in the diocese who could read a Latin deed without moving his lips.

There were nine people in the room.

Dr. Merivale, and a clerk at a side table, and Mr. Quill at a table by himself with his papers, and Mr. Tice in his best black with his hands folded, and my father, and Mr. Barrow, and Mr. Craddock, and me.

And Mr. Rooke, at the back by the door, because the auditor of the county had a right to a seat at the court and had used it.

And a man I did not know, at the back. He turned out to be the bishop’s secretary, and he was there to see that nothing was said that would have to be reported.

It was the eighth of July and it was hot. The windows were open and there was a smell of cut grass from the palace garden and, underneath it, the smell of the river.

✦

“Motion of the deputy registrar,” Dr. Merivale said, “to strike out the memorial of Rooke and Ashby on the ground that the party is dead. Mr. Quill.”

Mr. Quill stood up.

He was in a dark coat and his papers were square to the edge of the table and he had three pens.

“If it please you, sir. The memorial was lodged in this registry on the twentieth of May by Ellen Rooke of Nether Collow, widow, and Mercy Ashby of the same parish, spinster. Ellen Rooke died on the fifth of June. The memorial asks this court to declare void the suppression of an office at Nether Collow in the year 1806 and to restore the said office.

“The rule is that a party who asks a court for a remedy must be a person to whom the thing complained of was done. Ellen Rooke was that person. She is dead. The matter is therefore without a complainant and it is submitted that it cannot proceed.”

“That is the motion,” Dr. Merivale said. “Have you anything else to say to it?”

“I have a statement to make which is not in support of the motion.”

“Then why are you making it.”

“Because I am required to by a thing I have done, sir, and not by this court.”

Dr. Merivale looked at him for a moment.

“Go on.”

“The memorial states that the file of the office in the registry at Carrow is defective, and that the schedule and inventory in the box at Ely were made in 1811 by the deputy registrar in the place of papers destroyed by fire in 1809, and that they are not copies of any original.

“That statement is true. I made those documents. I made them out of my memory and out of what I could establish and I made them correctly and I called them a file. The originals were burnt at Ely in 1809 and I have known for twenty-two years that what is in that box is my own hand and not a copy of anything.

“That is my statement and I ask that it be entered.”

The clerk’s pen went on for a while.

“Mr. Quill,” Dr. Merivale said. “You are the moving party. You are asking the court to put an end to this matter and you have just told the court that the file on which the matter depends was made by you.”

“I have, sir.”

“Are you aware that this court may now require the file to be produced and may inquire into how it came to be made?”

“I am aware of it.”

“And that the consequence to you personally may be the loss of your office.”

“I am aware of that also.”

Dr. Merivale sat back.

“Then the court will hear the parish,” he said. “Mr. Tice.”

✦

Mr. Tice stood up and put his hands on the back of the chair in front of him.

“If it please you, sir, I am the vicar of Nether Collow and I have been since 1794 and I am here for the parish.

“I am not a lawyer and I have nothing to say about the rule. The parish has employed no counsel. Mr. Barrow offered to and I said no, and I said no because I have been in that parish thirty-seven years and I have never once said a thing in a room where it would cost me anything, and I have a great deal to say to this court and none of it is any use, and I would rather come and say it and be thought a fool than stay at home.

“In 1806 there was a commission in this county and an office in my parish was suppressed, and I was told of it by a man on a horse and I said that it was a pity and I did nothing.

“In the twenty-five years since, there has been a woman at the end of a lane in my parish in a bed, and I have visited her twice a year, and I have prayed with her, and I have never once asked her what she did before she was in that bed, and if I had asked her I would have known, and if I had known I would have had to do something.

“So I am here. That is all I have. I am here and I would like it entered that the vicar of Nether Collow attended.”

He sat down.

Dr. Merivale wrote something.

“Mr. Tice, the court is obliged to you,” he said, “and the court observes that a man who has nothing to say is generally the only honest witness in the room.”

✦

“Is there a presentee?”

“I am, sir,” I said.

I stood up. I had on the gray dress that had been my mother’s and cut down. The ring was on my right hand with the string round the back of it, because it is a ring made in 1711 for a hand that was not a woman’s. I had the book in my pocket, and my father’s page, and the toll book dates, and eleven shillings and fourpence written out in figures. I did not take any of it out.

Mr. Rooke was at the back by the door. I did not look at him once the whole morning, because looking at him would have been a thing I would have had to account for, and I had nothing left over for accounting. But I knew where he was the whole time, the way you know where the fire is in a cold room.

“You are Mercy Ashby.”

“I am.”

“You are presented by the parish of Nether Collow to an office which this court suppressed in 1806. What do you say to the motion that this matter is at an end?”

I had thought about it for a month. I had written four speeches and burnt all four. Standing there I found that I had none of them, and that what I had was one sentence my father had said at a kitchen table in June.

“Sir, the party is not dead.

“The party is a parish. A parish does not die. That is the whole reason there is such a thing as a parish. It is the one thing in this country that is not allowed to die, and the law has been at pains about it for nine hundred years, and a man may not be buried in a parish he does not belong to and a parish may not be dissolved because the people in it have died, because the parish is not the people in it.

“The office at Nether Collow was the parish’s office. Ellen Rooke held it. She did not own it. When this court suppressed it in 1806 it did not take it off her, it took it out of a book. And the parish has gone on paying out of its endowment for twenty-five years and it is paying still.

“And there is a thing in the parish book at Nether Collow which this court has not seen and which I ask leave to put in. It is the entry of the office in the book of offices of that parish, made in 1801, with the endowment against it, and in the remark column, in the hand of the clerk, three lines written in 1806: Office not to be struck out of this book until the county register is corrected.

“The parish has never struck it out. It is in the book now. It was in the book this morning.

“So the party is not dead and the office is not gone and I am not asking this court for a favour. I am asking it to look at a register that does not agree with itself, which is the one thing a court of this church exists to put right, and I have brought the book.”

I put the parish book on the table.

Dr. Merivale looked at it for a while without touching it.

“Miss Ashby, you are eighteen years old.”

“I am.”

“Who wrote your speech.”

“Nobody, sir. My father said one thing at a table in June and I have said it in my own words.”

“Your father is the clerk of that parish.”

“He is.”

“He is also the man who kept the accounts.”

“He is.”

“Has he anything to say to this court?”

My father stood up.

“Yes, sir,” he said. “I have kept two books for thirty-two years. I have brought the one that is not the parish book.”

✦

They kept my father an hour.

I am not going to put all of it down. He was asked about the two accounts and he answered every question and did not once say a word in his own defence, and when Dr. Merivale asked him why he had kept the second account he said, “Because I wanted there to be a true one somewhere, sir, and it did not seem to me that I could make the parish one true.”

And when Dr. Merivale asked him whether he understood that he had been guilty of a misfeasance in his office for thirty-two years, he said, “I do, sir, and I have known it every quarter of every one of those years, and I have never once been able to find a way to stop that did not put my wife in the road.”

And Dr. Merivale said, “Mr. Ashby, this court is not a court of criminal jurisdiction and I am not going to make you a martyr in front of your daughter. Stand down.”

Then he took off his spectacles and rubbed his eyes and said the thing that ended the morning.

“The motion is refused.

“It is refused on the narrowest ground available to me, and I want the parties to understand that I am refusing it on that ground and not on the wider one, because a court that decides a large question on a small motion is a court that has decided nothing.

“On the face of the memorial there are two complainants. The second of them is alive and is in this room and has been presented by a parish which has an interest. The motion is therefore bad on its face and I do not have to trouble myself with the parish.

“The court will hear the memorial in October.

“The registrar will produce the file of the office at Nether Collow, and the schedule and inventory deposited in the year 1811, and the whole of the correspondence of the commission of 1806 that survives, and the book of offices of the parish of Nether Collow, which is now in the custody of this court and will be returned to the parish when this matter is at an end.

“And the court will be obliged if the deputy registrar will remain in the building until he is sent for.”

Mr. Quill said, “I will remain, sir.”

✦

He was in the corridor when I came out. He stood at the far end by a window with his hands behind his back, looking down into the palace garden, where a man was mowing.

I went down the corridor and stood beside him.

“Mr. Quill.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“You did not have to say that about the file.”

“I did.” He did not turn round. “It was in the memorial. I put it there myself. A thing in a memorial is a thing a court may ask about, and if I had kept quiet and the court had asked, I would have been a man who was asked and did not answer. That is a worse thing to be than a man who is sent for.”

“They will take the office off you.”

“They will take the office off me, and they will be right to, and I have known since the twenty-ninth of March that it was coming and I have had three months to get used to it, and I am not used to it at all.”

He turned round.

“You did not argue the file,” he said. “In there. You had it. You could have stood up and said the file was made by me and the court would have had to look at it and the whole thing would have come down in an hour. It would have been the shortest way and it would have been true.

“You argued the parish instead.”

“My father argued the parish.”

“Yes,” Mr. Quill said. “He did. And you had the sense to say so in front of a court.”

He looked out of the window again.

“I have been in that building twenty-five years and I have never once been in that court. I have drawn four hundred documents for it and I have never heard one of them read out loud.

“Thank you for the morning.”

He went back in.

Mr. Rooke was at the end of the corridor by the other window. He had been there the whole morning and I had not once looked at him, and now the morning was over and there was nothing left in the day that could be taken away from me for looking.

I went and stood with him.

He did not say anything about the court. He took my hand, in a corridor, in daylight, with a bishop’s secretary at the far end of it and a man mowing the grass below, and he held it, and we looked out of the window together, and neither of us said one word the whole time, and it was the loudest thing anybody has ever said to me.

We both knew what it was going to cost him. It was in his hand, in the way his hand held on. He held on anyway.

✦

They gave judgment in October and it was four pages and it was dry and I have read it above two hundred times.

The court found that the entry of the office at Nether Collow in the schedule of 1806 was made upon a reason stated in the schedule, being that no return had been made of the said office since 1794, and that no return had been required of the said office at any time, and that a reason which is not true in fact cannot support a suppression.

It found that the office had continued to be paid out of its endowment for twenty-five years by the officers of the registry, and that the payments were either lawful, in which case the office was not suppressed, or unlawful, in which case the officers of the registry had for twenty-five years applied to the relief of the poor of a parish money which they had no authority to apply, and that the court would not lend itself to either conclusion upon a technical objection.

It declared the suppression of 1806 void as to the office of the letter of the dead at Nether Collow and ordered the register to be corrected.

It admitted me to the office.

It declined to order the payment of any arrears, on the ground that the endowment had been applied to the relief of the poor of the said parish for thirty-two years and that the court did not propose to take bread out of the mouths of the poor in order to put money into the hands of a woman who had a house.

It ordered the registrar to pay the costs.

And it ordered that a copy of the judgment be sent to the bishop, and that the file of the office at Nether Collow, having been made in 1811 by the deputy registrar in the place of papers destroyed by fire, be preserved in the registry at Ely and endorsed upon its face with the circumstances of its making, in the hand of the deputy registrar, and that the endorsement be signed by him.

Which meant that Mr. Quill had to write on the front of the file, in his own hand, that the file was his and not a copy of anything, and sign it, and put it in the box. And it would lie there.

He had wanted one line in a box.

He got eleven words, and he wrote them himself, and they were true.

32

The Twenty-Ninth of March

I was angry about the money for about a month.

I want to put that down because it was not a noble anger. It was the anger of a person who has been given a thing and has found that the thing has a hole in it. The court had looked at the whole of it and said that it would not take bread out of the mouths of the poor, and there was two hundred and fifty-four pounds in a twine-tied envelope in a drawer in a house at the end of a lane and that was the whole of what Ellen Rooke got out of thirty-two years.

I sat at my mother’s table in October with the judgment in front of me and said so.

And my mother said, “Write it down.”

“Write what down.”

“That you are angry. In the book. Under the answer.” She was peeling apples and she did not look up. “There is a place in that book for a woman to write what she thinks and there is nobody in this world who is going to read it but you and the one who comes after you, and if you leave out the anger she will think it was all fine.”

So I wrote it in. Four lines, in ink, under the answer about the dog.

✦

The office opened again on the second of November.

There is no ceremony in it. A thing is restored by a court and then a girl sits in a room and waits, and there is no bell and no paper and nobody comes.

I sat in that room for nine days and nobody came.

It is a good room.

I had the book on the table and the ring on my hand and a chair on the other side of the table, and I sat in that chair for nine days and looked at the empty one.

On the ninth day I said to my mother, “Nobody is coming.”

And my mother said, “It took the other one eleven years to be found out and she had a lane full of people who knew. You have been open nine days and you are eighteen and the whole county has been told by a court in Ely that you are a woman who reads things. Give it a month.”

On the eleventh day a woman came from Marle.

She had come seven miles and she stood in the door with her hands in her shawl and said, “Is it true that there is an office here.”

“It is true.”

“My sister died in 1827 and there was a thing I did not ask her and I have not slept since.”

I sat her down and gave her tea because my mother’s rule is that anybody who comes to a door is given tea, and then I said, “What is it you want to know.”

She told me.

I put my hand on the thing she had brought, which was a thimble, and I asked it her question, and I went away for three seconds, and I came back, and I told her.

She sat in that chair and cried for a while and then she wiped her face and said, “Well,” and drank her tea and went home.

That is the whole of the office. There is nothing in it that a man with a pen can put in a column. I was sick for two days and my nose bled on the first night and the answer was not a kind one and I have not written it down here because it is not mine to write down.

She came back in December with a hen.

I said she did not have to bring a hen. She said the hen was not for the office, the hen was for me, and there is a difference, and if I did not know the difference at my age I would learn it.

The hen is called Mrs. Kett. That is a joke and it is not a good one and I am not going to explain it.

✦

The parish book came back from Ely in the second week of November with the court’s seal on the flyleaf and a note in a clerk’s hand that said the book was returned by order and was to be kept in the chest at the church.

My father had it on the table for eight days before he touched it.

On the ninth day he sat down with the good ink and ruled two lines at the foot of the page and made the entry.

1831. October. The office of the letter of the dead at Nether Collow, suppressed by commission in the year 1806, is restored by decree of the consistory court at Ely and the register corrected. Mercy Ashby admitted by presentation of the parish, the ninth of October, no fee.

And underneath, in the remark column:

The entry of 1806 is not to be struck out. It is to stand above this one. A book that hides its own corrections is not a book.

I read it over his shoulder.

“Papa.”

“It is a better line than your grandfather’s,” he said, “and it has taken me thirty-one years to be able to write it, and I would like it noted that I wrote it at this table in a warm kitchen with my wife peeling apples, which is the only condition under which I have ever been able to do anything at all.”

He blotted it and shut the book and put his hand flat on the cover.

“There,” he said. “Now it is in the book, and the book is in the chest, and the chest is in the church, and the church is on the hill, and anybody in this parish who wants to know what happened in 1806 can go up there and read it in two hands and be told the truth.

“That is all a clerk is for. Nobody has ever believed it. It is all a clerk is for.”

✦

Mr. Rooke came on the fourteenth of November at about three in the afternoon with the light going and a bag in his hand.

He put the bag down in the yard and stood in front of me.

“I am not the auditor,” he said.

“I know.”

“They took it off me in October on the judgment, and they were right to, and it was done properly and there was no unpleasantness, and Mr. Gedge said a thing to me at the door that I am not going to repeat because it was kind and I do not know what to do with kind things.”

“Mr. Rooke.”

“Miss Ashby.”

“It is November,” I said. “You said June.”

“I said June and it is November and I am going to tell you why, and then I am going to ask you, and then I am going to go and stand in the road while you think about it, because a man who stands in a yard while a woman thinks about it is a man applying pressure.”

“Tell me why.”

“Because in June I had a place and a house with four rooms and a name in this county. In July I lost the place. I have spent four months finding out what I am without it, and I found that I am a man of forty-four who can read a page to the bottom and who has three hundred pounds and a house with four rooms and a garden he does not keep.

“That is not a great deal to bring to a woman of eighteen whose mother is ill and who has been given an office the whole county is watching.

“So I waited until November. Not because I wanted to be sure of you. I have been sure of you since the ninth of March, in a cold room, with a glove on the table.

“I waited until I was sure of me.”

He stood there with his hat in his hand.

“Miss Ashby. Will you have me.”

I looked at him standing in the yard in the last of the light. He had mud on his boots again.

“Yes,” I said. “And I am going to want it written down.”

He laughed. Once. The way he does.

“I have a pen,” he said.

I crossed the yard and kissed him.

That is the whole of the entry, and it is the one I would have put in the book if a book could hold it. His hat stayed in his hand at his side the whole time, and my hand was flat on his chest and I could feel his heart going like the bell at Collow, cracked note and all, and it was the second time we had kissed and we were exactly as bad at it as we had been the first, and it was the best thing ever said in that yard, and it was me that said it.

“Then come in and use it,” I said, “and my mother will give you tea tomorrow, because she will have heard it off the pump by then and I would like her to hear it from me and not from the pump, and I would like it entered before the light goes.”

✦

Mr. Quill wrote to me once, in December, from a lodging house at Bury, and the letter was one line and there was no compliment at the top of it.

It is in the minutes.

I have that letter in the drawer with the four words and the paper tag that says for J. A. and no thanks, and I take it out about once a year.

✦

My mother had ten months and she used them.

I have been given that morning twice.

The first time I had the hood off the long-case in the passage and the weights in my lap, and I heard her go, and I sat still, and I counted to nine before I stood up.

The second time I put the weights down on the flag and went up.

And she was on the floor by the bed with her hand out and her mouth open and there was a quarter of an hour left and I got her onto the bed and I got the window up and I shouted down the lane for Hettie, and Hettie came, and we got her round, and she was alive, and she stayed alive.

That is the whole of the difference. A quarter of an hour and a window and a girl who went upstairs.

She had ten months and she spent them the way she had spent the thirty-two years before them, which is to say she said very little and did a great deal and never once asked me what I had done or how I had known.

I have wondered about that. I have wondered it a great deal. There is no way for a woman to ask her mother whether she knows that her daughter has been here before, and I never found one, and in the last week I stopped looking for one.

But on the third of January, when she was very bad and the room smelled of the fire and the tallow and the thing that a sickroom smells of at the end, she said, “Come here.”

I came.

“You have been different since the fifth of March,” she said.

I sat down on the edge of the bed.

“Yes.”

“I do not want to know what it is.” Her hand was on the blanket and it was the hand of a woman who has sewed for forty years. “I have thought about it and I have decided that I do not want to know, and I am telling you so that you do not spend the rest of your life wondering whether you should have told me.

“There is a thing I want instead.”

“Anything.”

“You went upstairs,” my mother said. “The first time, you did not. I know that the way I know a thing in a dream and I am not going to argue about it. The first time you sat downstairs with the weights in your lap and you counted to nine.

“And then you came back and you came up the stair.

“That is the whole of it. That is what I have had, ten months of it, and I have not wasted one day of it being grateful, because a mother does not be grateful to her daughter. But I have known every day.

“Now. Sit where I can see you and do not do the thing.”

“What thing.”

“You know what thing.” She had her eyes shut. “You have a hand that goes away for three seconds. I have watched you not do it for ten months in this house. Every time I have been bad you have put your hand on the blanket and not on me, and I have seen you do it, and I have never said anything.

“So do not do it now. Not tonight. Not when it comes.”

“Mama.”

“Say it.”

“I will not do it.”

“Say it properly.”

“I will sit with you and I will hold your hand and I will not go away,” I said. “Not for three seconds. Not for one.”

“Good,” she said. “Now go and let Mrs. Trewin have her tea, she has been standing in that kitchen since six.”

✦

She died on the eleventh of January at about two in the morning and I was upstairs.

I want to put that down in the plainest words I have.

The first time, I was downstairs keeping a clock, and I counted to nine before I stood up, and I have carried those nine seconds for a great many years. Not the death. The nine.

This time I was upstairs and I had been upstairs for eleven days.

The room was hot and there was a fire and a candle and the window open two inches because a closed window in January is a thing only a well person can afford. I was in the chair by the bed with my shoes off. Hettie was asleep in the other chair with her mouth open and I did not wake her.

At about half past one my mother’s breathing changed. I have heard it since, at other beds, and it is the same every time and there is no mistaking it, and anybody who has sat through it once knows it for the rest of their life.

I took her hand.

I want to be exact about this, because it is the whole of the book.

I had her hand in mine and I had the thing in me that goes away for three seconds, and it was there the whole of that hour, quiet, at the back, the way a dog sits at a door.

I did not go.

I sat in a chair in a hot room in January with my mother’s hand in mine and I did not go away for three seconds, and it was the hardest thing I have ever done, and there was no cost to it at all, and no price, and nothing entered anywhere.

At about two she stopped.

I sat there for a while. Then I put her hand down on the blanket and I put my own hands in my lap.

Then I went and woke Hettie.

✦

We were married on the twenty-ninth of March 1832, which was a year to the day from the board, and it rained, and there was a great deal of ham.

Mr. Gedge came. Mr. Craddock came and stood at the back and left before the cake. Mr. Barrow gave us a clock and Hettie said afterwards that a clock was a very odd thing to give a woman who has an office full of them, and I said he had not thought about it, and Hettie said that was the whole trouble with men of property.

Hettie told the duck joke to Mr. Rooke, who did not laugh. Hettie said, “He did not laugh.” I said, “He laughed inside.” Hettie said, “That is not the same thing and I am not going to pretend it is.”

✦

The house at the end of the lane has three rooms down and two up, and the room at the top is the one I have slept in since November, and on the twenty-ninth of March it was cold enough in it to see your breath.

There were people who thought the wedding too soon after my mother. My father did not think so. He said at the door, “She would have been at the front and she would have complained about the ham,” and then he got into the gig and went home, and I have never loved him more than I did at that moment and I have never told him.

Mr. Rooke carried the candle up.

The flame bent backward with the climb and threw his shadow up the stair ahead of him, big on the wall, and the shadow got to the landing first and waited.

I want to put down how that was, because it is not a thing people write down. He had been married before and he had been a widower five years and he was forty-four, and he went up a stair in a house he had never slept in with a candle in his hand and his coat still on, and he was frightened. I am not going to soften that. He was frightened and so was I and we were frightened in the same room and neither of us had a word for it.

I stood with my back to him and said, “The hooks are down the left side. There are nine of them and the fourth does not undo properly and you will want to start at the bottom.”

He said, “I know where to start.”

“I am telling you where to start.”

“Tell me.”

So I did, and he did it, nine hooks and the fourth one twice, and his hands were not steady and I said nothing about that, because a man does not want to be told his hands are not steady. When it was open I held the front of it up with one hand, because a dress falls, and I was not going to stand in a cold room in my shift with a man looking at me while I found somewhere to put a dress.

There is no chair in that room. There is the bed and a chest and a hook on the door.

He took it out of my hand and put it on the hook, and it hung there all night, and in the morning there was a crease across the bodice that I never got out, and I have thought about that crease a hundred times and I have never once wished it out.

He put his hands on my waist. They were cold off the stair and I jumped, and he said, “Sorry,” and I said, “Do not be.” Then he said my name, which he had said once on the ninth of March and taken back and not said since, and it came out of him like a thing he had been carrying.

“Mercy.”

“Say it again.”

“Mercy.”

He kissed me then, the third time in our lives and the first of that night, standing up with his hands at my waist, and it was different from the landing at the George, because on the landing we had been proving a thing and here there was nothing left to prove. It went on. I learned that night that a kiss can go on past the end of itself the way a note goes on in a bell after the striking is done, and that a man who is frightened and a man who is certain can be the same man in the same minute, and that neither of them will show you anything in his face and both of them will show you everything in his hands.

That is all I am putting down of what was said, because the rest of it was not words, and I have never been able to remember the order of it and I am not going to invent an order.

I know that I got my hands under his coat and found the shirt under it, and that the shirt was cold and the skin under the shirt was not. I know he had his hand at the back of my neck, and that it is not a gentle thing, and that I have never once wanted it to be. I know he said, “Tell me if,” and I said, “I will,” and I know that I never had to.

Hettie told me at the pump in 1828 that it hurts, and that anybody who says otherwise is a liar or a man.

Hettie was right about the first part. She was not right about the whole of it, and I have never been able to explain the difference to her and I am not going to try here. Except to say that there was a moment when he stopped and held himself still with his arms braced and his jaw shut and his eyes closed and his weight on his elbows, and I put my hand on the back of his neck and pulled him down and said,

“Now.”

And it was not like the moment before. The moment before was a thing that happened to me. That was a thing I did.

After that there was no arithmetic in the room at all.

I have kept accounts since I was eleven. Boards, steps, pennies, offices, the four hundred and eleven on a schedule in a room in Carrow. Every day of my life. I stopped doing it that night, on a narrow bed in a cold room with a man of forty-four over me and my heels on the backs of his thighs, and I have never properly started again.

It was awkward twice and then it was not. He was heavy and he was hot and he was making a sound that was not a word, and I have heard that sound a great many times since and I have never told anybody about it and I am not going to.

There was the crease in the bodice on the hook. There was the candle, which went out at some point and neither of us got up for. There was the bell at Nether Collow at four in the morning, for somebody, and I heard it and I did not sit up, and I thought: there it goes again, and it is not for us, and one day it will be.

My hands were on him the whole time. His back. His shoulders. The back of his neck where the hair is short.

I did not go anywhere.

That is the only part of that night I would have put in a book, and it is the only part I am putting in this one. I have put my hand on a cup and gone away for three seconds. I have put my hand on a wall and paid for it at four in the morning. I have put my hand on a bed with a woman dead in it and been told a thing that took a year to come true.

I put my hands on a living man and I stayed where I was.

There was no price. I have thought about that for a year and a half and I know now what it means.

The price is for taking.

I did not take.

✦

In the morning he was down before me and had the fire going in the back room and had put the kettle on the wrong side of the hob, and I stood in the door in my shift and watched him do it and did not tell him, and I have never told him, and he still puts it on the wrong side.

The half sheet from the fourteenth of November is in the book at the back, with the blank pages. It says witnessed by nobody.

That was true then.

It is not true now.

33

And Then Be Quiet

The office opened on the second of November and nobody came for nine days, and then they came.

I had the room warm and the lamp trimmed and the frost came up the window at night in ferns, and the lamp burned through nine evenings for nobody, which I decided was not waste. It was kept.

I am going to put down three of them, because three is what a person remembers, and because the rest of them are in the book.

✦

The first was Hobbs, the blacksmith, on the fourteenth of December, with his hat off and his hands washed and his coat brushed on the sleeve where the soot sits.

His wife died in October. Alice Hobbs. I had written the burial and I had written it in the register and I had seen him in the yard afterwards with the same hat in the same hands.

He stood in the door of the back room and did not sit down.

“I want to know if she knew I was there,” he said.

“That is a question.”

“It is a question.”

“You are asking me to ask a dead woman a question about a living man.”

He looked at the floor.

“I am asking you to ask my wife whether she knew I was in the room,” he said. “I was in the room. I held her hand. I want to know if she knew it was mine.”

I had the shawl in the chest. It was in the box with the things nobody had come for.

I put my hand on it.

Three seconds.

There was a kitchen and a fire low and a man sitting with his back to the wall on a chair pulled up too close, and there was a hand, and the hand was known, and there was a woman who was past speaking and who knew the hand and had known it for thirty-one years and did not need to be told.

I came back.

“She knew,” I said. “She knew and she did not open her eyes, and it was not because she could not. It was because it was the last thing she had left to do and she was doing it.”

He stood in the door with his hat in his hands and cried, and he was a big man with a blacksmith’s arms, and he cried the way a big man does, which is silently and for a long time and with his whole face.

He did not thank me. When he went he put sixpence on the table and I pushed it back and he said, “Everybody has to be paid,” and I said, “Not for this,” and he said, “Then what is it for,” and I said, “It is for asking. Asking is free. That is the whole of the difficulty with it.”

✦

The second was a woman from Marle called Wicks, whose boy went into the river at the turn in February of 1829 and was three days in the water before he came up.

She wanted to know whether it took long.

It did not. It was a cold river in February and it took four minutes, and I know that, and I have never told a living soul how I know it and I am not going to put it down here either, because there are things a woman knows about a dead child and there are things she does not put in a book even when the book is the whole point of her.

I told her no. I told her it was quick.

She said, “Are you sure.” I said, “Yes.” She said, “How can you be sure.” I said, “Because I asked.”

She went out into the yard and stood with her hand on the wall for a while and then she went home to Marle, and Mrs. Trewin said afterwards that a woman who has been told a thing like that and believes it walks differently, and I have thought about that a great deal and I believe Mrs. Trewin is right.

That one cost me. The price is not always the same and it is not always at the time. A cup is nothing. A wall is a headache. A river in February is a nosebleed at four in the afternoon and a headache that lasted two days, and it was paid on the spot and I would pay it again.

✦

The third was a man from Wendon who came on a Tuesday in February in a good coat and with a manner, and who wanted to know where his father had put his money.

I said no.

He said, “You answer for the dead.”

“I answer a question put to the dead. I do not search a house for a man who is standing in it.”

“My mother is eighty.”

“Then ask your mother.”

“She will not tell me.”

“That is her right,” I said. “It is the only right in this room that is not mine.”

He went red and said a thing about girls and offices and went out, and Mrs. Trewin heard it from the yard and came in with the poker, and I had to take the poker off her, and she said, “I am sixty-one and I have never hit anybody and I would have started with him.”

There is a rule against money. There is a rule against asking who killed a person. There is a rule against taking a fee. And there is a rule against asking for yourself, which is the one I have broken and will not write about.

Four rules, and I did not make one of them.

✦

I wrote all three in the book, at the back, in my hand, under the year.

1831. December. Q: did she know it was his hand. A: she knew. 1832. February. Q: did it take long. A: no. Four minutes. 1832. February. Q: where is the money. A: not asked. Refused. Rule the first.

There were four hands in that book and one thing in it that is not a hand. Now there are five hands and one thing that is not.

I have thought about the fifth hand a great deal.

✦

My mother died on the eleventh of January and the office did not close for it.

That sounds hard. It was not hard. Mrs. Trewin put a cloth over the table in the back room and told the parish that the office would not sit for a week, and the parish sent a loaf and a piece of bacon and nobody came to the door, and on the eighth day Mrs. Trewin took the cloth off and I sat down again, and the first person through was a girl from Wendon wanting to know whether her sister had been frightened.

She had not been frightened.

That is the whole of what I did in the week my mother died.

✦

Mr. Rooke was at the house most evenings that winter. He was not the auditor and he was not the clerk, and my father was the clerk until Michaelmas. What he was, for four months, was a man with a garden he did not keep and three hundred pounds and nothing at all to do between eight in the morning and dark.

He did the garden. That is the whole of it. He went out in November with a spade and came in in March with a garden, and I have never asked him why, because I know why. A man who has been a clerk since he was nineteen does not know what to do with a morning and will do anything rather than sit in it.

He was in the yard on the afternoon of the nosebleed and he had a cloth in his hand before I got to the door, and he said nothing and he did not ask me what I had been doing, and he has never once asked me what I have been doing, and that is the whole of why I married him.

The rest of it is ham.

✦

There is a thing about an office that nobody warns you of, which is that it makes you a person in a parish whether you want to be one or not. In November there were three women in Nether Collow who would not speak to me at the pump. By April there were none, and I know exactly why, and it is not because they had changed their minds about what I do. It is because I had answered a question for one of them, and she had gone home, and she had told her sister.

There was one chair empty at that table and everybody knew whose it was and nobody said anything about it, which is the correct thing, and I have since decided that it is also the kind thing, and that a parish which says nothing about an empty chair is a parish that has understood something.

Mr. Rooke is the clerk of Nether Collow now. My father stood down at Michaelmas and the vestry chose him, and my father took him into the church and showed him the chest and the three books and the place where the key hangs, and came home and sat down and said, “That is thirty-one years,” and then did not say anything else for two days.

He is not unhappy. He has begun a thing he calls a catalogue, which is a list of every paper in the chest with a note against it of what it is and whether it can be found, and he works at it four hours a day and my mother is not there to tell him to stop.

✦

The letter came on the fourth of May 1832.

It came by the post and it was paid and it was addressed in a hand I did not know and it said, on the front:

To the Letter of the Dead, at Nether Collow, in the county of

There is a space after it and nothing in the space, as though the writer had got that far and then found that she did not want to put the name of her own county on the front of a letter like that in her own hand.

That is all. There was no name and no number and there was a line drawn under the address, and the postmark was a place I had never heard of, a hundred and eleven miles away.

I stood in the yard with it in my hand.

A year before, on the fourth of March, I had been eighteen years old and it had been my birthday, and my mother had been upstairs and I had been downstairs keeping a clock, and I had had no office and no name and no notion of what I was for.

I opened it.

There was one line inside, in the same hand, and it was not signed and there was no address to reply to.

I am told there is such an office. I have a question. I have had it eleven years.

I stood in the yard for a while. The pear tree was in bud and the water was going down the pipe into the butt.

Then I went in and sat down at the table and got out the book and turned to the last page, which has two lines on it in Ellen’s hand and a page before it that is blank, and under the blank page four lines in my hand about a dog, and then the four more I wrote in October under the heading of what I thought of the judgment.

I wrote:

1832. May. Q:

And then I stopped, because a question is a thing that has to be brought to you, and this one had come a hundred and eleven miles in a letter and I had not carried it to the table myself, and a woman who answers a question she has not been asked is a woman answering her own.

I sharpened the pen.

The ring says, on the inside where nobody sees it:

ASK, AND BE ANSWERED. AND THEN BE QUIET.

I have had it a year and a half. I can do the first two.
